And of the Romans (whom you will allow to have been a practical people) nothing is more certain than the value they set upon acquiring verse. To them it was not only (as Dr. Johnson said of Greek) “like old lace—you can never have too much of it.” They cultivated it with a straight eye to national improvement. Among them, as a scholar reminded us the other day, you find “an educational system deliberately and steadily directed towards the development of poetical talent. They were not a people of whom we can say, as we can of the Greeks, that they were born to art and literature.… The characteristic Roman triumphs are the triumphs of a material civilisation.” Rome’s role in the world was “the absorption of outlying genius.” Themselves an unimaginative race with a language not too tractable to poetry, they made great poetry, and they made it of patient set purpose, of hard practice. I shall revert to this and maybe amplify reasons in another lecture. For the moment I content myself with stating the fact that no nation ever believed in poetry so deeply as the Romans.

===Chinese===
我请你们练习书面写作时，是从得体、明晰、准确这些虽低下却必要的东西入手的。但*说服*——至少是最高形式的说服——没有美感意识便无从达成。现在我再来冲第二处激流——*我要你们练习写诗，而且要勤勉不辍地练*。……我是十分认真的。请容我提醒诸位：倘若历史上曾有过一个古代邦国，使我们大不列颠人有充分的权利、更应当有更大的雄心自居为其精神嗣子，那邦国便是帝制罗马。而罗马人（你们会承认他们是个讲求实际的民族）最无可置疑的一点，便是他们赋予习诗的价值。对他们来说，诗不仅仅像约翰逊博士（Dr. Johnson）论希腊文所说的那样——「像老花边——多多益善」。他们培养诗，目光笔直地瞄准民族的进步。就在前几天，一位学者还提醒我们，在他们那里，你会发现「一套自觉而坚定地导向诗才发展的教育制度。他们不像希腊人那样是一个我们可以说生而属于艺术与文学的民族。……罗马人典型的凯旋，是物质文明的凯旋。」罗马在世上的角色，是「吸纳四方边远之地的英才」。他们本身是个缺乏想象力的种族，语言也并不太顺从于诗，他们却造就了伟大的诗，而且是以耐心的定志、艰苦的练习造就的。这个话题我将在另一讲里重提，也许还要补充理由。此刻我只要陈述这个事实：从来没有一个民族像罗马人那样深信诗。

===Original===
Perpend this then, and do not too hastily deride my plea that you should practise verse-writing. I know most of the objections, though I may not remember all. *Mediocribus esse poetis*, etc.—that summarises most of them: yet of an infliction of much bad verse from you, if I am prepared to endure it, why should anyone else complain? I say that the youth of a University ought to practise verse-writing; and will try to bring this home to you by an argument convincing to me, though I have never seen it in print.

===Chinese===
那么请掂量此事，不要过于性急地嘲笑我请你们练习写诗的恳求。那些反对意见，我虽未必记得齐全，也大都知道。*Mediocribus esse poetis*（诗人平庸，神人共弃）云云——这已概括了其中的大多数：然而，你们若硬要塞给我大量坏诗，而我又准备忍受，旁人还有什么可抱怨的呢？我说，一个大学的青年就应当练习写诗；下面这个论证虽从未见诸铅字，我自己却深为信服，愿借此把它送到你们心里。

===Original===
What are the great poetical names of the last hundred years or so? Coleridge, Wordsworth, Byron, Shelley, Landor, Keats, Tennyson, Browning, Arnold, Morris, Rossetti, Swinburne—we may stop there. Of these all but Keats, Browning, Rossetti were University men; and of these three Keats, who died young, cut off in his prime, was the only one not fairly well-to-do. It may seem a brutal thing to say, and it is a sad thing to say: but, as a matter of hard fact, the theory that poetical genius bloweth where it listeth, and equally in poor and rich, holds little truth. As a matter of hard fact, nine out of those twelve were University men: which means that somehow or other they procured the means to get the best education England can give. As a matter of hard fact, of the remaining three you know that Browning was well-to-do, and I challenge you that, if he had not been well-to-do, he would no more have attained to writing “Saul” or *The Ring and the Book* than Ruskin would have attained to writing *Modern Painters* if his father had not dealt prosperously in business. Rossetti had a small private income; and, moreover, he painted. There remains but Keats; whom Atropos slew young, as she slew John Clare in a madhouse, and James Thomson by the laudanum he took to drug disappointment. These are dreadful facts, but let us face them. It is—however dishonouring to us as a nation—certain that, by some fault in our commonwealth, the poor poet has not in these days, nor has had for two hundred years, a dog’s chance. Believe me—and I have spent a great part of the last ten years in watching some 320 Elementary Schools—we may prate of democracy, but actually a poor child in England has little more hope than had the son of an Athenian slave to be emancipated into that intellectual freedom of which great writings are born.

===Chinese===
近百年来伟大的诗名有哪些？柯尔律治（Coleridge）、华兹华斯（Wordsworth）、拜伦（Byron）、雪莱（Shelley）、兰多（Landor）、济慈（Keats）、丁尼生（Tennyson）、勃朗宁（Browning）、阿诺德（Arnold）、莫里斯（Morris）、罗塞蒂（Rossetti）、斯温伯恩（Swinburne）——数到这里可以打住了。这批人里，除济慈、勃朗宁、罗塞蒂外全是上过大学的人；而这三人中，英年早逝、正当盛年便被剪除的济慈，是唯一一个家境不算相当宽裕的。这话说出来近乎残忍，说出来也令人心伤：但作为冷硬的事实，所谓诗才随其所欲而吹拂、贫富一例均沾的那套理论，实在没有多少真理。作为冷硬的事实：那十二人中有九个上过大学——这就是说，他们总归设法弄到了受英格兰所能给予的最优良教育的门路。作为冷硬的事实：剩下三人中，你们知道勃朗宁家境宽裕，而且我敢向你们断言：倘若他不是家境宽裕，他绝写不出《扫罗》（"Saul"）或《指环与书》（*The Ring and the Book*），正如罗斯金（Ruskin）的父亲若非生意兴隆，他也写不出《近代画家》（*Modern Painters*）。罗塞蒂有一份小小的私人收入；何况他还画画。剩下的只有济慈一人；命运女神阿特罗波斯（Atropos）使他夭亡，正如她把约翰·克莱尔（John Clare）杀死在疯人院里，把詹姆斯·汤姆逊（James Thomson）杀死于他用以麻醉失意的鸦片酊。这些都是可怕的事实，但让我们直面它们。尽管这有损我们作为民族的体面，以下一点却是确定的：由于我们共和国体的某种缺失，无论今日还是过去两百年间，穷诗人从未得过半点渺茫的机会。相信我——过去十年间，我花了很大一部分时间去考察大约 320 所初等学校（Elementary Schools）——我们可以高谈民主，可实际上，英格兰的穷孩子要被解放进那孕育伟大著作的思想自由，其希望并不比雅典奴隶之子多出多少。

===Original===
What do I argue from this? I argue that until we can bring more intellectual freedom into our State, more “joy in widest commonalty spread,” upon you, a few favoured ones, rests an obligation to see that the springs of English poetry do not fail. I put it to you that of this glory of our birth and state *you* are the temporary stewards. I put it to the University, considered as a dispenser of intellectual light, that to treat English poetry as though it had died with Tennyson and your lecturers had but to compose the features of a corpse, is to abnegate high hope for the sake of a barren convenience. I put it to the Colleges, considered as disciplinary bodies, that the old way of letting Coleridge slip, chasing forth Shelley, is, after all, not the wisest way. Recollect that in Poesy as in every other human business, the more there are who practise it the greater will be the chance of *someone’s* reaching perfection. It is the impetus of the undistinguished host that flings forward a Diomed or a Hector. And when you point with pride to Milton’s and those other mulberry trees in your Academe, bethink you “What poets are they shading today? Or are their leaves but feeding worms to spin gowns to drape Doctors of Letters?”

===Chinese===
由此我推出什么结论？我的结论是：在我们能把更多思想自由、更多「在最广大的共同体中散播的喜悦」（joy in widest commonalty spread）带进我们的国家之前，你们这少数蒙受眷顾的人，便负有义务看守英国诗的泉源，不使它枯竭。我向诸位申言：这份与生俱来的荣光与地位，*你们*只是临时的管家。我向作为思想之光施与者的大学申言：把英国诗当作已随丁尼生一同死去、你们的讲师们只须为一具尸骸整饰面容，这是为了贫瘠的便利而弃绝崇高的希望。我向作为训导机构的诸学院申言：当年听任柯尔律治悄然溜走、逐走雪莱的那套老办法，终究不是最明智的办法。请记取：在诗这门行当里，正如在人类一切其他行当里一样，习之者众，才愈有人臻于至境。正是无名大众的冲力，把一位狄俄墨得斯（Diomed）或一位赫克托耳（Hector）掷向前方。而当你们自豪地指点弥尔顿（Milton）手植的以及你们学院里另外那些桑树时，请自问：「它们今日荫蔽的是哪些诗人？还是它们的叶子只喂了蚕，让蚕吐丝织成袍子，去披挂一位位文学博士（Doctors of Letters）？」

===Original===
In the life of Benvenuto Cellini you will find this passage worth your pondering.—He is telling how, while giving the last touches to his Perseus in the great square of Florence, he and his workmen inhabited a shed built around the statue. He goes on:—

===Chinese===
在本韦努托·切利尼（Benvenuto Cellini）的生平记事里，你们会发现下面这段值得玩味。——他讲到，在佛罗伦萨大广场上为他的珀尔修斯（Perseus）像作最后修润时，他和工人们就住在围着雕像搭起的一座棚屋里。他接着写道：——

===Original===
> The folk kept on attaching sonnets to the posts of the door.… I believe that, on the day when I opened it for a few hours to the public, more than twenty were nailed up, all of them overflowing with the highest panegyrics. Afterwards, when I once more shut it off from view, everyone brought sonnets, with Latin and Greek verses: for the University of Pisa was then in vacation, and all the doctors and scholars kept vying with each other who could produce the best.

===Chinese===
> 百姓们不断把十四行诗张贴在门柱上。……我相信，在我向公众开放几个小时的那一天，钉上去的就有二十多首，全都洋溢着最高的赞颂。后来，当我再次把它遮蔽起来不见外人时，人人又纷纷送来十四行诗，还有拉丁文和希腊文的诗行：因为比萨大学（University of Pisa）那时正放假，所有的博士和学者们你追我赶，看谁能拿出最好的。

===Original===
I may not live to see the doctors and scholars of this University thus employing the Long Vacation; as perhaps we shall wait some time for another Perseus to excite them to it. But I do ask you to consider that the Perseus was not entirely cause nor the sonnets entirely effect; that the age when men are eager about great work is the age when great work gets itself done; nor need it disturb us that most of the sonnets were, likely enough, very bad ones—in Charles Lamb’s phrase, very like what Petrarch might have written if Petrarch had been born a fool. It is the impetus that I ask of you: the will to try.

===Chinese===
我未必活得到看见本大学的博士与学者们这样度过暑假（Long Vacation）；我们或许还得等上一阵，才能等到另一尊珀尔修斯来激发他们。但我确实要请你们思量：珀尔修斯不全是原因，十四行诗也不全是结果；人们热切关注伟大作品的时代，正是伟大作品得以成就的时代；而那些十四行诗大半多半很糟，也不必搅扰我们——用查尔斯·兰姆（Charles Lamb）的话说，很可能非常像彼特拉克（Petrarch）倘若生而为傻子所会写出的东西。我向你们要求的正是那股冲力：去尝试的意志。

===Original===
Lastly, Gentlemen, do not set me down as one who girds at your preoccupation, up here, with bodily games; for, indeed, I hold “gymnastic” to be necessary as “music” (using both words in the Greek sense) for the training of such youths as we desire to send forth from Cambridge. But I plead that they should be balanced, as they were in the perfect young knight with whose words I will conclude today:—

===Chinese===
最后，诸位先生，不要把我看作一个讥诮你们在此间一心沉迷身体竞技的人；因为说实话，我认为「体育」（gymnastic）与「音乐」（music）——两词皆取希腊义——对于造就我们希望从剑桥送出去的这种青年，是同样必要的。但我请求两者应当均衡，正如那位完美无瑕的年轻骑士身上所做到的那样；今天我就以他的诗行作结：——

===Original===
> Having this day my horse, my hand, my lance
 
 
 Guided so well that I obtained the prize,
 
 
 Both by the judgment of the English eyes
 
 
 And of some sent by that sweet enemy France;
 
 
 Horsemen my skill in horsemanship advance,
 
 
 Town-folk my strength, a daintier judge applies
 
 
 His praise to sleight which from good use doth rise;
 
 
 Some lucky wits impute it but to chance;
 
 
 Others, because of both sides I do take
 
 
 My blood from them who did excel in this,
 
 Think Nature me a man-at-arms did make.
 
 
 How far they shot awry! the true cause is,
 
 
 Stella looked on; and from her heavenly face
 
 
 Sent forth the beams which made so fair my race.

===Chinese===
> 今日我将我的马、我的手、我的长枪

驾驭得这般出色，赢得了奖赏，

这既凭着英国人的眼力评判，

也凭着可爱的敌国法兰西遣来的裁判；

骑手们推许我驭马的技艺，

市民们称道我的膂力，还有更雅致的评判者

将赞誉给予由熟练而生的巧技；

有些走运的机灵人只把它归于机缘；

另一些人，因为我身上的血

取自两族中皆精于此道的先人，

便以为天生注定我做披甲的武人。

他们射得何其偏斜！真正的缘由是：

斯特拉（Stella）在凝望；从她那天国的面庞

放射出光辉，使我的驰骋如此漂亮。

===Original===
“Untrue,” you say? Well, there is truth of emotion as well as of fact; and who is there among you but would fain be able not only to win such a guerdon but to lay it in such wise at your lady’s feet?

===Chinese===
「不真实，」你们会说？好吧，情感之真与事实之真并存；而你们中间，有谁不是巴望着不仅能赢得这样一份酬赏，还能以这般方式把它安放在你们淑女的脚边呢？

===Original===
That then was Philip Sidney, called the peerless one of his age; and perhaps no Englishman ever lived more graciously or, having used life, made a better end. But you have seen this morning’s newspaper: you have read of Captain Scott and his comrades, and in particular of the death of Captain Oates; and you know that the breed of Sidney is not extinct. Gentlemen, let us keep our language noble: for we still have heroes to commemorate!1

===Chinese===
那便是菲利普·西德尼（Philip Sidney），时人称其一代无双；或许再没有哪个英国人活得比他更优雅从容，也没有谁在用尽生命之后，比他收束得更圆满。但今天早晨的报纸你们已经看过：你们读到了斯科特上校（Captain Scott）和他的战友们，特别是奥茨上尉（Captain Oates）之死；于是你们知道，西德尼的血脉并未断绝。诸位先生，让我们保持语言的高贵：因为我们仍有英雄需要纪念！1

## Lecture III On the Difference Between Verse and Prose / III 论诗与散文之别

===Original===
Wednesday, February 26

===Chinese===
2 月 26 日，星期三

===Original===
You will forgive me, Gentlemen, that having in my second lecture encouraged you to the practice of verse as well as of prose, I seize the very next opportunity to warn you against confusing the two, which differ on some points essentially, and always so as to demand separate rules—or rather (since I am shy of the word “rules”) a different concept of what the writer should aim at and what avoid. But you must, pray, understand that what follows will be more useful to the tiro in prose than to the tiro in verse; for while even a lecturer may help you to avoid writing prose in the manner of Milton, only the gods—and they hardly—can cure a versifier of being prosaic.

===Chinese===
诸位先生，请原谅：我在第二讲里既鼓励你们练习诗（韵文，verse），也鼓励你们练习散文（prose），如今却紧接着抓住头一个机会警告你们切莫混淆这两者——它们在某些要点上有本质的差异，而且始终需要各别的规则——或者更确切地说（因为我对「规则」一词心存怯惧），对于作家应当追求什么、避免什么，需要有一种不同的概念。但请你们务必明白，接下去所讲的内容，对初学散文的人比对初学写诗的人更为有用；因为尽管一位讲师或许还能帮你们避免把散文写成弥尔顿（Milton）的那个样子，唯有诸神——而且他们也未必成——才治得好一个写韵文的人流于平庸散文的毛病。

===Original===
We started upon a promise to do without scientific definitions; and in drawing some distinctions today between verse and prose I shall use only a few rough ones; good, as I hope, so far as they go; not to be found contrary to your scientific ones, if ever, under another teacher you attain to them; yet for the moment used only as guides to practice, and pretending to be no more.

===Chinese===
我们当初约定不做科学式的定义；今天在诗与散文之间作些区分时，我也只用几个粗略的界说；但愿它们在所及的范围之内是妥帖的；日后你们若在别的老师门下果真达到了科学的定义，也不至于发现与之相悖；眼下它们只用作实践的指引，且绝不敢自命更多。

===Original===
Thus I go some way—though by no means all the way—towards defining literature when I remind you that its very name (*litterae*—letters) implies the written rather than the spoken word; that, for example, however closely they approximate one to the other as we trace them back, and even though we trace them back to identical beginnings, the Writer—the Man of Letters—does today differ from the Orator. There was a time, as you know, when the poet and the historian had no less than the orator, and in the most literal sense, to “get a hearing.” Nay, he got it with more pains: for the orator had his senate-house or his law-court provided, whereas Thespis jogged to fairs in a cart, and the Muse of History, like any street acrobat, had to collect her own crowd. Herodotus in search of a public packed his history in a portmanteau, carted it to Olympia, found a favourable “pitch,” as we should say, and wooed an audience to him much as on a racecourse nowadays do those philanthropic gentlemen who ply a dubious trade with three half-crowns and a gold chain. It would cost us an effort to imagine the late Bishop Stubbs thus trying his fortune with a bag full of select Charters at Queen’s Club or at Kempton Park, and exerting his lungs to retrieve a crowd that showed some disposition to edge off towards the ring or the rails.

===Chinese===
因此，当我提醒你们，文学之名本身（*litterae*——文字）所指的便是笔下的文字而非口中的言语，我已朝界定文学走了一段路——尽管远非全程：例如，无论我们往上追溯时二者何等逼近，甚至追溯到同一源头，「写作者」——文人——今日毕竟有别于「演说家」。诸位都知道，曾有一个时代，诗人与历史家同演说家一样，必须就最字面的意义而言去「赢得听众」。不，他赢得听众还更费气力：演说家自有元老院或法庭可供使用，而忒斯庇斯（Thespis）只能赶着大车颠簸赶集，历史女神则像任何街头卖艺者一样，得自己去聚拢人群。希罗多德（Herodotus）为觅公众，把他的历史装进旅行皮包，运到奥林匹亚，占下一个如我们今天所说的好「场子」，把听众招揽到身边，那模样恰似今日赛马场上那些以三枚半克朗和一条金链子经营可疑行当的「慈善家」绅士们。若要想象已故的斯塔布斯主教（Bishop Stubbs）也这样提着一袋《特许状选编》（Select Charters），在女王俱乐部（Queen’s Club）或肯普顿公园（Kempton Park）碰运气，扯开嗓门去把一群已露出往投注围场或围栏那边溜之态的看客喊回来，那可得费我们一番想象。

===Original===
The historian’s conditions have improved; and like any other sensible man he has advanced his claim with them, and revised his method. He writes nowadays with his eye on the printed book. He may or may not be a dull fellow: being a dull fellow, he may or may not be aware of it; but at least he knows that, if you lay him upside down on your knee, you can on awaking pick him up, resume your absorption, and even turn back some pages to discover just where or why your interest flagged: whereas a Hellene who deserted Herodotus, having a bet on the Pentathlon, not only missed what he missed but missed it for life.

===Chinese===
历史家的境况已经改善；他与任何明白人一样，随着境况抬高了自己的要求，也修订了自己的方法。如今他写作时，眼睛盯的是印刷成书。他也许乏味，也许不乏味；倘若乏味，他自己也许知道，也许不知道；但至少他明白：你若把他面朝下倒扣在膝上打了个盹，醒来仍可把他拾起，继续沉潜其中，甚至翻回几页，去查究究竟在哪里、又为什么你的兴味衰减了：而一个在五项全能上下了注的希腊人一旦抛开希罗多德，那就不但当时错过了所错过的，而且今生永远错过了。

===Original===
The invention of print, of course, has made all, or almost all, the difference.

===Chinese===
印刷术的发明，自然造成了全部的、或几乎全部的差别。

===Original===
I do not forget that the printed book—the written word—presupposes a speaking voice, and must ever have at its back some sense in us of the speaking voice. But in writing prose nowadays, while always recollecting that prose has its origin in speech—even as it behoves us to recollect that Homer intoned the Iliad to the harp and Sappho plucked her passion from the lyre—we have to take things as they are. Except Burns, Heine, Béranger (with Moore, if you will), and you will find it hard to compile in all the lyrical poetry of the last 150 years a list of half a dozen first-class or even second-class bards who wrote primarily to be sung. It may help you to estimate how far lyrical verse has travelled from its origins if you will but remind yourselves that a “sonnet” and a “sonata” were once the same thing, and that a “ballad” meant a song accompanied by dancing—the word *ballata* having been specialised down, on the one line to the *ballet*, in which Mademoiselle Genée or the Russian performers will dance for our delight, using no words at all; on the other to “Sir Patrick Spens” or “Clerk Saunders,” “ballads” to which no one in his senses would dream of pointing a toe.

===Chinese===
我并未忘记，印刷的书——笔写的文字——以一个说话的声音为前提，其背后永远须有我们心中对说话之声的某种意识。但如今写散文，尽管始终要记着散文源出于言语——正如我们理应记得荷马（Homer）曾和着竖琴吟诵《伊利亚特》（Iliad）、萨福（Sappho）曾从里拉琴上拨出她的激情——我们仍得就事物的现状来接受它们。除了彭斯（Burns）、海涅（Heine）、贝朗瑞（Béranger）（倘若你愿意，再算上穆尔（Moore）），在近 150 年的全部抒情诗中，你很难凑出半打主要为了供人歌唱而写作的一流、乃至二流的歌人。你们只消提醒自己：「十四行诗」（sonnet）与「奏鸣曲」（sonata）曾是一回事，「歌谣」（ballad）原指伴舞而唱的歌——*ballata* 一词沿一条线专门化为*芭蕾*（ballet），热奈小姐（Mademoiselle Genée）或俄国舞者们将在其中为我们起舞娱宾，全然不用一个字；沿另一条线，则专门化为《帕特里克·斯宾斯爵士》（Sir Patrick Spens）或《书记桑德斯》（Clerk Saunders）那样的「歌谣」——神志清醒的人做梦也不会朝它们踮起脚尖。

===Original===
Thus with Verse the written (or printed) word has pretty thoroughly ousted the speaking voice and its auxiliaries—the pipe, the lute, the tabor, the chorus with its dance movements and swaying of the body; and in a quieter way much the same thing is happening to prose. In the Drama, to be sure, we still write (or we should) for the actors, reckon upon their intonations, their gestures, lay account with the tears in the heroine’s eyes and her visible beauty: though even in the Drama today you may detect a tendency to substitute dialectic for action and paragraphs for the *στιχομυθἱα*, the sharp outcries of passion in its give-and-take. Again we still—some of us—deliver sermons from pulpits and orations in Parliament or upon public platforms. Yet I am told that the vogue of the sermon is passing; and (by journalists) that the leading article has largely superseded it. On that point I can offer you no personal evidence; but of civil oratory I am very sure that the whole pitch has been sensibly lowered since the day of Chatham, Burke, Sheridan; since the day of Brougham and Canning; nay, ever since the day of Bright, Gladstone, Disraeli. Burke, as everyone knows, once brought down a Brummagem dagger and cast it on the floor of the House. Lord Chancellor Brougham in a peroration once knelt to the assembled peers, “Here the noble lord inclined his knee to the Woolsack” is, if I remember, the stage direction in Hansard. Gentlemen, though in the course of destiny one or another of you may be called upon to speak daggers to the Treasury Bench, I feel sure you will use none; while, as for Lord Brougham’s genuflections, we may agree that to emulate them would cost Lord Haldane an effort. These and even far less flagrant or flamboyant tricks of virtuosity have gone quite out of fashion. You could hardly revive them today and keep that propriety to which I exhorted you a fortnight ago. They would be out of tune; they would grate upon the nerves; they would offend against the whole style of modern oratory, which steadily tends to lower its key, to use the note of quiet businesslike exposition, to adopt more and more the style of written prose.

===Chinese===
于是在诗这一面，笔写的（或印出的）文字已相当彻底地驱逐了说话之声及其辅翼——笛管、鲁特琴、塔波鼓，连同载歌载舞、摇曳身躯的合唱队；而散文也在以更安静的方式经历着大体相同的事。诚然，在戏剧里，我们如今仍（或者说仍应）为演员而写作，指望他们的语调与姿势，把女主角眼中的泪光与她看得见的美貌也记在账上：不过即便在今日的戏剧里，你们也能察觉一种倾向——以论辩代替动作，以成段的文字代替*στιχομυθἱα*（一来一往的急促对白），那原是激情短兵相接时的尖锐呼喊。再者，我们中有些人仍在讲坛上布道，在议会里或公共讲台上发表演说。然而我听说，布道的风光正在过去；又（听新闻界人士说）社论已大抵取它而代之。这一点我提不出亲身的证据；但对于公共演说，我深信不疑：自查塔姆（Chatham）、伯克（Burke）、谢里丹（Sheridan）的时代以来，自布鲁厄姆（Brougham）与坎宁（Canning）的时代以来，不，自布莱特（Bright）、格莱斯顿（Gladstone）、迪斯累利（Disraeli）的时代以来，整个调门已切实降低了。众所周知，伯克有一次曾把一柄「伯明翰货」匕首（Brummagem dagger，意即赝品）摔在议院的地板上。大法官布鲁厄姆勋爵有一次在结论陈词里竟向与会的贵族们屈膝下跪，「此时这位高贵的勋爵向羊毛袋（Woolsack，大法官席位）俯下了膝」，如果我没记错，这便是《议会实录》（Hansard）里的舞台指示。诸位先生，虽然命运的进程或许会召你们中间这一位或那一位去对财政大臣席（Treasury Bench）「口出匕首」，我深信你们谁也不会真用匕首；至于布鲁厄姆勋爵的屈膝礼，我们尽可同意：要仿效它，哈尔丹勋爵（Lord Haldane）也须费一番力气。这些以及远不及它们骇人、远不及它们炫弄的炫技花招，都已完全过时。今日你们很难复活它们而还保得住两周前我劝勉你们恪守的那份得体。它们会走了调；会磨锉神经；会冒犯现代演说的整个风格——那风格正稳步降低音高，采用安静而干练的陈述口吻，越来越采纳书面散文的风格。

===Original===
Let me help your sense of this change, by a further illustration. Burke, as we know, was never shy of declaiming—even of declaiming in a torrent—when he stood up to speak: but almost as little was he shy of it when he sat down to write. If you turn to his “Letters on a Regicide Peace”—no raw compositions, but penned in his latter days and closing, or almost closing, upon that tenderest of farewells to his country—

===Chinese===
让我再举一例，帮助你们体会这一变化。如我们所知，伯克站起来说话时从不怯于高谈阔论——甚至不怯于洪流般的铺陈：而他坐下来写作时，也几乎同样不怯于此。你们若翻开他的《论弑君者和平的书信》（Letters on a Regicide Peace）——那并非早年的生涩之作，而是晚年手笔，末尾差不多正收束在他对祖国最柔情的一次道别上——

===Original===
> In this good old House, where everything at least is well aired, I shall be content to put up my fatigued horses and here take a bed for the long night that begins to darken upon me—

===Chinese===
> 在这座美好的老宅里——这里至少样样东西都晾晒得通透——我心甘情愿安顿下我疲惫的马匹，并在此要一张床，度过那场开始向我头上压下来的长夜——

===Original===
if, I say, you turn to these “Letters on the Regicide Peace” and consult the titlepage, you will find them ostensibly addressed to “a Member of the present Parliament”; and the opening paragraphs assume that Burke and his correspondent are in general agreement. But skim the pages and your eyes will be arrested again and again by sentences like these:—

===Chinese===
我说，你们若翻开这些《论弑君者和平的书信》，查看扉页，会发现它们表面上是写给「本届议会的一位议员」的；开首几段也假定伯克与这位通信人意见大体一致。但只消把书页匆匆掠过，你们的目光便会一再被这样的句子拦住：——

===Original===
> The calculation of profit in all such wars is false. On balancing the account of such wars, ten thousand hogsheads of sugar are purchased at ten thousand times their price—the blood of man should never be shed but to redeem the blood of man. It is well shed for our family, for our friends, for our God, for our country, for our kind. The rest is vanity; the rest is crime.

===Chinese===
> 这类战争的利润核算是虚假的。结算这类战争的账目时，一万桶糖是以一万倍于其价的代价购来的——人的血，除为赎回人的血之外，永远不该流淌。为我们的家庭、为我们的朋友、为我们的上帝、为我们的国家、为我们的同类而流，是流得其所。其余皆是虚妄；其余皆是罪恶。

===Original===
Magnificent, truly! But your ear has doubtless detected the blank verse—three iambic lines:—

===Chinese===
宏伟之至，真的！不过你们的耳朵想必已经听出了素体诗（blank verse）——三行抑扬格：——

===Original===
> Are purchased at ten thousand times their price…

> Be shed but to redeem the blood of man…

> The rest is vanity; the rest is crime.

===Chinese===
> 是以一万倍于其价的代价购来……

> 除为赎回人的血外永不流淌……

> 其余皆是虚妄；其余皆是罪恶。

===Original===
Again Burke catches your eye by rhetorical inversions:—

===Chinese===
伯克又以修辞的倒装攫住你们的眼睛：——

===Original===
> But too often different is rational conjecture from melancholy fact.

> Well is it known that ambition can creep as well as soar.

===Chinese===
> 然而往往，理性的推测与可悲的事实竟是两回事。

> 世人深知，野心既能高翔，亦能匍匐。

===Original===
by repetitions:—

===Chinese===
又以反复：——

===Original===
> Never, no never, did Nature say one thing and Wisdom say another… Algiers is not near; Algiers is not powerful; Algiers is not our neighbour; Algiers is not infectious. Algiers, whatever it may be, is an old creation; and we have good data to calculate all the mischief to be apprehended from it. When I find Algiers transferred to Calais, I will tell you what I think of that point—

===Chinese===
> 从来，从来不曾，自然说一件事而智慧说另一件……阿尔及尔不远；阿尔及尔不强；阿尔及尔不是我们的近邻；阿尔及尔没有传染之虞。阿尔及尔不管是什么，总是一宗古旧的造物；我们握有充分的数据，足以推算从它那里可以预期的一切祸害。哪天我看见阿尔及尔搬到了加来，我再告诉你我对这一点的看法——

===Original===
by quick staccato utterances, such as:—

===Chinese===
又以急促顿挫的短句，例如：——

===Original===
> And is this example nothing? It is everything. Example is the school of mankind, and they will learn at no other—

===Chinese===
> 而这个先例算得了什么？它就是一切。先例是人类的大课堂，人除了在这里，不会在别处受教——

===Original===
or

===Chinese===
或者

===Original===
> Our dignity? That is gone. I shall say no more about it. Light lie the earth on the ashes of English pride!

===Chinese===
> 我们的尊严？早已去了。对此我不再多说。愿泥土轻轻覆盖在英国骄傲的灰烬之上！

===Original===
I say that the eye or ear, caught by such tropes, must (if it be critical) recognize them at once as *rhetoric*, as the spoken word masquerading under guise of the written. Burke may pretend to be seated, penning a letter to a worthy man who will read it in his slippers: but actually Burke is up and pacing his library at Beaconsfield, now striding from fireplace to window with hands clasped under his coat tails, anon pausing to fling out an arm with some familiar accustomed gesture in a House of Commons that knows him no more, towards a Front Bench peopled by shades. In fine the pretence is Cicero writing to Atticus, but the style is Cicero denouncing Catiline.

===Chinese===
我说，被这类修辞格（tropes）攫住的眼睛或耳朵，若是具有批评之明，便必须立刻认出它们是*修辞*（rhetoric），是乔装成书面文字模样的口头言辞。伯克尽可以装作安坐着，给一位将趿着拖鞋捧读此信的忠厚君子修书：可实际上，伯克正起身在贝肯斯菲尔德（Beaconsfield）的书房里踱步，时而双手拢在礼服燕尾之下、从壁炉边大步走到窗前，时而又顿住，伸出一臂，摆出某个熟惯的姿势——在一座再也不认识他的下议院里，朝着一排坐满幽灵的前排议席。总而言之，那姿态是西塞罗（Cicero）致书阿提库斯（Atticus），那风格却是西塞罗痛斥喀提林（Catiline）。

===Original===
As such it is not for your imitation. Burke happened to be a genius, with a swoop and range of mind, as of language to interpret it, with a gift to enchant, a power to strike and astound, which together make him, to my thinking, the man in our literature most nearly comparable with Shakespeare. Others may be more to your taste; you may love others better: but no other two leave you so hopeless of discovering *how it is done*. Yet not for this reason only would I warn you against imitating either. For like all great artists they accepted their conditions and wrought for them, and those conditions have changed. When Jacques wished to recite to an Elizabethan audience that

===Chinese===
如此说来，它不可供你们仿效。伯克恰是一位天才，其心智的俯冲与辽阔，恰如那用以诠释心智的语言的俯冲与辽阔；他有一种蛊惑人心的天赋，有一种足以撼人心魄、令人惊愕的力量——凡此合在一起，依我之见，使他成为我们文学中最近乎可与莎士比亚（Shakespeare）并举的一人。你们或许更中意旁人，或许更爱旁人：但再没有别的哪两位，能像他们俩那样，令你们对*这是如何做到的*感到全然无望。然而我要告诫你们莫仿效这两人，并非仅仅为此一端。因为同一切伟大的艺术家一样，他们接受了各自的境况并为之营作，而那些境况已经变了。当杰奎斯（Jacques）要向伊丽莎白时代的观众诵出

===Original===
> All the world’s a stage,

> And all the men and women merely players—

===Chinese===
> 全世界是一座舞台，

> 所有的男男女女不过是一些演员——

===Original===
or Hamlet to soliloquise

===Chinese===
或者哈姆莱特（Hamlet）要独白

===Original===
> To be, or not to be: that is the question—

===Chinese===
> 生存还是毁灭：这是一个问题——

===Original===
the one did not stretch himself under a property oak, nor did the other cast himself back in a chair and dangle his legs. They both advanced boldly from the stage, down a narrow platform provided for such recitations and for that purpose built boldly forward into the auditorium, struck an attitude, declaimed the purple passage, and returned, covered with applause, to continue the action of the play. This was the theatrical convention; this the audience expected and understood; for this Shakespeare wrote. Similarly, though the device must have been wearing thin even in 1795–6, Burke cast a familiar epistle into language proper to be addressed to Mr. Speaker of the House of Commons. Shakespeare wrote, as Burke wrote, for his audience; and their glory is that they have outlasted the conditions they observed. Yet it was by observing them that they gained the world’s ear. Let us, who are less than they, beware of scorning to belong to our own time.

===Chinese===
前者并不曾伸展开四肢躺到一株道具橡树之下，后者也不曾仰面倒进椅子里晃荡双腿。他们两人都从舞台上大胆向前，走上一条专为这类朗诵而设、并为此目的大胆伸入观众席的窄台，摆好一个姿势，朗诵完那段华彩的段落，然后带着满身掌声返回，继续戏的情节。这便是当年戏剧的成规；观众期待它、也懂得它；莎士比亚正是为此而写。同样地，尽管这一招即便在 1795–6 年间想必已经磨得渐薄，伯克仍把一封家常书简铸成了宜于向下议院议长先生（Mr. Speaker）致辞的语言。莎士比亚同伯克一样，是为其观众而写；他们的光荣在于，他们比自己所遵守的那些条件活得更久。然而正是靠着遵守那些条件，他们才赢得了世人的耳朵。我们既不及他们，就切勿鄙薄属于自己的时代。

===Original===
For my part I have a great hankering to see English Literature feeling back through these old modes to its origins. I think, for example, that if we studied to write verse that could really be sung, or if we were more studious to write prose that could be read aloud with pleasure to the ear, we should be opening the pores to the ancient sap; since the roots are always the roots, and we can only reinvigorate our growth through them.

===Chinese===
就我而言，我极愿看到英国文学循着这些古老的方式，摸索回它的源头。譬如我想，倘若我们用心去写真正能供吟唱的诗，或者更用心去写朗读起来耳中愉悦的散文，我们便是在为古老的浆汁打开毛孔；因为根终究是根，我们唯有通过根，才能重新振起我们的生长。

===Original===
Unhappily, however, I cannot preach this just yet; for we are aiming at practice, and at Cambridge (they tell me) while you speak well, you write less expertly. A contributor to *The Cambridge Review*, a fortnight ago, lamented this at length: so you will not set the aspersion down to me, nor blame me if these early lectures too officiously offer a kind of “First Aid”: that, while all the time eager to descant on the *affinities* of speech and writing, I dwell first on their *differences*; or that, in speaking of Burke, an author I adore only “on this side idolatry,” I first present him in some aspects for your avoidance. Similarly I adore the prose of Sir Thomas Browne, yet should no more commend it to you for instant imitation than I could encourage you to walk with a feather in your cap and a sword under your gown. Let us observe proprieties.

===Chinese===
然而不幸，眼下我还不能宣讲这一层道理；因为我们的目标在于实践，而在剑桥（人家告诉我），你们虽然说得好，笔下却欠娴熟。两周前《剑桥评论》（The Cambridge Review）上便有一位撰稿人就此慨叹良久：所以你们不要把这番贬词记到我账上，也不要怪这些开初几讲过分热心地提供某种「急救」——即：我明明时刻渴望细谈言说与书写的*亲缘*（affinities），却先盯着它们的*差异*（differences）；又或者：谈论伯克这位我热爱到「仅止于偶像崇拜这一侧」（on this side idolatry）的作家时，却先把他好些方面当作你们应当回避的东西呈上来。同样，我热爱托马斯·布朗爵士（Sir Thomas Browne）的散文，却不会把它推荐给你们供即刻仿效，正如我不能怂恿你们帽上插着羽毛、长袍底下藏着剑走路。让我们恪守得体之分。

===Original===
To return to Burke.—At his most flagrant, in these “Letters on the Regicide Peace,” he boldly raids Shakespeare. You are all, I doubt not, conversant with the Prologue to *Henry the Fifth*:—

===Chinese===
回到伯克。——在他最肆无忌惮处，在这些《论弑君者和平的书信》里，他公然劫掠莎士比亚。你们想必个个都熟稔《亨利五世》（Henry the Fifth）的序曲：——

===Original===
> O for a Muse of fire, that would ascend

> The brightest heaven of invention!

> A kingdom for a stage, princes to act

> And monarchs to behold the swelling scene!

> Then should the warlike Harry, like himself,

> Assume the port of Mars: and at his heels,

> Leash’d in like hounds, should Famine, Sword and Fire

> Crouch for employment.

===Chinese===
> 啊，但愿有一位火红的缪斯，她将登上

> 幻想的最明亮的天堂！

> 拿一个王国做舞台，让亲王们充任演员，

> 让君主们瞻眺这波澜壮阔的场景！

> 那时，好战的哈利才像他本来的自己，

> 摆出战神的威仪：而在他的脚跟后，

> 像猎犬般拴上皮带的，应是「饥荒」「刀剑」与「烈火」，

> 蹲伏待命。

===Original===
Well, this passage Burke, assuming his correspondent to be familiar with it, boldly claps into prose and inserts into a long diatribe against Pitt for having tamely submitted to the rebuffs of the French Directory. Thus it becomes:—

===Chinese===
好，这一段，伯克既认定他的通信人对它烂熟，便大胆地把它拍进散文，嵌进一篇斥责皮特（Pitt）对法兰西督政府（French Directory）的挫辱一味逆来顺受的长篇檄文里。于是它变成了：——

===Original===
> On that day it was thought he would have assumed the port of Mars: that he would bid to be brought forth from their hideous kennel (where his scrupulous tenderness had too long immured them) those impatient dogs of War, whose fierce regards affright even the minister of vengeance that feeds them; that he would let them loose in Famine, Plagues and Death, upon a guilty race to whose frame and to all whose habit, Order, Peace, Religion and Virtue, are alien and abhorrent.

===Chinese===
> 那一天，人们本以为他会摆出战神的威仪：本以为他会吩咐把那些不耐烦的战犬从其狰狞的犬舍（他那谨小慎微的温柔把它们幽禁得太久了）里牵出来——它们凶猛的目光甚至吓得住喂养它们的复仇之仆；本以为他会在「饥荒」「瘟疫」与「死亡」之中放开它们，去扑向一个有罪的种族——这种族的肌体、这种族的全部习性，都与秩序、和平、宗教与美德相异而相憎。

===Original===
Now Shakespeare is but apologising for the shortcomings of his playhouse, whereas Burke is denouncing his country’s shame and prophesying disaster to Europe. Yet do you not feel with me that while Shakespeare, using great words on the lowlier subject, contrives to make them appropriate, with Burke, writing on the loftier subject, the same or similar words have become tumid, turgid?

===Chinese===
莎士比亚不过是在为他那座戏园的短处告罪，伯克却是在痛陈邦国之耻、预言欧洲的灾殃。然而你们难道不与我同感：莎士比亚以较卑的题材用雄伟的字眼，尚能设法使它们各得其所；伯克以更高的题材落笔，同样的、或相似的字眼却变得虚浮臃肿了么？

===Original===
Why? I am sure that the difference lies not in the two men: nor is it all the secret, or even half the secret, that Burke is mixing up the spoken with the written word, using the one while pretending to use the other. That has carried us some way; but now let us take an important step farther. The root of the matter lies in certain essential differences between verse and prose. We will keep, if you please, to our rough practical definitions. Literature—the written word—is a permanent record of memorable speech; a record, at any rate, intended to be permanent. We set a thing down in ink—we print it in a book—because we feel it to be memorable, to be worth preserving. But to set this memorable speech down we must choose one of two forms, verse or prose; and I define verse to be a record in metre and rhythm, prose to be a record which, dispensing with metre (abhorring it indeed), uses rhythm laxly, preferring it to be various and unconstrained, so always that it convey a certain pleasure to the ear.

===Chinese===
为什么？我确信，这差异不在这两个人身上：而且，伯克把口说之辞同笔写之辞搅和在一起、用着前者却假装用着后者，这也不是全部的秘密，甚至连一半的秘密都不是。这已带我们走了一程；但现在让我们再迈出重要的一步。事情的根子，在于诗与散文之间某些本质的差异。请容我们仍守着那几个粗略而实用的定义。文学——笔写的文字——是对值得纪念的言辞所作的一份永久记录；无论如何，是一份意在永久的记录。我们用墨水把一件事写下来——把它印成书——是因为我们感到它值得纪念、值得保存。但要把这值得纪念的言辞记下来，我们必须在两种形式中择取其一：诗，或散文。我把诗定义为以格律与节奏作成的记录；把散文定义为这样一份记录：它免用格律（实在是憎恶格律），宽松地使用节奏，宁取其多变、不受拘束——只是永远须给耳朵传递某种愉悦。

===Original===
You observe that I avoid the term Poetry, over which the critics have waged, and still are waging, a war that promises to be endless. Is Walt Whitman a poet? Is the Song of Songs (which is not Solomon’s)—is the Book of Job—are the Psalms—all of these as rendered in our Authorised Version of Holy Writ—are all of these poetry? Well “yes,” if you want my opinion; and again “yes,” I am sure. But truly on this field, though scores of great men have fought across it—Sidney, Shelley, Coleridge, Scaliger (I pour the names on you at random), Johnson, Wordsworth, the two Schlegels, Aristotle with Twining his translator, Corneille, Goethe, Warton, Whately, Hazlitt, Emerson, Hegel, Gummere—but our axles grow hot. Let us put on the brake: for in practice the dispute comes to very little: since literature is an art and treats scientific definitions as J. K. Stephen recommended. From them

===Chinese===
你们当已注意到，我避而不用「诗歌」（Poetry）这个词——批评家们围绕它打了一场、而且还在打着一场有望永无休止的战争。沃尔特·惠特曼（Walt Whitman）算诗人么？《雅歌》（其实并不是所罗门的）——《约伯记》——《诗篇》——所有这些，照我们钦定本（Authorised Version）圣书所译出的样子——都算诗歌么？好吧，「算」，如果你们要问我的意见；而且我敢肯定，还是「算」。可是说真的，在这片战场上，虽有大群的伟人曾横越鏖战——锡德尼（Sidney）、雪莱（Shelley）、柯尔律治（Coleridge）、斯卡利格（Scaliger）（这些名字我是随手向你们倾倒的）、约翰逊（Johnson）、华兹华斯（Wordsworth）、施莱格尔兄弟（the two Schlegels）、亚里士多德（Aristotle）连同他的译者特威宁（Twining）、高乃依（Corneille）、歌德（Goethe）、沃顿（Warton）、惠特利（Whately）、赫兹里特（Hazlitt）、爱默生（Emerson）、黑格尔（Hegel）、甘米尔（Gummere）——可我们的车轴已经发烫了。让我们踩下闸来：因为在实践中，这场争吵实在谈不出多少名堂：因为文学是一门艺术，它对待科学定义的方式，正照 J. K. 斯蒂芬（J. K. Stephen）所劝告的那样。它从定义那里

===Original===
> It finds out what it cannot do,

> And then it goes and does it.

===Chinese===
> 先探明自己做不到什么，

> 然后便径自去把它做到。

===Original===
I am journeying, say, in the West of England. I cross a bridge over a stream dividing Devon from Cornwall. These two counties, each beautiful in its way, are quite unlike in their beauty: yet nothing happened as I stepped across the brook, and for a mile or two or even ten I am aware of no change. Sooner or later that change will break upon the mind and I shall be startled, awaking suddenly to a land of altered features. But at what turn of the road this will happen, just how long the small multiplied impressions will take to break into surmise, into conviction—that nobody can tell. So it is with poetry and prose. They are different realms, but between them lies a debatable land which a De Quincey or a Whitman or a Paul Fort or a Marinetti may attempt. I advise you who are beginners to keep well one side or other of the frontier, remembering that there is plenty of room and what happened to Tupper.

===Chinese===
譬如说，我正在英格兰西部旅行。我跨过一座桥，桥下的溪水分隔着德文郡（Devon）与康沃尔郡（Cornwall）。这两郡各以其道成其美，其美却判然有别：然而我跨过溪水时什么事也没有发生，走出一两英里、甚至十英里，我都不觉得有变。迟早，那变化会在心头豁然砸下，我会悚然一惊，骤然醒来面对一片面目已改的土地。但这变化会在道路的哪个转弯上发生，那些细小而累积的印象要多久才会破成猜测、进而破成确信——那谁也说不上来。诗歌与散文正是如此。它们是两个不同的国度，但两国之间横亘着一片可争之地，德·昆西（De Quincey）、惠特曼、保罗·福尔（Paul Fort）或马里内蒂（Marinetti）之流或可闯上一闯。我奉劝你们这些初学者稳稳守在边界的这一侧或那一侧，记住两侧之内尽有天地，也记住塔珀（Tupper）的下场。

===Original===
If we restrict ourselves to the terms “verse” and “prose,” we shall find the line much easier to draw. Verse is memorable speech set down in metre with strict rhythms; prose is memorable speech set down without constraint of metre and in rhythms both lax and various—so lax, so various, that until quite recently no real attempt has been made to reduce them to rule. I doubt, for my part, if they can ever be reduced to rule; and after a perusal of Professor Saintsbury’s latest work, *A History of English Prose Rhythm*, I am left doubting. I commend this book to you as one that clears up large patches of forest. No one has yet so well explained what our prose writers, generation after generation, have tried to do with prose: and he has, by the way, furnished us with a capital anthology—or, as he puts it, with “divers delectable draughts of example.” But the road still waits to be driven. Seeking practical guidance—help for our present purpose—I note first that many a passage he scans in one way may as readily be scanned in another; that when he has finished with one and can say proudly with Wordsworth:—

===Chinese===
倘若我们只用「诗」（韵文）与「散文」这两个名目，便会发现这条界线好画得多。诗，是以格律和严格的节奏记下的值得纪念的言辞；散文，是不受格律约束、以宽松而多变的节奏记下的值得纪念的言辞——其宽松、其多变至于如此，以至于直到晚近之前，还没有人认真尝试过把它们归纳为规则。就我个人而言，我怀疑它们究竟能否归纳为规则；而在通读圣茨伯里教授（Professor Saintsbury）的新著《英语散文节奏史》（A History of English Prose Rhythm）之后，我仍旧疑着。我把这本书推荐给你们：它清出了一大片密林。至今还没有人这样清楚地解释过，我们的散文作家一代又一代想拿散文做些什么：而且他还顺便供给了我们一部上好的选集——或者照他自己的说法，「种种甘美可口的示例佳酿」。但那条路仍等着人去开通。寻求实用的指引——寻求切合我们眼下目的的帮助——我首先注意到：他按这一种方式划分节律的许多段落，换另一种方式同样划得通；以至于当他处理完一段、能同华兹华斯一起自豪地说：——

===Original===
> I’ve measured it from side to side,

> ’Tis three feet long and two feet wide,

===Chinese===
> 我把它从这边量到那边，

> 它三英尺长、两英尺宽，

===Original===
we still have a sensation of coming out (our good master with us) by that same door wherein we went; and I cannot as yet after arduous trial discover much profit in his table of feet—Paeons, Dochmiacs, Antispasts, Proceleusmatics and the rest—an Antispast being but an iamb followed by a trochee, and Proceleusmatic but two pyrrhics, or four consecutive short syllables—when I reflect that, your possible number of syllables being as many as five to a foot, you may label them (as Aristotle would say) until you come to infinity, where desire fails, without getting nearer any rule of application.

===Chinese===
我们仍旧只觉得（我们那位好老师与我们一道）从当初进去的那同一扇门里走了出来；至于他的音步表——帕安格（Paeons）、多克米格（Dochmiacs）、安提斯帕斯特格（Antispasts）、四短格（Proceleusmatics）等等——安提斯帕斯特格不过是一个抑扬格后接一个扬抑格，四短格不过是两个双短格、即四个连续的短音节——经过艰苦的试验，我至今看不出其中有多少实益；尤其想到，一个音步所能容纳的音节既可多至五个，你便可以（照亚里士多德的说法）给它们逐一贴标签，一直贴到无穷、贴到欲望衰竭之处，却始终挨不着任何一条应用的规则。

===Original===
Let us respect a genuine effort of learning, though we may not detect its immediate profit. In particular let us respect whatever Professor Saintsbury writes, who has done such splendid work upon English verse-prosody. I daresay he would retort upon my impatience grandly enough, quoting Walt Whitman:—

===Chinese===
让我们尊重一项真正的学问努力，尽管我们未必看得出它的眼前之益。尤其是，圣茨伯里教授在英语诗律学（verse-prosody）上建树如此辉煌，凡他所写都值得我们尊重。我敢说，他会相当堂皇地回敬我的不耐，引沃尔特·惠特曼的话：——

===Original===
> I am the teacher of athletes;

> He that by me spreads a wider breast than my own proves the width of my own;

> He most honours my style who learns under it to destroy the teacher.

===Chinese===
> 我是竞技者的教师；

> 凡借我而展开比我自己更宽阔胸膛的人，恰恰证明了我自己胸膛的宽度；

> 在我这里学会了摧毁教师的人，最敬重我的风格。

===Original===
His speculations may lead to much in time; though for the present they yield us small instruction in the path we seek.

===Chinese===
他的这些玄思，假以时日或能引出许多东西；只是眼下，在我们所寻求的道路上，它们给我们的教益甚微。

===Original===
It is time we harked back to our own signposts. Verse is written in metre and strict rhythm; prose, without metre and with the freest possible rhythm. That distinction seems simple enough, but it carries consequences very far from simple. Let me give you an illustration taken almost at hazard from Milton, from the Second Book of *Paradise Regained*:—

===Chinese===
也该回到我们自己的路标上来了。诗以格律与严格的节奏写成；散文则不用格律，而用尽可能自由的节奏。这一区分看似够简单，它带来的后果却远不简单。让我从弥尔顿《复乐园》（Paradise Regained）第二卷里几乎信手拈来一例：——

===Original===
> Up to a hill anon his steps he reared

> From whose high top to ken the prospect round,

> If cottage were in view, sheepcote or herd;

> But cottage, herd, or sheepcote, none he saw.

===Chinese===
> 随即他把脚步抬上一座小山，

> 要从那高顶眺望四野的景色，

> 看有没有村舍入眼，羊圈或牛群；

> 但村舍、牛群或羊圈，他一无所见。

===Original===
These few lines are verse, are obviously verse with the accent of poetry; while as obviously they are mere narrative and tell us of the simplest possible incident—how Christ climbed a hill to learn what could be seen from the top. Yet observe, line for line and almost word for word, how strangely they differ from prose. Mark the inversions: “Up to a hill anon his steps he reared,” “But cottage, herd, or sheepcote, none he saw.” Mark next the diction—“his steps he reared.” In prose we should not rear our steps up the Gog-magog hills, or even more Alpine fastnesses; nor, arrived at the top, should we “ken” the prospect round; we might “con,” but should more probably “survey” it. Even “anon” is a tricky word in prose, though I deliberately palmed it off on you a few minutes ago. Mark thirdly the varied repetition, “if cottage were in view, sheepcote or herd—but cottage, herd, or sheepcote, none he saw.” Lastly compare the whole with such an account as you or I or Cluvienus would write in plain prose:—

===Chinese===
这几行是诗，显然是带着诗歌重音的诗；而同样显然，它们纯属叙述，讲的是再简单不过的一桩事——基督（Christ）如何爬上一座山，想了解从山顶能望见什么。然而请逐行、并且几乎逐字地观察，它们与散文何等奇妙地不同。注意那些倒装：「Up to a hill anon his steps he reared」（随即他把脚步抬上一座小山）、「But cottage, herd, or sheepcote, none he saw」（但村舍、牛群或羊圈，他一无所见）。再注意那措辞——「his steps he reared」（他把脚步抬上）。写散文时，我们不会把脚步「抬」（rear）上戈格玛格丘陵（Gog-magog），连更阿尔卑斯式的险峰也不行；登了顶，我们也不会去「眺」（ken）四野的景色——或许会「细看」（con），但多半是「纵览」（survey）。连「随即」（anon）在散文里也是个滑头的词，尽管几分钟前我故意把它偷偷塞给了你们。第三，注意那变化了的重复：「if cottage were in view, sheepcote or herd」——「but cottage, herd, or sheepcote, none he saw」（若有村舍入眼，羊圈或牛群——但村舍、牛群、羊圈，一无所见）。最后，请把整段同这样一篇记述作比——那是你、是我、或是克卢维埃努斯（Cluvienus）都会用平白的散文写下的：——

===Original===
> Thereupon he climbed a hill on the chance that the view from its summit might disclose some sign of human habitation—a herd, a sheepcote, a cottage perhaps. But he could see nothing of the sort.

===Chinese===
> 于是他爬上一座山，指望山顶的视野或能显出人烟的迹象——一群牛、一个羊圈、或许一座村舍。但诸如此类的东西，他一样也没有看见。

===Original===
But you will ask, “*Why* should verse and prose employ diction so different? *Why* should the one invert the order of words in a fashion not permitted to the other?” and I shall endeavour to answer these questions together with a third which, I dare say, you have sometimes been minded to put when you have been told—and truthfully told—by your manuals and histories, that when a nation of men starts making literature it invariably starts on the difficult emprise of verse, and goes on to prose as by an afterthought. Why should men start upon the more difficult form and proceed to the easier? It is not their usual way. In learning to skate, for instance, they do not cut figures before practising loose and easy propulsion.

===Chinese===
但你们会问：「*为什么*诗与散文要使用如此不同的措辞？*为什么*这一方可以用那一方不许的方式颠倒词序？」我将设法一并回答这两个问题，外加第三个——我敢说，当你们的读本和文学史告诉你们（而且说得属实）：一个人类族群一旦开始制造文学，总是无一例外地从诗这件艰难的大业起步，把散文当作事后追加的想法——你们有时想必也起意要问：人为什么偏从较难的形式着手，再进到较易的形式？这可不是他们惯常的路数。譬如学滑冰，人们并不先练花式滑法，再练宽松自如的滑行。

===Original===
The answer is fairly simple. Literature (once more) is a record of memorable speech; it preserves in words a record of such thoughts or of such deeds as we deem worth preserving. Now if you will imagine yourself a very primitive man, lacking paper or parchment; or a slightly less primitive, but very poor, man to whom the price of parchment and ink is prohibitive; you have two ways of going to work. You can carve your words upon trees or stones (a laborious process) or you can commit them to memory and carry them about in your head; which is cheaper and handier. For an illustration, you find it useful, anticipating the tax-collector, to know how many days there are in the current month. But further you find it a nuisance and a ruinous waste of time to run off to the tribal tree or monolith whenever the calculation comes up; so you invent a formula, and you cast that formula into *verse* for the simple reason that verse, with its tags, alliterations, beat of syllables, jingle of rhymes (however your tribe has chosen to invent it), has a knack, not possessed by prose, of sticking in your head. You do not say, “Quick thy tablets, memory! Let me see—January has 31 days, February 28 days, March 31 days, April 30 days.” You invent a verse:—

===Chinese===
答案相当简单。文学（再说一遍）是对值得纪念的言辞的记录；它以文字保存我们认为值得保存的那些思想或事迹。现在，倘若你们肯设想自己是一个很原始的人，没有纸，也没有羊皮纸；或者一个稍不原始、却穷得厉害的人，羊皮纸和墨水的价钱贵得叫他不敢问津；那么行起事来，路子有两条。你可以把话刻在树上或石头上（一道费工的工序），也可以把它们托付给记忆、顶在脑袋里随身携带；这更便宜，也更顺手。举个例子：赶在税吏前头，先知道本月有多少天，对你是有用的。可你再进一步又发现，每逢这桩计算冒头，便得跑去部落的那棵树或那块独石跟前，既惹人厌烦，又旷时废日得厉害；于是你发明一个程式，又把这程式铸成*韵文*——理由简单得很：诗，凭着它的口头搭子、头韵、音节的拍子、韵脚的叮当（任凭你的部落发明的是哪一样），自有一股散文所无的巧劲，能牢牢粘在你的脑袋里。你不会说：「记忆，快取你的蜡板来！让我想想——一月有 31 天，二月 28 天，三月 31 天，四月 30 天。」你会发明一首诗：——

===Original===
> Thirty days hath September,

> April, June and November…

===Chinese===
> 九月有三十天，

> 四月、六月和十一月……

===Original===
Nay, it has been whispered to me, Gentlemen, that in this University some such process of memorising in verse has been applied by bold bad irreverently-minded men even to the *Evidences* of our cherished Paley.

===Chinese===
不，诸位先生，还有人向我咬耳朵说，在这所大学里，竟有胆大包天、心术不正、全无敬意之徒，把这路用诗记诵的法子，连我们宝爱的佩利（Paley）的《论证据》（*Evidences*）都套了上去。

===Original===
This, you will say, is mere verse, and not yet within measurable distance of poetry. But wait! The men who said the more memorable things, or sang them—the men who recounted deeds and genealogies of heroes, plagues and famines, assassinations, escapes from captivity, wanderings and conquests of the clan, all the “old, unhappy, far-off things and battles long ago”—the men who sang these things for their living, for a supper, a bed in the great hall, and something in their wallet to carry them on to the next lordship—these were gentlemen, scops, bards, minstrels (call them how you will), a professional class who had great need of a full repertory in a land swarming with petty chieftains, and to adapt their strains to the particular hall of entertainment. It would never do, for example, to flatter the prowess of the Billings in the house of the Hoppings, their hereditary foes, or to bore the Wokings (who lived where the crematorium now is) with the complicated genealogy of the Tootings: for this would have been to miss that appropriateness which I preached to you in my second lecture as a preliminary rule of good writing. Nay, when the Billings intermarried with the Tootings—when the Billings took to cooing, so to speak—a hasty blend of excerpts would be required for the “Epithalamium.” So it was all a highly difficult business, needing adaptability, a quick wit, a goodly stock of songs, a retentive memory and every artifice to assist it. Take “Widsith,” for example, the “far-travelled man.” He begins:—

===Chinese===
你们会说，这不过是韵文罢了，离诗歌还远得没法丈量。且慢！那些道出更值得纪念之事的人，或者唱出它们的人——那些讲述英雄的功业与谱系、瘟疫与饥馑、行刺与脱逃、宗族的流徙与征服的人，讲述一切「古老的、不幸的、遥远的事物，和很久以前的战役」（old, unhappy, far-off things and battles long ago）的人——那些靠唱这些过活的人，为了一顿晚饭、大厅里的一张床位、以及行囊里足以支撑他们走到下一处领主的些微之物——这些人是绅士、斯科普（scops）、巴德（bards）、游吟艺人（minstrels）（随你们怎么称呼），是一个职业阶层；在一块小酋长多如过江之鲫的土地上，他们极需要一套丰富的常备曲目，且能把曲调改裁得合于眼下设宴的那座厅堂。譬如说，在霍平家（Hoppings）——比林家（Billings）的世仇——的厅上颂扬比林家的武勇，那是万万使不得的；拿图廷家（Tootings）那套繁难的谱系去烦沃金家（Wokings，他们住在如今火葬场所在的地方），也不成：因为那就错失了我在第二讲里向你们宣讲过的那条好文章的初步规则——切合（appropriateness）。不但如此，当比林家与图廷家通婚时——不妨这么说，当比林家咕咕地求起偶来——还得匆匆把各家选段调和成一首「婚歌」（Epithalamium）。可见这全是极难的营生，需要随机应变、敏捷的才思、可观的歌曲库存、牢靠的记忆，以及辅助记忆的一切技巧。就拿《威德西斯》（Widsith，意为「远行者」）来说吧。他开篇道：——

===Original===
> Widsith spake: he unlocked his word-hoard.

===Chinese===
> 威德西斯开口：他打开了他的语词宝库。

===Original===
So he had a hoard of words, you see: and he must have needed them, for he goes on:—

===Chinese===
瞧，他有一座语词的囤积呢：而且想必真用得着，因为他接下去唱道：——

===Original===
> Forþon ic maeg singan *and* secgan spell,

> Maenan fore mengo in meoduhealle,

> Hu me cynegode cystum dohten.

> Ic waes mid Hunum *and* mid Hreð-ʒotum,

> Mid Sweom *and* mid ʒeatum, *and* mid Suð-Denum.

> Mid Wenlum ic waes *and* mid Waernum *and* mid Wicingum.

> Mid ʒefþum ic waes *and* mid Winedum.…2

===Chinese===
> Forþon ic maeg singan *and* secgan spell,

> Maenan fore mengo in meoduhealle,

> Hu me cynegode cystum dohten.

> Ic waes mid Hunum *and* mid Hreð-ʒotum,

> Mid Sweom *and* mid ʒeatum, *and* mid Suð-Denum.

> Mid Wenlum ic waes *and* mid Waernum *and* mid Wicingum.

> Mid ʒefþum ic waes *and* mid Winedum.…2（中译大意：因此我能歌唱、能讲述故事，能在蜜酒大厅里当众称颂诸王待我的恩义；我曾与匈奴人、与瑞德哥特人为伍，与斯维阿人、与耶阿特人、与南丹麦人为伍；我曾与温那人、与瓦尔纳人、与维京人为伍；我曾与格夫塔人、与温德人为伍……）

===Original===
and so on for a full dozen lines. I say that the memory of such men must have needed every artifice to help it: and the chief artifice to their hand was one which also delighted the ears of their listeners. They sang or intoned to the harp.

===Chinese===
如此等等，足足十二行。我断言，这类人的记忆必定需要一切技巧来帮扶：而他们手边那件最主要的技巧，恰也是一件能愉悦听众耳朵的东西。他们和着竖琴歌唱或吟诵。

===Original===
There you have it, Gentlemen. I have purposely, skimming a wide subject, discarded much ballast; but you may read and scan and read again, and always you must come back to this, that the first poets sang their words to the harp or to some such instrument: and just there lies the secret why poetry differs from prose. The moment you introduce music you let in emotion with all its sway upon speech. From that moment you change everything, down to the order of the words—the *natural* order of the words: and (remember this) though the harp be superseded, the voice never forgets it. You may take up a Barrack Room Ballad of Kipling’s, and it is there, though you affect to despise it for a banjo or concertina:—

===Chinese===
诸位先生，要害就在这里。我在这门宽泛的题目上是有意只作浮掠，抛却了许多压舱之物；但任凭你们读了又析、析了又读，你们总得回到这一条：最初的诗人是和着竖琴或诸如此类的乐器唱出他们的话的：而诗歌之所以有别于散文，秘密恰恰就在这里。音乐一引进，你便放情感进了门，连同它对言语的全部支配之力。从那一刻起，一切都变了，直变到词序——词的*自然*顺序：而且（记住这一点）竖琴纵然被取代，嗓音却永远不曾忘记它。你们尽可以拿起吉卜林（Kipling）的一首「营房歌谣」（Barrack Room Ballad），它就在那里，尽管你们佯装看不起它，嫌它不过是班卓琴或六角手风琴的调调：——

===Original===
> Ford—ford—ford of Kabul river…

===Chinese===
> 渡口——渡口——喀布尔河的渡口……

===Original===
“Bang, whang, whang goes the drum, tootle-te-tootle the fife.” From the moment men introduced music they made verse a thing essentially separate from prose, from its natural key of emotion to its natural ordering of words. Do not for one moment imagine that when Milton writes:—

===Chinese===
「鼓敲得嘭、锵、锵，笛吹得嘟-滴-嘟。」自人们引入音乐的那一刻起，他们便把诗造就成一件在本质上与散文分立的东西，从其情感的自然基调，到其语词的自然排布，无不如此。你们千万不要以为，弥尔顿写下

===Original===
> But cottage, herd, or sheepcote, none he saw.

===Chinese===
> 但村舍、牛群或羊圈，他一无所见。

===Original===
or

===Chinese===
或者

===Original===
> Of man’s first disobedience and the fruit

> Of that forbidden tree…

===Chinese===
> 关于人类最初的违抗，和那棵

> 禁树所结的果实……

===Original===
—where you must seek down five lines before you come to the verb, and then find it in the imperative mood—do not suppose for a moment that he is here fantastically shifting words, inverting phrases out of their natural order. For, as St. Paul might say, there is a natural order of prose and there is a natural order of verse. The natural order of prose is:—

===Chinese===
——在这里你必须往下寻足五行才寻到动词，而且寻到时它还端坐在祈使语气里——切不要有一刻以为，他这是在异想天开地搬移字眼、把短语倒出它们的自然秩序。因为，正如圣保罗（St. Paul）或许会说的：有散文的自然词序，也有诗的自然词序。散文的自然词序是：——

===Original===
> I was born in the year 1632, in the City of York, of a good family, though not of that county; my father being a foreigner of Bremen, who settled first in Hull.

===Chinese===
> 我于 1632 年出生在约克城，出身于一户体面人家，虽则并非约克郡的土著；我父亲是不来梅来的外乡人，起初定居在赫尔。

===Original===
or

===Chinese===
或者

===Original===
> Further I avow to your Highness that with these eyes I have beheld the person of William Wooton, B.D., who has written a good sizeable volume against a friend of your Governor (from whom, alas! he must therefore look for little favour) in a most gentlemanly style, adorned with the utmost politeness and civility.

===Chinese===
> 此外我向殿下自陈：我曾亲眼——就用这双眼睛——见过威廉·伍顿（William Wooton）神学学士其人；此人写过一部篇幅可观的书，攻讦您总督的一位朋友（唉！他因此休想再从这位朋友那里讨得多少恩宠），文风极其绅士，装点着极度的礼数与谦恭。

===Original===
The natural order of poetry is:—

===Chinese===
诗的自然词序是：——

===Original===
> Thus with the year

> Seasons return, but not to me returns

> Day, or the sweet approach of Ev’n or Morn,

> Or sight of vernal bloom, or Summer’s Rose,

> Or flocks, or herds, or human face divine.

===Chinese===
> 就这样，随着年岁

> 季节回来了，但回到我这儿的已不再是

> 白昼，不再是黄昏或清晨甜美的临近，

> 不再见春日的花、夏天的玫瑰，

> 不再见羊群牛群，不见人面上神圣的容光。

===Original===
or

===Chinese===
或者

===Original===
> But cottage, herd, or sheepcote, none he saw.

===Chinese===
> 但村舍、牛群或羊圈，他一无所见。

===Original===
and this basal difference you must have clear in your minds before, in dealing with prose or verse, you can practise either with profit or read either with intelligent delight.

===Chinese===
而这一根本差异，你们必须先在心里弄得清清楚楚；如此，执笔为散文也好、为诗也好，才能行之有益；阅读散文也好、阅读诗也好，才能读出会心的愉悦。

## Lecture IV On the Capital Difficulty of Verse / IV 论诗之大难点

===Original===
Thursday, April 17

===Chinese===
4 月 17 日，星期四

===Original===
In our last lecture, Gentlemen, we discussed the difference between verse, or metrical writing, and prose. We traced that difference (as you will remember) to Music—to the harp, the lyre, the dance, the chorus, all those first necessary accompaniments which verse never quite forgets; and we concluded that, as Music ever introduces emotion, which is indeed her proper and only means of persuading, so the natural language of verse will be keyed higher than the natural language of prose; will be keyed higher throughout and even for its most ordinary purposes—as for example, to tell us that So-and-so sailed to Troy with so many ships.

===Chinese===
诸位先生，上一讲里，我们讨论了诗（verse），也就是合律的文字，与散文（prose）之间的区别。我们（你们会记得）把这一区别追溯到音乐——追溯到竖琴、里拉琴、舞蹈、歌队，追溯到诗始终不曾全然忘怀的那一切最初的必要伴奏；我们还得出结论：正如音乐总要引入情感——情感确乎是她固有的、也是唯一的说服手段——诗的自然语言也就必然定调高于散文的自然语言；而且通篇定调更高，哪怕为了最寻常的目的也不例外——例如，告诉我们某甲带着这么许多艘船航到了特洛伊（Troy）。

===Original===
I grant you that our steps to this conclusion were lightly and rapidly taken: yet the stepping-stones are historically firm. Verse does precede prose in literature; verse does start with musical accompaniment; musical accompaniment does introduce emotion; and emotion does introduce an order of its own into speech. I grant you that we have travelled far from the days when a prose-writer, Herodotus, labelled the books of his history by the names of the nine Muses. I grant you that if you go to the Vatican and there study the statues of the Muses (noble, but of no early date) you may note that Calliope, Muse of the Epic—unlike her sisters Euterpe, Erato, Thalia—holds for symbol no instrument of music, but a stylus and a tablet. Yet the earlier Calliope, the Calliope of Homer, was a Muse of Song.

===Chinese===
我承认，我们走向这个结论，步子迈得轻快了些：然而一块块垫脚石在历史上是坚牢的。在文学中，诗确实先于散文；诗确实以音乐伴奏发端；音乐伴奏确实引入情感；而情感确实给言语引入了一种它自己的秩序。我承认，我们距离那位散文作家希罗多德（Herodotus）以九位缪斯（Muses）之名标目其历史各卷的时代，已经走出很远了。我也承认，倘若你们到梵蒂冈去细看那些缪斯雕像（气象高贵，却并非早期之作），你们会注意到史诗的缪斯卡利俄佩（Calliope）——不同于她的姐妹欧忒耳佩（Euterpe）、厄拉托（Erato）、塔利亚（Thalia）——手持为标志的并非什么乐器，而是一支铁笔和一方书板。然而更早的卡利俄佩，荷马（Homer）的卡利俄佩，本是一位歌唱的缪斯。

===Original===
> Μῆνιν ἃειδε, Θεἁ—

===Chinese===
> Μῆνιν ἃειδε, Θεἁ—

===Original===
“Had I a thousand tongues, a thousand hands.”—For what purpose does the poet wish for a thousand tongues, but to sing? for what purpose a thousand hands, but to pluck the wires? not to dip a thousand pens in a thousand inkpots.

===Chinese===
「但愿我有一千条舌头，一千只手。」——诗人想望一千条舌头，若不是为了歌唱，是为了什么？想望一千只手，若不是为了拨动琴弦，又是为了什么？总不是为了把一千支笔蘸进一千个墨水瓶。

===Original===
I doubt, in fine, if your most learned studies will discover much amiss with the frontier we drew between verse and prose, cursorily though we ran its line. Nor am I daunted on comparing it with Coleridge’s more philosophical one, which you will find in the *Biographia Literaria* (c. XVIII)—

===Chinese===
总而言之，尽管我们划线划得仓促，我怀疑你们最渊博的研究，也未必能在我们为诗与散文所划的这条界线上找出多少差错。把它同柯尔律治（Coleridge）那条更富哲思的界线——你们可以在《文学传记》（*Biographia Literaria*）第十八章里找到——两相比较，我也不胆怯：——

===Original===
> And first for the origin of metre. This I would trace to the balance in the mind effected by that spontaneous effort which strives to hold in check the workings of passion. It might be easily explained likewise in what manner this salutary antagonism is assisted by the very state which it counteracts, and how this balance of antagonism becomes organised into metre (in the usual acceptation of that term) by a supervening act of the will and judgment consciously and for the foreseen purpose of pleasure.

===Chinese===
> 且先说格律的起源。我要把它追溯到心中由一种自发的努力所达成的平衡，这努力力求遏住激情的运作。同样也不难解释：这种有益的对抗，如何恰恰得到它所对抗的那种状态本身的助力；这对抗的平衡，又如何经由意志与判断随后自觉的介入、并为了可预见的快乐目的，而被组织成格律（取该词通常的涵义）。

===Original===
I will not swear to understand precisely what Coleridge means here, though I believe that I do. But at any rate, and on the principle that of two hypotheses, each in itself adequate, we should choose the simpler, I suggest in all modesty that we shall do better with our own than with Coleridge’s, which has the further disadvantage of being scarcely amenable to positive evidence. We can say with historical warrant that Sappho struck the lyre, and argue therefrom, still within close range of correction, that her singing responded to the instrument: whereas to assert that Sappho’s mind “was balanced by a spontaneous effort which strove to hold in check the workings of passion” is to say something for which positive evidence will be less handily found, whether to contradict or to support.

===Chinese===
我不敢起誓说自己确切明白柯尔律治在这里的意思，尽管我相信我明白。但无论如何，依照「两个假说若各自都能自圆其说，我们应取其更简者」的原则，我谦逊地提议：用我们自己的界线，会比用柯尔律治的更见成效；何况他那条还有一层不便，就是几乎无从诉诸实证。我们能有历史依据地说萨福（Sappho）弹过里拉琴，并由此论证——仍在就近可纠的范围之内——她的歌唱是应和着琴的：而断言萨福的心「由一种力求遏住激情运作的自发努力所平衡」，说的却是一件无论要反驳还是要佐证、实证都不那么容易到手的事。

===Original===
Yet if you choose to prefer Coleridge’s explanation, no great harm will be done: since Coleridge, who may be presumed to have understood it, promptly goes on to deduce that,

===Chinese===
然而，倘若你们宁可取柯尔律治的解释，也无大碍：因为柯尔律治——他想必是懂得自己的解释的——紧接着便演绎出这样一个结论，

===Original===
> as the elements of metre owe their existence to a state of increased excitement, so the metre itself should be accompanied by the natural language of excitement.

===Chinese===
> 由于格律的诸要素皆因兴奋状态的增强而生，格律自身便应辅以兴奋的自然语言。

===Original===
which is precisely where we found ourselves, save that where Coleridge uses the word “excitement” we used the word “emotion.”

===Chinese===
而这恰恰正是我们先前到达的地方，只不过柯尔律治用「兴奋」（excitement）一词之处，我们用的是「情感」（emotion）。

===Original===
Shall we employ an illustration before proceeding?—some sentence easily handled, some commonplace of the moralist, some copybook maxim, I care not what. “Contentment breeds Happiness”—that is a proposition with which you can hardly quarrel; sententious, sedate, obviously true; provoking delirious advocacy as little as controversial heat; in short a very fair touchstone. Now hear how the lyric treats it, in these lines of Dekker—

===Chinese===
往下讲之前，我们要不要用一个例证？——随便一个容易摆弄的句子，一条道德家的老生常谈，一句习字帖格言，什么都行。「知足生幸福」——这条命题你们几乎无从挑剔；它是警句式的、端凝的、一望而知为真的；既挑不起如痴如狂的鼓吹，也挑不起论战的火气；总之是一块非常公道的试金石。现在且听抒情诗怎样处置它，就在戴克（Dekker）的这些诗行里——

===Original===
> Art thou poor, yet hast thou golden slumbers?
 

 O sweet content!
 

 Art thou rich, yet is thy mind perplex’d?
 

 O punishment!
 

 Dost thou laugh to see how fools are vex’d
 

 To add to golden numbers golden numbers?
 

 O sweet content! O sweet, O sweet content!
 

 Work apace, apace, apace, apace;
 

 Honest labour wears a lovely face;
 

 Then hey, nonny nonny—hey, nonny nonny!
 

 Canst drink the waters of the crystal spring?
 

 O sweet content!
 

 Swim’st thou in wealth, yet sink’st in thine own tears?
 

 O punishment!
 

 Then he that patiently want’s burden bears
 

 No burden bears, but is a king, a king!
 

 O sweet content! O sweet, O sweet content!
 

 Work apace, apace, apace, apace;
 

 Honest labour wears a lovely face;
 

 Then hey, nonny nonny—hey, nonny nonny!

===Chinese===
> 你贫穷吗？可你有黄金一般的睡眠？

哦，甜美的知足！

你富有吗？可你的心神烦乱惶惑？

哦，这惩罚！

你可笑看愚人们怎样自苦，

总想给黄金的数目再添上黄金的数目？

哦，甜美的知足！哦甜美、哦甜美的知足！

干活儿吧，赶紧，赶紧，赶紧，赶紧；

诚实的劳作有一张可爱的脸；

于是嘿，诺尼诺尼——嘿，诺尼诺尼！

你能畅饮那水晶泉的水吗？

哦，甜美的知足！

你游在财富里，可你沉在自己的泪里？

哦，这惩罚！

那么，那耐心背负着贫困重担的人

背的便不是重担，他是一位君王，一位君王！

哦，甜美的知足！哦甜美、哦甜美的知足！

干活儿吧，赶紧，赶紧，赶紧，赶紧；

诚实的劳作有一张可爱的脸；

于是嘿，诺尼诺尼——嘿，诺尼诺尼！

===Original===
There, in lines obviously written for music, you have our sedate sentence, “Contentment breeds Happiness,” converted to mere emotion. Note (to use Coleridge’s word) the “excitement” of it. There are but two plain indicative sentences in the two stanzas—(1) “Honest labour wears a lovely face” (used as a refrain), and (2) “Then he that patiently want’s burden bears no burden bears, but is a king, a king!” (heightened emotionally by inversion and double repetition). Mark throughout how broken is the utterance; antithetical question answered by exclamations: both doubled and made more antithetical in the second stanza: with cunning reduplicated inversions to follow, and each stanza wound up by an outburst of emotional nonsense—“hey, nonny nonny—hey, nonny nonny!”—as a man might skip or whistle to himself for want of thought.

===Chinese===
瞧，在这明显为音乐而写的诗行里，我们那句端凝的「知足生幸福」已化作纯粹的情感。请留意（借用柯尔律治的词）其中的「兴奋」。两节诗里只有两个平直的陈述句——（一）「诚实的劳作有一张可爱的脸」（用作叠句），（二）「那么，那耐心背负着贫困重担的人背的便不是重担，他是一位君王，一位君王！」（靠倒装与双重复沓在情感上加以拔高）。通篇留意那语调何其断续：对偶式的问题以感叹作答；到第二节里，两者又各各翻倍、对偶得愈加严整；随后更有巧妙的叠沓倒装；而每一节都以一阵情感性的胡话收束——「嘿，诺尼诺尼——嘿，诺尼诺尼！」——就像一个人心里没了念头，便独自蹦跳或吹起口哨来。

===Original===
Now (still keeping to our same subject of Contentment) let us *prosify* the lyrical order of language down to the lowest pitch to which genius has been able to reduce it and still make noble verse. You have all read Wordsworth’s famous Introduction to the *Lyrical Ballads*, and you know that Wordsworth’s was a genius working on a theory that the languages of verse and of prose are identical. You know, too, I dare say, into what banalities that theory over and over again betrayed him: banalities such as—

===Chinese===
现在（仍旧扣着知足这个题目）让我们把语言的诗序*散文化*（prosify）下来，一直压到天才所能压到的最低调门——压到那一步而仍写得出高贵的诗。你们都读过华兹华斯（Wordsworth）著名的《抒情歌谣集》（*Lyrical Ballads*）序言，你们知道华兹华斯是一位依循「诗的语言与散文的语言本属同一」这一理论而运作的天才。我敢说，你们也知道那理论一而再、再而三地把他诱进过怎样的平庸：平庸诸如——

===Original===
> His widowed mother, for a second mate
 

 Espoused the teacher of the village school:
 

 Who on her offspring zealously bestowed
 

 Needful instruction.

===Chinese===
> 他那寡居的母亲，为结第二次婚

嫁给了村塾的教书先生：

这位先生向她生养的子女

殷勤地授予了必要的教诲。

===Original===
—and the rest. Nevertheless Wordsworth was a genius; and genius working persistently on a narrow theory will now and again “bring it off” (as they say). So he, amid the flat waste of his later compositions, did undoubtedly “bring it off” in the following sonnet:—

===Chinese===
——等等。然而华兹华斯毕竟是天才；而天才死抱着一条狭隘理论持之以恒地干，偶尔也能「办成」（如人们所说）。于是，在他后期作品的一片平芜之中，他确确实实在下面这首十四行诗里「办成了」：——

===Original===
> These times strike monied worldlings with dismay:
 

 Ev’n rich men, brave by nature, taint the air
 

 With words of apprehension and despair;
 

 While tens of thousands, thinking on the affray,
 

 Men unto whom sufficient for the day
 

 And minds not stinted or untill’d are given,
 

 Sound healthy children of the God of Heaven,
 

 Are cheerful as the rising sun in May.
 

 What do we gather hence but firmer faith
 

 That every gift of noble origin
 

 Is breath’d upon by Hope’s perpetual breath;
 

 That Virtue and the faculties within
 

 Are vital; and that riches are akin
 

 To fear, to change, to cowardice, and death?

===Chinese===
> 这时代使有钱的俗辈惊惶沮丧：

连天生豪迈的富人，也用忧惧

与绝望的言词败坏了空气；

而成千上万惦念着那场争斗的人，

那些既得了当日之需、

又蒙赐不曾俭啬、不曾荒芜之心智的人，

上天之上帝健壮的儿女，

却欢欣如五月初升的太阳。

由此我们收获的，岂非只是更坚定的信念：

凡禀赋之出身高贵者，

必蒙希望之永恒气息吹拂；

德性与内在的种种官能

皆是活物；而财富原与恐惧、

与变故、与怯懦、与死亡沾亲？

===Original===
Here, I grant, are no repetitions, no inversions. The sentences, though metrical, run straightforwardly, verb following subject, object verb, as in strict prose. In short here you have verse reduced to the order and structure of prose as nearly as a man of genius, working on a set theory, could reduce it while yet maintaining its proper emotional key. But first let me say that you will find very few like instances of success even in Wordsworth; and few indeed to set against innumerable passages wherein either his verse defies his theory and triumphs, or succumbs to it and, succumbing, either drops sheer to bathos or spreads itself over dead flats of commonplace. Let me tell you next that the instances you will find in other poets are so few and so far between as to be negligible; and lastly that even such verse as the above has only to be compared with a passage of prose and its emotional pitch is at once betrayed. Take this, for example, from Jeremy Taylor:—

===Chinese===
这里，我承认，没有复沓，没有倒装。句子虽合乎格律，却进行得平直，动词跟着主语、宾语跟着动词，一如严格的散文。简言之，你在这里看到的是诗被缩减到散文的秩序与结构，缩减到一位按既定理论行事的天才所能做到的极致，而又维持住了它固有的情感调门。但首先我要说：即便在华兹华斯那里，你们也找不到几例同样的成功；而拿来同无数段落相对照的，更是寥寥无几——在那些段落里，他的诗要么违抗他的理论而取胜，要么屈服于它，而一经屈服，不是笔直跌入突降（bathos），便是摊展在死气沉沉的平庸平川之上。其次我要说：你们在其他诗人那里将找到的此类实例，稀少而间隔迢迢，尽可忽略不计；最后我要说：就连上面这样的诗，只消拿一段散文来一比，它的情感音高便立刻露了底。试举一例，出自杰里米·泰勒（Jeremy Taylor）：——

===Original===
> Since all the evil in the world consists in the disagreeing between the object and the appetite, as when a man hath what he desires not, or desires what he hath not, or desires amiss, he that compares his spirit to the present accident hath variety of instance for his virtue, but none to trouble him, because his desires enlarge not beyond his present fortune: and a wise man is placed in a variety of chances, like the nave or centre of a wheel in the midst of all the circumvolutions and changes of posture, without violence or change, save that it turns gently in compliance with its changed parts, and is indifferent which part is up, and which is down; for there is some virtue or other to be exercised whatever happens—either patience or thanksgiving, love or fear, moderation or humility, charity or contentedness.

===Chinese===
> 世间的恶，既然全在于对象与欲求之不合——譬如一个人有他所不愿要的，或求他所没有的，或求非其所应求——那么，凡能把自己的心境比照当下际遇的人，便有种种机缘修习他的德性，却没有一桩能搅扰他，因为他的欲望并不扩张到眼前的际遇之外：智者置身于种种变数之间，恰似车轮的毂，即轮之中心，任周遭一切回旋转动、姿势翻变，它自安然无恙，只顺着变动的诸辐轻轻转动，至于哪一侧重上去、哪一侧落下来，它漠无所系；因为无论发生什么，总有这样那样的德性可以操练——或是忍耐，或是感恩，或是爱，或是敬畏，或是节制，或是谦卑，或是慈善，或是知足。

===Original===
Or, take this from Samuel Johnson:—

===Chinese===
或者，取塞缪尔·约翰逊（Samuel Johnson）的这一段：——

===Original===
> The fountain of contentment must spring up in the mind; and he who has so little knowledge of human nature as to seek happiness by changing anything but his own disposition, will waste his life in fruitless efforts and multiply the griefs which he purposes to remove.

===Chinese===
> 知足之泉必须从心底涌出；谁若对人性知之少到如此地步，竟想靠改变自身性情以外的任何东西去寻求幸福，谁就将在徒劳无功中虚掷一生，反把他本想去除的忧伤越弄越多。

===Original===
Now, to be frank, I do not call that first passage very good prose. Like much of Jeremy Taylor’s writing it is prose tricked out with the trappings and odds-and-ends of verse. It starts off, for example, with a brace of heroics—“Since all the evil in the world consists”… “between the object and the appetite.” You may say, further, that the simile of the wheel, though proper enough to prose, is poetical too: that Homer might have used it (“As in a wheel the rim turns violently, while the nave, though it turns also, yet seems to be at rest”—something of that sort). Nevertheless you will agree with me that, in exchanging Wordsworth for Taylor and Johnson, we have relaxed something with the metre, something that the metre kept taut; and this something we discover to be the emotional pitch.

===Chinese===
喏，说句实话，我并不把第一段称作很好的散文。它像杰里米·泰勒的许多文字一样，是被诗的行头与零七八碎装点起来的散文。比方说，它开篇就来了一对英雄体——「Since all the evil in the world consists」（「世间的恶既然全在于」）……「between the object and the appetite」（「对象与欲求之不合」）。你们还可以进一步说，车轮那个明喻，虽施于散文颇为得体，却也带着诗意：荷马也尽用得（「正如车轮，轮辋剧烈地转动，而轮毂虽也随着转，看上去却似静着」——诸如此类）。然而你们会同意我：把华兹华斯换成泰勒和约翰逊，我们随着格律一同放松了某样东西，一样被格律绷紧着的东西；而这东西，我们发现，就是情感音高。

===Original===
But let me give you another illustration, supplied (I dare say quite unconsciously) by one who combined a genuine love of verse—in which, however, he was no adept—with a sure instinct for beautiful prose. Contentment was a favourite theme with Isaak Walton: *The Complete Angler* is packed with praise of it; and in *The Complete Angler* occurs this well-known passage:—

===Chinese===
不过，让我再给你们一个例证，由一位人物（我敢说是不自觉地）提供：他兼有对诗的真爱——尽管在诗一道上并非高手——与对美妙散文的可靠本能。知足是艾萨克·沃尔顿（Isaak Walton）偏爱的主题：《高明的垂钓者》（*The Complete Angler*）里满是对知足的赞美；而在《高明的垂钓者》里有这样一段有名的文字：——

===Original===
> But, master, first let me tell you, that very hour which you were absent from me, I sat down under a willow tree by the waterside, and considered what you had told me of the owner of that pleasant meadow in which you then had left me; that he had a plentiful estate, and not a heart to think so; that he had at this time many lawsuits depending, and that they both damped his mirth and took up so much of his time and thoughts that he had no leisure to take the sweet content that I, who pretended no title to them, took in his fields: for I could there sit quietly; and looking on the water, see some fishes sport themselves in the silver streams, others leaping at flies of several shapes and colours; looking on the hills, I could behold them spotted with woods and groves; looking down the meadows, could see, here a boy gathering lilies and lady-smocks, and there a girl cropping culverlocks and cowslips, all to make garlands suitable to this present month of May. These and many other field-flowers so perfumed the air that I thought that very meadow like that field in Sicily of which Diodorus speaks, where the perfumes arising from the place make all dogs that hunt in it to fall off and lose their hottest scent. I say, as I thus sat, joying in my own happy condition, and pitying this poor rich man that owned this and many other pleasant groves and meadows about me, I did thankfully remember what my Saviour said, that the meek possess the earth; or rather they enjoy what the others possess and enjoy not; for Anglers and meek quiet-spirited men are free from those high, those restless thoughts which corrode the sweets of life; and they, and they only can say as the poet has happily exprest it:

===Chinese===
> 可是，师傅，先让我告诉您：就在您离开我的那个时辰，我在水边一棵柳树底下坐下来，思量您对我讲起的、您当时把我留在其中的那片可爱草地的主人：说他有一份丰厚的产业，心却想不到这一层；说他此时悬着许多未决的官司，这些官司既压灭了他的欢乐，又占去他这么多时间和心思，使他无暇去领受那份甜美的知足——而我，虽不曾对这些田野主张过什么名分，却在其中领受到了：因为我在那里能安静闲坐；望着水，看见一些鱼在银色的溪流里自娱，另一些跃起捕捉形形色色的飞虫；望着山，我看见它们缀满树林与丛树；顺着草地望下去，看得见这边一个男孩在采百合花和白花碎米荠，那边一个女孩在剪耧斗菜和黄花九轮草，都是要编与眼前这五月相称的花环。这些以及许多别的田野花草，把空气熏得这样香，以致我觉得那片草地恰像狄奥多罗斯（Diodorus）笔下西西里的那片田野——那里升腾的香气，使一切入内行猎的狗都颓然失去最热的嗅觉。我说，我这样坐着，正庆幸自己处境有福，又怜悯这位拥有这片以及我周围许多可爱丛树草地的可怜富人时，便满怀感恩地记起我的救主的话：温柔的人承受地土；或者不如说，他们享受着别人占有却享受不到的东西；因为垂钓的人和温柔恬静的人，没有那些侵蚀生活之甜美的高傲而不宁的念头；他们，也只有他们，才能像诗人妙笔所道的那样说：

===Original===
There you have a passage of felicitous prose culminating in a stanza of trite and fifth-rate verse. Yes, Walton’s instinct is sound; for he is keying up the pitch; and verse, even when mediocre in quality, has its pitch naturally set above that of prose. So, if you will turn to your Walton and read the page following this passage, you will see that, still by a sure instinct, he proceeds from this scrap of reflective verse to a mere rollicking “catch”:

===Chinese===
这里你们看到一段美妙的散文，到头来收束于一节陈腐的五流诗句。是的，沃尔顿的本能是可靠的；因为他正在把调门往上抬；而诗，纵使质地平庸，其调门也天然定在散文之上。所以，倘若你们翻开手头的沃尔顿，读一读这段之后的下一页，就会看到他凭着同样可靠的本能，从这一小片沉思式的诗，进一步走向一支纯然嬉闹的「轮唱曲」（catch）：

===Original===
> Man’s life is but vain, for ’tis subject to pain
 

 And sorrow, and short as a bubble;
 

 ’Tis a hodgepodge of business and money and care,
 

 And care, and money and trouble…

===Chinese===
> 人生终是虚妄，因为它免不了

悲伤与苦痛，又短促得像水泡；

它是事务、金钱与操心的一锅乱炖，

外加操心、金钱与烦恼……

===Original===
—which is even worse rubbish, and yet a step upwards in emotion because Venator actually sings it to music. “Ay marry, sir, this is music indeed,” approves Brother Peter; “this cheers the heart.”

===Chinese===
——这更是等而下之的破烂，可在情感上却是往上迈了一步，因为维纳托（Venator）当真要配着乐唱它。「哎，对啦，先生，这才叫音乐呢，」彼得兄弟（Brother Peter）赞许道；「这才叫提神。」

---

===Original===
In this and the preceding lecture, Gentlemen, I have enforced at some length the opinion that to understand the many essential differences between verse and prose we must constantly bear in mind that verse, being metrical, keeps the character originally imposed on it by musical accompaniment and must always, however far the remove, be referred back to its origin and to the emotion which music excites.

===Chinese===
诸位先生，在本讲与前一讲里，我用了相当篇幅申说这个意见：要理解诗与散文之间诸多本质的区别，我们必须时刻记着，诗既是有格律的，便保存着音乐伴奏当初加于它的品性；因而无论相去多远，总须一直被追溯回它的源头，追溯到音乐所激发的情感。

===Original===
Mr. George Bernard Shaw having to commit his novel *Cashel Byron’s Profession* to paper in a hurry, chose to cast it in blank verse as being more easily and readily written so: a performance which brilliantly illuminates a half-truth. Verse—or at any rate, unrhymed iambic verse—is easier to write than prose, if you care to leave out the emotion which makes verse characteristic and worth writing. I have little doubt that, had he chosen to attempt it, Mr. Shaw would have found his story still more ductile in the metre of *Hiawatha*. But the experiment proves nothing: or no more than that, all fine art costing labour, it may cost less if burlesqued in a category not its own.

===Chinese===
萧伯纳先生（Mr. George Bernard Shaw）赶着要把他的小说《卡歇尔·拜伦的职业》（*Cashel Byron’s Profession*）匆匆落于纸面，便选择用素体诗（blank verse）来写，因为那样写起来更轻易顺当：这一手漂亮地照亮了半个真理。诗——至少，无韵的抑扬格诗——确比散文容易写，只要你肯略去那使诗成其为诗、值得一写的情感。我几乎不怀疑，倘若他当初肯一试，萧先生还会发现他的故事在《海华沙》（*Hiawatha*）的格律里更加柔顺听话。但这个实验什么也证明不了：或者说，它证明的不过是——既然一切精细的艺术都要花费劳力，那么把它放进不属于它的门类里滑稽模仿一番，或许能少费一点。

===Original===
Let me take an example from a work with which you are all familiar—*The Student’s Handbook to the University and Colleges of Cambridge*. On p. 405 we read:—

===Chinese===
让我从一部你们都熟悉的书里举个例子——《剑桥大学与学院学生手册》（*The Student’s Handbook to the University and Colleges of Cambridge*）。第 405 页上我们读到：——

===Original===
> The Medieval and Modern Languages Tripos is divided into ten sections, A, A2, B, C, D, E, F, G, H and I. A student may take either one or two sections at the end of his second year of residence, and either one or two more sections at the end of his third or fourth year of residence; or he may take two sections at the end of his third year only. Thus this Tripos can be treated either as a divided or as an undivided Tripos at the option of the candidate.

===Chinese===
> 中世纪与现代语言荣誉考试（Tripos）分为十个部分：A、A2、B、C、D、E、F、G、H 与 I。学生可在修业第二年年终应考一个或两个部分，并在第三或第四年年终再考一个或两个部分；也可以只在第三年年终应考两个部分。因此，这场荣誉考试既可当分卷的对待，也可当不分卷的对待，悉凭考生自择。

===Original===
Now I do not hold that up to you for a model of prose. Still, lucidity rather than emotion being its aim, I doubt not that the composer spent pains on it; more pains than it would have cost him to convey his information metrically, thus:—

===Chinese===
喏，我并不把这段文字举给你们当散文的典范。尽管如此，既然它的目标在明晰而不在情感，我不怀疑执笔者在它上面费过心思；比他用格律传达同样信息所费的更多，比如这样：——

===Original===
> There is a Tripos that aspires to blend
 

 The Medieval and the Modern tongues
 

 In one red burial (Sing Heavenly Muse!)
 

 Divided into sections A, A2,
 

 B, C, D, E, F, G and H and I.
 

 A student may take either one or two
 

 (With some restrictions mention’d in a footnote)
 

 At th’ expiration of his second year:
 

 Or of his third, or of his fourth again
 

 Take one or two; or of his third alone
 

 Take two together. Thus this tripos is
 

 (Like nothing in the Athanasian Creed)
 

 Divisible or indivisible
 

 At the option of the candidate—Gadzooks!

===Chinese===
> 有一门荣誉考试，立志要把

中世纪的诸语与现代的诸语

合葬进一座猩红的坟（唱吧，天上的缪斯！）

它分作 A、A2、

B、C、D、E、F、G 又 H 又 I。

一个学生可以应考一门或两门

（有若干限制，脚注中曾有载明）

在他修业第二年年终之时：

或在第三年，或再待到第四年

考一两门；或者只在第三年

连考两门。于是这荣誉考试便

（与《阿塔那修信经》里的名目无一相类）

既可分而又不可分，

悉凭考生自择——我的老天！

===Original===
This method has even some advantage over the method of prose in that it is more easily memorised; but it has, as you will admit, the one fatal flaw that it imports emotion into a theme which does not properly admit of emotion, and that so it offends against our first rule of writing—that it should be appropriate.

===Chinese===
这个方法甚至比散文的方法还多一层长处，就是更容易记诵；但它有——你们会承认——一个致命的破绽：它把情感硬灌进一个本不容情感的主题，如此便触犯了我们写作的第一条规则——应当得体。

===Original===
Now if you accept the argument so far as we have led it—that verse is by nature more emotional than prose—certain consequences would seem to follow: of which the first is that while the capital difficulty of verse consists in saying ordinary things the capital difficulty of prose consists in saying extraordinary things; that while with verse, keyed for high moments, the trouble is to manage the intervals, with prose the trouble is to manage the high moments.

===Chinese===
现在，倘若你们接受我们一路引领到此的论证——诗在本性上比散文更富于情感——那么某些后果似乎便随之而来：其中的第一条是：诗的大难点在于说平常之事，散文的大难点在于说不平常之事；诗既为高昂时刻定了调，麻烦就在处置各间隔；散文呢，麻烦则在处置高昂时刻。

===Original===
Let us dwell awhile on this difference, for it is important. You remember my quoting to you in my last lecture these lines of Milton’s:—

===Chinese===
让我们在这个区别上流连片刻，因为它事关紧要。你们记得，上一讲我向你们引用过弥尔顿（Milton）的这几行诗：——

===Original===
> Up to a hill anon his steps he reared
 

 From whose high top to ken the prospect round,
 

 If cottage were in view, sheepcote or herd;
 

 But cottage, herd, or sheepcote, none he saw.

===Chinese===
> 随即他把脚步抬上一座小山，

要从那高顶眺望四野的景色，

看有没有村舍入眼，羊圈或牛群；

但村舍、牛群或羊圈，他一无所见。

===Original===
We agreed that these were good lines, with the accent of poetry: but we allowed it to be a highly exalted way of telling how So-and-so climbed a hill for a better view but found none. Now obviously this exaltation does not arise immediately out of the action described (which is as ordinary as it well could be), but is *derivative*. It borrows its wings, its impetus, from a previous high moment, from the emotion proper to that moment, from the speech proper to that emotion: and these sustain us across to the next height as with the glide of an aeroplane. Your own sense will tell you at once that the passage would be merely bombastic if the poet were starting to set forth how So-and-so climbed a hill for the view—just that, and nothing else: as your own sense tells you that the swoop is from one height to another. For if bathos lay ahead, if Milton had but to relate how the Duke of York, with twenty thousand men, “marched up a hill and then marched down again,” he certainly would not use diction such as:—

===Chinese===
我们一致认为这些是好诗行，带着诗的口音：但我们也承认，这是把「某甲爬上一座山想看得更远些、结果什么也没看见」讲得极为崇高的一种讲法。显然，这种崇高并非直接从所述的行动里生出来（那行动平常得无以复加），而是*派生的*。它借来先前某个高昂时刻的翅膀与冲力，借来那个时刻固有的情感、那种情感固有的言语：靠着这些，我们才像飞机的滑翔一般，被托送过区间，抵达下一个高处。你们自己的语感立刻会告诉你们：倘若诗人是开篇就要讲述某甲如何为了看风景而爬山——仅此而已，别无其他——这段文字便纯属浮夸；正如你们的语感也会告诉你们，这一记俯冲是从一处高处扑向另一处高处。因为，倘若前方等着的是突降，倘若弥尔顿要讲的不过是约克公爵（Duke of York）率两万人「爬上了一座山，然后又爬了下来」，他断然不会用这样的措辞：——

===Original===
> Up to a hill anon his steps he reared.

===Chinese===
> 随即他把脚步抬上一座小山。

===Original===
Even as it is, I think we must all detect a certain artificiality in the passage, and confess to some relief when Satan is introduced to us, ten lines lower down, to revivify the story. For let us note that, in the nature of things, the more adorned and involved our style (and Milton’s is both ornate and involved) the more difficulty we must find with these flat pedestrian intervals. Milton may “bring it off,” largely through knowing how to dodge the interval and contrive that it shall at any rate be brief: but, as Bagehot noted, when we come to Tennyson and find Tennyson in “Enoch Arden” informing us of a fish-jowter, that:—

===Chinese===
即便照现在这样，我想我们也都察觉得出这段文字里某种造作的气息，并且承认，当下文相隔十行之处撒旦登场、给故事重新注入生命时，我们松了口气。因为让我们注意：按事理之常，我们的风格越是雕琢、越是迂曲（而弥尔顿的风格既雕琢又迂曲），我们在这些平淡无奇的间隔上遇到的困难就越大。弥尔顿或许能「办成」，大半靠的是懂得怎样闪避间隔、设法叫它至少短促一点：可是，正如白芝浩（Bagehot）指出的，当我们来到丁尼生（Tennyson）跟前，发现《伊诺克·阿登》（“Enoch Arden”）里的丁尼生正向我们讲说一个鱼贩子（fish-jowter），说道：——

===Original===
