这音调也回响在特洛伊罗斯（Troilus）的哀诉里，回响在格丽泽尔达（Griselda）重见孩子们时的狂喜里：——

===Original===
> O tendre, O dere, O yongë children myne,

> Your woful moder wendë stedfastly

> That cruel houndës or some foul vermyne

> Hadde eten you; but God of his mercy

> And your benignë fader tendrely

> Hath doon you kept…

===Chinese===
> 啊，柔嫩的、亲爱的、我年幼的孩子们，

> 你们的愁苦母亲曾深信不疑，

> 以为残暴的猎犬，或什么污秽的虫豸

> 已把你们吞吃；但上帝以他的慈悯，

> 和你们仁善的父亲以温柔的眷顾，

> 已使你们得蒙保全……

===Original===
You will find a note quite as sincere in many a carol, many a ballad, of that time:—

===Chinese===
在那个时代的许多卡罗尔颂歌、许多歌谣里，你们也会找到同样真挚的音调：——

===Original===
> He came al so still

> There his mother was,

> As dew in April

> That falleth on the grass.

> He came al so still

> To his mother’s bour,

> As dew in April

> That falleth on the flour.

> He came al so still

> There his mother lay,

> As dew in April

> That falleth on the spray.

> Mother and maiden

> Was never none but she;

> Well may such a lady

> Goddes mother be.

===Chinese===
> 他来得何等静悄，

> 来到他母亲所在的地方，

> 如同四月的甘露

> 降落在青草之上。

> 他来得何等静悄，

> 来到他母亲的闺房，

> 如同四月的甘露

> 降落在花朵之上。

> 他来得何等静悄，

> 来到他母亲安卧的地方，

> 如同四月的甘露

> 降落在嫩枝之上。

> 身为母亲又身为贞女的，

> 从来唯她一人；

> 这样一位淑女，理当作

> 上帝的母亲。

===Original===
You get the most emotional note of the Ballad in such a stanza as this, from “The Nut-Brown Maid”:—

===Chinese===
而在下面这样一节里，你们会听到歌谣最动情的音调，它出自《栗色少女》（The Nut-Brown Maid）：——

===Original===
> Though it be sung of old and young

> That I should be to blame,

> Their’s be the charge that speak so large

> In hurting of my name;

> For I will prove that faithful love

> It is devoid of shame;

> In your distress and heaviness

> To part with you the same:

> And sure all though that do not so

> True lovers are they none:

> For, in my mind, of all mankind

> I love but you alone.

===Chinese===
> 尽管老老少少都在歌里唱，

> 说我理应受责备，

> 那么大放厥词、中伤我名声的，

> 罪责才该他们领受；

> 因为我要证明，忠贞的爱

> 与羞耻无涉：

> 在你的困厄与忧伤之中

> 与你厮守不分：

> 而凡不肯这样做的人，确实

> 都算不得真正的恋人：

> 因为在我心中，芸芸众生，

> 我爱的唯独你一人。

===Original===
All these notes, again, you will admit to be exquisite: but they gush straight from the unsophisticated heart: they are nowise deep save in innocent emotion: they are not *thoughtful*. So when Barbour breaks out in praise of Freedom, he cries

===Chinese===
这些音调，你们也会承认无不精美：但它们是从未凿过的真心直接涌出来的：除了天真无邪的情感，它们全不深：它们不*含思想*。所以，当巴伯（Barbour）迸发出对自由的赞颂时，他喊的是——

===Original===
> A! Fredome is a noble thing!

===Chinese===
> 啊！自由是何等高贵的东西！

===Original===
And that is really as far as he gets. He goes on

===Chinese===
而这实在也就是他所能达到的极限了。他接着写道——

===Original===
> Fredome mayse man to hafe liking.

===Chinese===
> 自由使人得遂所愿。

===Original===
(Freedom makes man to choose what he likes; that is, makes him free)

===Chinese===
（自由使人得以拣选他所喜爱的；也就是说，使他自由）

===Original===
> Fredome all solace to man giffis,

> He livis at ese that frely livis!

> A noble hart may haif nane ese,

> Na ellys nocht that may him plese,

> Gif fredome fail’th: for fre liking

> Is yharnit ouer all othir thing…

===Chinese===
> 自由把一切慰藉赐给人，

> 自由而活的人才活得安适！

> 高贵的心得不到安适，

> 也再没有什么能使他欢愉，

> 一旦自由缺席：因为自由的意愿

> 被渴慕于一切事物之上……

===Original===
—and so on for many lines; all saying the same thing, that man yearns for Freedom and is glad when he gets it, because then he is free; all hammering out the same observed fact, but all knocking vainly on the door of thought, which never opens to explain what Freedom *is*.

===Chinese===
——如此等等，绵绵许多行；说的都是同一件事：人渴慕自由，得到了便欢喜，因为那时他是自由的；都在锤打同一个见到的事实，却都在徒劳地叩思想之门，那门从不开启，不肯解释自由*究竟是什么*。

===Original===
Now let us take a leap as we did with prose, and “taking off” from the Nut-Brown Maid’s artless confession,

===Chinese===
现在，让我们像方才对待散文那样作一次跃进：以栗色少女那不加雕饰的自白为「起飞点」——

===Original===
> in my mind, of all mankind

> I love but you alone,

===Chinese===
> 在我心中，芸芸众生，

> 我爱的唯独你一人，

===Original===
let us alight on a sonnet of Shakespeare’s—

===Chinese===
让我们降落在莎士比亚的一首十四行诗上——

===Original===
> Thy bosom is endearèd with all hearts

> Which I by lacking have supposèd dead:

> And there reigns Love, and all Love’s loving parts,

> And all those friends which I thought burièd.

> How many a holy and obsequious tear

> Hath dear religious love stolen from mine eye

> As interest of the dead!—which now appear

> But things removed, that hidden in thee lie.

> Thou art the grave where buried love doth live,

> Hung with the trophies of my lovers gone,

> Who all their parts of me to thee did give;

> —That due of many now is mine alone:

> Their images I loved I view in thee,

> And thou, all they, hast all the all of me.

===Chinese===
> 你的胸中珍存着所有人的心，

> 那些心，我因失去而只当早已死去：

> 那里君临着爱，和爱的一切可爱的部分，

> 还有我以为早已入土的众朋友。

> 多少回神圣而哀悼的泪水，

> 被挚诚虔敬的爱从我眼中偷去，

> 权当付给死者的利息！——如今它们显现，

> 不过是被移开之物，隐秘地卧在你身里。

> 你是一座坟墓，被埋葬的爱在其中活着，

> 悬挂着我逝去的恋人们留下的战利品，

> 他们把我的各个部分都交给了你；

> ——那原属众人的权益，如今归我一人：

> 我曾爱过的他们的形象，我在你身上看见；

> 而你，兼有他们全体，占有了我全部的全部。

===Original===
What a new way of talking about love! Not a happier way—there is less of heart’s-ease in these doubts, delicacies, subtleties—but how much more thoughtful! How has our Nut-Brown Maid eaten of the tree of knowledge!

===Chinese===
谈论爱情，这是何等新奇的一种方式！不是更幸福的一种——在这些疑虑、精微、幽婉之中，心的安宁少了——却何等地更有思想！我们的栗色少女，这是尝了知识之树（the tree of knowledge）上的果子啊！

===Original===
Well, there happened a Shakespeare, to do this for English Verse: and Shakespeare was a miracle which I cheerfully leave others to rationalise for you, having, for my own part and so far as I have fared in life, found more profit in a capacity for simple wonder.

===Chinese===
就这样，出了一位莎士比亚，替英语诗办成了这件事：而莎士比亚是一个奇迹，这个奇迹我乐于留给别人去向你们作合理化的解释；就我自己而言，以我此生走过的路程，我发觉一种单纯惊叹的能力裨益更多。

===Original===
But I can tell you how the path was made straight to that miracle. The shock of the New Learning upon Europe awoke men and unsealed men’s eyes—unsealed the eyes of Englishmen in particular—to discover a literature, and the finest in the world, which *habitually philosophised life*: a literature which, whether in a chorus of Sophocles or a talk reported by Plato, or in a ribald page of Aristophanes or in a knotty chapter of Thucydides, was in one guise or another forever asking *Why?* “What is man doing here, and why is he doing it?” “What is his purpose? his destiny?” “How stands he towards those unseen powers—call them the gods, or whatever you will—that guide and thwart, provoke, madden, control him so mysteriously?” “What are these things we call good and evil, life, love, death?”

===Chinese===
但我能告诉你们，通往那个奇迹的道路是如何修直的。新学问（the New Learning）对欧洲的冲击唤醒了人，揭开了人的眼封——尤其是揭开了英国人的眼封——让他们发现一种文学，而且是世上最好的文学，一种*惯于对人生作哲思*的文学：这种文学，无论在索福克勒斯（Sophocles）的一段合唱里，在柏拉图（Plato）笔下所记的一席谈话里，在阿里斯托芬（Aristophanes）恣肆放诞的一页里，还是在修昔底德（Thucydides）一个费解的章节里，总以这一种或那一种面目，永远在问*为什么？*——「人在此处做什么？他为什么做这些？」「他的目的何在？他的归宿何在？」「他同那些看不见的力量——你们愿意叫它们诸神就叫诸神，随你们的便——处于怎样的关系？它们引领他又阻挠他，撩拨他、激怒他，又这般神秘地辖制着他。」「我们所谓善与恶、生、爱、死，这些东西究竟是什么？」

===Original===
These are questions which, once raised, haunt Man until he finds an answer—some sort of answer to satisfy him. Englishmen, hitherto content with the Church’s answers but now aware of this great literature which answered so differently—and having other reasons to suspect what the Church said and did—grew aware that their literature had been as a child at play. It had never philosophised good and evil, life, love or death: it had no literary forms for doing this; it had not even the vocabulary. So our ancestors saw that to catch up their leeway—to make their report worthy of this wonderful, alluring discovery—new literary forms had to be invented—new, that is, in English: the sonnet, the drama, the verse in which the actors were to declaim, the essay, the invented tale. Then, for the vocabulary, obviously our fathers had either to go to Greek, which had invented the A.B.C. of philosophising; or to seek in the other languages which were already ahead of English in adapting that alphabet; or to give our English Words new contents, new connotations, new meanings; or lastly, to do all three together.

===Chinese===
这些问题一经提出，便如影随形地纠缠着人，直到他找到一个答案——某种足以餍足他的答案。英国人先前对教会给出的答案心满意足，如今既察觉这一伟大文学给出的答案何其不同——又有别的缘由去怀疑教会的所言所行——便渐渐明白：他们自己的文学一直像个嬉戏中的孩童。它从不曾对善恶、生、爱、死作过哲思：它没有作此哲思的文学形式；甚至连词汇也没有。于是我们的祖先看清了：要追回落下的航程——要使他们的记述配得上这一奇妙而诱人的发现——就必须发明新的文学形式——所谓新，是在英语里的新：十四行诗、戏剧、供演员诵念的诗行、随笔、虚构的故事。至于词汇，我们的先人显然只有几条路：要么求诸希腊语，它发明了哲思的入门字母；要么求诸其他语言，它们在移用这套字母上已先英语一步；要么赋予我们的英语词语以新的内容、新的内涵、新的意义；要么索性，三路并进。

===Original===
Well, it was done; and in verse very fortunately done; thanks of course to many men, but thanks to two especially—to Sir Thomas Wyat, who led our poets to Italy, to study and adopt the forms in which Italy had cast its classical heritage; and to Marlowe, who impressed blank verse upon the drama. Of Marlowe I shall say nothing; for with what he achieved you are familiar enough. Of Wyat I may speak at length to you, one of these days; but here, to prepare you for what I hope to prove—that Wyat is one of the heroes of our literature—I will give you three brief reasons why we should honour his memory:—

===Chinese===
而事情办成了；并且在诗这方面，办得非常幸运；这固然要归功于许多人，但尤其要归功于两位——托马斯·怀亚特爵士（Sir Thomas Wyat），他引领我们的诗人前往意大利，去研习并采用意大利用以浇铸其古典遗产的那些形式；还有马洛（Marlowe），他把素体诗（blank verse）烙进了戏剧。关于马洛我不准备多讲；因为他的成就是你们早已熟稔的。关于怀亚特，我日后或许会同你们从容细谈；但在今天，为了替我希望证明的一件事——即怀亚特是我们文学的一位英雄——先作铺垫，我给你们三条简短的理由，说明我们为何应敬重对他的纪念：——

===Original===
(1) He led the way. On the value of that service I shall content myself with quoting a passage from Newman:—

===Chinese===
（1）他开了先路。关于这桩功绩的价值，我只需引用纽曼（Newman）的一段话，便已满足：——

===Original===
> When a language has been cultivated in any particular department of thought, and so far as it has been generally perfected, an existing want has been supplied, and there is no need for further workmen. In its earliest times, while it is yet unformed, to write in it at all is almost a work of genius. It is like crossing a country before roads are made communicating between place and place. The authors of that age deserve to be Classics both because of what they do and because they can do it. It requires the courage and force of great talent to compose in the language at all; and the composition, when effected, makes a permanent impression on it.

===Chinese===
> 当一种语言已在某个特定的思想领域得到耕耘，并且就总体而言已臻完善之时，一种既存的需求便已得到供给，无须再添后来的工匠。而在它最早的岁月里，在它尚未成形之时，仅仅用它写作，便几乎已是一桩天才的功业。这就好比在沟通各地的道路尚未修成之前，徒步横越一片国土。那个时代的作家们理应身列经典，既因为他们所做的事，也因为他们竟能做成这些事。哪怕只是用这种语言撰作，也需要大才的胆魄与力量；而文章一经写成，便在这语言上留下永久的印痕。

===Original===
This Wyat did. He was a pioneer and opened up a new country to Englishmen. But he did more.

===Chinese===
这正是怀亚特所做的。他是一位拓荒者，为英国人开辟了一片新的疆土。但他所做的还不止于此。

===Original===
(2) Secondly, he had the instinct to perceive that the lyric, if it would philosophise life, love, and the rest, must boldly introduce the personal note: since in fact when man asks questions about his fortune or destiny he asks them most effectively in the first person. “What am *I* doing? Why are *we* mortal? Why do *I* love *thee*?”

===Chinese===
（2）第二，他有一种本能，洞察到抒情诗若要对人生、对爱情等等作哲思，就必须大胆引入个人的声口：因为事实上，人一旦就自己的命运或归宿发出追问，用第一人称来问才最为有力。「*我*在做什么？为什么*我们*终有一死？为什么*我*爱*你*？」

===Original===
This again Wyat did: and again he did more.

===Chinese===
这一点，怀亚特也做到了：而他同样还不止于此。

===Original===
For (3) thirdly—and because of this I am surest of his genius—again and again, using new thoughts in unfamiliar forms, he wrought out the result in language so direct, economical, natural, easy, that I know to this day no one who can better Wyat’s best in combining straight speech with melodious cadence. Take the lines *Is it possible?*—

===Chinese===
因为（3）第三——也正是为了这一条，我对他的天才最为确信——他一次又一次，以生疏的形式承载新的思想，锻出的成品却语言如此直截、经济、自然、从容，以至于时至今日，我尚不知道有谁能在把直白的言语与悠扬的腔调熔于一炉这件事上，胜过怀亚特最好的篇章。且看《这可能吗？》（*Is it possible?*）一诗：——

===Original===
> Is it possible?

> For to turn so oft;

> To bring that lowest that was most aloft:

> And to fall highest, yet to light soft?

> Is it possible?

> All is possible!

> Whoso list believe;

> Trust therefore first, and after preve;

> As men wed ladies by licence and leave,

> All is possible!

===Chinese===
> 这可能吗？——

> 竟这样屡屡变心；

> 把原本至高的打落最低：

> 又自极高处跌落，竟能轻轻着地？

> 这可能吗？

> 万事都可能！——

> 谁愿意，便去信；

> 所以先信，后再验证；

> 正如人们凭特许与允准迎娶贵妇，

> 万事都可能！

===Original===
or again—

===Chinese===
或者再来一首——

===Original===
> Forget not! O forget not this!—

> How long ago hath been, and is,

> The mind that never meant amiss:

> Forget not yet!

===Chinese===
> 别忘记！啊，别忘却这件事！——

> 这颗心从不曾存半点恶意，

> 它由来已久，至今犹是：

> 可千万别忘记！

===Original===
or again (can personal note go straighter?)—

===Chinese===
或者再来一首（个人的声口还有比这更直截的吗？）——

===Original===
> And wilt thou leave me thus?

> Say nay, say nay, for shame!

> To save thee from the blame

> —Of all my grief and grame.

> And wilt thou leave me thus?

> Say nay! say nay!5

===Chinese===
> 你竟要这样舍我而去吗？

> 说声不，说声不，不可这样不知羞！

> 也好救你自己免受指摘，

> ——免为我满心的悲伤与怨愤受过。

> 你竟要这样舍我而去吗？

> 说声不！说声不！5

===Original===
No: I have yet to mention the straightest, most natural of them all, and will read it to you in full—

===Chinese===
不：还有一首，比它们全都更直截、更自然的，我尚未提到，且让我把它一字不漏地读给你们听——

===Original===
> What should I say?

> Since Faith is dead

> And Truth away

> From you is fled?

> Should I be led

> With doubleness?

> Nay! nay! mistress.

> I promised you

> And you promised me

> To be as true

> As I would be:

> But since I see

> Your double heart,

> Farewell my part!

> Thought for to take

> Is not my mind;

> But to forsake

> One so unkind;

> And as I find,

> So will I trust,

> Farewell, unjust!

> Can ye say nay

> But that you said

> That I alway

> Should be obeyed?

> And—thus betrayed

> Or that I wist!

> Farewell, unkist!

===Chinese===
> 我还有什么可说？

> 既然信义已死，

> 真理也已逃离，

> 从你那里逃开？

> 难道我该被

> 口是心非牵着走？

> 不！不！小姐。

> 我曾向你许诺，

> 你也曾向我许诺，

> 要彼此一样忠诚，

> 一如我的本心：

> 但如今我既看见

> 你怀着二心，

> 别了，我那一份！

> 费心思设谋

> 原非我的本意；

> 我只是要撇下

> 这样一个薄情的人；

> 而且我察见什么，

> 便信托什么，

> 别了，无义的人！

> 你能否认，

> 你不曾说过

> 我将永远

> 该受人遵从？

> ——就这样受了骗，

> 但愿我从不曾知晓！

> 别了，未得一吻的人！

===Original===
I observe it noted on p. 169 of Volume III of *The Cambridge History of English Literature* that Wyat “was a pioneer and perfection was not to be expected of him. He has been described as a man stumbling over obstacles, continually falling but always pressing forward.” I know not to what wiseacre we owe that pronouncement: but what do you think of it, after the lyric I have just quoted? I observe, further, on p. 23 of the same volume of the same work, that the Rev. T. M. Lindsay, D.D., Principal of the Glasgow College of the United Free Church of Scotland, informs us of Wilson’s *Arte of Rhetorique* that

===Chinese===
我注意到《剑桥英国文学史》（*The Cambridge History of English Literature*）第三卷第 169 页有这样的话，说怀亚特「是一位拓荒者，本不可期望他臻于完美。有人形容他是一个在障碍上绊跌的人，不断跌倒，却总是奋力向前」。我不知道这句高论该记在哪位自作聪明者名下：但在我方才所引的那首抒情诗之后，你们对它作何感想？我还注意到，在同一部书的同一卷第 23 页，苏格兰联合自由教会格拉斯哥学院院长、神学博士 T. M. 林赛牧师（Rev. T. M. Lindsay, D.D.）就威尔逊（Wilson）的《修辞术》（*Arte of Rhetorique*）告诉我们说：

===Original===
> there is little or no originality in the volume, save, perhaps, the author’s condemnation of the use of French and Italian phrases and idioms, which he complains are “counterfeiting the kinges Englishe.” The warnings of Wilson will not seem untimely if to be remembered that the earlier English poets of the period—Sir Thomas Wyatt the elder, and the Earl of Surrey—drew their inspiration from Petrarch and Ariosto, that their earlier attempts at poetry were translations from Italian sonnets, and that their maturer efforts were imitations of the sweet and stately measures and style of Italian poesie. The polish which men like Wyatt and Surrey were praised for giving to our “rude and homely manner of vulgar poesie” might have led to some degeneration.

===Chinese===
> 这部书里几乎没有、或者说全无独创性，唯一的例外或许是作者对使用法语、意大利语词组和习语的谴责，他抱怨说这些是在「伪造国王的英语」。倘若记得下列事实，威尔逊的警告便不见得是不合时宜的：这一时期较早的英国诗人——老托马斯·怀亚特爵士与萨里伯爵（the Earl of Surrey）——其灵感得自彼特拉克（Petrarch）与阿里奥斯托（Ariosto），他们早期学诗的功课，是意大利语十四行诗的迻译，而较成熟的努力，则是对意大利诗歌甜美而庄严的格律与风格的模仿。像怀亚特与萨里这样的人为我们「粗陋质朴的本土诗风」添上的那层光润——他们正因此备受称许——本可能引致某种堕落。

===Original===
Might it, indeed? As another Dominie would have said, “Pro‑digious.”6

===Chinese===
哦，当真可能招致堕落么？正如另一位塾师（Dominie）会说的那样：「惊——人啊！」6

===Original===
But I have lingered too long with this favourite poet of mine and left myself room only to hand you the thread by following which you will come to the melodious philosophising of Shakespeare’s Sonnets—

===Chinese===
但在我这位心爱的诗人身上流连得太久了，只余下一点篇幅，把那条线头交到你们手里：循着它走去，你们便会抵达莎士比亚《十四行诗》（Sonnets）那乐音悠扬的哲思——

===Original===
> Let me not to the marriage of true Minds

> Admit impediment. Love is not love

> Which alters where it alteration finds

> Or bends with the remover to remove.

===Chinese===
> 我绝不承认两颗真心的结合

> 会有任何障碍。爱算不得爱，

> 若它一遇变迁便随之改变，

> 或对方要离去时便俯身相从。

===Original===
Note the Latin words “impediment,” “alteration,” “remove.” We are using the language of philosophy here or, rather, the “universal language,” which had taken over the legacy of Greek. You may trace the use of it growing as, for example, you trace it through the Elizabethan songbooks: and then (as I said) comes Shakespeare, and with Shakespeare the miracle.

===Chinese===
请注意那几个拉丁词：「障碍」（impediment）、「变迁」（alteration）、「离去」（remove）。我们在此处使用的是哲学的语言，或者不如说，是那继承了希腊遗产的「通用语言」。你们可以追踪它的用法如何日渐增长，譬如，循着伊丽莎白时代的歌集一路追过去：然后（如我所说）莎士比亚来了，随莎士比亚而来的，便是奇迹。

===Original===
The education of Prose was more difficult, and went through more violent convulsions. I suppose that the most of us—if, after reading a quantity of Elizabethan prose, we had the courage to tell plain truth, undaunted by the name of a great epoch—would confess to finding the mass of it clotted in sense as well as unmusical in sound, a disappointment almost intolerable after the simple melodious clarity of Malory and Berners. I, at any rate, must own that the most of Elizabethan prose pleases me little; and I speak not of Elizabethan prose at its worst, of such stuff as disgraced the already disgraceful Martin Marprelate Controversy, but of such as a really ingenious and ingenuous man like Thomas Nashe could write at his average. For a sample:—

===Chinese===
散文的教养来得更艰难，经历的痉挛也更剧烈。我想，我们中的多数人——倘若读了一大批伊丽莎白时代的散文之后，有勇气不顾一个伟大时代的名头、说出朴素的真相——都会承认，那些文字大半意思凝滞、声响也不谐和，在马洛礼与伯纳斯那种简单、悦耳、清朗之后，令人失望得几乎难以忍受。至少我自己必须承认，伊丽莎白时代的散文大半不合我的意；我说的还不是它最不堪的部分，不是那种连本已不堪的马丁·马普雷拉特论战（Martin Marprelate Controversy）都被糟蹋了名声的货色，而是像托马斯·纳什（Thomas Nashe）这样一位真正才思横溢而天性坦率的人，按其平常水准写出的文字。举一个样本：——

===Original===
> English Seneca read by candlelight yields many good sentences such as “Blood is a beggar” and so forth; and if you entreat him fair on a frosty morning, he will afford you whole Hamlets, I should say handfuls of tragical speeches.… Sufficeth them [that is, modern followers of Seneca] to bodge up a blank verse with “if’s” and “and’s,” and others, while for recreation after their candle-stuff, having starched their beards most curiously, to make a peripatetical path into the inner parts of the city, and spend two or three hours in turning over the French *Doudie*, where they attract more infection in one minute than they can do eloquence all the days of their life by conversing with any authors of like argument.

===Chinese===
> 英文的塞内加（Seneca）在烛光下诵读，能产出许多好句子，比如「血是个乞丐」云云；你若在一个霜晨好言相待，他还会给你整部整部的《哈姆雷特》（Hamlet）——我该说，整把整把的悲剧台词。……他们（也就是塞内加的现代追随者）只需拿「如果」「并且」之类把素体诗粗粗补缀起来也就够了；而在烛下功课之余消遣时，他们先把胡须浆得极其讲究，再逍遥学派式地踱出一条路来，走进内城深处，花上两三个钟头翻检法国人的《杜迪》（*Doudie*），在那里一分钟沾染的邪气，比他们终生与同题旨的任何作家交谈所能沾到的雄辩还要多。

===Original===
This may be worth studying historically, to understand the difficulties our prose had to encounter and overcome. But no one would seriously propose it as a model for those who would write well, which is our present business. I have called it “clotted.” It is, to use a word of the time, “farced” with conceits; it needs straining.

===Chinese===
这段文字，就理解我们散文当年不得不遭遇并克服的种种困难而言，或许值得作一番史学的研究。但谁也不会当真把它奉为范本，举给愿意写好文章的人——那才是我们眼下的正业。我方才说它「凝滞」。用当时的一个字眼说，它是「填了馅」（farced）的，馅料便是奇喻（conceits）；它需要过滤。

===Original===
Its one merit consists in this, that it is struggling, fumbling, to say something: that is, to *make* something. It is not, like modern Jargon, trying to dodge something. English prose, in short, just here is passing through a period of puberty, of green sickness: and, looking at it historically, we may own that its throes are commensurate with the stature of the grown man to be.

===Chinese===
它唯一的长处在于：它在挣扎、在摸索，想说点什么：也就是说，想*造*出点什么。它并不像现代的套话那样，想要躲闪掉点什么。简言之，英语散文恰在此处正经历一段青春期、一场萎黄病（green sickness）：而以历史的眼光看，我们尽可承认，它阵痛之剧，正与那位待长成的成年男子日后的身量相称。

===Original===
These throes tear it every way. On the one hand we have Ascham, pendantically enough, apologising that he writes in the English tongue (yet with a sure instinct he does it):—

===Chinese===
这些阵痛把它向四面八方撕扯。一面是阿斯克姆（Ascham），十足学究气地为自己用英语写作而道歉（然而凭着一种可靠的直觉，他终究还是写了）：——

===Original===
> If any man would blame me, either for taking such a matter in hand, or else for writing it in the English tongue, this answer I may make him, that what the best of the realm think it honest for them to use, I, one of the meanest sort, ought not to suppose it vile for me to write… And as for the Latin or Greek tongue, everything is so excellently done in them that none can do better. In the English tongue, contrary, everything in a manner so meanly, both for the matter and the handling, that no man can do worse.

===Chinese===
> 倘若有人要责备我，或怪我不该揽这样一桩题材在手，或怪我不该用英语来写它，我可以这样回答他：国中最有身份的人既认为用它言之无愧，我这样一个最微贱的人，便不该认为由我来写就卑陋了……至于拉丁语和希腊语，一切都已做得那样出色，无人能做得更好；而英语恰恰相反，无论题材还是处理，样样都做得这样卑陋，无人能做得更坏。

===Original===
On the other hand you have Euphuism with its antithetical tricks and poises, taking all prose by storm for a time: Euphuism, to be revived two hundred years later, and find a new avatar in the Johnsonian balance; Euphuism, dead now, yet alive enough in its day.

===Chinese===
另一面，你们有尤弗伊斯体（Euphuism）带着它对偶的花巧与秤锤般的均衡，一时间横扫了全部散文：尤弗伊斯体，两百年后死而复生，在约翰逊式的均衡句法里寻得一个新的化身；尤弗伊斯体，如今虽已死了，当年却也活得兴旺。

===Original===
For all these writers were alive: and I tell you it is an inspiriting thing to be alive and trying to write English. All these authors were alive and trying to *do* something. Unconsciously for the most part they were striving to philosophise the vocabulary of English prose and find a rhythm for its periods.

===Chinese===
因为这些作家个个都生气勃勃：我告诉你们，活着并且努力写英语，这本身就是一桩令人振奋的事。这些作家个个活着，都努力想*做*出点什么。他们多半在无意之间，正奋力为英语散文的词汇注入哲思，为它的句子寻一种节奏。

===Original===
And then, as already had happened to our Verse, to our Prose too there befell a miracle.

===Chinese===
随后，正如先前已在我们诗的身上发生过的，我们的散文也遭逢了一个奇迹。

===Original===
You will not ask me “What miracle?” I mean, of course, the Authorised Version of the Bible.

===Chinese===
你们不会问我「什么奇迹？」吧。我指的当然是圣经的钦定本（the Authorised Version）。

===Original===
I grant you, to be sure, that the path to the Authorised Version was made straight by previous translators, notably by William Tyndale. I grant you that Tyndale was a man of genius, and Wyclif before him a man of genius. I grant you that the forty-seven men who produced the Authorised Version worked in the main upon Tyndale’s version, taking that for their basis. Nay, if you choose to say that Tyndale was a miracle in himself, I cheerfully grant you that as well. But, in a lecture one must not multiply miracles *praeter necessitatem*; and when Tyndale has been granted you have yet to face the miracle that forty-seven men—not one of them known, outside of this performance, for any superlative talent—sat in committee and almost consistently, over a vast extent of work—improved upon what Genius had done. I give you the word of an old committeeman that this is not the way of committees—that only by miracle is it the way of any committee. Doubtless the forty-seven were all good men and godly: but doubtless also good and godly were the Dean and Chapter who dealt with Alfred Steven’s tomb of the Duke of Wellington in St. Paul’s Cathedral; and you know what *they* did. Individual genius such as Tyndale’s or even Shakespeare’s, though we cannot explain it, we may admit as occurring somehow, and not incredibly, in the course of nature. But that a large committee of forty-seven should have gone steadily through the great mass of Holy Writ, seldom interfering with genius, yet, when interfering, seldom missing to improve: that a committee of forty-seven should have captured (or even, let us say, should have retained and improved) a rhythm so personal, so constant, that our Bible has the voice of one author speaking through its many mouths: that, Gentlemen, is a wonder before which I can only stand humble and aghast.

===Chinese===
诚然，我承认，通往钦定本的路，已由先前的译者铺直，尤其是威廉·廷代尔（William Tyndale）。我承认廷代尔是一位天才，他之前的威克里夫（Wyclif）也是一位天才。我承认，成就钦定本的那四十七人，大体就是在廷代尔的译本上工作，以它为底本。不但如此，你们若偏要说廷代尔本人就是一个奇迹，我也乐意照样承认。但是，在一堂讲座里，不可*无此必要而倍增*奇迹（*praeter necessitatem*）；而廷代尔既已承认了，你们还须面对这个奇迹：四十七个人——没有一个因这项工程之外的表现而以任何超群的才具著称——坐在委员会里，在浩瀚的工作范围之内，几乎一以贯之地——比天才所已成就的更上层楼。我以一个老委员会中人的话向你们担保：这不是委员会行事的常规——任何委员会若要这样行事，非有奇迹不可。无疑，那四十七位个个善良而虔敬：但无疑，圣保罗大教堂（St. Paul's Cathedral）里经手阿尔弗雷德·史蒂文斯（Alfred Stevens）所造威灵顿公爵陵墓的那位教长与众教士，也一样善良而虔敬；而*他们*干了什么，你们是知道的。像廷代尔那样、甚至莎士比亚那样的个人天才，我们虽然无从解释，倒还可以承认它是在自然进程中以某种方式发生的事，未必难以置信。可是，一个四十七人的庞大委员会，竟能稳步走完圣书（Holy Writ）的浩浩卷帙，很少干预天才，而一旦干预，又很少不使之更臻完善：一个四十七人的委员会，竟能捕获（或者哪怕只是说，保持并改进）一种如此个人、如此恒定的节奏，以致我们的圣经通体是同一位作者的声音，借许多张口说出：这，诸位先生，才是一个奇迹，站在它面前，我唯有谦卑而骇然。

===Original===
Does it or does it not strike you as queer that the people who set you “courses of study” in English Literature never include the Authorised Version, which not only intrinsically but historically is out and away the greatest book of English Prose. Perhaps they can pay you the silent compliment of supposing that you are perfectly acquainted with it?… I wonder. It seems as if they thought the Martin Marprelate Controversy, for example, more important somehow.

===Chinese===
给你们规定英国文学「课程」的人们，居然从不把钦定本列入，而无论就其本身还是就历史而言，它都是遥遥领先、无出其右的英语散文第一巨著——你们可觉得这有点蹊跷么？也许，他们是在向你们行一个无言的敬礼，只当你们对它早已烂熟于心？……我可存疑。看那样子，他们仿佛倒觉得，比方说，马丁·马普雷拉特论战还更要紧些似的。

===Original===
> So when this corruptible shall have put on incorruption, and this mortal shall have put on immortality…

> Many waters cannot quench love, neither can the floods drown it: if a man would give all the substance of his house for love, it would utterly be contemned.

> The king’s daughter is all glorious within: her clothing is of wrought gold.

> Thine eyes shall see the King in his beauty: they shall behold the land that is very far off.

> And a man shall be as an hiding-place from the wind, and a covert from the tempest; as rivers of water in a dry place, as the shadow of a great rock in a weary land.

===Chinese===
> 这必朽坏的既穿上不朽，这必死的既穿上不死……

> 爱情，众水不能熄灭，大水也不能淹没；人若拿家中所有的财宝要换爱情，就必全被藐视。

> 王女在宫里极其荣华，她的衣服是用金线绣的。

> 你的眼必见王的荣美，必见辽阔之地。

> 必有一人像避风所和避暴雨的隐秘处，又像河流在干旱之地，像大磐石的影子在疲乏之地。

===Original===
When a nation has achieved this manner of diction, those rhythms for its dearest beliefs, a literature is surely established. Just there I find the effective miracle, making the blind to see, the lame to leap. Wyclif, Tyndale, Coverdale and others before the forty-seven had wrought. The Authorised Version, setting a seal on all, set a seal on our national style, thinking and speaking. It has cadences homely and sublime, yet so harmonises them that the voice is always one. Simple men—holy and humble men of heart like Isaak Walton or Bunyan—have their lips touched and speak to the homelier tune. Proud men, scholars—Milton, Sir Thomas Browne—practice the rolling Latin sentence; but upon the rhythms of our Bible they, too, fall back. “The great mutations of the world are acted, or time may be too short for our designs.” “Acquaint thyself with the Choragium of the stars.” “There is nothing immortal but immortality.” The precise man Addison cannot excel one parable in brevity or in heavenly clarity: the two parts of Johnson’s antithesis come to no more than this, “Our Lord has gone up to the sound of a trump: with the sound of a trump our Lord has gone up.” The Bible controls its enemy Gibbon as surely as it haunts the curious music of a light sentence of Thackeray’s. It is in everything we see, hear, feel, because it is in us, in our blood.

===Chinese===
当一个民族已为其最珍贵的信仰获得了这样的措辞、那样的节奏，一门文学便确实奠定了。正是在这里，我找到了那应验生效的奇迹——叫瞎眼的看见，叫瘸腿的跳跃。威克里夫、廷代尔、科弗代尔（Coverdale），以及四十七人之前的其他诸家，都曾为之劳作。钦定本给这一切盖下了印玺，也就给我们民族思维与言说的风格盖下了印玺。它的顿挫有家常的，也有崇高的，却调和得那样妥帖，以致那声音自始至终只是一个。质朴的人——像艾萨克·沃尔顿（Isaak Walton）或班扬（Bunyan）那样心地圣洁而谦卑的人——嘴唇被它一触，便说起那家常些的调子。高傲的人、饱学之士——弥尔顿（Milton）、托马斯·布朗爵士（Sir Thomas Browne）——操练着滚滚而下的拉丁句式；但一到我们圣经的节奏跟前，他们也不得不退回来倚靠它。「世界的大变局都已演罢，再不然，便是留给我们的时日太短，不够成就我们的图谋。」「去熟识群星的合唱领班（Choragium）吧。」「除了不朽本身，没有什么是不朽的。」讲究精确的艾迪生（Addison），论简洁或论天上的明澈，超不过随便哪一则比喻：约翰逊式对偶的两半，归结起来不过是这么一句——「我们的主在号角声中升天了：随着号角之声，我们的主升天了。」圣经控制着它的仇敌吉本（Gibbon），正如它萦绕不去于萨克雷（Thackeray）某个轻快句子那奇妙的音乐。它在我们所见、所听、所感的万事万物之中，因为它在我们之内，在我们的血里。

===Original===
What madman, then, will say “Thus or thus far shalt thou go” to a prose thus invented and thus with its free rhythms, after three hundred years, working on the imagination of Englishmen? Or who shall determine its range, whether of thought or of music? You have received it by inheritance, Gentlemen: it is yours, freely yours—to direct your words through life as well as your hearts.

===Chinese===
那么，对这样一种被如此创造出来、三百年来又以其自由的节奏作用于英国人想象力的散文，还有哪一个狂人，敢对它说「你到此为止、走这么远就够了」？又有谁能划定它的疆域——无论思想的疆域，还是音乐的疆域？诸位先生，你们已凭继承领受了它：它是你们的，完完全全是你们的——既引导你们一生的心，也引导你们一生的话。

## Lecture VII Some Principles Reaffirmed / VII 重申若干原则

===Original===
Thursday, May 29

===Chinese===
5 月 29 日，星期四

===Original===
Let me begin today, Gentlemen, with a footnote to my last lecture. It ended, as you may remember, upon an earnest appeal to you, if you would write good English, to study the Authorised Version of the Scriptures; to learn from it, moreover, how by mastering *rhythm*, our Prose overcame the capital difficulty of Prose and attuned itself to rival its twin instrument, Verse; compassing almost equally with Verse man’s thought however sublime, his emotion however profound.

===Chinese===
诸位先生，今天请允许我从上一讲的一条脚注讲起。你们或许记得，那一讲的结尾是一番恳切的吁请：倘若你们想写出好的英语，就请研读圣经钦定本（Authorised Version）；并且从中学得，我们的散文（Prose）如何通过驾驭*节奏*（rhythm），克服了散文的大难点，把自己调校得足以与它的孪生乐器——诗（Verse）——分庭抗礼；如何与诗几乎同样地包容人的思想，无论其何等崇高，人的情感，无论其何等深沉。

===Original===
Now in the course of my remarks I happened﻿—maybe a little incautiously﻿—to call the Authorised Version a “miracle”; using that word in a colloquial sense, in which no doubt you accepted it; meaning no more than that the thing passed my understanding. I have allowed that the famous forty-seven owed an immense deal to earlier translators﻿—to the Bishops, to Tyndale, to the Wyclif Version, as themselves allowed it eagerly in their preface:﻿—

===Chinese===
然而在我讲述的过程中，我一时——也许有点失于轻率——把钦定本称作了一个「奇迹」；用的是这个词的口头意义，诸位无疑也是照这个意思领会的；无非是说，这桩事情超出了我的理解。我已经承认，那著名的四十七位译者受惠于前辈译者良多——受惠于《主教圣经》（the Bishops）、受惠于廷代尔（Tyndale）、受惠于威克里夫译本（the Wyclif Version），正如他们本人在序言里热切承认的那样：——

===Original===
> Truly (good Christian reader) wee never thought from the beginning that we should needs to make a new Translation, nor yet to make of a bad one a good one﻿ ﻿… but to make a good one better, or out of many good ones one principall good one, not justly to be excepted against: that hath bene our indeavour, that our marke.7

===Chinese===
> 真的（善良的基督徒读者啊），我们从不曾想过要另造一种新译本，也不曾想过把一个坏的译成好的……而是要把好的变得更好，或是从许多好的里面做出一部最卓越的好译本，使人无从正当地指摘：这向来是我们的努力，这向来是我们的鹄的。7

===Original===
Nevertheless the Authorised Version astounds me, as I believe it will astound you when you compare it with earlier translations. Aristotle (it has been said) invented Chance to cover the astonishing fact that there were certain phenomena for which he found himself wholly unable to account. Just so, if one may compare very small things with very great, I spoke of the Authorised Version as a “miracle.” It was, it remains, marvellous to me.

===Chinese===
尽管如此，钦定本仍令我惊叹，而且我相信，当你们把它与更早的诸译本并比时，它也会令你们惊叹。据说，亚里士多德（Aristotle）发明了「偶然」，好掩盖那个令他惊愕的事实：世上竟有一些现象，他发现自己全然无法解释。同样地——倘若可以把极小的事与极大的事相比——我才把钦定本说成是一个「奇迹」。它过去是、现在依然是一件令我惊异不置的东西。

===Original===
Should these deciduous discourses ever come to be pressed within the leaves of a book, I believe their general meaning will be as clear to readers as I hope it is to you who give me so much pleasure by pursuing them﻿—almost (shall I say?) like Wordsworth’s Kitten with those other falling leaves:﻿—

===Chinese===
倘若这些落叶般次第飘坠的讲稿，有朝一日果真被压进一本书的书叶之间，我相信它们的大意对读者会像我期望于诸位的那样明白——诸位一路追随这些讲稿，给了我多大的快乐——几乎（我可以这样说吗？）像华兹华斯（Wordsworth）笔下的小猫追逐别的落叶那样：——

===Original===
> That almost I could repine
 

 That your transports are not mine.

===Chinese===
> 我几乎要心生怨羡
 

 你们的欢狂不是我的。

===Original===
But meanwhile certain writers in the newspapers are assuming that by this word “miracle” I meant to suggest to you a something like plenary inspiration at once supernatural and so authoritative that it were sacrilege now to alter their text by one jot or tittle.

===Chinese===
但与此同时，报纸上有几位撰稿人却认定，我用「奇迹」这个词，是想向诸位暗示某种类似「完全默感」（plenary inspiration）的东西——既属超自然，又如此权威，以至于如今对他们的经文改动一点一画，都算是亵渎。

===Original===
Believe me, I intended nothing of the sort: for that, in my plain opinion, would be to make a fetish of the book. One of these days I hope to discuss with you what inspiration is: with what accuracy﻿—with what meaning, if any﻿—we can say of a poet that he is inspired; questions which have puzzled many wise men from Plato downwards.

===Chinese===
相信我，我绝无此意：因为依我的浅见直言，那将是一种把这书奉为物神的做法。日后我希望与诸位讨论灵感（inspiration）究竟是什么：我们能在多大的准确性上——带着什么意义，倘若竟有的话——说一位诗人是受了灵感；这些问题，从柏拉图（Plato）以降，已使许多智者困惑不已。

===Original===
But certainly I never dreamt of claiming plenary inspiration for the forty-seven. Nay, if you will have it, they now and again wrote stark nonsense. Remember that I used this very same word “miracle” of Shakespeare, meaning again that the total Shakespeare quite outpasses my comprehension; yet Shakespeare, too, on occasion talks stark nonsense, or at any rate stark bombast. He never blotted a line﻿—“I would he had blotted a thousand” says Ben Jonson: and Ben Jonson was right. Shakespeare could have blotted out two or three thousand lines: he was great enough to afford it. Somewhere Matthew Arnold supposes us as challenging Shakespeare over this and that weak or bombastic passage, and Shakespeare answering with his tolerant smile, that no doubt we were right, but after all, “Did it greatly matter?”

===Chinese===
但可以肯定，我从未想过为那四十七位要求完全默感的名分。不，倘若诸位一定要听实话，他们偶尔也写出过十足的胡话。请记住，我对莎士比亚（Shakespeare）也用过同一个「奇迹」的字眼，意思同样是说，整个儿的莎士比亚全然超出了我的理解；然而莎士比亚偶尔也说过十足的胡话，或者至少是十足的浮夸。他从没涂改过一行——「我倒愿意他涂掉过一千行」，本·琼森（Ben Jonson）说；本·琼森说得对。莎士比亚本可以涂掉两三千行：他伟大得足以承担这个损失。马修·阿诺德（Matthew Arnold）在某处设想我们拿这一段那一段孱弱或浮夸的文字去向莎士比亚诘难，而莎士比亚带着他那宽容的微笑回答说，无疑我们是对的，但归根结底，「这真有多大关系吗？」

===Original===
So we offer no real derogation to the forty-seven in asserting that here and there they wrote nonsense. They could afford it. But we do stultify criticism if, adoring the grand total of wisdom and beauty, we prostrate ourselves indiscriminately before what is good and what is bad, what is sublime sense and what is nonsense, and forbid any reviser to put forth a hand to the ark.

===Chinese===
因此，断言他们东一处西一处写过胡话，我们并不是真正贬损那四十七位。他们承担得起。但倘若我们一面崇拜这智慧与美的总和，一面却不分好歹、无论是崇高的义理还是胡话，都一概匍匐在下，并且禁止任何修订者向约柜（the ark）伸出一只手来，那我们便着实使批评沦为愚行了。

===Original===
The most of us Christians go to church on Christmas Day, and there we listen to this from Isaiah 9:1﻿–﻿7:﻿—

===Chinese===
我们大多数基督徒在圣诞节那天上教堂去，在那里，我们会听到引自《以赛亚书》（Isaiah）九章一至七节的这样一段话：——

===Original===
> Nevertheless the dimness shall not be such as was in her vexation, when at the first he lightly afflicted the land of Zebulun and the land of Naphtali, and afterwards did more grievously afflict her by the way of the sea, beyond Jordan, in Galilee of the nations.
> The people that walked in darkness have seen a great light: they that dwell in the land of the shadow of death, upon them hath the light shined.
> Thou hast multiplied the nation, and not increased the joy: they joy before thee according to the joy in harvest, and as men rejoice when they divide the spoil.
> For thou hast broken the yoke of his burden, and the staff of his shoulder, the rod of his oppressor, as in the day of Midian.
> For every battle of the warrior is with confused noise, and garments rolled in blood: but this shall be with burning and fuel of fire.
> For unto us a child is born, unto us a son is given.

===Chinese===
> 虽然如此，那幽暗必不像她先前受窘迫时那样：起先，他轻罚了西布伦地和拿弗他利地，后来却沿海边的路、约旦河外、外邦人的加利利，更重地苦待了她。
> 在黑暗中行走的百姓看见了大光；住在死荫之地的居民，有光照耀在他们身上。
> 你使这国民繁多，却不加增喜乐：他们在你面前欢喜，像收割时的欢喜，又像人分掳物时那样快乐。
> 因为你已折断他所负的重轭、他肩头的杖、和欺压他之人的棍，好像在米甸的日子一样。
> 因为战士的每一场争战都在混乱的喧嚣里，衣裳滚在血中；但这必以焚烧、以燃料之火而来。
> 因有一婴孩为我们而生，有一子赐给我们。

===Original===
The forty-seven keep their majestic rhythm. But have you ever, sitting in church on a Christmas morning, asked yourself what it all means, or if it mean anything more than a singsong according somehow with the holly and ivy around the pillars? “Thou hast multiplied the nation, and not increased the joy: they joy before thee according to the joy in harvest,” But why﻿—if the joy be not increased? “For every battle of the warrior is with confused noise, and garments rolled in blood: but this shall be with burning and fuel of fire.” Granted the rhythmical antithesis, where is the real antithesis, the difference, the improvement? If a battle there must be, how is burning better than garments rolled in blood? And, in fine, what is it all about? Now let us turn to the Revised Version:﻿—

===Chinese===
四十七位保住了他们庄严的节奏。可是诸位可曾在圣诞节早晨坐在教堂里，问过自己这一切究竟是什么意思——或者，除了与柱子四周的冬青和常春藤隐隐相称的一串顺口溜之外，它到底还有没有别的意思？「你使这国民繁多，却不加增喜乐：他们在你面前欢喜，像收割时的欢喜」——可是为什么——倘若喜乐不曾加增？「因为战士的每一场争战都在混乱的喧嚣里，衣裳滚在血中；但这必以焚烧、以燃料之火而来」——节奏上的对偶姑且承认，可真正的对偶在哪里？差别在哪里？长进在哪里？倘若争战势所难免，焚烧怎么就比衣裳滚在血中更好？归根结底，这一切究竟说的是什么？现在让我们转到修订本（Revised Version）：——

===Original===
> But there shall be no gloom to her that was in anguish. In the former time he brought into contempt the land of Zebulun and the land of Naphtali, but in the latter time hath he made it glorious, by the way of the sea, beyond Jordan, Galilee of the nations.
> The people that walked in darkness have seen a great light: they that dwelt in the land of the shadow of death, upon them hath the light shined. Thou hast multiplied the nation, thou hast increased their joy: they joy before thee according to the joy in harvest, as men rejoice when they divide the spoil.
> For the yoke of his burden, and the staff of his shoulder, the rod of his oppressor, thou hast broken as in the day of Midian.
> For all the armour of the armed man in the tumult, and the garments rolled in blood, shall even be for burning, for fuel of fire.
> For unto us a child is born, unto us a son is given; and the government shall be upon his shoulder: and his name shall be called Wonderful, Counsellor, Mighty God, Everlasting Father, Prince of Peace.

===Chinese===
> 但那受过痛苦的必不再见幽暗。从前神使西布伦地和拿弗他利地被藐视，末后却使这沿海的路，约旦河外，外邦人的加利利地得着荣耀。
> 在黑暗中行走的百姓看见了大光；住在死荫之地的人有光照耀他们。你使这国民繁多，加增他们的喜乐；他们在你面前欢喜，像收割的欢喜，像人分掳物那样的快乐。
> 因为他们所负的重轭和肩头上的杖，并欺压他们人的棍，你都已经折断，好像在米甸的日子一样。
> 战士在乱杀之间所穿戴的盔甲，并那滚在血中的衣服，都必作为可烧的，当作燃料。
> 因有一婴孩为我们而生；有一子赐给我们。政权必担在他的肩头上；他名称为奇妙策士、全能的神、永在的父、和平的君。

===Original===
I say (knowing no Hebrew, merely assuming our Revisers to be at least no worse scholars than the forty-seven) that here, with the old cadences kept so far as possible, we are given sense in place of nonsense: and I ask you to come to the Revised Version with a fair mind. I myself came to it with some prejudice; in complete ignorance of Hebrew, and with no more than the usual amount of Hellenistic Greek. I grant at once that the Revised New Testament was a literary fiasco; largely due (if gossip may be trusted) to trouble with the Greek Aorist, and an unwise decision﻿—in my opinion the most gratuitously unwise a translator can take﻿—to use one and the same English word, always and in every connotation, as representing one and the same Greek word: for in any two languages few words are precisely equivalent. A fiasco at any rate the Revised New Testament was, deserving in a dozen ways and in a thousand passages the scorn which Professor Saintsbury has recently heaped on it. But I protest against the injustice of treating the two Revisions﻿—of the New Testament and of the Old﻿—as a single work, and saddling the whole with the sins of a part. For two years I spent half-an-hour daily in reading the Authorised and Revised Versions side by side, marking as I went, and in this way worked through the whole﻿—Old Testament, Apocrypha, New Testament. I came to it (as I have said) with some prejudice; but I closed the books on a conviction, which my notes sustain for me, that the Revisers of the Old Testament performed their task delicately, scrupulously, on the whole with great good judgment; that the critic does a wrong who brings them under his indiscriminate censure; that on the whole they have clarified the sense of the Authorised Version while respecting its consecrated rhythms; and that﻿—to name an example, that you may test my words and judge for yourselves﻿—the solemn splendour of that most wonderful poem, the story of Job, *διαλαμπει*, “shines through” the new translation as it never shone through the old.
---

===Chinese===
我要说（我全然不懂希伯来文，只是姑且假定我们的修订者们至少是不劣于四十七位的学者）：在这里，旧的抑扬顿挫在可能的范围内被保留下来，同时无意义被换成了有意义；我请诸位怀着公道的心去接近修订本。我自己当初来读它时原带着几分偏见；对希伯来文全然无知，希腊化的希腊文（Hellenistic Greek）也只具备寻常的那一点。我立刻承认，修订的新约在文学上是一次惨败；其缘由大半（倘若闲话可信）在于希腊文不定过去时（Aorist）惹出的麻烦，以及一项不明智的决定——在我看来，那是译者所能做出的最平白无端的不明智决定——即始终不变、在每一种涵义里都一概如此地用同一个英语词去代表同一个希腊词：因为在任何两种语言之间，精确对等的词本来寥寥。无论如何，修订的新约是一场惨败，在十来种方面、在上千个段落里，都当得起圣茨伯里教授（Professor Saintsbury）近来倾倒于它的鄙夷。但我抗议这样一桩不公道：把新约与旧约的两次修订当作一部作品看待，叫整体去背负局部的罪愆。有两年工夫，我每天花半个钟头把钦定本与修订本并排对读，一路圈点，就这样通读了全部——旧约、次经（Apocrypha）、新约。我（如前所说）带着几分偏见去读它；但合上书时，我已抱定一个信念——我的笔记可以为我作证——旧约的修订者们把他们的任务完成得细腻、审慎，总的说来判断极佳；批评家将他们笼统地一并苛责，是冤屈了他们；总的说来，他们廓清了钦定本的意义，同时又尊重了它那些已奉为神圣的节奏；而且——姑举一例，好让诸位检验我的话、自行裁断——那首最美妙的诗篇、约伯（Job）的故事，其庄严的辉光，*διαλαμπει*，「透射生辉」于新译本之中，一如它在旧译本中从未透射过的那样。
---

===Original===
And now Gentlemen (as George Herbert said on a famous occasion), let us tune our instruments.

===Chinese===
而现在，诸位先生（正如乔治·赫伯特〔George Herbert〕在某次著名场合所说的那样），让我们来调准我们的乐器吧。

===Original===
Before discussing with you another and highly important question of style in writing, I will ask you to look back for a few moments on the road we have travelled.

===Chinese===
在与诸位讨论写作风格（Style）的另一个高度重要的问题之前，我想请诸位花上片刻，回顾一下我们已走过的路程。

===Original===
We have agreed that our writing should be *appropriate*: that it should fit the occasion; that it should rise and fall with the subject, be grave where that is serious, where it is light not afraid of what Stevenson in *The Wrong Box* calls “a little judicious levity.” If your writing observe these precepts, it will be well-mannered writing.

===Chinese===
我们已经商定，我们的写作应当*得体*（appropriate）：应当切合场合；应当随题目的轻重而起伏，题目严肃处便庄重，题目轻快处也不怕用上史蒂文森（Stevenson）在《错箱》（The Wrong Box）里所说的「一点得宜的轻佻」。你们的写作若遵守这些箴言，便会是知礼的写作。

===Original===
To be sure, much in addition will depend on yourself﻿—on what you are or have made yourself, since in writing the style can never be separated from the man. But neither can it in the practice of virtue: yet, though men differ in character, I do not observe that moralists forbear from laying down general rules of excellence. Now if you will recall our further conclusion, that writing to be good must be persuasive (since persuasion is the only true intellectual process), and will test this by a passage of Newman’s I am presently to quote to you, from his famous “definition of a gentleman,” I think you will guess pretty accurately the general law of excellence I would have you, as Cambridge men, tribally and particularly obey.

===Chinese===
诚然，此外还有许多要取决于你们自己——取决于你们是什么样的人，或者已把自己造就成了什么样的人，因为在写作里，风格永远无法与人分离。但在德行的修习上，风格同样无法与人分离：然而，尽管人的品性各不相同，我却不曾见到伦理学家因此就拒绝为卓越立下通则。现在，倘若你们肯回想我们进一步的结论——写作要写得好，必须有说服力（persuasiveness），因为说服是唯一真正的智识过程——并愿意用一段我马上就要向诸位引述的纽曼（Newman）文字来检验它，那段文字出自他那著名的「绅士的定义」，那么我想，你们便能相当准确地猜到那条我愿你们作为剑桥人、以部族的和特别的方式去遵奉的卓越通则。

===Original===
Newman says of a gentleman that among other things:

===Chinese===
纽曼谈到绅士时说，除其他品质外：

===Original===
> He is never mean or little in his disputes, never takes unfair advantage, never mistakes personalities or sharp sayings for arguments, or insinuates evil which he dare not say out.﻿ ﻿… If he engages in controversy of any kind, his disciplined intellect preserves him from the blundering discourtesy of better perhaps, but less educated minds; who, like blunt weapons, tear and hack instead of cutting clean, who mistake the point in argument, waste their strength on trifles, misconceive their adversary, and leave the question more involved than they found it. He may be right or wrong in his opinion: but he is too clear-sighted to be unjust. He is simple as he is forcible, and as brief as he is decisive.

===Chinese===
> 他在争辩中从不卑鄙小气，从不占人不公的便宜，从不把人身攻击或刻薄的俏皮话当作论据，也从不影射他不敢明说的坏话。……他若参与任何一种论战，他那受过训练的才智都能使他免于那些或许更为优秀、却较少受过教育的头脑所犯的笨拙失礼；那样的头脑，像钝了的兵器，只会撕扯砍剁而不能干净利落地切割，抓不住论辩的要害，把力气浪费在琐事上，误解对手，把问题弄得比原先更纠缠不清。他的见解或对或错：但他目光太清明，不会不公。他朴实一如其有力，简洁一如其果断。

===Original===
Enough for the moment on this subject: but commit these words to your hearts, and you will not only triumph in newspaper controversy. You will do better: you will avoid it.

===Chinese===
关于这个题目，眼下说这些就够了：但请把这些话铭记在心，那么你们在报纸论战中不仅会大获全胜。你们会做得更好：你们会干脆避开它。

===Original===
To proceed.﻿—We found further that our writing should be *accurate*: because language expresses thought﻿—is, indeed, the only expression of thought﻿—and if we lack the skill to speak precisely, our thought will remain confused, ill-defined. The editor of a mining paper in Denver, U.S.A., boldly the other day laid down this law, that niceties of language were mere “frills”: all a man needed was to “get there,” that is, to say what he wished in his own way. But just here, we found, lies the mischief. You will not get there by hammering away on your own untutored impulse. You must first be your own reader, chiselling out the thought definitely for yourself: and, after that, must carve out the intaglio yet more sharply and neatly, if you would impress its image accurately upon the wax of other men’s minds. We found that even for Men of Science this neat clean carving of words was a very necessary accomplishment. As Sir James Barrie once observed, “The Man of Science appears to be the only man who has something to say, just now﻿—and the only man who does not know how to say it.” But the trouble by no means ends with Science. Our poets﻿—those gifted strangely prehensile men who, as I said in my first lecture, seem to be born with filaments by which they apprehend, and along which they conduct, the half-secrets of life to us ordinary mortals﻿—our poets would appear to be scamping artistic labour, neglecting to reduce the vague impressions to the clearly cut image which is, after all, what helps. It may be a triumph that they have taught modern French poetry to be suggestive. I think it would be more profitable could they learn from France﻿—that nation of fine workmen﻿—to be definite.

===Chinese===
继续讲下去。——我们还发现，我们的写作应当*准确*（accurate）：因为语言表达思想——它实在是思想的唯一表达——倘若我们缺乏精确说话的技能，我们的思想便会始终混乱、界限不明。美国丹佛（Denver）一家矿业报纸的主编，前几天大胆立下了这样一条法则：语言的精微讲究纯属「花边」（frills）；一个人所需要的只是「到达那里」（get there），也就是说，用自己的方式说出自己想说的话。但恰恰在这里，我们发现了祸害所在。靠着自己未经调教的冲动一味猛敲，是到不了那里的。你必须先做自己的读者，为你自己把思想凿凿实实地雕琢出来：随后，若要把它的形象精确地钤印到他人心智的蜡面上，还须把那枚凹雕（intaglio）刻得愈加锋利、愈加干净。我们发现，即使对科学界人士（Men of Science）而言，这种对语词干净利落的雕琢，也是一项极必要的造诣。正如詹姆斯·巴里爵士（Sir James Barrie）有一次所说：「眼下，科学界人士似乎是唯一有话要说的人——也是唯一不知道该怎么说的人。」但这麻烦绝不止于科学界。我们的诗人们——那些天赋异禀、仿佛生有灵掌可攫的奇人，如我在第一讲里所说，他们生来就似乎带着细丝，凭这些细丝他们捉住生命的半隐之秘，又沿着这些细丝把秘密传导给我们这些寻常凡人——我们的诗人似乎正在敷衍他们的艺术劳作，懒得把模糊的印象还原为轮廓分明的意象，而后者终究才是有用处的。他们教会了现代法国诗歌去求暗示，这也许是一场胜利。我倒以为，倘若他们能向法兰西——那个精工良匠之国——学来明确，获利会更丰。

===Original===
But about “getting there”﻿—I ask you to remember Wolfe, with the seal of his fate on him, stepping into his bateau on the dark St. Lawrence River and quoting as they tided him over:﻿—

===Chinese===
至于「到达那里」——我请诸位记住沃尔夫（Wolfe）：身上盖着他命运的封印，在漆黑的圣劳伦斯河（St. Lawrence River）上跨进他的小艇，当小艇乘潮载他渡河时，他口中吟诵的是：——

===Original===
> The boast of heraldry, the pomp of power,
 

 And all that beauty, all that wealth e’er gave,
 

 Await alike th’ inevitable hour;
 

 The paths of glory lead but to the grave.

===Chinese===
> 门第的夸耀，权势的煊赫，
 

 以及美色所能给予、财富所能带来的一切，
 

 都同样等候着那无可回避的时辰；
 

 荣耀的道路无非通向坟墓。

===Original===
“I had rather have written those lines,” said Wolfe, “than conquer Canada.” That is how our forefathers valued noble writing. The Denver editor holds that you may write as you please so long as you get there. Well, Wolfe got there: and so, in Wolfe’s opinion, did Gray: but perhaps to Wolfe and Gray, and to the Denver editor, “there” happened to mean two different places. Wolfe got to the Heights of Abraham.

===Chinese===
「我宁愿写出过那几行诗，」沃尔夫说，「也不愿征服加拿大。」我们的先辈就是这样估量高贵的写作的。丹佛那位主编认为，只要你能到达那里，尽可以随心所欲地写。好吧，沃尔夫到达了那里：而且在沃尔夫看来，格雷（Gray）也到达了那里：只是，对沃尔夫和格雷来说，和对丹佛主编来说，「那里」碰巧意味着两个不同的地方。沃尔夫到达的是亚伯拉罕高地（Heights of Abraham）。

===Original===
Further, it was against this loose adaptation of words to thought and to things that we protested in our interpolated lecture on Jargon, which is not so much bad writing as the avoidance of writing. The man who employs Jargon does not get “there” at all, even in a raw rough pioneering fashion: he just walks around “there” in the ambient tracks of others. Let me fly as high as I can and quote you two recent achievements by Cabinet Ministers, as reported in the Press:﻿—(1) “Mr. McKenna’s reasons for releasing from Holloway Prison Miss Lenton while on remand charged *in connection with* (sweet phrase!) the firing of the tea pavilion in Kew Gardens are given in a letter which he has *caused to be forwarded* to a correspondent who inquired *as to* the circumstances of the release. The letter says ‘I am desired by the Home Secretary to say that Lilian Lenton was reported by the medical officer at Holloway Prison to be in a state of collapse and in imminent danger of death *consequent upon* her refusal to take food. Three courses were open﻿—(1) To leave her to die; (2) To attempt to feed her forcibly, which the medical officer advised would probably entail death in her existing condition: (3) To release her. The Home Secretary adopted the last course.’ ”

===Chinese===
再者，我们在论套话（Jargon）的那次插叙一讲里所抗议的，正是这种语词对思想、对事物的松弛贴合；套话与其说是坏的写作，不如说是对写作的逃避。使用套话的人根本到不了「那里」，哪怕以一种生粗糙粝的拓荒方式也到不了：他只是踩着旁人在四周环踏出的辙印，绕着「那里」兜圈子。让我尽量往高处飞，给诸位引两件近来内阁大臣的杰作，均照报章所载：——（一）「麦肯纳先生（Mr. McKenna）关于自霍洛韦监狱（Holloway Prison）开释伦顿小姐（Miss Lenton）——她还押期间曾*因牵涉*（in connection with，多甜的措辞！）邱园（Kew Gardens）茶亭纵火案而受指控——的理由，载于一封信中；他已*促使将该信转致*（caused to be forwarded）一位曾*就*（as to）开释情节进行函询的通信者。信中说：『内政大臣嘱我申明：莉莲·伦顿经霍洛韦监狱医务官报告，已处于衰竭状态，并*缘于*（consequent upon）拒绝进食而濒临迫近的死亡危险。当时可循的途径有三——（1）听任她死去；（2）试图强行喂食，医务官提出意见说，以她眼下的状况，此举多半将招致死亡；（3）予以开释。内政大臣采纳了最后一条。』」

===Original===
“Would probably entail death in her existing condition”! Will anyone tell me how Mr. McKenna or anyone else could kill, or (as he prefers to put it) entail death upon, Miss Lenton in a non-existing condition?

===Chinese===
「以她眼下的状况，此举多半将招致死亡」！有谁能告诉我，麦肯纳先生或者随便哪一位，怎样才能在一个并不存在的状况之下杀死伦顿小姐——或者，照他更爱用的说法，招致她的死亡呢？

===Original===
(2) Next take the Chancellor of the Exchequer. As we know, the Chancellor of the Exchequer can use incisive speech when he chooses. On May 8th as reported in next day’s *Morning Post*, Mr. Lloyd George, answering a question, delivered himself of this to an attentive Senate:﻿—

===Chinese===
（二）其次请看财政大臣（Chancellor of the Exchequer）。我们都知道，财政大臣高兴的时候，是能够使用锋利的言辞的。五月八日，据次日的《晨邮报》（Morning Post）所载，劳合·乔治先生（Mr. Lloyd George）在答复一项质询时，向一座凝神谛听的元老院（Senate）发表了如下宏论：——

===Original===
> With regard to Mr. Noel Buxton’s questions, I cannot answer for an enquiry which is *of a private and confidential character*, for although I am associated with it I am not associated with it as a Minister of the Crown.﻿ ﻿… Those enquiries are of a very careful systematic and scientific character, and are being conducted by the ablest investigators in this country, some of whom have reputations of international character. I am glad to think that the investigation is of a most impartial character.

===Chinese===
> 关于诺埃尔·巴克斯顿先生（Mr. Noel Buxton）的质询，我无法为一项*属于私人而机密性质*（of a private and confidential character）的调查作答，因为尽管我与该项调查有关联，我并非以王室大臣（Minister of the Crown）的身份与之相关联。……那些调查属于一种极为审慎、有系统而科学的性质，正由本国最能干的调查者们主持进行，其中若干位享有国际性质（of international character）的声誉。我很欣慰地认为，该项调查属于一种最为公正无私的性质。

===Original===
It must be a comforting thought, that an inquiry of a private and confidential character is also of a very systematic and scientific character, and besides being of a most impartial character, is conducted by men of international character﻿—whatever that may happen to mean. What *is* an international character, and what would you give for one?

===Chinese===
这想必是个令人宽慰的想法：一项属于私人而机密性质的调查，同时也是具有高度系统而科学性质的，而且除了具有最公正无私的性质之外，还由具有国际性质（international character）的人物们主持——不管这话碰巧是什么意思。一个国际性质*究竟*是什么东西，你又肯出什么价钱买上一个呢？

===Original===
We found that this way of talking, while pretending to be something pontifical, is really not prose at all, nor reputable speech at all, but Jargon; nor is the offence to be excused by pleading, as I have heard it pleaded, that Mr. Lloyd George was not using his own phraseology but quoting from a paper supplied him by some permanent official of the Treasury: since we select our civil servants among men of decent education and their salaries warrant our stipulating that they shall be able, at least, to speak and write their mother tongue.

===Chinese===
我们发现，这种言谈方式虽佯装出几分教谕（pontifical）的庄严，其实根本算不上散文，也根本算不上体面的言语，而只是套话；而且这桩过犯也不能靠如下辩解来开脱——我听过有人这样辩解——说劳合·乔治先生用的并非他自己的措辞，而是引自财政部（Treasury）某位常任官员提供给他的一纸文稿：既然我们的文官是从受过像样教育的人们中间遴选出来的，他们的薪俸也足以担保我们提出约定：他们至少应当能说、能写他们的母语。

===Original===
We laid down certain rules to help us in the way of straight Prose:﻿—
1. Always always prefer the concrete word to the abstract.
2. Almost always prefer the direct word to the circumlocution.
3. Generally, use transitive verbs, that strike their object; and use them in the active voice, eschewing the stationary passive, with its little auxiliary “its’s” and “was’s,” and its participles getting into the light of your adjectives, which should be few. For, as a rough law, by his use of the straight verb and by his economy of adjectives you can tell a man’s style, if it be masculine or neuter, writing or “composition.”

===Chinese===
我们立下了几条规则，来帮助我们走上笔直的散文之路：——
1. 永远、永远宁取具体的词，不取抽象的词。
2. 几乎永远宁取径直的词，不取迂说（circumlocution）。
3. 一般而言，要用及物动词，叫它们击中宾语；且要用主动语态，避用那呆立不动的被动式，连同它那些小小的辅助词——「it's」们与「was」们——以及它那些分词（它们总要挤进你们形容词的光线里，而形容词本该寥寥）。因为，照一条粗略的法则，凭一个作家使用径直动词的功夫与他节用形容词的克制，你便能判断他的风格：是阳刚还是中性，是在写作还是在「作文」（composition）。

===Original===
The authors of that capital handbook *The King’s English*, which I have already recommended to you, add two rules:﻿—
1. Prefer the short word to the long.
2. Prefer the Saxon word to the Romance.

===Chinese===
那部一流的手册《国王的英语》（The King's English）——我已向诸位推荐过——的作者们补上了两条规则：——
1. 宁取短的词，不取长的。
2. 宁取撒克逊（Saxon）词，不取罗曼（Romance）词。

===Original===
But these two precepts you would have to modify by so long a string of exceptions that I do not commend them to you. In fact I think them false in theory and likely to be fatal in practice. For, as my last lecture tried to show, you no sooner begin to philosophise things instead of merely telling a tale of them than you must go to the Mediterranean languages: because in these man first learnt to discuss his “why” and “how,” and these languages yet guard the vocabulary.

===Chinese===
但这两条箴言，你们得用长长一串例外去修正，因此我不把它们推荐给你们。实际上，我认为它们在理论上是错误的，在实践中则很可能是致命的。因为，正如我上一讲试图表明的，你们一旦开始对事物作哲学的思考，而不再是仅仅讲一讲关于它们的故事，就必须求诸地中海诸语言：因为人正是在这些语言里首先学会讨论他的「为何」与「如何」，而这些语言至今守护着那套词汇。

===Original===
Lastly, we saw how, by experimenting with rhythm, our prose “broke its birth’s invidious bar” and learnt to scale the forbidden heights.

===Chinese===
最后我们看到，我们的散文如何通过试验节奏，「冲破了它与生俱来、惹人忌恨的栅栏」，学会了攀上那禁止攀援的高处。

===Original===
Now by attending to the few plain rules given above you may train yourselves to write sound, straightforward, work-a-day English. But if you would write melodious English, I fear the gods will require of you what they ought to have given you at birth﻿—something of an ear. Yet the most of us have ears, of sorts; and I believe that, though we can only acquire it by assiduous practice, the most of us can wonderfully improve our talent of the ear.

===Chinese===
如今，只要遵守上面那几条浅明的规则，你们便能训练自己写出扎实、径直、日常实用的英语。但倘若你们想写旋律优美的英语，我怕诸神要向你们索取一样他们本该在你们出生时就交给你们的东西——一点耳朵。不过我们大多数人总还算有耳朵，纵然只是勉强算数的耳朵；而且我相信，尽管唯有靠勤勉的练习才能获得，我们大多数人仍能奇妙地改进自己耳朵的天赋。

===Original===
If you will possess yourselves of a copy of Quintilian or borrow one from any library (Bohn’s translation will do) and turn to his ninth book, you will find a hundred ways indicated, illustrated, classified, in which a writer or speaker can vary his Style, modulate it, lift or depress it, regulate its balance.

===Chinese===
倘若你们愿意自购一部昆体良（Quintilian），或者从任何一家图书馆借一部（博恩〔Bohn〕的译本就够用了），翻到他的第九卷，你们会看到一百种方法，被一一指点、例证、归类，一位作家或演说家可以借此变换他的风格，为之调声，使之扬起或沉落，调节它的均衡。

===Original===
All these rules, separately worth studying, if taken together may easily bewilder and dishearten you. Let me choose just two, and try to hearten you by showing that, even with these two only, you can go a long way.

===Chinese===
所有这些规则，分开来都值得研习，合在一起却很容易叫你们迷乱而气馁。让我只挑出两条，并试着给你们打气：让你们看到，哪怕只有这两条，你们也能走得很远。

===Original===
Take the use of right emphasis. What Quintilian says of right emphasis﻿—or the most important thing he says﻿—is this:﻿—

===Chinese===
且拿正当的强调（emphasis）的用法来说。昆体良论正当强调所说的话——或者他说过的最重要的话——是这个：——

===Original===
> There is sometimes an extraordinary force in some particular word, which, if it be placed in no very conspicuous position in the middle part of a sentence, is likely to escape the attention of the hearer and to be obscured by the words surrounding it; but if it be put at the end of the sentence is urged upon the reader’s sense and imprinted on his mind.

===Chinese===
> 某个特定的词有时带着一种非同寻常的力量；倘若把它放在句子中部一个不很显眼的位置，它便很可能逃过听者的注意，被周围的词所遮蔽；但倘若把它放在句末，它就会被推送到读者的感官之前，铭印在他的心上。

===Original===
That seems obvious enough, for English use as well as for Latin. “The wages of sin is Death”﻿—anyone can see how much more emphatic that is than “Death is the wages of sin.” But let your minds work on this matter of emphasis, and discover how emphasis has always its right point somewhere, though it be not at all necessarily at the end of the sentence. Take a sentence in which the strong words actually repeat themselves for emphasis:﻿—

===Chinese===
这个道理，对英语的用处和对拉丁文一样，都够明显的了。「罪的工价乃是死」——谁都看得出，这比「死乃是罪的工价」有力得多。但请让你们的头脑在强调这件事上多做做工夫，去发现强调总在某处有一个恰当的落点，虽然它绝不一定要落在句末。试取这样一个句子，其中重词为了强调竟真的自我重复：——

===Original===
> Babylon is fallen, is fallen, that great city.

===Chinese===
> 巴比伦倾倒了，倾倒了，那大城。

===Original===
Our first impulse would be to place the emphasis at the end:﻿—

===Chinese===
我们的第一冲动会是把强调放在句末：——

===Original===
> Babylon, that great city, is fallen, is fallen.

===Chinese===
> 巴比伦，那大城，倾倒了，倾倒了。

===Original===
The Latin puts it at the beginning:﻿—

===Chinese===
拉丁文把它放在开头：——

===Original===
> *Cecidit, cecidit, Babylonia illa magna.*
 
 

 Fallen, fallen, is Babylon, that great city.

===Chinese===
> *Cecidit, cecidit, Babylonia illa magna.*（倾倒了，倾倒了，那大城巴比伦。）
 
 

 倾倒了，倾倒了，是巴比伦，那座大城。

===Original===
The forty-seven preserved the “falling close” so exquisite in the Latin; the emphasis, already secured by repetition, they accentuated by lengthening the pause. I would urge on you that in every sentence there is just a right point of emphasis which you must train your ears to detect. So your writing will acquire not only emphasis, but balance, and you will instinctively avoid such an ill-emphasised sentence as this, which, not naming the author, I will quote for your delectation:﻿—

===Chinese===
四十七位保留了拉丁文里那个精妙之至的「坠落收尾」；那已由重复稳稳取得的强调，他们又通过拉长停顿来加以加重。我要向诸位力陈：每一个句子里都恰恰有一个恰当的强调点，你们必须训练自己的耳朵去把它侦查出来。这样，你们的写作获得的就不只是强调，还有均衡；你们便会本能地避开这样一个强调失当的句子——不说出作者姓名，我引在这里供诸位怡情：——

===Original===
> “Are Japanese Aprils always as lovely as this?” asked the man in the light tweed suit of two others in immaculate flannels with crimson sashes round their waists and puggarees folded in cunning plaits round their broad Terai hats.

===Chinese===
> 「日本的四月总是这样可爱吗？」那位穿浅色斜纹软呢西装的男子问道——他问的是另外两个人；那两位穿着一尘不染的法兰绒衣裤，腰间围着猩红的饰带，宽阔的特莱（Terai）帽上，遮阳巾折叠出精巧的褶裥。

===Original===
Explore, next, what (though critics have strangely neglected it) to my mind stands the first, or almost the first, secret of beautiful writing in English, whether in prose or in verse; I mean that interplay of vowel-sounds in which no language can match us. We have so many vowel sounds indeed, and so few vowels to express them, that the foreigner, mistaking our modesty, complains against God’s plenty. We alone, for example, sound by a natural vowel that noble *I*, which other nations can only compass by diphthongs. Let us consider that vowel for a moment or two and mark how it leads off the dance of the Graces, its sisters:﻿—

===Chinese===
其次，请探究一下这个东西——尽管批评家们奇怪地忽略了它，在我看来，它却是英语美文写作的头一条、或者几乎头一条秘诀，无论散文还是诗；我指的是元音的交相辉映（interplay of vowel-sounds），在这一层上，没有任何语言比得上我们。我们的元音实在太多，而拼写元音的字母又实在太少，以致外国人错把我们的谦抑当作了本相，对着上帝这份过量的丰赐抱怨起来。举例说，唯有我们用一个天然的元音发出那个高贵的 *I*，别的民族只能靠双元音去凑近它。让我们花一两分钟琢磨这个元音，看看它如何领着它的姐妹们——美惠三女神（the Graces）——起舞：——

===Original===
> Arise, shine; for thy light is come, and the glory of the Lord is risen upon thee.

===Chinese===
> 兴起，发光吧！因为你的光已经来到，主的荣耀已经升起，照耀着你。

===Original===
Mark how expressively it drops to the solemn vowel “O,” and anon how expressively it reasserts itself to express re-arisen delight:﻿—

===Chinese===
请留意它如何富于表情地沉降到那庄严的元音「O」上，片刻之后，又如何富于表情地重新挺立起来，表达再度升起的欢欣：——

===Original===
> Arise, shine, for thy light is come, and the glory of the Lord is risen upon thee. For behold the darkness shall cover the earth, and gross darkness the people: but the Lord shall arise upon thee, and his glory shall be seen upon thee. And the Gentiles shall come to thy light, and Kings to the brightness of thy rising.

===Chinese===
> 兴起，发光吧！因为你的光已经来到，主的荣耀已经升起，照耀着你。看哪，黑暗遮盖大地，幽暗遮盖万民，主却要升起照耀你，他的荣耀要显现在你身上。万国要来就你的光，君王要来就你升起的光辉。

===Original===
Take another passage in which the first lift of this *I* vowel yields to its graver sisters as though the sound sank into the very heart of the sense.

===Chinese===
再取另一段文字，其中 *I* 元音最初的上扬让位给它那些更庄重的姐妹，仿佛声音一直沉进了意义的心脏深处。

===Original===
> I will arise and go to my father, and will say unto him, “Father, I have sinned against heaven, and before thee, and am no more worthy to be called thy son.”

===Chinese===
> 我要起来，到我父亲那里去，向他说：父亲，我得罪了天，又得罪了你；从今以后，我不配称为你的儿子。

===Original===
“And am n*o* m*o*re worthy to be called thy s*o*n.” Mark the deep *O*’s. “For this my son was dead and is alive again; he was lost and is found.” “O my son, my son Absalom”﻿—observe the *I* and *O* how they interchime, until the *O* of sorrow tolls the lighter note down:﻿—

===Chinese===
「从今以后，我不配称为你的儿子。」留意那深沉的 *O* 音。「因为我这个儿子是死而复活，失而又得的。」「我儿押沙龙啊，我儿」——看 *I* 与 *O* 如何交相鸣响，直到悲伤的 *O* 把那较轻的音符沉沉敲落：——

===Original===
> O my son Absalom, my son, my son Absalom! Would God I had died for thee, O Absolom, my son, my son!

===Chinese===
> 我儿押沙龙啊，我儿，我儿押沙龙啊！我恨不得替你死，押沙龙啊，我儿，我儿！

===Original===
Or take this lyric, by admission one of the loveliest written in this present age, and mark here too how the vowels play and ring and chime and toll.

===Chinese===
或者取这首抒情诗——公认是当今时代所写出的最可爱的诗篇之一——也请留意这里的元音如何嬉游、鸣响、谐鸣、如钟低唱。

===Original===
> I will arise and go now, and go to Innisfree,
 

 And a small cabin build there, of clay and wattles made;
 

 N*i*ne b*e*an rows will I have there, a h*i*ve for the h*o*ney b*ee*,
 

 And l*i*ve al*o*ne in a b*ee*-l*ou*d gl*a*de.8
> And I shall have some peace there, for peace comes dropping slow,
 

 Dropping from the veils of the morning to where the cricket sings;
 

 There midnight’s all a glimmer, and noon a purple glow,
 

 And evening full of the linnet’s wings.
 

 I will arise and go now, for always night and day
 

 I hear lake-water lapping, with low sounds by the shore;
 

 While I stand on the roadway, or on the pavements grey,
 

 I hear it in the deep heart’s core.

===Chinese===
> 现在我就要动身前去，去茵尼斯弗利岛（Innisfree），
 

 在那里筑一座小屋，以泥笆和枝条编成；
 

 我要在那里种九垄豆子，养一巢蜜蜂，
 

 独自住在蜂鸣嗡嗡的林间空地。8
> 在那里我将享得安宁，因为安宁徐徐滴落，
 

 从清晨的面纱滴落到蟋蟀吟唱的地方；
 

 那里午夜微光闪烁，正午一片紫辉，
 

 黄昏盈满了红雀的翅膀。
 

 现在我就要动身前去，因为无论日夜，
 

 我总听见湖水轻舔，在岸边低声拍响；
 

 不论我站在大路上，还是站在灰色的人行道上，
 

 我都听见它在我心底至深之处。

===Original===
I think if you will but open your ears to this beautiful vowel-play which runs through all the best of our prose and poetry, whether you ever learn to master it or not, you will have acquired a new delight, and one various enough to last you though you live to a very old age.

===Chinese===
我想，只要你们肯把耳朵向这美妙的元音演奏敞开——它流贯于我们全部散文与诗歌的菁华之中——那么无论你们日后学不学得会驾驭它，你们都已经获得了一种新的愉悦，而且这愉悦足够变化多端，尽够你们消受，哪怕你们活到很高很高的年纪。

===Original===
All this of which I am speaking is Art: and Literature being an Art, do you not see how personal a thing it is﻿—how it cannot escape being personal? No two men (unless they talk Jargon) say the same thing in the same way. As is a man’s imagination, as is his character, as is the harmony in himself, as is his ear, as is his skill, so and not otherwise he will speak, so and not otherwise than they can respond to that imagination, that character, that order of his intellect, that harmony of his soul, his hearers will hear him. Let me conclude with this great passage from Newman which I beg you, having heard it, to ponder:﻿—

===Chinese===
我正在谈的这一切都是艺术（Art）：而文学既是一门艺术，诸位难道看不出它是何等个人的一件事——它如何不可能不带上个人的印记？没有哪两个人（除非他们说套话）会用同一种方式说同一件事。一个人的想象力是什么样，他的品性是什么样，他内在的和谐是什么样，他的耳朵、他的技艺是什么样，他就会那样、而且只会那样说话；他怎样说话，便怎样被听见：他的听者所能回应的，不外乎那份想象力、那份品性、他心智的那副秩序、他灵魂的那份和谐——他们也只能在这样的范围里听见他。让我以纽曼的这一伟大段落作结，恳请诸位听过之后细细玩味：——

===Original===
> If then the power of speech is as great as any that can be named﻿—if the origin of language is by many philosophers considered nothing short of divine﻿—if by means of words the secrets of the heart are brought to light, pain of soul is relieved, hidden grief is carried off, sympathy conveyed, experience recorded, and wisdom perpetuated﻿—if by great authors the many are drawn up into unity, national character is fixed, a people speaks, the past and the future, the East and the West are brought into communication with each other﻿—if such men are, in a word, the spokesmen and the prophets of the human family﻿—it will not answer to make light of Literature or to neglect its study: rather we may be sure that, in proportion as we master it in whatever language, and imbibe its spirit, we shall ourselves become in our own measure the ministers of like benefits to others﻿—be they many or few, be they in the obscurer or the more distinguished walks of life﻿—who are united to us by social ties, and are within the sphere of our personal influence.

===Chinese===
> 倘若言语的力量真如人们所能指名的任何力量那样伟大——倘若语言的起源被许多哲学家认为简直近乎神授——倘若凭借语词，心底的秘密得以揭明，灵魂的苦痛得以宽解，暗藏的悲戚得以携除，同情得以传递，经验得以记录，智慧得以永续——倘若凭着伟大的作家，众生被提携而归于一体，民族的品格得以奠定，一个民族得以发言，过去与未来、东方与西方得以互通声气——倘若这样的人，一言以蔽之，正是人类大家庭的代言人与先知——那么，轻看文学或疏忽对它的研习，是说不过去的：我们反倒可以确信，我们在何种程度上、以无论哪种语言掌握它、吸收它的精神，我们自己便将在相应的分量上，成为同类惠益的施行者，施于他人——无论其人或多或寡，无论他们身处较幽暗还是较显达的人生行旅——他们借社会的纽带与我们相连，处于我们个人影响的范围之内。

## Lecture VIII On the Lineage of English Literature (I) / VIII 英国文学的谱系（上）

===Original===
Wednesday, October 22

===Chinese===
10 月 22 日，星期三

===Original===
You may think it strange, Gentlemen, that of a course of ten lectures which aim to treat English Literature as an affair of practice, I should propose to spend two in discussing our literary lineage: a man’s lineage and geniture being reckoned, as a rule, among the things he cannot be reasonably asked to amend. But since of high breeding is begotten (as most of us believe) a disposition to high thoughts, high deeds; since to have it and be modestly conscious of it is to carry within us a faithful monitor persuading us to whatsoever in conduct is gentle, honourable, of good repute, and so silently dissuading us from base thoughts, low ends, ignoble gains; seeing, moreover, that a man will often do more to match his father’s virtue than he would to improve himself; I shall endeavour, in this and my next lecture, to scour that spur of ancestry and present it to you as so bright and sharp an incentive that you, who read English Literature and practise writing here in Cambridge, shall not pass out from her insensible of the dignity of your studies, or without pride or remorse according as you have interpreted in practice the motto, “Noblesse oblige.”

===Chinese===
诸位先生，你们或许觉得奇怪：这一系列共十讲的课程，宗旨是把英国文学当作一件实践之事来讲，我竟打算拨出两讲来讨论我们的文学谱系——因为照例说，一个人的血统与出身，向来被归在他无法被合情合理地要求改善的事物之列。但是，既然（如我们大多数人所信）高贵的门第能孕育出向往高洁思想、高洁功业的性情；既然拥有这份门第而又对它怀有谦逊的自觉，就等于胸中坐着一位忠实的谏士，劝我们凡行事皆温文、端正、可敬，从而默默劝阻我们远离卑污的念头、低下的目标、不义的钱财；何况，一个人为追配父辈的德行所肯出的力，往往比为修养自身所肯出的更多；那么，在这一讲和下一讲里，我将勉力把「祖先」这只马刺擦拭干净，把它作为一件光彩而锋利的激励呈献给你们，使你们这些在剑桥此地攻读英国文学、练习写作的人，走出她的门墙时，既不致对自己学业的尊严无所感应，也不致既无自豪亦无悔恨——自豪抑或悔恨，全看你们在实践中如何践行那句箴言：「Noblesse oblige」（贵族地位，责无旁贷）。

===Original===
> ’Tis wisdom, and that high,
 

 For men to use their fortune reverently
 

 Even in youth.

===Chinese===
> 这是智慧，而且是高超的智慧：
让人虔敬地对待自己的际遇，
哪怕正当青春年少。

===Original===
Let me add that, just as a knowledge of his family failings will help one man in economising his estate, or warn another to shun for his health the pleasures of the table, so some knowledge of our lineage in letters may put us, as Englishmen, on the watch for certain national defects (for such we have), on our guard against certain sins which too easily beset us. Nay, this watchfulness may well reach down from matters of great moment to seeming trifles. It is good for us to recognise with Wordsworth that

===Chinese===
还要补充一句：正如了解家族的积弊，能帮助这一位节省家计，或警示那一位为健康起见远避口腹之乐；同样，对我们的文学谱系有所认识，也能使我们英国人提防某些民族性的缺陷（这种缺陷我们确有），戒备某些极易缠上我们的罪过。不仅如此，这种警惕尽可以从头等重大的事情，一直贯注到貌似琐屑的小事上去。我们最好同华兹华斯（Wordsworth）一起承认：——

===Original===
> We must be free or die, who speak the tongue
 

 That Shakespeare spake; the faith and morals hold
 

 Which Milton held. In everything we are sprung
 

 Of Earth’s first blood, have titles manifold.

===Chinese===
> 我们必须自由，否则便死：我们说的是
莎士比亚（Shakespeare）所说的语言；持守的是
弥尔顿（Milton）所持守的信仰与德行。我们在一切方面
都出自大地的最初血脉，握有种种名分。

===Original===
But, though less important, it is good also to recognise that, as sons of Cambridge, we equally offend against her breeding when in our scientific writings we allow ourselves to talk of a microbe as an “antibody.”

===Chinese===
但是，虽说事情次要，同样有益的是认识到：作为剑桥的儿子，当我们在科学著述里放任自己把一种微生物唤作「抗体」（antibody）时，我们便同样违逆了她的教养。

===Original===
Now, because a great deal of what I have to say this morning, if not heretical, will yet run contrary to the vogue and practice of the Schools for these thirty years, I will take the leap into my subject over a greater man’s back and ask you to listen with particular attention to the following long passage from a writer whose opinion you may challenge, but whose authority to speak as a master of English prose no one in this room will deny.

===Chinese===
那么，由于今天上午我要说的许多话——即便不算异端——也将与三十年来此间学院的风尚与实践背道而驰，我便要踩着一位更伟大人物的脊背跃入本题，并请你们格外留心地去听下面这一长段文字：它出自一位作家之笔，他的见解你们尽可质疑，但他作为英语散文大师发言的权威，本室之中无人会否认。

===Original===
> When [says Cardinal Newman] we survey the stream of human affairs for the last three thousand years, we find it to run thus:﻿—At first sight there is so much fluctuation, agitation, ebbing and flowing, that we may despair to discern any law in its movements, taking the earth as its bed and mankind as its contents; but on looking more closely and attentively we shall discern, in spite of the heterogeneous materials and the various histories and fortunes which are found in the race of man during the long period I have mentioned, a certain formation amid the chaos﻿—one and one only﻿—and extending, though not over the whole earth, yet through a very considerable portion of it. Man is a social being and can hardly exist without society, and in matter of fact societies have ever existed all over the habitable earth. The greater part of these associations have been political or religious, and have been comparatively limited in extent and temporary. They have been formed and dissolved by the force of accidents, or by inevitable circumstances; and when we have enumerated them one by one we have made of them all that can be made. But there is one remarkable association which attracts the attention of the philosopher, not political nor religious﻿—or at least only partially and not essentially such﻿—which began in the earliest times and grew with each succeeding age till it reached its complete development, and then continued on, vigorous and unwearied, and still remains as definite and as firm as ever it was. Its bond is a *common civilisation*: and though there are other civilisations in the world, as there are other societies, yet *this* civilisation, together with the society which is its creation and its home, is so distinctive and luminous in its character, so imperial in its extent, so imposing in its duration, and so utterly without rival on the face of the earth, that the association may fitly assume to itself the title of “Human Society,” and *its* civilisation the abstract term “Civilisation.”
> There are indeed great outlying portions of mankind which are not, perhaps never have been, included in this Human Society; still they are outlying portions and nothing else, fragmentary, unsociable, solitary and unmeaning, protesting and revolting against the grand central formation of which I am speaking, but not uniting with each other into a second whole. I am not denying, of course, the civilisation of the Chinese, for instance, though it be not our civilisation; but it is a huge, stationary, unattractive, morose civilisation. Nor do I deny a civilisation to the Hindus, nor to the ancient Mexicans, nor to the Saracens, nor (in a certain sense) to the Turks; but each of these races has its own civilisation, as separate from one another as from ours.
> I do not see how they can be all brought under one idea.﻿ ﻿…
> Gentlemen, let me here observe that I am not entering upon the question of races, or upon their history. I have nothing to do with ethnology; I take things as I find them on the surface of history and am but classifying phenomena. Looking, then, at the countries which surround the Mediterranean Sea as a whole, I see them to be from time immemorial, the seat of an association of intellect and mind such as to deserve to be called the Intellect and the Mind of the Human Kind. Starting as it does, and advancing from certain centres, till their respective influences intersect and conflict, and then at length intermingle and combine, a common Thought has been generated, and a common Civilisation defined and established. Egypt is one such starting point, Syria, another, Greece a third, Italy a fourth and North Africa a fifth﻿—afterwards France and Spain. As time goes on, and as colonisation and conquest work their changes, we see a great association of nations formed, of which the Roman Empire is the maturity and the most intelligible expression: an association, however, not political but mental, based on the same intellectual ideas and advancing by common intellectual methods.﻿ ﻿… In its earliest age it included far more of the Eastern world than it has since; in these later times it has taken into its compass a new hemisphere; in the Middle Ages it lost Africa, Egypt and Syria, and extended itself to Germany, Scandinavia and the British Isles. At one time its territory was flooded by strange and barbarous races, but the existing civilisation was vigorous enough to vivify what threatened to stifle it, and to assimilate to the old social forms what came to expel them: and thus the civilisation of modern times remains what it was of old; not Chinese, or Hindu, or Mexican, or Saracen﻿ ﻿… but the lineal descendant, or rather the continuation﻿—*mutatis mutandis*﻿—of the civilisation which began in Palestine and Greece.

===Chinese===
> ［纽曼枢机（Cardinal Newman）说道］当我们纵览近三千年来人间事务的洪流，我们发现它是这样奔流的：——乍看之下，其中波动、激荡、涨落如此之繁，我们或许要绝望，以为这条以大地为河床、以人类为河水的洪流，其运动中辨不出任何法则；但更切近、更凝神地看去，尽管在我所说的漫长时期内，人类这个种族中存有种种异质的材料、种种互异的经历与际遇，我们仍会从混沌之中辨出一种确定的构造——独一无二的一种——它虽未遍及整个大地，却延展于其中极可观的一部分。人是社会性的存在，离了社会几乎无法生存；而事实上，在地球上一切可居之处，社会从来都存在。这些结社大半是政治性或宗教性的，范围相对有限，为时相对短暂。它们因偶然之事或不可避免之境遇的力量而结成，又因而解体；我们把它们一一数过之后，便已穷尽了它们身上所能得出的一切。但有一个不同寻常的结社吸引着哲学家的注意——它既非政治性的，亦非宗教性的，或者至少只是局部如此、而非本质如此——它始于最古的年代，随每个相继的时代而生长，直至达到完全的发育，随后继续前行，精力充沛、不知倦怠，至今仍同往昔一样明确、一样坚固。它的纽带是一种*共同的文明*（civilisation）：尽管世上还有别的文明，正如还有别的社会，然而*这一*文明，连同以它为造物、以它为家园的那个社会，在性质上如此独特而明亮，在幅员上如此帝国般广被，在绵延上如此令人敬畏，在地面之上如此绝无对手，以至于这个结社尽可以自居「人类社会」（Human Society）的名号，而*它的*文明也尽可以领受那个抽象的名词——「文明」（Civilisation）。
> 诚然，人类有一些广大的边远部分不曾、或许从来不曾包括在这个人类社会之内；但它们终究只是边远部分而已，此外什么也不是——碎裂、寡合、孤单而无意义，抗议着、反叛着我所说的这个宏伟的中央构造，却不曾彼此联合成第二个整体。当然，我并不否认比如中国人的文明，尽管它不是我们的文明；但它是一种庞大、停滞、无吸引力、阴郁的文明。我也不否认印度人有一种文明，不否认古代墨西哥人、萨拉森人以及（在某种意义上的）土耳其人有其文明；但这些种族各各有自己的文明，彼此之隔绝，一如同我们之隔绝。
> 我看不出怎能把它们统统归于一个观念之下。……
> 诸位先生，请容我在此说明：我并不打算进入种族问题，也不打算谈它们的历史。我与人种学毫无干系；我把事物按其在历史表面上呈现的样子接过来，所做的不过是给现象分类。于是，把环绕地中海的诸国当作一个整体来看，我看到它们自邃古以来，便是一个智识与心灵之结社的坐席，其崇高配得上被称为人类的智识与人类的心灵。它从若干中心出发、推进，直到各家的影响交错、冲突，最终又彼此渗透、结合，于是一种共同的思想被生成，一种共同的文明被界定、被确立。埃及是一个这样的起点，叙利亚是第二个，希腊是第三个，意大利是第四个，北非是第五个——随后又有法兰西和西班牙。随着时间推移，随着殖民与征服施展它们的变化，我们看到一个伟大的民族结社形成了，罗马帝国便是它的成熟形态和最明了的表达：然而这个结社不是政治性的，而是心智性的，它立足于同样的智识观念，循着共同的智识方法而推进。……在最早的年代，它所包括的东方世界远多于后世；在晚近的时代，它把一个新的半球纳入怀抱；在中世纪，它失去了阿非利加、埃及和叙利亚，而把自己伸展到德意志、斯堪的纳维亚和不列颠诸岛。有一个时期，它的领土曾被陌生而野蛮的种族淹没，但既存的文明足够强健，能赋生机于那威胁着要闷死它的事物，能把那前来驱逐旧社会形式的东西同化进旧形式里去：于是近代的文明依然是它昔日的样子；不是中国的、印度的、墨西哥的、萨拉森的……而是那个肇始于巴勒斯坦（Palestine）和希腊的文明之嫡系后裔——或者毋宁说，是它的延续——*mutatis mutandis*（细节上作相应变通）。

===Original===
To omit, then, all minor debts such as what of arithmetic, what of astronomy, what of geography, we owe to the Saracen, from Palestine we derive the faith of Europe shared (in the language of the Bidding Prayer) by all Christian people dispersed throughout the world; as to Greece we owe the rudiments of our Western art, philosophy, letters; and not only the rudiments but the continuing inspiration, so that﻿—though entirely superseded in worship, as even in the Athens of Pericles they were worshipped only by an easy, urbane, more than half humorous tolerance﻿—Apollo and the Muses, Zeus and the great ones of Olympus, Hermes and Hephaestus, Athene in her armour, with her vanquisher the foam-born irresistible Aphrodite, these remain the authentic gods of our literature, beside whom the gods of northern Europe﻿—Odin, Thor, Freya﻿—are strangers, unhomely, uncanny as the shadows of unfamiliar furniture on the walls of an inn. Sprung though great numbers of us are from the loins of Northmen, it is in these gracious deities of the South that we find the familiar and the real, as from the heroes of the sister-island, Cucullain and Concobar, we turn to Hercules, to Perseus, to Bellerophon, even to actual men of history, saying “Give us Leonidas, give us Horatius, give us Regulus. These are the mighty ones we understand, and from whom, in a direct line of tradition, we understand Harry of Agincourt, Philip Sidney and our Nelson.”

===Chinese===
那么，撇开一切细小的债务不谈——比如我们从萨拉森人那里得来的那点算术、那点天文学、那点地理学——我们从巴勒斯坦得来了欧洲的信仰，即用吁祷文（Bidding Prayer）的话说，为散布于世界各地的全体基督徒子民所共同分享的信仰；而向希腊，我们欠着西方艺术、哲学、文学的初基；且不止初基，还有持续的灵感；以致——尽管在崇拜上已被彻底取代，因为即便在伯里克利（Pericles）的雅典，人们对他们的崇拜也不过出于一种从容的、温文尔雅的、大半出于幽默的宽容——阿波罗（Apollo）与缪斯诸神、宙斯（Zeus）与奥林波斯（Olympus）的诸位大神、赫尔墨斯（Hermes）与赫菲斯托斯（Hephaestus）、披甲的雅典娜（Athene），连同她的征服者、生于泡沫、无可抗拒的阿佛洛狄忒（Aphrodite），依然是我们文学真正的神祇；相形之下，北欧的诸神——奥丁（Odin）、托尔（Thor）、弗雷娅（Freya）——却是陌生人，生分、怪异，宛如旅店墙壁上陌生家具投下的影子。我们中间尽管有大群人出自北人的腰胯，我们却在南方的这些温雅神祇身上觅得亲切与真实；正如我们从姊妹之岛的英雄库丘林（Cucullain）与康纳尔（Concobar）那里转过身来，转向赫拉克勒斯（Hercules）、珀尔修斯（Perseus）、柏勒罗丰（Bellerophon），甚至转向历史上真实的人物，说道：「给我们列奥尼达（Leonidas），给我们贺拉提乌斯（Horatius），给我们雷古卢斯（Regulus）。这些才是我们懂得的伟人；也正是从他们，循着一条直接的传承之线，我们才懂得阿金库尔的哈利（Harry of Agincourt）、菲利普·西德尼（Philip Sidney）和我们的纳尔逊（Nelson）。」

===Original===
Now since, of the Mediterranean peoples, the Hebrews discovered the Unseen God whom the body of Western civilisation has learnt to worship; since the Greeks invented art, philosophy, letters; since Rome found and developed the idea of imperial government, of imperial colonies as superseding merely fissiparous ones, of settling where she conquered (*ubi Romanus vicit ibi habitat*) and so extending with Government that system of law which Europe still obeys; we cannot be surprised that Israel, Greece, Rome﻿—each in turn﻿—set store on a pure ancestry. Though Christ be the veritable Son of God, his ancestry must be traced back through his supposed father Joseph to the stem of Jesse, and so to Abraham, father of the race.
