Again, as jealously as the Evangelist claimed Jesus for a Hebrew of the Hebrews, so, if you will turn to the “Menexenus” of Plato in the Oration of Aspasia over the dead who perished in battle, you hear her claim that “No Pelopes nor Cadmians, nor Egyptians, nor Dauni, nor the rest of the crowd of born foreigners dwell with us; but ours is the land of pure Hellenes, free from admixture.” These proud Athenians, as you know, wore brooches in the shape of golden grasshoppers, to signify that they were *αὐτὁχθονες*, children of Attica, sprung direct from her soil. And so, again, the true Roman, while enlarging Rome’s citizenship over Asia, Africa, Gaul, to our remote Britain, insisted, even in days of the later Empire, on his pure descent from Aeneas and Romulus﻿—

===Chinese===
那么，既然在地中海诸民族中间，是希伯来人发现了那位无形的上帝，而西方文明的主体已学会敬拜他；既然是希腊人发明了艺术、哲学、文学；既然是罗马人发现并发展了帝国治理的观念——以帝国式的殖民取代纯然裂殖式的殖民，在征服之处定居（*ubi Romanus vicit ibi habitat*，罗马人所胜之地即其所居之地），并随着治理一同扩展那套欧洲至今仍然遵从的法系——那么，以色列、希腊、罗马相继都珍重纯粹的世系，我们便无从诧怪了。基督虽是上帝亲生的儿子，他的世系仍必须经由他名义上的父亲约瑟（Joseph），上溯到耶西（Jesse）的根干，再上溯到亚伯拉罕（Abraham），即种族之父。再者，正如福音书作者那样急切地把耶稣认作「希伯来人中的希伯来人」，你们若去翻柏拉图（Plato）的《美涅克塞努篇》（"Menexenus"）里阿斯帕西娅（Aspasia）悼念阵亡者的那篇演说，也会听到她声称：「佩洛普斯（Pelopes）的苗裔与卡德摩斯人（Cadmians）、埃及人、道尼人（Dauni），以及其余那群生来的外邦人，都不同我们同住；我们的土地是纯粹希腊人（Hellenes）的土地，不容混杂。」这些高傲的雅典人，如你们所知，佩戴黄金蚱蜢形状的胸针，以表明自己是 *αὐτὁχθονες*（土生土长者），是阿提卡（Attica）的孩子，直接从她的土壤里生出来。同样，真正的罗马人，一面把罗马的公民权扩及亚细亚、阿非利加、高卢，直到我们偏远的布列颠，一面直到帝国后期仍坚执自己是埃涅阿斯（Aeneas）与罗慕路斯（Romulus）的纯粹苗裔——

===Original===
> Unde Remnes et Quirites proque prole posterum
 

 Romuli matrem crearet et nepotem Caesarem.

===Chinese===
> Unde Remnes et Quirites proque prole posterum
 

 Romuli matrem crearet et nepotem Caesarem.

===Original===
> With the Ramnes, Quirites, together ancestrally proud as they drew
 

 From Romulus down to our Caesar-last, best of that blood, of that threw.

===Chinese===
> 连同拉姆尼斯人（Ramnes）与奎里特斯人（Quirites）——同以先祖自豪，他们把世系
从罗慕路斯一路数到我们的恺撒——那条血脉之最后的、最好的一脉。

===Original===
Here is a boast that we English must be content to forgo. We may wear a rose on St. George’s day, if we are clever enough to grow one. The Welsh, I dare say, have less difficulty with the leek. But April the 23rd is not a time of roses that we can pluck them as we pass, nor can we claim St. George as a compatriot﻿—*Cappadocius nostras*. We have, to be sure, a few legendary heroes, of whom King Arthur and Robin Hood are (I suppose) the greatest; but, save in some Celtic corners of the land, we have few fairies, and these no great matter; while, as for tutelary gods, our springs, our wells, our groves, cliffs, mountainsides, either never possessed them or possess them no longer. Not of our landscape did it happen that

===Chinese===
这一份夸口，我们英国人只好甘愿放弃。圣乔治日（St. George's day）我们尽可以戴一朵玫瑰，如果我们本事大到种得出一朵的话。威尔士人应付那棵韭葱，我敢说，困难总要小些。但 4 月 23 日不是玫瑰时节，容不得我们路过时信手摘取；我们也不能把圣乔治认作同胞——*Cappadocius nostras*（咱们卡帕多西亚老乡）。诚然，我们有几位传说英雄，其中最伟大的（我想）要数亚瑟王（King Arthur）与罗宾汉（Robin Hood）；但除了这国土的几个凯尔特角落，我们几乎没有仙人，仅有的那几位也无足轻重；至于守护神，我们的泉、我们的井、我们的树丛、峭壁、山坡，要么从不曾拥有过，要么已不再拥有。下面的情形，可不曾发生在我们的风景里——

===Original===
> The lonely mountains o’er,
 

 And the resounding shore,
 

 A voice of weeping heard, and loud lament;
 

 From haunted spring and dale
 

 Edg’d with poplar pale,
 

 The parting Genius is with sighing sent.

===Chinese===
> 寂寞的群山之上，
和轰响的海岸之上，
曾听见哭泣的声音、高声的哀恸；
从精灵出没的泉与谷，
镶着苍白杨树的边缘，
告辞的守护神在叹息中被送走；

===Original===
—for the sufficient reason that no tutelary gods of importance were ever here to be dispersed.

===Chinese===
——理由至为充分：此地从来不曾有过任何重要的守护神，等着被驱散。

===Original===
Let me press this home upon you by an illustration which I choose with the double purpose of enforcing my argument and sending you to make acquaintance (if you have not already made it) with one of the loveliest poems written in our time.

===Chinese===
让我用一个例证把这一点向你们敲实。选这个例证，我怀有双重用意：既为坐实我的论点，也为打发你们去结识（如果你们还不曾结识）我们这个时代所写的最可爱的诗篇之一。

===Original===
In one of Pliny’s letters you will find a very pleasant description of the source of the Clitumnus, a small Umbrian river which, springing from a rock in a grove of cypresses, descends into the Tinia, a tributary of the Tiber. “Have you ever,” writes Pliny to his friend Romanus﻿—

===Chinese===
在普林尼（Pliny）的一封信里，你们会读到对克利图姆努斯（Clitumnus）泉源的一段十分愉快的描写：那是翁布里亚（Umbria）的一条小河，从一片柏树林中的一块岩石间涌出，下注入蒂尼亚河（Tinia）——台伯河（Tiber）的一条支流。「你可曾见过」——普林尼写信给他的朋友罗玛努斯（Romanus）说——

===Original===
> Have you ever seen the source of the Clitumnus? I suppose not, as I never heard you mention it. Let me advise you to go there at once. I have just visited it and am sorry that I put off my visit so long. At the foot of a little hill, covered with old and shady cypress trees, a spring gushes and bursts into a number of streamlets of various size. Breaking, so to speak, forth from its imprisonment, it expands into a broad basin, so clear and transparent that you may count the pebbles and little pieces of money which are thrown into it. From this point the force and weight of the water, rather than the slope of the ground, hurry it onward. What was a mere spring becomes a noble river, broad enough to allow vessels to pass each other as they sail with or against the stream. The current is so strong, though the ground is level, that barges of beam, as they go down, require no assistance of oars; while to go up is as much as can be done with oars and long poles.﻿ ﻿… The banks are clothed with abundant ash and poplar, so distinctly reflected in the transparent waters that they seem to be growing at the bottom of the river and can be counted with ease. The water is as cold as snow and as pure in colour. Hard by the spring stands an ancient and venerable temple with a statue of the river-god Clitumnus, clothed in the customary robe of state. The Oracles here delivered attest the presence of the deity. Close in the precinct stand several little chapels dedicated to particular gods, each of whom owns his distinctive name and special worship, and is the tutelary deity of a runlet. For beside the principal spring, which is, as it were, the parent of all the rest, there are several smaller ones which have their distinct sources but unite their waters with the Clitumnus, over which a bridge is thrown, separating the sacred part of the river from that which is open to general use. Above the bridge you may only go in a boat; below it, you may swim. The people of the town of Hispallum, to whom Augustus gave this place, furnish baths and lodgings at the public expense. There are several small dwelling-houses on the banks, in specially picturesque situations, and they stand quite close to the waterside. In short, everything in the neighbourhood will give you pleasure. You may also amuse yourself with numberless inscriptions on the pillars and walls, celebrating the praises of the stream and of its tutelary god. Many of these you will admire, and some will make you laugh. But no! You are too well cultivated to laugh at such things. Farewell.

===Chinese===
> 克利图姆努斯的泉源？我想没有，因为我从未听你提起过。让我劝你立刻就去。我刚刚到过那里，只后悔把这趟拖延了这么久。在一座小山脚下——山上覆满古老而荫翳的柏树——一股泉水涌出，迸作大小不一的许多细流。它仿佛冲破了囚禁，豁然扩展成一片开阔的水潭，清澈透明，潭底的卵石与投进去的小额钱币都数得出来。从这里起，催它前行的是水流自身的力量与分量，而非地面的坡度。一脉单纯的泉水就此成为一条堂堂的河，宽阔得足够让上下水的船只彼此交错驶过。地面虽然平坦，水势却如此强劲，宽体的驳船顺流而下时无须桨助；而逆流而上，便是划桨加长篙一齐上，也够人使尽气力的了。……两岸生满繁茂的梣树与杨树，倒映在透明的河水里，历历分明，仿佛就长在河底，数也数得清。这水凉得像雪，颜色也纯得像雪。泉边矗立着一座古老而庄严的神庙，供着河神克利图姆努斯的像，披着惯常的礼服。此地颁下的神谕，证明神明确实临在。围墙内紧挨着还立着几座小祠，分献给各别的神；每一位都有自己专名和特异的祭祀，都是一道细流的守护神。因为除那道主泉——它仿佛是其余诸泉的母亲——之外，还有几股较小的泉，各有各的源头，而水流则与克利图姆努斯相汇；河上架着一座桥，把河的圣洁部分同向公众开放的部分分开。桥的上游只许乘船；桥的下游可以游泳。希斯佩卢姆（Hispallum）镇的人民——奥古斯都（Augustus）把此地赐给了他们——以公费供设浴场与宿处。岸上有几座小小的居所，各占着格外如画的形势，紧贴着水边。总之，左近的一切都会给你愉悦。你还可以拿柱上墙上数不清的题铭自娱，它们颂扬这道泉流和它的守护神。这些题铭有许多你会叹赏，有一些会让你发笑。可是不！你的教养太深，不会笑话这些东西。珍重。

===Original===
Clitumnus still gushes from its rocks among the cypresses, as in Pliny’s day. The god has gone from his temple, on the frieze of which you may read this later inscription﻿—“*Deus Angelorum, qui fecit Resurrectionem.*” After many centuries and almost in our day, by the brain of Cavour and the sword of Garibaldi, he has made a resurrection for Italy. As part of that resurrection (for no nation can live and be great without its poet) was born a true poet, Carducci. He visited the bountiful, everlasting source, and of what did he sing? Possess yourselves, as for a shilling you may, of his Ode “Alle fonte del Clitumno,” and read: for few nobler poems have adorned our time. He sang of the weeping willow, the ilex, ivy, cypress and the presence of the god still immanent among them. He sang of Umbria, of the ensigns of Rome, of Hannibal swooping down over the Alps; he sang of the nuptials of Janus and Comesena, progenitors of the Italian people; of nymphs, naiads, and the moonlight dances of Oreads; of flocks descending to the river at dusk, of the homestead, the barefooted mother, the clinging child, the father, clad in goatskins, guiding the ox-wagon; and he ends on the very note of Virgil’s famous apostrophe

===Chinese===
克利图姆努斯至今仍像普林尼时代那样，从柏树林间的岩石里喷涌。神明已从他的神庙离去，庙的檐壁上，你们可以读到这样一行后代的铭文——「*Deus Angelorum, qui fecit Resurrectionem*」（众天使之神，祂使复活成就）。许多个世纪之后，几乎就在我们的时代，凭加富尔（Cavour）的头脑与加里波第（Garibaldi）的剑，他为意大利成就了一场复活。作为那场复活的一部分（因为没有哪个民族能够没有自己的诗人而存活而伟大），诞生了一位真正的诗人——卡尔杜齐（Carducci）。他造访了那道丰沛不竭的泉源，他歌唱了什么呢？花上一个先令，你们便能把他的那首颂歌《在克利图姆努斯泉边》（"Alle fonte del Clitumno"）据为己有——去读吧：因为装点了我们时代的更高贵的诗篇，为数无几。他歌唱垂柳、冬青槲、常春藤、柏树，歌唱依然内寓于它们中间的神明的临在。他歌唱翁布里亚，歌唱罗马的军旗，歌唱汉尼拔（Hannibal）掠过阿尔卑斯山俯冲而下；他歌唱雅努斯（Janus）与科梅塞纳（Comesena）——意大利人的先祖——的婚配；歌唱宁芙、水泽仙子（naiads）与俄瑞阿得斯（Oreads）的月光之舞；歌唱黄昏时分下到河边的羊群，歌唱农舍、赤脚的母亲、偎依的孩子、身披山羊皮赶着牛车的父亲；而他的收束，恰恰落在维吉尔（Virgil）那句著名呼告的音调上——

===Original===
> Sed neque Medorum silvae, ditissima terra﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
> Sed neque Medorum silvae, ditissima terra﻿ ﻿…

===Original===
with an invocation of Italy﻿—Italy, mother of bullocks for agriculture, of wild colts for battle, mother of corn and of the vine, Roman mother of enduring laws and medieval mother of illustrious arts. The mountains, woods and waters of green Umbria applaud the song, and across their applause is heard the whistle of the railway train bearing promise of new industries and a new national life.

===Chinese===
——而以对意大利的一篇祈唤收束——意大利，农耕犍牛之母，战阵烈驹之母，谷与葡萄之母，恒久法律的罗马之母，显赫艺术的中世纪之母。绿色的翁布里亚，它的群山、树林与流水为这支歌鼓掌；而穿过这片掌声，传来铁路列车的汽笛声，载着新工业与新的民族生活的许诺。

===Original===
> E tu, pia madre di giovenchi invitti
 

 a franger glebe e rintegrar maggesi
 

 e d’ annitrenti in guerra aspri polledri,
 

 Italia madre,
> madre di biade e viti e leggi eterne
 

 ed incliti arti a raddolcir la vita
 

 salve! a te i canti de l’ antica lode
 

 io rinovello.
> Plaudono i monti al carme e i boschi e l’ acque
 

 de l’ Umbria verde: in faccia a noi fumando
 

 ed anelando nuove industrie in corsa
 

 fischia il vapore.

===Chinese===
> E tu, pia madre di giovenchi invitti
 

 a franger glebe e rintegrar maggesi
 

 e d’ annitrenti in guerra aspri polledri,
 

 Italia madre,
> madre di biade e viti e leggi eterne
 

 ed incliti arti a raddolcir la vita
 

 salve! a te i canti de l’ antica lode
 

 io rinovello.
> Plaudono i monti al carme e i boschi e l’ acque
 

 de l’ Umbria verde: in faccia a noi fumando
 

 ed anelando nuove industrie in corsa
 

 fischia il vapore.

===Original===
> And thou, O pious mother of unvanquished
 

 Bullocks to break glebe, to restore the fallow,
 

 And of fierce colts for neighing in the battle:
 

 Italy, mother,
> Mother of corn and vines and of eternal
 

 Laws and illustrious arts the life to sweeten,
 

 Hail, hail, all hail! The song of ancient praises
 

 Renew I to thee!
> The mountains, woods and waters of green Umbria
 

 Applaud the song: and here before us fuming
 

 And longing for new industries, a-racing
 

 Whistles the white steam.9

===Chinese===
> 而你，哦虔诚的母亲，养着所向无敌的
犍牛，去破土开垄、回复休耕的田地，
又养着烈性的驹子，供战阵上的嘶鸣：
意大利，母亲，

谷物与葡萄的母亲，恒久
法律与显赫艺术的母亲，以它们甜化人生，
万福，万福，全天之福！那支古老的颂歌
我向你重新唱起！

绿色的翁布里亚，它的群山、树林与流水
为这歌声鼓掌：而就在我们眼前，喷吐着烟，
渴望着新的工业，一路竞驰，
鸣响汽笛的，是那白汽。9

===Original===
I put it to you, Gentlemen, that, worthy as are the glories of England to be sung, this note of Carducci’s we cannot decently or honestly strike. Great lives have been bled away into Tweed and Avon: great spirits have been oared down the Thames to Traitor’s Gate and the Tower. Deeds done on the Cam have found their way into history. But I once traced the Avon to its source under Naseby battlefield, and found it issuing from the fragments of a stucco swan. No god mounts guard over the headwater of the Thames; and the only Englishman who boldly claims a divine descent is (I understand) an impostor who runs an Agapemone. In short we are a mixed race, and our literature is derivative. Let us confine our pride to those virtues, not few, which are honestly ours. A Roman noble, even today, has some excuse for reckoning a god in his ancestry, or at least a wolf among its wet-nurses: but of us English even those who came over with William the Norman have the son of a tanner’s daughter for escort. I very well remember that, the other day, writers who vindicated our hereditary House of Lords against a certain Parliament Act commonly did so on the ground that since the Reform Bill of 1832, by inclusion of all that was eminent in politics, war and commerce, the Peerage had been so changed as to know itself no longer for the same thing. That is our practical way.

===Chinese===
诸位先生，我把这一点摆到你们面前：英格兰的光荣固然配得上歌唱，但卡尔杜齐的这个音调，我们无法体面地、诚实地奏响。伟大的生命曾放血似的流尽在特威德河（Tweed）与埃文河（Avon）里；伟大的魂灵曾被桨载着顺泰晤士河（Thames）而下，送进叛国者门（Traitor's Gate）与伦敦塔。康河（Cam）上成就的事迹，也曾写入历史。但我有一次沿着埃文河上溯到纳斯比（Naseby）战场脚下寻它的源头，发现它竟从一只灰泥天鹅的碎片中流出。没有哪位神明看守泰晤士河的源头；而唯一敢公然声称自己有着神圣血统的英国人（就我所知），是个经营「阿加佩门」（Agapemone，意即「爱之居」）的骗子。总而言之，我们是一个混血的种族，我们的文学是派生的。让我们把自豪限定在那几样光明正大属于我们自己的美德上吧——它们为数并不少。一位罗马贵族，即使在今天，也还有几分理由在自己的世系里算进一位神明，或者至少在乳母里算进一头母狼：而我们英国人，就连随诺曼人威廉（William the Norman）渡海而来的那些人，为他们开道的也不过是制革匠女儿的儿子。我记得很清楚：前些日子，为我们的世袭贵族院辩护、以对抗某项《议会法》（Parliament Act）的作者们，通常是这样立论的——自 1832 年《改革法案》以来，由于把政治、战争与商业中一切显赫的人物尽数收了进来，贵族爵位已被改变得认不出自己还是不是原来那个东西了。这便是我们讲求实际的路数。

===Original===
At all events, the men who made our literature had never a doubt, as they were careless to dissimulate, that they were conquering our tongue to bring it into the great European comity, the civilisation of Greece and Rome. An Elizabethan writer, for example, would begin almost as with a formula by begging to be forgiven that he has sought to render the divine accent of Plato, the sugared music of Ovid, into our uncouth and barbarous tongue. There may have been some mock-modesty in this, but it rested on a base of belief. Much of the glory of English Literature was achieved by men who, with the splendour of the Renaissance in their eyes, supposed themselves to be working all the while upon pale and borrowed shadows.

===Chinese===
无论如何，缔造我们文学的那些人从不曾怀疑——他们也不屑于掩饰——他们是在征服我们的语言，好把它带进欧洲那个伟大的共同体，带进希腊与罗马的文明。譬如，一位伊丽莎白时代的作家，开篇几乎像套公式一样，先乞求原谅：说他竟试图把柏拉图（Plato）的神妙笔调、奥维德（Ovid）的甜润乐音，移译进我们粗野不文的舌头里。这里面或许有几分佯装的谦卑，但底下垫着的却是信念。英国文学的荣光，有很大一部分是由这样的人成就的：他们眼中辉映着文艺复兴的壮采，却始终以为自己不过在摆弄一些苍白而借来的影子。

===Original===
Let us pass the enthusiasms of days when “bliss was it in that dawn to be alive” and come down to Alexander Pope and the Age of Reason. Pope at one time proposed to write a History of English Poetry, and the draft scheme of that History has been preserved. How does it begin? Why thus:﻿—

===Chinese===
让我们越过那些年代的狂热——那时节「活在那个黎明已是至福」——下到亚历山大·蒲柏（Alexander Pope）与理性时代（the Age of Reason）。蒲柏一度打算写一部《英国诗歌史》（History of English Poetry），而那部史的草拟纲目保存了下来。它是怎样开头的？喏，是这样：——

===Original===
—and so on. The scheme after Pope’s death came into the hands of Gray, who for a time was fired with the notion of writing the History in collaboration with his friend Mason. Knowing Gray’s congenital self-distrust, you will not be surprised that in the end he declined the task and handed it over to Warton. But, says Mant in his Life of Warton, “their design”﻿—that is, Gray’s design with Mason﻿—“was to introduce specimens of the Proveçal poetry, and of the Scaldic, British and Saxon, as preliminary to what first deserved to be called English poetry, about the time of Chaucer, from whence their history properly so called was to commence.” A letter of Gray’s on the whole subject, addressed to Warton, is extant, and you may read it in Dr. Courthope’s *History of English Poetry*.

===Chinese===
——如此等等。蒲柏身后，这份纲目落到了格雷（Gray）手里，他一度燃起念头，要同他的朋友梅森（Mason）合写这部史。知道格雷与生俱来的自疑，你们便不会惊讶：他终究推辞了这桩差事，把它移交给了沃顿（Warton）。但是，曼特（Mant）在他的《沃顿传》（Life of Warton）里说，「他们的计划」——即格雷同梅森的计划——「是以普罗旺斯诗歌以及斯卡尔德体、布立吞体和撒克逊体诗歌的样品作引子，引出大约乔叟（Chaucer）时代方才最早配称英国诗歌的东西，他们严格意义上的历史便应从那里开端。」格雷就这整个问题写给沃顿的一封信尚存于世，你们可以在库思罗普博士（Dr. Courthope）的《英国诗歌史》（*History of English Poetry*）里读到。

===Original===
Few in this room are old enough to remember the shock of awed surmise which fell upon young minds presented, in the late seventies or early eighties of the last century, with Freeman’s *Norman Conquest* or Green’s *Short History of the English People*; in which as through paring clouds of darkness, we beheld our ancestry, literary as well as political, radiantly legitimised; though not, to be sure, in the England that we knew﻿—but far away in Sleswick, happy Sleswick! “Its pleasant pastures, its black-timbered homesteads, its prim little townships looking down on inlets of purple water, were then but a wild waste of heather and sand, girt along the coast with sunless woodland, broken here and there with meadows which crept down to the marshes and to the sea.” But what of that? There﻿—surely there, in Sleswick﻿—had been discovered for us our august mother’s marriage lines; and if the most of that bright assurance came out of an old political skit, the *Germania* of Tacitus, who recked at the time? For along followed Mr. Stopford Brooke with an admirable little Primer published at one shilling, to instruct the meanest of us in our common father’s actual name﻿—Beowulf.

===Chinese===
本室之中，年纪大到记得那阵敬畏的惊疑之震的人寥寥无几：上世纪七十年代末或八十年代初，年轻的心灵一接触到弗里曼（Freeman）的《诺曼征服》（*Norman Conquest*）或格林（Green）的《英国人民简史》（*Short History of the English People*），那震动便降临了。在这两本书里，仿佛透过劈开的黑暗云翳，我们看见自己的世系——文学的兼政治的——焕然获得了合法的名分；固然不是在我们所认识的英格兰，而是在遥远的斯勒斯维克（Sleswick），快乐的斯勒斯维克！「它那怡人的草场、黑木梁架的农舍、俯瞰紫色水湾的齐整小镇，当时还只是一片石南与黄沙的荒野，沿岸裹着不见天日的林地，间或被一路爬向沼泽与海滩的草甸截断。」可那又何妨？在那里——肯定就是在那里，在斯勒斯维克——为我们查明了我们威严母亲的婚书；即便那份明亮的把握，大半是从一册老旧的政治讽刺小册子里掏出来的——塔西佗（Tacitus）的《日耳曼尼亚志》（*Germania*）——当时又有谁放在心上？因为紧跟着又来了斯托普福德·布鲁克先生（Stopford Brooke），带着一册定价一先令、令人叹服的小《入门读本》（Primer），来教我们中间最不济的人认识我们共同父亲的真名实姓——《贝奥武甫》（*Beowulf*）。

===Original===
> *Beowulf* is an old English Epic.﻿ ﻿… There is not one word about our England in the poem.﻿ ﻿… The whole poem, pagan as it is, is English to its very root. It is sacred to us; our Genesis, the book of our origins.

===Chinese===
> 《贝奥武甫》是一部古英语史诗。……诗中没有一字提到我们的英格兰。……整首诗尽管是异教的，却彻头彻尾是英语的。它于我们是神圣的；是我们的《创世记》，我们起源的书。

===Original===
Now I am not only incompetent to discuss with you the more recondite beauties of *Beowulf* but providentially forbidden the attempt by the conditions laid down for this Chair. I gather﻿—and my own perusal of the poem and of much writing about it confirms the belief﻿—that it has been largely overpraised by some critics, who have thus naturally provoked others to underrate it. Such things happen. I note, but without subscribing to it, the opinion of Vigfússon and York Powell, the learned editors of the *Corpus Poeticum Boreale*, that in the *Beowulf* we have “an epic completely metamorphosed in form, blown out with long-winded empty repetitions and comments by a book poet, so that one must be careful not to take it as a type of the old poetry,” and I seem to hear as from the grave the very voice of my old friend the younger editor in that unfaltering pronouncement. But on the whole I rather incline to accept the cautious surmise of Professor W. P. Ker that “a reasonable view of the merit of Beowulf is not impossible, though rash enthusiasm may have made too much of it; while a correct and sober taste may have too contemptuously refused to attend to Grendel and the Firedrake,” and to leave it at that. I speak very cautiously because the manner of the late Professor Freeman, in especial, had a knack of provoking in gentle breasts a resentment which the mind in its frailty too easily converted to a prejudice against his matter: while to men trained to admire Thucydides and Tacitus and acquainted with Lucian’s “Way to Write History” (*Πῶς δεῑ ἰστορἱαν συγγρἁφειν*) his loud insistence that the art was not an art but a science, and moreover recently invented by Bishop Stubbs, was a perpetual irritant.

===Chinese===
如今，要同你们讨论《贝奥武甫》更幽微的佳处，我不仅力有不逮，而且为本讲席设定的条件所限，天意也不容我作此尝试。我的印象是——我自己对这首诗以及对大量相关论著的研读也证实了这一信念——它被一些批评家大大地捧过了头，于是又自然惹得另一些人把它贬得过低。这种事在所难免。《北方诗歌集成》（*Corpus Poeticum Boreale*）的两位博学的编者维格菲松（Vigfússon）与约克·鲍威尔（York Powell）认为，在《贝奥武甫》里，我们得到的是「一部形式上被彻底改造过的史诗，被一位书本诗人用冗长空洞的复沓与评注吹胀起来，所以人们须小心，不要把它当作古代诗歌的典型」；我记下这个意见，但并不附和——在那条斩钉截铁的断语里，我仿佛听见我的老友、两位编者中年少的一位从坟墓里传来的声口。但总的说来，我更倾向于接受 W. P. 克尔教授（Professor W. P. Ker）那审慎的揣度：「对《贝奥武甫》的价值持一种公允的看法并非不可能，尽管轻率的热情可能把它捧得过高；而一种正确而清醒的趣味，也可能过于轻蔑地拒不分一顾给格伦德尔（Grendel）与火龙（the Firedrake）」——就让它到此为止。我说话如此审慎，是因为已故弗里曼教授的做派——尤其是他的做派——自有一种本领，能在温文的心胸里激起怨愤，而心智在其脆弱中又太容易把这怨愤化成对他笔下内容的成见；而对于受过推重修昔底德（Thucydides）与塔西佗的训练、又熟识琉善（Lucian）那篇《怎样撰写历史》（"Way to Write History"，*Πῶς δεῑ ἰστορἱαν συγγρἁφειν*）的人，他那一味高声坚持说这门技艺不是技艺而是科学、而且还是斯塔布斯主教（Bishop Stubbs）新近发明的，简直是一桩永久的刺激。

===Original===
But to return to *Beowulf*﻿—You have just heard the opinions of scholars whose names you must respect. I, who construe Anglo-Saxon with difficulty, must admit the poem to contain many fine, even noble, passages. Take for example Hrothgar’s lament for Æschere:﻿—

===Chinese===
但且回到《贝奥武甫》上来——你们方才听到了那些必须敬重的学者的意见。我这个解读盎格鲁-撒克逊文尚且吃力的人，也必须承认这首诗里有许多精彩的、甚至高贵的段落。且举赫罗斯加（Hrothgar）哀悼埃施雷尔（Æschere）的一段为例：——

===Original===
> Hróðgar maþelode, helm Scyldinga:
 

 “Ne frin þú æfter sǣlum; sorh is geniwod
 

 Denigea leódum; deád is Æschere,
 

 Yrmenláfes yldra bróþor,
 

 Mín rún-wita, ond min rǣd-bora;
 

 Eaxl-gestealla, ðonne we on orlege
 

 Hafelan wéredon, þonne hniton feþan,
 

 Eoferas cnysedan: swylc scolde eorl wesan
 

 *Æþeling* ǣr-gód, swylc Æschere wæs.”10

===Chinese===
> Hróðgar maþelode, helm Scyldinga:
 

 “Ne frin þú æfter sǣlum; sorh is geniwod
 

 Denigea leódum; deád is Æschere,
 

 Yrmenláfes yldra bróþor,
 

 Mín rún-wita, ond min rǣd-bora;
 

 Eaxl-gestealla, ðonne we on orlege
 

 Hafelan wéredon, þonne hniton feþan,
 

 Eoferas cnysedan: swylc scolde eorl wesan
 

 *Æþeling* ǣr-gód, swylc Æschere wæs.”10

===Original===
This is simple, manly, dignified. It avoids the besetting sin of the Anglo-Saxon gleeman﻿—the pretentious trick of calling things “out of their right names” for the sake of literary effect (as if e.g. the sea could be improved by being phrased into “the seals’ domain”). Its Anglo-Saxon staccato, so tiresome in sustained narrative, here happens to suit the broken utterance of mourning. In short, it exhibits the Anglo-Saxon Muse at her best, not at her customary. But set beside it a passage in which Homer tells of a fallen warrior﻿—at haphazard, as it were, a single corpse chosen from the press of battle﻿—

===Chinese===
这一段质朴、有丈夫气、庄重。它避开了盎格鲁-撒克逊吟游诗人的痼疾——为了文学效果而把事物「叫出其正名之外」的矜夸伎俩（仿佛譬如说，海一经措辞为「海豹的领域」便能得到什么长进似的）。它的盎格鲁-撒克逊式顿挫，在绵长的叙事里甚是累人，在这里却恰巧合于哀悼那断续的声口。简言之，它展示的是盎格鲁-撒克逊缪斯处于其最佳状态，而非其惯常状态。但在它旁边放上荷马（Homer）讲述一位阵亡战士的一段——信手拈来，仿佛从战阵的挤压里单挑出的一具尸身——

===Original===
> πολλἁ δἑ χερμἁδια μεγἁλ’ ἀσπιδας ἐστυφἑλιξαμ μαρναμἑνον ἀμφ’ αὐτον’ ὀ δ ἐν στροφἁλιγγι κονἱης κεῑτο μἑγας μεγαλοστἱ, λελασμἑνος ἰπποσυνἁων.

===Chinese===
> πολλἁ δἑ χερμἁδια μεγἁλ’ ἀσπιδας ἐστυφἑλιξαμ μαρναμἑνον ἀμφ’ αὐτον’ ὀ δ ἐν στροφἁλιγγι κονἱης κεῑτο μἑγας μεγαλοστἱ, λελασμἑνος ἰπποσυνἁων.

===Original===
Can you﻿—can anyone﻿—compare the two passages and miss to see that they belong to two different kingdoms of poetry? I lay no stress here on “architectonics.” I waive that the *Iliad* is a well-knit epic and the story of *Beowulf* a shapeless monstrosity. I ask you but to note the difference of note, of accent, of mere music. And I have quoted you but a passage of the habitual Homer. To assure yourselves that he can rise even from this habitual height to express the extreme of majesty and of human anguish in poetry which betrays no false note, no strain upon the store of emotion man may own with self-respect and exhibit without derogation of dignity, turn to the last book of the *Iliad* and read of Priam raising to his lips the hand that has murdered his son. I say confidently that no one unable to distinguish this, as poetry, from the very best of *Beowulf* is fit to engage upon business as a literary critic.

===Chinese===
你们——随便哪一位——能把这两段放在一起比较，却看不出它们属于诗的两个不同的王国吗？我在此处不着重「整体构筑」（architectonics）。我也暂且不提《伊利亚特》（*Iliad*）是一部结构严密的史诗，而《贝奥武甫》的故事是一团不成形状的庞然怪物。我只请你们留意音调之别、重音之别、纯然的音乐之别。何况我引给你们的，不过是荷马惯常水准的一段。要向你们自己证实：他还能从这个惯常的高度再往上升，用不露一丝败笔、不透支一个自尊之人所能拥有、且能不失尊严地展示的情感储量的诗，去表达庄严与人类剧痛的极点——请翻到《伊利亚特》的末卷，去读普里阿摩斯（Priam）把那只杀害了他儿子的手举到自己唇边的段落。我满有把握地说：凡不能把这一段当作诗、同《贝奥武甫》最精彩的段落区分开来的人，都还不够资格以文学批评为业。

===Original===
In *Beowulf* then, as an imported poem, let us allow much barbarian merit. It came of dubious ancestry, and it had no progeny. The pretence that our glorious literature derives its lineage from *Beowulf* is in vulgar phrase “a put up job”; a falsehood grafted upon our textbooks by Teutonic and Teutonising professors who can bring less evidence for it than will cover a threepenny-piece. Its run for something like that money, in small educational manuals, has been in its way a triumph of pedagogic *réclame*.

===Chinese===
那么，对于《贝奥武甫》这样一首移植进来的诗，让我们承认它有不少蛮族的优点。它家世可疑，又没有后嗣。硬说我们光荣的文学由《贝奥武甫》承其谱系，用句俗话说，是「一桩事先做好局子的买卖」；是条顿的与条顿化的教授们嫁接到我们教科书上的一个谎言——他们能为它提出的证据，还不够盖住一枚三便士硬币。它在小型教育手册里跑出来的那点行情——约莫也就值那三便士——在它的本行里，倒也算得上教学「扬名术」（*réclame*）的一场胜利。

===Original===
Our rude forefathers﻿—the author of “The Rape of the Lock” and of the “Elegy Written in a Country Churchyard”﻿—knew nothing of the Exeter and Vercelli Books, nothing of the Ruthwell Cross. But they were poets, practitioners of our literature in the true line of descent, and they knew certain things which all such artists know by instinct. So, before our historians of thirty-odd years ago started to make Chaucer and Beowulf one, these rude forefathers made them two. “Nor am I confident they erred.” Rather I am confident, and hope in succeeding lectures to convince you, that, venerable as Anglo-Saxon is, and worthy to be studied as the mother of our vernacular speech (as for a dozen other reasons which my friend Professor Chadwick will give you), its value is historical rather than literary, since from it our Literature is not descended. Let me repeat it in words that admit of no misunderstanding﻿—*From Anglo-Saxon Prose, from Anglo-Saxon Poetry our living Prose and Poetry have, save linguistically, no derivation*. I shall attempt to demonstrate that, whether or not Anglo-Saxon literature, such as it was, died of inherent weakness, die it did, and of its collapse the *Vision of Piers Plowman* may be regarded as the last dying spasm. I shall attempt to convince you that Chaucer did not inherit any secret from Caedmon or Cynewulf, but deserves his old title, “Father of English Poetry,” because through Dante, through Boccaccio, through the lays and songs of Provence, he explored back to the Mediterranean, and opened for Englishmen a commerce in the true intellectual mart of Europe. I shall attempt to heap proof on you that whatever the agency﻿—whether through Wyat or Spenser, Marlowe or Shakespeare, or Donne, or Milton, or Dryden, or Pope, or Johnson, or even Wordsworth﻿—always our literature has obeyed, however unconsciously, the precept *Antiquam exquirite matrem*, “Seek back to the ancient mother”; always it has recreated itself, has kept itself pure and strong, by harking back to bathe in those native﻿—yes, *native*﻿—Mediterranean springs.

===Chinese===
我们那些古朴的前辈——《夺发记》（"The Rape of the Lock"）与《墓园挽歌》（"Elegy Written in a Country Churchyard"）的两位作者——对《埃克塞特书》（Exeter Book）与《韦尔切利书》（Vercelli Book）一无所知，对鲁斯韦尔十字架（Ruthwell Cross）也一无所知。但他们是诗人，是我们文学真正传承线上的实践者，他们懂得某些一切此类艺术家凭本能便懂得的事情。所以，早在三十年前我们的历史学家动手把乔叟与《贝奥武甫》合而为一之前，这些古朴的前辈早已把他们分作两个。「我也不敢确言他们错了。」我毋宁确信——并指望在随后的几讲里说服你们——盎格鲁-撒克逊文尽管古老可敬，尽管值得当作我们本土语言的母亲来研究（至于另外十来条理由，我的朋友查德威克教授（Professor Chadwick）自会讲给你们听），其价值是史学上的而非文学上的，因为我们的文学并不自它而降。让我用不容误解的话把它再说一遍——*从盎格鲁-撒克逊的散文、从盎格鲁-撒克逊的诗，我们活着的散文与诗，除语言上的承袭外，别无渊源*。我将试图证明：那式微的盎格鲁-撒克逊文学，无论是不是死于内在的孱弱，总归是死了；而《农夫皮尔斯之幻象》（*Vision of Piers Plowman*）不妨看作它崩塌时最后一次垂死的痉挛。我将试图使你们信服：乔叟并不曾从卡德蒙（Caedmon）或塞内武甫（Cynewulf）手里继承到任何秘密；他配得上他那旧有的称号——「英国诗歌之父」（Father of English Poetry），因为他经由但丁（Dante），经由薄伽丘（Boccaccio），经由普罗旺斯（Provence）的歌谣与吟唱，一路探回地中海，为英国人在欧洲真正的智识集市上开出一门买卖。我将试图向你们堆叠证据：无论经由什么中介——经由怀亚特（Wyat）或斯宾塞（Spenser），马洛（Marlowe）或莎士比亚，或多恩（Donne），或弥尔顿，或德莱顿（Dryden），或蒲柏，或约翰逊（Johnson），甚至华兹华斯——我们的文学始终都在遵行，哪怕并不自觉，那条箴言：*Antiquam exquirite matrem*，「寻回那古老的母亲」；它始终都在再造自身，靠的是一再回溯，去那些本土的——是的，*本土的*——地中海泉源中沐浴，从而保住自身的纯正与强健。

---

===Original===
Do not presume me to be right in this. Rather, if you will, presume me to be wrong until the evidence is laid out for your judgment. But at least understand today how profoundly a man, holding that view, must deplore the whole course of academical literary study during these thirty years or so, and how distrust what he holds to be its basal fallacies.

===Chinese===
在这件事上，不要贸然认定我是对的。你们若愿意，不如先认定我是错的，等证据摆将出来、听凭你们裁断。但至少要在今天懂得：一个持这种见解的人，对这三十年左右学院派文学研究的整个进程，必然何等痛心；对那些他认作其根基性谬误的东西，又必然何等不能轻信。

===Original===
For, literature being written in language, yet being something quite distinct, and the development of our language having been fairly continuous, while the literature of our nation exhibits a false start﻿—a break, silence, repentance, then a renewal on right glorious lines﻿—our students of literature have been drilled to follow the specious continuance while ignoring the actual break, and so to commit the one most fatal error in any study; that of mistaking the inessential for the essential.

===Chinese===
因为，文学虽用语言写成，却全然是另一种东西；我们的语言，其发展相当连续，而我们民族的文学却呈现出一次错误的起步——中断、沉默、悔悟，然后沿着正当而光荣的路线重新出发——我们的文学学子却被操练得只知追随那貌似连续的表象而无视那实际存在的中断，于是犯下了一切研究中最致命的那一个错误：把非本质的认作了本质的。

===Original===
As I tried to persuade you in my Inaugural Lecture, our first duty to Literature is to study it absolutely, to understand, in Aristotelian phrase, its *τὀ τἱ ἧν εἷναι*; what it *is* and what it *means*. If that be our quest, and the height of it be realised, it is nothing to us﻿—or almost nothing﻿—to know of a certain alleged poet of the fifteenth century, that he helped us over a local or temporary disturbance in our vowel-endings. It is everything to have acquired and to possess such a norm of Poetry within us that we know whether or not what he wrote was **Poetry**.

===Chinese===
正如我在就职讲演中试图说服你们的那样，我们对文学的第一桩本分，是绝对地研究它，是用亚里士多德（Aristotle）式的说法，理解它的 *τὀ τἱ ἧν εἷναι*（它之所曾是，即本质）；理解它*是*什么、*意味着*什么。如果这就是我们的求索，而且它的高度也被认识到了，那么，得知某位所谓的十五世纪诗人曾在元音词尾的一处局部的、一时的骚动里帮过我们一把，对我们便算不得什么——或者几乎算不得什么。一切的一切，在于在我们心中获得并持有那样一种诗的准则，使我们足以知道：他写下的究竟是不是**诗**。

---

===Original===
Do not think this easy. The study of right literary criticism is much more difficult than the false path usually trodden; so difficult, indeed, that you may easily count the men who have attempted to grasp the great rules and apply them to writing as an art to be practised. But the names include some very great ones﻿—Aristotle, Horace, Quintilian, Corneille, Boileau, Dryden, Johnson, Lessing, Coleridge, Goethe, Sainte-Beuve, Arnold: and the study, though it may not find its pattern in our time, is not unworthy to be proposed for another attempt before a great University.

===Chinese===
不要以为这很容易。研究正当的文学批评，比通常被人踩熟了的那条歧路艰难得多；艰难到这个地步——试图把握那些伟大的法则、并把它们应用于「写作是一门须加实践的艺术」的人，你们扳着指头就数得过来。但这些名字里有几个极其伟大的——亚里士多德、贺拉斯（Horace）、昆体良（Quintilian）、高乃依（Corneille）、布瓦洛（Boileau）、德莱顿、约翰逊、莱辛（Lessing）、柯尔律治（Coleridge）、歌德（Goethe）、圣伯夫（Sainte-Beuve）、阿诺德（Arnold）：这门学问，虽则在我们这个时代未必找得到它的典范，却仍配得上一所伟大学府将它重新提起、再作一次尝试。

## Lecture IX On the Lineage of English Literature (II) / IX 英国文学的谱系（下）

===Original===
Wednesday, November 5

===Chinese===
11 月 5 日，星期三

===Original===
Some of you whose avocations call them, from time to time, to Newmarket may have noted, at a little distance out from Cambridge, a byroad advertised as leading to Quy and Swaffham. It also leads to the site of an old Roman villa; but you need not interrupt your business to visit this, since the best thing discovered there﻿—a piece of tessellated pavement﻿—has been removed and deposited in the Geological Museum here in Downing Street, where you may study it very conveniently. It is not at all a first-class specimen of its kind: not to be compared, for example, with the wonderful pavement at Dorchester, or with that (measuring thirty-five feet by twenty) of the great villa unearthed, a hundred years ago, at Stonesfield in Oxfordshire: but I take it as the handiest, and am going to build a small conjecture upon it, or rather a small suggestion of a guess. Remember there is no harm in guessing so long as we do not pretend our guesswork to be something else.

===Chinese===
诸位当中，有些人因各自的营生不时前往纽马基特（Newmarket），也许曾注意到，出剑桥不远，有一条小路，路牌上标着通往夸伊（Quy）与斯瓦弗姆（Swaffham）。这条路也通向一座古罗马别墅的遗址；但诸位不必为此中断正事前去探访，因为那里发现的最好一件东西——一段镶嵌铺道——已经起出，存放在本城唐宁街（Downing Street）的地质博物馆里，诸位尽可在那里从容研赏。它绝非同类中的一流标本：比方说，比不了多切斯特（Dorchester）那条美妙的铺道，也比不了一百年前在牛津郡（Oxfordshire）斯通斯菲尔德（Stonesfield）出土的那座大别墅里的那一条（长三十五英尺、宽二十英尺）：但我取它作最顺手的一个例证，打算在它上面构筑一个小小的推测，或者不如说，给一个猜测作一点小小的提示。请记住，猜测并无害处，只要我们不把猜测试冒充成别的东西。

===Original===
I will ask you to consider first that in these pavements, laid bare for us as “the whistling rustic tends his plough,” we have work dating somewhere between the first and fifth centuries, work of unchallengeable beauty, work of a beauty certainly not rivalled until we come to the Norman builders of five or six hundred years later. I want you to let your minds dwell on these long stretches of time﻿—four hundred years or so of Roman occupation (counting, not from Caesar’s raids, but from the serious invasion of 43 AD under Aulus Plautius, say to some while after the famous letter of Honorius, calling home the legions). You may safely put it at four hundred years, and then count six hundred as the space before the Normans arrive﻿—a thousand years altogether, or but a fraction﻿—one short generation﻿—less than the interval of time that separates us from King Alfred. In the great Cathedral of Winchester (where sleep, by the way, two gentle writers specially beloved, Isaak Walton and Jane Austen) above the choir-screen to the south, you may see a line of painted chests, of which the inscription on one tells you that it holds what was mortal of King Canute.

===Chinese===
我想先请诸位考虑：在这些铺道里——「吹口哨的庄稼人照料着他的犁」，便把它们为我们翻见天日——我们拥有断代大约介于公元一至五世纪之间的作品，其美无可置疑；而这份美，直到五六百年后诺曼营造者出现之前，确乎再无匹敌。我要请诸位让心思在这些漫长的岁月上多流连一会儿——约莫四百年的罗马占领（不是从恺撒（Caesar）的袭掠算起，而是从公元 43 年奥卢斯·普劳提乌斯（Aulus Plautius）麾下的大举入侵算起，下限姑且算到霍诺留（Honorius）那道召军团还国的著名信谕之后的某一时分）。诸位尽可放心算它四百年，然后再算六百年，作为诺曼人到来之前的间隔——合计一千年，或者只算一小段——短短一代人的工夫——比我们与阿尔弗雷德王（King Alfred）之间相隔的岁月还要短些。在温彻斯特（Winchester）大教堂里——顺便一说，那里安眠着两位特别受人爱戴的温厚作家，艾萨克·沃尔顿（Isaak Walton）与简·奥斯汀（Jane Austen）——唱诗班屏风上方的南侧，诸位可以看到一列彩绘的棺柜，其中一口上面的铭文告诉诸位，里面安放着克努特王（King Canute）的凡躯。

===Original===
> Here are sands, ignoble things,
 

 Dropp’d from the ruin’d sides of Kings.

===Chinese===
> 这里是沙土，微贱的东西，
 

 从诸王残破的身侧洒落。

===Original===
But if you walk around to the north of the altar you will find yourself treading on tiles not so very far short of twice that antiquity. Gentlemen, do not think that I would ever speak lightly of our lineage: only let us make as certain as we may what that lineage is.

===Chinese===
但诸位若绕行到祭坛北侧，便会发觉自己脚下踩着的铺砖，其年代离方才那份古老的整整两倍，相去也不很远。诸位先生，切莫以为我会出言轻慢我们的谱系：只是让我们尽己所能弄确实，这谱系究竟是什么。

===Original===
I want you today to understand just what such a pavement as that preserved for your inspection in Downing Street meant to the man who saw it laid and owned it these fifteen hundred years﻿—more or less﻿—ago. *Ubi Romanus vicit, ibi habitat*﻿—“where the Roman has conquered, there he settles”: but whether he conquered or settled he carried these small tiles, these *tessellae*, as religiously as ever Rachel stole her teraphin. “Wherever his feet went there went the tessellated pavement for them to stand on. Even generals on foreign service carried in panniers on mule-back the little coloured cubes or *tessellae* for laying down a pavement in each camping-place, to be taken up again when they moved forward. In England the same sweet emblems of the younger gods of poetic legend, of love, youth, plenty, and all their happy naturalism, are found constantly repeated.”11 I am quoting these sentences from a local historian, but you see how these relics have a knack of inspiring prose at once scholarly and imaginative, as (for a more famous instance) the urns disinterred at Walsingham once inspired Sir Thomas Browne’s. To continue and adapt the quotation﻿—

===Chinese===
今天我要请诸位理解的正是：这样一条铺道——如今保存在唐宁街供诸位检视的那条——对一千五百年前——或多或少吧——那个眼看它铺下、又拥有它的人，究竟意味着什么。*Ubi Romanus vicit, ibi habitat*——「罗马人征服到哪里，就在哪里住下」：但无论他是征服还是住下，他携带这些小方砖、这些 *tessellae*（镶嵌小方砖）时的虔诚，一如当年拉结（Rachel）偷取她的家神像。「他的双脚走到哪里，镶嵌铺道就跟到哪里，供它们踩踏。就连在海外领兵的将领，也用驮篮装在骡背上，驮着那些彩色小方块即 *tessellae*，好在每一处营地铺下一条铺道，开拔时再收起来。在英格兰，诗性传说中那些年轻的神祇——爱、青春、丰饶，连同他们全部欢快的自然主义——的同样甜美的意象，被发现一遍又一遍地反复出现。」11 我引这几句出自一位本地史家，但诸位看得出，这类遗迹自有本领激发出既富学问又富想象力的散文，正如（举一个更有名的例子）沃尔辛厄姆（Walsingham）出土的瓮罐曾激发托马斯·布朗爵士（Sir Thomas Browne）的散文一样。且容我延续并化用这段引文——

===Original===
> Bacchus with his wild rout, Orpheus playing to a spellbound audience, Apollo singing to the lyre, Venus in Mars’ embrace, Neptune with a host of seamen, scallops, and trumpets, Narcissus by the fountain, Jove and Ganymede, Leda and the swan, wood-nymphs and naiads, satyrs and fauns, masks, hautboys, cornucopiæ, flowers and baskets of golden fruit﻿—what touches of home they must have seemed to these old dwellers in the Cambridgeshire wilds!

===Chinese===
> 巴克斯带着他的狂欢队伍，俄耳甫斯对着听入了迷的听众弹奏，阿波罗和着里拉琴歌唱，维纳斯偎在马尔斯的臂抱里，海神携着一队水手、扇贝与号角，那喀索斯伫立泉边，朱庇特与伽倪墨得斯，勒达与天鹅，林中仙女与水泽仙子，萨提尔与法恩，面具，双簧管，丰饶之角，花卉与盛着金色果子的篮子——对这些剑桥郡荒野中的老居民，这一切该是多么亲切的家乡风物啊！

===Original===
Yes, touches of home! For the owner of this villa (you may conceive) is the grandson or even great-great-grandson of the colonist who first built it, following in the wake of the legionaries. The family has prospered and our man is now a considerable landowner. He was born in Britain: his children have been born here: and here he lives a comfortable, well-to-do, out-of-door life, in its essentials I daresay not so very unlike the life of an English country squire today. Instead of chasing foxes or hares he hunts the wolf and the wild boar; but the sport is good and he returns with an appetite. He has added a summer parlour to the house, with a northern aspect and no heating-flues: for the old parlour he has enlarged the præfurnium, and through the long winter evenings sits far better warmed than many a master of a modern country-house. A belt of trees on the brow of the rise protects him from the worst winds, and to the south his daughters have planted violet-beds which will breathe odorously in the spring. He has rebuilt and enlarged the slave-quarters and outhouses, replaced the stucco pillars around the atrium with a colonnade of polished stone, and, where stucco remains, has repainted it in fresh colours. He knows that there are no gaps or weak spots in his stockade fence﻿—wood is always cheap. In a word he has improved the estate; is modestly proud of it; and will be content, like the old Athenian, to leave his patrimony not worse but something better than he found it.

===Chinese===
是的，家乡的风物！因为（诸位可以想见）这座别墅的主人，是当初尾随军团而来的那殖民者的孙子，甚至曾孙。家道殷盛，我们这位主人如今已是颇有分量的地主。他生在不列颠：他的儿女也生在这里：他在这里过着舒适、富足、户外的日子，就其大要而言，我敢说与今日英国乡绅的生活相去不远。他不追逐狐狸野兔，而是猎狼猎野猪；但游猎照样尽兴，归来时胃口大开。他给宅子添了一间避暑的客堂，朝北，不设火道：旧客堂那边，他把 *præfurnium*（火室）扩大了，漫长的冬夜坐在其中，比许多现代乡村宅邸的主人暖和得多。坡脊上一带树木替他挡住最烈的风，南侧他的女儿们栽下了紫罗兰花圃，春天一到，便幽香四溢。他重建并扩建了奴隶居所和杂屋，把中庭四周的灰泥柱换成了一圈磨光的石柱廊，灰泥尚存之处，也重新漆上鲜亮的颜色。他知道他的木栅围墙没有一处豁口或薄弱之处——木料总是便宜的。总而言之，他把这份产业经营得更好了；他为此怀着适度的自豪；并且愿意像那位古雅典人一样，把祖产不逊于、且略胜于受之于前人地传诸后人。

===Original===
Sensible men﻿—and the Romans were eminently that﻿—as a rule contrive to live decently, or, at least, tolerably. What struck Arthur Young more than anything else in his travels through France on the very eve of the Revolution seems to have been the general good-tempered happiness of the French gentry on their estates. We may moralise of the Roman colonists as of the French proprietors that “unconscious of their doom the little victims played”; but we have no right to throw back on them the shadow of what was to come or to cloud the picture of a useful, peaceable, maybe more than moderately happy life, with our later knowledge of disaster mercifully hidden from it.

===Chinese===
明理的人——而罗马人恰恰极其明理——照例总能把日子过得体面，或者至少过得去。在大革命前夜游历法国的阿瑟·杨（Arthur Young），最让他触目的，似乎莫过于法国绅士们在自家庄园上普遍的那种温良快乐。对于罗马殖民者，我们也像对那些法国业主一样，尽可以发一番「小小的受难者浑然不觉厄运将至，自顾嬉戏」的感慨；但我们无权把后来之事的阴影投在他们身上，也无权凭着事后方知、而他们幸而不知的灾祸，去败坏那幅有用的、安宁的、也许还相当快乐的生活的图景。

===Original===
Although our colonist and his family have all been born in Britain, are happy enough here on the whole, and talk without more than half meaning it, and to amuse themselves with speculations half-wistful, of daring the tremendous journey and setting eyes on Rome some day, their pride is to belong to her, to Rome, the imperial City, the city afar: their windows open back towards her as Daniel’s did towards Jerusalem﻿—*Urbs quam dicunt Roman﻿—the* City. Along the great road, hard by, her imperial writ runs. They have never subscribed to the vow of Ruth, “Thy people shall be my people and thy God my God.” They dwell under the Pax Romana, not merely protected by it but as *citizens*. Theirs are the ancestral deities portrayed on that unfading pavement in the very centre of the villa﻿—Apollo and Daphne, Bacchus and Ariadne﻿—

===Chinese===
我们的殖民者和他的一家，虽然都生在不列颠，在这里总的来说也够快活，偶尔谈起敢不敢踏上那骇人的旅程、有朝一日亲眼看看罗马，也不过是半真半假、聊以自遣、半带怅惘的悬想，他们的骄傲却在于隶属于她——罗马，帝都，远方的城：他们的窗子朝她敞开，一如当年但以理（Daniel）的窗子朝向耶路撒冷——*Urbs quam dicunt Roman*——那座城。沿着近旁的大道，她的帝国敕令畅行无阻。他们从未许过路得（Ruth）的誓愿：「你的民就是我的民，你的神就是我的神。」他们生活在罗马和平（Pax Romana）之下，不仅受它保护，而且以*公民*（citizens）的身份。别墅正中那条永不褪色的铺道上绘着的祖先神祇，正是属于他们的——阿波罗与达芙妮，巴克斯与阿里阿德涅——

===Original===
> Forever warm and still to be enjoyed,
 

 Forever panting, and forever young.

===Chinese===
> 永远温热，仍可供享用，
 

 永远喘息，永远年轻。

===Original===
Parcels come to them, forwarded from the near military station; come by those trade-routes, mysterious to us, concerning which a most illuminating book waits to be written by somebody. There are parcels of seeds﻿—useful vegetables and potherbs, helichryse (marigold as we call them now) for the flower garden, for the colonnade even roses with real Italian earth damp about their roots. There are parcels of books, too﻿—rolls rather, or tablets﻿—wherein the family reads about Rome; of its wealth, the uproar of its traffic, the innumerable chimneys smoking, *fumum et opes strepitumque*. For they are always reading of Rome; feeling themselves, as they read, to belong to it, to be neither savage nor even rustic, but by birthright *of the city*, urbane; and what these exiles read is of how Horace met a bore on the Sacred Road (which would correspond, more or less, with our Piccadilly)﻿—

===Chinese===
包裹寄到他们手上，由近旁的军事驿站转递；走的是那些我们看来神秘莫测的商路——关于这些商路，还有一部极具启发性的书，正等着什么人来写。有种子包裹——有用的菜蔬与香草，供花圃栽种的蜡菊（helichryse，也就是我们如今叫作金盏花的），甚至还有供柱廊栽种的玫瑰，根须上还带着湿润的真意大利泥土。也有书卷包裹——不如说是卷轴，或者书板——一家人从中读到罗马：读到它的财富，它街市的喧嚣，它无数冒烟的烟囱，*fumum et opes strepitumque*（烟尘、财富与喧嚣）。因为他们无时不在读罗马；一边读，一边觉得自己属于它，既非蛮人，甚至也非村夫，而是生来*属于那座城*的、有城市气派的人；而这些流寓者读到的是：贺拉斯（Horace）如何在神圣大道上遇上一个缠人的家伙（那条大道大致相当于我们的皮卡迪利〔Piccadilly〕）——

===Original===
> Along the Sacred Road I strolled one day
 

 Deep in some bagatelle (you know my way)
 

 When up comes one whose face I scarcely knew﻿—
 

 “The dearest of dear fellows! how d’ye do?”
 

 —He grasped my hand. “Well, thanks! The same to you?”

===Chinese===
> 一日我沿着神圣大道信步，
 

 心里正盘算一桩琐事（你们知道我的路数）
 

 迎面来了个面孔依稀相识的家伙——
 

 「顶顶亲爱的朋友！你好呀？」
 

 ——他一把攥住我的手。「托福，谢谢！也问你好？」

===Original===
—or of how Horace apologises for protracting a summer jaunt to his country seat:﻿—

===Chinese===
——或者读到贺拉斯如何为把夏日的乡间小住拖得过长而致歉：——

===Original===
> Five days I told you at my farm I’d stay,
 

 And lo! the whole of August I’m away.
 

 Well but, Maecenas, you would have me live,
 

 And, were I sick, my absence you’d forgive.
 

 So let me crave indulgence for the fear
 

 Of falling ill at this bad time of year.
 

 When, thanks to early figs and sultry heat,
 

 The undertaker figures with his suite;
 

 When fathers all and fond mammas grow pale
 

 At what may happen to their young heirs male,
 

 And courts and levees, town-bred mortals’ ills,
 

 Bring fevers on, and break the seals of wills.12

===Chinese===
> 我本说好田庄上只住五天，
 

 看哪！整个八月我都没回还。
 

 可是梅塞纳斯，你盼我康健，
 

 我若染恙，缺席自蒙你宽免。
 

 那么请容我讨个恩典，恕我担心
 

 在这一年里最坏的时节染病。
 

 这时节，多亏早熟的无花果与溽暑，
 

 殡葬师带着他的一班扈从出场；
 

 这时节，父亲们和慈母们个个面无人色，
 

 生怕他们年轻的男性继承人出了差错，
 

 这时节，诉讼与朝会——城里人的病痛——
 

 招致热病缠身，戳破遗嘱的封缄。12

===Original===
Consider those lines; then consider how long it took the inhabitants of this island﻿—the cultured ones who count as readers or writers﻿—to recapture just that note of urbanity. Other things our forefathers﻿—Britons, Saxons, Normans, Dutch or French refugees﻿—discovered by the way; worthier things if you will; but not until the eighteenth century do you find just that note recaptured; the note of easy confidence that our London had become what Rome had been, the Capital city. You begin to meet it in Dryden; with Addison it is fairly established. Pass a few years, and with Samuel Johnson it is taken for granted. His *London* is Juvenal’s Rome, and the same satire applies to one as applied to the other. But against the urbane lines written by one Horace some while before Juvenal let us set a passage from another Horace﻿—Horace Walpole, seventeen hundred years later and some little while ahead of Johnson. He, like our Roman colonist, is a settler in a new country, Twickenham; and like Flaccus he loves to escape from town life.

===Chinese===
细想这些诗行；再想想这座岛的居民——那些算得上读者或作者的有教养者——用了多久，才重新捕捉到恰恰这一种雍容的城市笔调。别的东西，我们的祖先——布立吞人、撒克逊人、诺曼人、荷兰或法国的流亡者——沿途也有所发现；倘若诸位愿意，还可以说是更可贵的东西；但直到十八世纪，诸位才发现恰恰这种笔调被重新捕捉到；那是一种从容自信的调子：我们的伦敦已经成为当年的罗马，成为都城。这调子在德莱顿（Dryden）笔下初露头角；到艾迪生（Addison）便已牢固确立。再过若干年，到塞缪尔·约翰逊（Samuel Johnson）那里，它已被视为当然。他的《伦敦》（*London*）就是尤维纳利斯（Juvenal）的罗马，同一首讽刺诗，既适用于此，也适用于彼。但是，拿尤维纳利斯之前一位贺拉斯写下的雍容诗行作一方，让我们再摆上另一位贺拉斯的一段文字作另一方——霍勒斯·沃波尔（Horace Walpole），晚了一千七百年，又比约翰逊略早些许。他同我们的罗马殖民者一样，是一片新国土上的移居者，特威克纳姆（Twickenham）；又同弗拉库斯（Flaccus）一样，乐于逃出城市生活。

===Original===
> To the Hon. H. S. Conway.
> You perceive by my date that I am got into a new camp, and have left my tub at Windsor. It is a little plaything-house that I got out of Mrs. Chevenix’s shop, and the prettiest bauble you ever saw. It is set in enamelled meadows with filagree hedges:
> Two delightful roads; that you would call dusty, supply me continually with coaches and chaises: barges as solemn as Barons of the Exchequer move under my window; Richmond Hill and Ham Walks bound my prospect; but, thank God! the Thames is between me and the Duchess of Queensberry. Dowagers as plenty as flounders inhabit all around, and Pope’s ghost is just now skimming under my window by the most poetical moonlight.﻿ ﻿… The Chevenixes had tricked it out for themselves; up two pairs of stairs is what they call Mr. Chevenix’s library, furnished with three maps, one shelf, a bust of Sir Isaac Newton and a lame telescope without any glasses. Lord John Sackville *predeceased* me here and instituted certain games called *cricketalia*, which has been celebrated this very evening in honour of him in a neighbouring meadow.
> You will think I have removed my philosophy from Windsor with my tea-things hither; for I am writing to you in all tranquility while a Parliament is bursting about my ears. You know it is going to be dissolved.﻿ ﻿… They say the Prince has taken up two hundred thousand pounds, to carry elections which he won’t carry﻿—he had much better have saved it to buy the Parliament after it is chosen.

===Chinese===
> 致尊敬的康韦（H. S. Conway）先生。
> 你从我信首的日期便可看出，我已迁入一座新营帐，把温莎的那只木桶丢下了。这是一座小小的玩物房子，是我从舍维尼克斯太太（Mrs. Chevenix）的铺子里买来的，是你所见过的最漂亮的珍玩。它坐落在珐琅般的草地上，围着金银丝细工的树篱：
> 两条可爱的大路——你会说它们尘土飞扬——源源不断地给我送来马车与双轮轻便车；庄重得如同财政法庭男爵（Barons of the Exchequer）一般的驳船，从我窗下缓缓驶过；里士满山（Richmond Hill）与哈姆步道（Ham Walks）框定我的眼界；但是，感谢上帝！泰晤士河隔在我与昆斯伯里公爵夫人（Duchess of Queensberry）之间。四邻住满了寡居的夫人，多得如同比目鱼；而蒲柏（Pope）的幽灵此刻正趁着最富诗意的月光，从我窗下掠过。……舍维尼克斯夫妇从前把它装点供自己受用；两截楼梯之上，是他们所谓的舍维尼克斯先生的书斋，陈设计有三幅地图、一层书架、一尊艾萨克·牛顿爵士（Sir Isaac Newton）的半身像，外加一架不带镜片的跛脚望远镜。约翰·萨克维尔勋爵（Lord John Sackville）在此地*先行谢世*，并创设了几种名为 *cricketalia*（板球赛会）的游戏；就在今天傍晚，邻近的一片草地上还刚刚举行过纪念他的盛会。
> 你会以为我把我的哲学连同我的茶具一道从温莎搬到了此地；因为此刻一届议会正在我耳边炸响，我却好整以暇地给你写信。你知道它就要解散了。……他们说亲王拿出了二十万英镑，去买那些他买不赢的选举——那笔钱还不如省下来，等议会选出之后再去把议会买下，倒还划算。

===Original===
There you have Horatio Walpole, the man-about-town, almost precisely echoing Horatius Flaccus, the man-about-town; and this (if you will bring your minds to it) is just the sort of passage a Roman colonist in Britain would open upon, out of his parcel of new books, and read, *and understand*, some eighteen hundred years ago.
---

===Chinese===
诸位在此看到的，是城中名士霍雷肖·沃波尔（Horatio Walpole），几乎分毫不差地应和着城中名士贺拉提乌斯·弗拉库斯（Horatius Flaccus）；而这（倘若诸位肯凝神一想）恰恰是这样一段文字：一千八百年前，一位罗马不列颠的殖民者，正会从他的新书包裹里抽出它来，读它，*并且读懂它*。
---

===Original===
What became of it all?﻿—of that easy colonial life, of the men and women who trod those tessellated pavements? “Wiped out,” say the historians, knowing nothing, merely guessing: for you may with small trouble assure yourselves that the fifth and sixth centuries in the story of this island are a blind spot, concerning which one man’s guess may be as good as another’s. “Wiped out,” they will commonly agree; for while, as I warned you in another lecture, the pedantic mind, faced with a difficulty, tends to remove it conveniently into a category to which it does not belong, still more prone is the pedantic mind to remove it out of existence altogether. So “wiped out” is the theory; and upon it a sympathetic imagination can invent what sorrowful pictures it will of departing legions, the last little cloud of dust down the highway, the lovers by the gate watching it, not comprehending; the peaceful homestead in the background, ripe for doom﻿—and whatnot.

===Chinese===
这一切后来都怎样了？——那种安逸的殖民生活，那些踩过镶嵌铺道的男男女女？「一扫而光了」，历史家们说，实则一无所知，只不过在猜：因为诸位只需稍费力气便可确信：这座岛的故事里，第五、第六两个世纪是一块盲区，关于它，此人的猜测未必就劣于彼人的。「一扫而光了」，他们通常会异口同声；因为——正如我在另一讲里提醒过诸位的——迂腐的心智面对一个难题，固然倾向于把它顺手挪进一个它本不属于的范畴，迂腐的心智更倾向于干脆把它挪出存在之外。于是「一扫而光」便成了定论；在这定论之上，一副富于同情的想象力尽可以臆造出它想臆造的种种凄恻图景：开拔的军团，大路上最后一小团尘雾，门边的恋人望着它出神，茫然不解；背景里那安宁的农舍，熟透得正待劫数——诸如此类。

===Original===
Or, stay! There is another theory to which the late Professor Freeman inclined (if so sturdy a figure could be said to incline), laying stress on a passage in Gildas, that the Romans in Britain, faced by the Saxon invader, got together their money, and bolted away into Gaul. “The Romans that were in Britain gathered together their gold-hoard, hid part in the ground and carried the rest over to Gaul,” writes Gildas. “The hiding in the ground,” says Freeman, “is of course a guess to explain the frequent finding of Roman coins”﻿—which indeed it *does* explain better than the guess that they were carried away, and perhaps better than the schoolboy’s suggestion that during their occupation of Britain the Romans spent most of their time in dropping money about. Likely enough, large numbers of the colonists did gather up what they could and flee before the approaching storm; but by no means all, I think. For (since, where all is uncertain, we must reason from what is probable of human nature) in the first place men with large estates do not behave in that way before a danger which creeps upon them little by little, as this Saxon danger did. These colonists could not dig up their fields and carry them over to Gaul. They did not keep banking accounts; and in the course of four hundred years their main wealth had certainly been sunk in the land. They could not carry away their villas. We know that many of them did not carry away the *tessellae* for which (as we have seen) they had so peculiar a veneration; for these remain. Secondly, if the colonists left Britain in a mass, when in the middle of the sixth century we find Belisarius offering the Goths to trade Britain for Sicily, as being “much larger and this long time subservient to Roman rule,”13 we must suppose either (as Freeman appears to suppose) that Belisarius did not know what he was offering, or that he was attempting a gigantic “bluff,” or lastly that he really was offering an exchange not flatly derisory; of which three possible suppositions I prefer the last as the likeliest. Nor am I the less inclined to choose it, because these very English historians go on to clear the ground in a like convenient way of the Celtic inhabitants, exterminating them as they exterminated the Romans, with a wave of the hand, quite in the fashion of Mr. Podsnap. “This is un-English: therefore for me it merely ceases to exist.”

===Chinese===
且慢，还有另一种理论！已故的弗里曼教授（Professor Freeman）倾向于它（倘若如此壮实的一条汉子也说得上「倾向」的话）；它倚重吉尔达斯（Gildas）的一段话：面对撒克逊入侵者，在不列颠的罗马人敛起钱财，逃之夭夭，奔往高卢。「在不列颠的罗马人聚拢了他们的金窖，一部分埋入土中，其余的运过高卢去了。」吉尔达斯写道。「埋入土中，」弗里曼说，「自然是为了解释罗马钱币屡有发现而作的一个猜测」——这个猜测的确比「钱币被运走了」更能自圆其说，也许还比那个学童式的提议更能自圆其说：占领不列颠期间，罗马人把大部分光阴都用来往各处撒钱。很有可能，大批殖民者确曾收拾起能带走的，赶在逼近的风暴之前逃走了；但我认为绝非全体。因为（既然一切都不确定，我们就必须依据人性之常来推理）首先，坐拥大宗田产的人，面对一种像这场撒克逊危险这样一点点悄然进逼的危险，是不会如此行事的。这些殖民者总不能把田地掘起来运往高卢。他们不设银行账户；而四百年下来，他们的主要财富必定已沉投在土地里。他们搬不走他们的别墅。我们知道，许多人并未搬走那些（如我们所见）他们怀有如此特殊虔敬的 *tessellae*；因为这些还留在原地。其次，倘若殖民者成群结队地离开了不列颠，那么到了六世纪中叶，当我们看到贝利撒留（Belisarius）向哥特人提议以不列颠换取西西里，理由是前者「大得多，而且久已臣服于罗马的统治」13 时，我们就只好假定：要么（如弗里曼似乎假定的那样）贝利撒留不知道自己拿出来作交换的是什么，要么他在耍一场天大的「唬人」把戏，要么最后，他提出的这桩交换实在并非全然可笑；这三条可能的假定之中，我取最后一条为最近情理。而我之所以并不因此减少选择它的倾向，还因为恰恰是这些英国历史家，接下去又以同样便利的方式把凯尔特居民清扫出场，像灭绝罗马人一样把他们灭绝掉，手一挥便算完事，十足是波德斯纳普先生（Mr. Podsnap）的做派：「这不是英国的东西：因此对我而言，它干脆就不复存在。」

===Original===
“Probable extirpation of the Celtic inhabitants” jots down Freeman in his margin, and proceeds to write:

===Chinese===
「凯尔特居民或已遭根绝」——弗里曼在页边如此信笔记下，接着便写道：

===Original===
> In short, though the literal extirpation of a nation is an impossibility, there is every reason to believe that the Celtic inhabitants of those parts of Britain which had become English at the end of the sixth century had been as nearly extinguished as a nation could be. The women doubtless would be largely spared, but as far as the male sex is concerned we may feel sure that death, emigration, or personal slavery were the only alternatives which the vanquished found at the hands of our fathers.

===Chinese===
> 简而言之，尽管把一个民族从字面上斩尽杀绝是不可能的事，却有充分理由相信，在不列颠那些于六世纪末已成为英格兰的地方，凯尔特居民已被消灭得达到了一个民族所能被消灭的极限。妇女想必大半会得到宽宥，但就男性而言，我们可以确信，死亡、流徙或人身奴役，便是被征服者从我们先人手中所能得到的仅有的几种下场。

===Original===
Upon this passage, if brought to me in an undergraduate essay, I should have much to say. The style, with its abstract nouns (“the literal extirpation of a nation is an impossibility”), its padding and periphrasis (“there is every reason to believe”﻿ ﻿… “as far as the male sex is concerned we may feel sure”) betrays the loose thought. It begins with “in short” and proceeds to be long-winded. It commits what even schoolboys know to be a solecism by inviting us to consider three “alternatives”; and what can I say of “the women doubtless would be largely spared,” save that besides scanning in iambics it says what Freeman never meant and what no-one outside of an Aristophanic comedy could ever suggest? “The women doubtless would be largely spared”! It reminds me of the young lady in Cornwall who, asked by her vicar if she had been confirmed, admitted blushingly that “she had reason to believe, partially so.”

===Chinese===
这样一段文字，倘若当作一篇本科生论文交到我手上，我有很多话可说。这文体，连同它的抽象名词（「把一个民族从字面上斩尽杀绝是不可能的事」）、它的填絮与迂说（「有充分理由相信」……「就男性而言我们可以确信」），泄露了思想的松散。它以「简而言之」开头，行文却冗长拖沓。它竟请我们考虑三个「二者择一」，犯了连学童都知道的语病；至于「妇女想必大半会得到宽宥」，我还能说什么呢——它除了能排成抑扬格的诗行之外，说的是弗里曼从未想说的意思，也是出了阿里斯托芬（Aristophanes）式喜剧便无人能提出的意思。「妇女想必大半会得到宽宥」！这叫我想起康沃尔（Cornwall）的那位年轻小姐：牧师问她行过坚振礼没有，她红着脸承认，「她有理由相信，算是行过一部分」。

===Original===
“The women doubtless would be largely spared”!﻿—But I thank the professor for teaching me that phrase, because it tries to convey just what I am driving at. The Jutes, Angles, Saxons, did not extirpate the Britons, whatever you may hold concerning the Romans. For, once again, men do not behave in that way, and certainly will not when a live slave is worth money. Secondly, the very horror with which men spoke, centuries after, of Anderida quite plainly indicates that such a wholesale massacre was exceptional, monstrous. If not exceptional, monstrous, why should this particular slaughter have lingered so ineffaceably in their memories? Finally﻿—and to be as curt as the question deserves﻿—the Celtic Briton in the island was not exterminated and never came near to being exterminated: but on the contrary, remains equipollent with the Saxon in our blood, and perhaps equipollent with that mysterious race we call Iberian, which came before either and endures in this island today, as anyone travelling it with eyes in his head can see. Pict, Dane, Norman, Frisian, Huguenot French﻿—these and others come in. If mixture of blood be a shame, we have purchased at the price of that shame the glory of catholicism; and I know of nothing more false in science or more actively poisonous in politics or in the arts than the assumption that we belong as a race to the Teutonic family.

===Chinese===
「妇女想必大半会得到宽宥」！——但我感谢这位教授教给我这个说法，因为它极力要传达的，恰恰是我要说的那个意思。朱特人、盎格鲁人、撒克逊人并没有根绝布立吞人——无论诸位对罗马人的下场作何看法。因为，再说一遍，人不会那样行事，当一名活的奴隶值着钱的时候，就更不会了。其次，时过数百年，人们提起安德里达（Anderida）犹自惊惶，这份恐惧本身便再明白不过地表明：那样一场不分皂白的大屠杀是反常的、骇人听闻的。倘若并不反常、并不骇人听闻，为什么偏偏这一场屠戮在他们记忆中留下了如此磨灭不掉的印痕？最后——就照这个问题应得的简洁作答——岛上的凯尔特布立吞人没有被灭绝，也从未接近于被灭绝：恰恰相反，他在我们的血统里与撒克逊人旗鼓相当，也许还与那支我们称之为伊比利亚人（Iberian）的神秘种族旗鼓相当——那支种族来得比这两者都早，至今仍存留在这座岛上，只要一个人头上有眼睛、肯在岛上走走，便看得到。皮克特人、丹麦人、诺曼人、弗里西亚人、法国的胡格诺派——这些以及其他族类都汇了进来。倘若血统的混杂是一桩耻辱，那么我们是以那耻辱为代价，购得了兼容并包（catholicism）的光荣；而据我所知，无论在科学里，还是在政治或艺术里，没有什么比「我们作为一个种族属于条顿家族」这一假定更虚假、为祸更烈的了。

===Original===
Dane, Norman, Frisian, French Huguenot﻿—they all come in. And will you refuse a hearing when I claim that the Roman came in too? Bethink you how deeply Rome engraved itself on this island and its features. Bethink you that, as human nature is, no conquering race ever lived or could live﻿—even in garrison﻿—among a tributary one without begetting children on it. Bethink you yet further of Freeman’s admission that in the wholesale (and quite hypothetical) general massacre “the women doubtless would be largely spared”; and you advance nearer to my point. I see a people which for four hundred years was permeated by Rome. If you insist on its being a Teutonic people (which I flatly deny) then you have one which *alone of Teutonic peoples* has inherited the Roman gift of consolidating conquest, of colonising in the wake of its armies; of driving the road, bridging the ford, bringing the lawless under its sense of law. I see that this nation of ours concurrently, when it seeks back to what alone can inspire and glorify these activities, seeks back, not to any supposed native North, but south to the Middle Sea of our civilisation and steadily to Italy, which we understand far more easily than France﻿—though France has helped us times and again. Putting these things together, I retort upon the ethnologists﻿—for I come from the West of England, where we suffer incredible things from them﻿—“*Semper ego auditor tantum?*” I hazard that the most important thing in our blood is that purple drop of the imperial murex we derive from Rome.

===Chinese===
丹麦人、诺曼人、弗里西亚人、法国的胡格诺派——他们都汇了进来。那么，当我声称罗马人也汇了进来时，诸位还肯不肯听呢？请想想，罗马在这座岛及其形貌上镌刻得何等之深。请想想，以人性之常，从来不曾有一个征服种族，能够在向它纳贡的种族之中生活、或得以生活——哪怕是屯戍——而不与之生养子女的。请再想想弗里曼自己承认：在那场大规模的（而且全然出于假定的）总屠杀里，「妇女想必大半会得到宽宥」；这样诸位便离我的论点更近了。我看到的是一个四百年间被罗马浸透的民族。倘若诸位硬要说这是一个条顿民族（这一点我断然否认），那么诸位得到的是这样一个民族：*在条顿诸民族中独一无二地*承袭了罗马的天赋——把征服巩固下来，尾随大军之后殖民垦拓；开路，筑桥渡津，使不法之徒归依于它的法律意识。我又看到，我们这个民族，每当它回溯那唯一能给这些作为以灵感与荣光的东西时，回溯的并不是什么假想中的本土北方，而是向南，回到我们文明的地中海，坚定地回到意大利——我们对意大利的理解远比对法国容易——虽然法国也屡屡帮我们的忙。把这些放到一起，我便回敬那些人种学家——因为我来自英格兰西部，在那边我们受够了他们种种难以置信的摆布——「*Semper ego auditor tantum?*」（难道我永远只做听众么？）我冒昧断言：我们血液中最重要的一样东西，是我们从罗马承袭的那一滴帝紫骨螺的紫液。

===Original===
You must, of course, take this for nothing more than it pretends to be﻿—a conjecture, a suggestion. I will follow it up with two statements of fact, neither doubtful nor disputable.

===Chinese===
当然，诸位只应把它当作它所自称的东西——一个推测，一个提示。下面我用两条事实陈述来承续它，两条都无可怀疑、无可争辩。

===Original===
The first is, that when English poetry awoke, long after the Conquest (or, as I should prefer to put it, after the Crusades) it awoke a new thing; in its vocabulary as much like Anglo-Saxon poetry as ever you will, but in metre, rhythm, lilt﻿—and more, in style, feeling, imaginative play﻿—and yet more again, in knowledge of what it aimed to be, in the essentials, in the qualities that make Poetry Poetry﻿—as different from Anglo-Saxon poetry as cheese is from chalk, and as much more nutritious. Listen to this﻿—

===Chinese===
第一条是：当英语诗歌在征服之后很久（或者，如我更愿意说的那样，在十字军东征之后）醒来时，它醒来已是一种新东西；论词汇，它同盎格鲁-撒克逊诗歌要多像有多像；但论格律、节奏、轻盈的曲调——更进一步，论风格、情感、想象的嬉戏——再进一步，论它对自身鹄的的认识，论那些根本的东西，论使诗之所以为诗的品质——它之不同于盎格鲁-撒克逊诗歌，一如奶酪之不同于粉笔，而且滋养得多。请听这个——

===Original===
> Bytuene Mershe ant Averil
 

 When spray biginnith to spring,
 

 The lutel foul hath hire wyl
 

 On hire lud to synge:
 

 Ich libbe in love-longinge
 

 For semlokest of alle thynge,
 

 He may me blisse bringe,
 

 Icham in hire bandoun.
 

 An hendy hap ichabbe y-hent,
 

 Ichot from hevene it is me sent,
 

 From alle wymmen my love is lent,
 

 And lyht on Alisoun.

===Chinese===
> 三月与四月之间，
 

 当枝条开始抽芽，
 

 小小的鸟儿便起了兴致
 

 在她的枝头歌唱：
 

 我活在爱的渴念里，
 

 为着万物中最明媚的一位，
 

 她能给我带来欢喜，
 

 我已在她的辖制之中。
 

 一桩美事我已到手中，
 

 我知道这是上天所赐，
 

 我的爱脱离了众女，
 

 落在了阿莉森（Alisoun）身上。

===Original===
Here you have alliteration in plenty; you even have what some hold to be the pattern of Anglo-Saxon alliterative verse (though in practice disregarded, may be, as often as not), the chosen initial used twice in the first line and once at least in the second:

===Chinese===
这里头韵多的是；诸位甚至还能看到某些人认作盎格鲁-撒克逊头韵诗范型的东西（虽说在实践中或许有一半的次数是被撇在一边的）：选定的起首字母在第一行里用两次，在第二行里至少用一次：

===Original===
> From alle wymmen my *l*ove is *l*ent,
 

 And *l*yht on A*l*isoun.

===Chinese===
> 我的爱脱离了众女，
 

 落在了阿莉森身上。

===Original===
But if a man cannot see a difference infinitely deeper than any similarity between this song of Alison and the old Anglo-Saxon verse﻿—*a difference of nature*﻿—I must despair of his literary sense.

===Chinese===
但是，倘若一个人看不出这首阿莉森之歌与古盎格鲁-撒克逊诗之间，有一种比任何相似都深远得多的差异——*一种本质上的差异*——那我对他的文学感受力便只能绝望了。

===Original===
What has happened? Well, in Normandy, too, and in another tongue, men are singing much the same thing in the same way:

===Chinese===
发生了什么？喏，在诺曼底，人们也在用另一种语言、以同样的方式，唱着大同小异的东西：

===Original===
> A la fontenelle
 

 Qui sort seur l’araine,
 

 Trouvai pastorella
 

 Qui n’iert pas vilaine﻿ ﻿…
 

 Merci, merci, douce Marote,
 

 N’oçiez pas vostre ami doux,

===Chinese===
> 在那小泉边，
 

 泉水淌上沙滩，
 

 我遇见一位牧羊姑娘，
 

 她可毫不粗鄙……
 

 求你，求你，温柔的玛罗特，
 

 别不理你温柔的情人，

===Original===
and this Norman and the Englishman were singing to a new tune, which was yet an old tune re-set to Europe by the Provence, the Roman Province; by the troubadours﻿—Pons de Capdeuil, Bernard de Ventadour, Bertrand de Born, Pierre Vidal, and the rest, with William of Poitou, William of Poitiers. Read and compare; you will perceive that the note then set persists and has never perished. Take Giraud de Borneil﻿—

===Chinese===
而这位诺曼人和这位英格兰人唱的是一支新调子，其实却是一支旧调子，由普罗旺斯——那罗马行省——为欧洲重新定下的；由那些行吟诗人（troubadours）——蓬·德·卡普德伊（Pons de Capdeuil）、贝尔纳·德·旺塔多恩（Bernard de Ventadour）、贝特朗·德·博恩（Bertrand de Born）、皮埃尔·维达尔（Pierre Vidal），以及其余诸人，还有普瓦图的威廉（William of Poitou）、普瓦捷的威廉（William of Poitiers）。请去对读比较；诸位会察觉，当时定下的那个调子延续至今，从未消亡。试取吉罗·德·博内尔（Giraud de Borneil）——

===Original===
> Bel companhos, si dormetz o velhatz
 

 Non dortmatz plus, qu’el jorn es apropchatz﻿—

===Chinese===
> 好伙伴，无论你睡着还是醒着，
 

 别再睡了，日子已经临近——

===Original===
and set it beside a lyric of our day, written without a thought of Giraud de Borneil﻿—

===Chinese===
——再把它同我们今日的一首抒情歌诗并排放在一起，那首诗落笔时全然没有想到过吉罗·德·博内尔——

===Original===
> Heigh! Brother mine, art a-waking or a-sleeping:
 

 Mind’st thou the merry moon a many summers fled?
 

 Mind’st thou the green and the dancing and the leaping?
 

 Mind’st thou the haycocks and the moon above them creeping?﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
> 嘿！我的兄弟，你是醒着还是睡着：
 

 可记得许多个夏天以前那轮欢乐的月亮？
 

 可记得那青葱、那舞蹈、那欢蹦欢跳？
 

 可记得那些干草堆，还有草堆上缓缓爬升的月亮？……

===Original===
Or take Bernard de Ventadour’s﻿—

===Chinese===
或者取贝尔纳·德·旺塔多恩的这一首——

===Original===
> Quand erba vertz, e fuelha par
 

 E’l flor brotonon per verjan,
 

 E’l rossinhols autet e clar
 

 Leva sa votz e mov son chan,
 

 Joy ai de luy, e joy ai de la flor,
 

 Joy ai de me, e de me dons maior.

===Chinese===
> 当青草返绿，叶片初绽，
 

 花蕾沿着篱垣开放，
 

 夜莺在高处，清亮地
 

 提起嗓音，展开歌喉，
 

 我为它欢喜，也为花欢喜，
 

 为自己欢喜，为我的夫人欢喜更深。

===Original===
Why, it runs straight off into English verse﻿—

===Chinese===
可不是么，它径直流进了英语诗——

===Original===
> When grass is green and leaves appear
 

 With flowers in bud the meads among,
 

 And nightingale aloft and clear
 

 Lifts up his voice and pricks his song,
 

 Joy, joy have I in song and flower,
 

 Joy in myself, and in my lady more.

===Chinese===
> 当草色转青，叶片显现，
 

 花蕾夹杂在草场之间，
 

 夜莺在高空清越地
 

 提起嗓音，催紧了歌喉，
 

 欢喜，欢喜，我为歌为花而欢喜，
 

 为我自己欢喜，为我的夫人欢喜更深。

===Original===
And that may be doggerel; yet what is it but

===Chinese===
这也许只是打油诗；然而它不是别的，正是——

===Original===
> It was a lover and his lass,
 

 With a hey and a ho and a hey nonino,
 

 That o’er the green cornfield did pass
 

 In the springtime, the only pretty ring-time﻿—

===Chinese===
> 那是一个情郎和他的姑娘，
 

 唱着一声嗨哟、一声嗬哟、一声嗨哟诺尼诺，
 

 他们双双走过青青的谷田，
 

 正当春天，唯一美好的婚戒时节——

===Original===
or

===Chinese===
或者

===Original===
> When daffodils begin to peer,
 

 With heigh! the doxy over the dale,
 

 Why then comes in the sweet o’ the year;
 

 For the red blood reigns in the winter’s pale.

===Chinese===
> 当水仙花开始探头露脸，
 

 嗨！那浪荡姑娘正翻过山坳，
 

 一年里最甜美的时令便到来了；
 

 因为赤热的血在寒冬的藩篱里称王。

===Original===
Nay, flatter the Anglo-Saxon tradition by picking its very best﻿—and I suppose it hard to find better than the much-admired opening of *Piers Plowman*, in which that tradition shot up like the flame of a dying candle:

===Chinese===
不，哪怕格外抬举盎格鲁-撒克逊传统，专拣它最好的东西——我想很难找到比《农夫皮尔斯》（*Piers Plowman*）那备受激赏的开篇更好的了；在那开篇里，这一传统像一支将熄的蜡烛蹿起的火苗，最后腾亮了一下：

===Original===
> Bote in a Mayes Morwnynge﻿—on Malverne hulles
 

 Me bi-fel a ferly﻿—a Feyrie me thouhte;
 

 I was weori of wandringe﻿—and wente me to reste
 

 Under a brod banke﻿—bi a Bourne syde,
 

 And as I lay and leonede﻿—and lokede on the watres,
 

 I slumberde in a slepynge﻿—hit sownede so murie.

===Chinese===
> 但在五月的一个清晨——在马尔文山上，
 

 我遇上了一桩奇事——恍若身入仙境；
 

 我浪游得乏了——便去歇息，
 

 在一道宽阔的土坡下——傍着一条溪流，
 

 而当我躺卧倚凭——凝望那流水，
 

 我便沉沉睡去——水声如此悦耳。

===Original===
This is good, solid stuff, no doubt: but tame, inert, if not actually lifeless. As M. Jusserand says of Anglo-Saxon poetry in general, it is like the river Saône﻿—one doubts which way it flows. How tame in comparison with this, for example!﻿—

===Chinese===
这无疑是扎实的好东西：但温吞、滞重，若非全无生气，也相去不远。正如儒瑟朗先生（M. Jusserand）评盎格鲁-撒克逊诗歌整体时所说，它像索恩河（Saône）——人们拿不准它往哪个方向流。相形之下，比如下面这首，何其遒劲！——

===Original===
> In somer, when the shawes be sheyne,
 

 And leves be large and long,
 

 Hit is full mery in feyre foreste
 

 To here the foulys song:
> To se the dere draw to the dale
 

 And leve the hilles hee,
 

 And shadow hem in the leves grene
 

 Under the grene-wode tre.
> Hit befell on Whitsontide,
 

 Erly in a May mornyng,
 

 The Son up feyre can shyne,
 

 And the briddis mery can syng.
> “This is a mery mornyng,” said litell John,
 

 “Be Hym that dyed on tre;
 

 A more mery man than I am one
 

 Lyves not in Cristianté.
> “Pluk up thi hert, my dere mayster,”
 

 Litull John can sey,
 

 “And thynk hit is a full fayre tyme
 

 In a mornyng of May.”

===Chinese===
> 夏日里，当小树林明丽秀美，
 

 树叶又宽又长，
 

 在美丽的森林里真是快活，
 

 听那众鸟歌唱：
> 看牡鹿纷纷走向山谷，
 

 离开高高的山冈，
 

 把身影藏进绿叶，
 

 栖在那绿林树的下方。
> 时逢圣灵降临节，
 

 五月清早的时分，
 

 太阳升起来，晴光朗照，
 

 鸟儿们喜气洋洋地唱。
> 「这是个快活的早晨，」小约翰说道，
 

 「凭那位死在十字架上的主起誓：
 

 比我更快活的人，
 

 基督教世界里找不出一个。
> 「打起精神来，我亲爱的师父，」
 

 小约翰说道，
 

 「想想这是多好的时光，
 

 在五月的清早。」

===Original===
There is no doubting which way *that* flows! And this vivacity, this new beat of the heart of poetry, is common to Chaucer and the humblest ballad-maker; it pulses through any book of lyrics printed yesterday, and it came straight to us out of Provence, the Roman Province. It was the Provençal Troubadour who, like the Prince in the fairy tale, broke through the hedge of briers and kissed Beauty awake again.
---

===Chinese===
*那首*往哪个方向流，可毫无悬念！而这股生气勃勃的活力，这颗诗之心的新的搏动，为乔叟（Chaucer）与最卑微的谣曲作者所共有；它搏动在昨日付印的任何一部抒情诗集里；而它是径直从普罗旺斯、从那罗马行省来到我们这里的。正是普罗旺斯的行吟诗人，像童话里的王子一样，冲破荆棘的围篱，一吻唤醒了睡美人。
---

===Original===
You will urge that he wakened Poetry not in England alone but all over Europe, in Dante before our Chaucer, in the trouvères and minnesingers as well as in our ballad-writers. To that I might easily retort, “So much the better for Europe, and the more of it the merrier, to win their way into the great comity.” But here I put in my second assertion, that we English have had above all nations lying wide of the Mediterranean, the instinct to refresh and renew ourselves at Mediterranean wells; that again and again our writers﻿—our poets especially﻿—have sought them as the hart panteth after the water-brooks. If you accept this assertion, and if you believe as well that our literature, surpassing Rome’s, may vie with that of Athens﻿—if you believe that a literature which includes Chaucer, Spenser, Shakespeare, Pope, Wordsworth, Shelley﻿—the Authorised Version of Holy Writ, with Browne, Bunyan, Swift, Addison, Johnson, Arnold, Newman﻿—has entered the circle to take its seat with the first﻿—why then, heartily believing this with you, I leave you to find some better explanation than mine if you can.

===Chinese===
诸位会进言说，他唤醒的不只是英格兰的诗，而是全欧洲的诗：在我们乔叟之前的但丁（Dante）那里，在法北行吟诗人（trouvères）与德国恋歌歌手（minnesingers）那里，一如在我们的谣曲作者那里。对此我不难回敬一句：「那对欧洲再好不过；人越多越热闹，正好一并赢得进入那个伟大文明共同体的门路。」但在这里我要提出我的第二条断言：在所有远离地中海的民族之中，唯独我们英格兰人，本能地会到地中海的井泉边汲取活力、更新自身；我们的作家——尤其是我们的诗人——一次又一次地追寻那些井泉，如鹿之切慕溪水。倘若诸位接受这条断言，又倘若诸位进而相信，我们的文学足以超越罗马、与雅典的文学一争高下——倘若诸位相信，一部收罗了乔叟、斯宾塞（Spenser）、莎士比亚（Shakespeare）、蒲柏、华兹华斯（Wordsworth）、雪莱（Shelley）——收罗了圣经钦定本，连同布朗、班扬（Bunyan）、斯威夫特（Swift）、艾迪生、约翰逊、阿诺德（Arnold）、纽曼（Newman）——的文学，已经进入了那个圈子，与第一流者并肩而坐——那么，既然我与诸位一同由衷相信这一点，寻找一个比我的更好的解释这件事，我就留给诸位了，倘若诸位找得到的话。

===Original===
But what I content myself with asserting here you can scarcely deny. Chaucer’s initial and enormous debt to Dante and Boccaccio stands in as little dispute as Dunbar’s to Chaucer. On that favourite poet of mine, Sir Thomas Wyat, I descanted in a former lecture. He is one of your glories here, having entered St. John’s College at the age of twelve (which must have been precocious even for those days.) Anthony Wood asserts that after finishing his course here, he proceeded to Cardinal Wolsey’s new College at Oxford; but, as Christchurch was not founded until 1524, and Wyat, still precocious, had married a wife two years before that, the statement (to quote Dr. Courthope) “seems no better founded than many others advanced by that patriotic but not very scrupulous author.” It is more to the point that he went travelling, and brought home from France, Italy, afterwards Spain﻿—always from Latin altars﻿—the flame of lyrical poetry to England; the flame of the Petrarchists, caught from the Troubadours, clarified (so to speak) by the salt of humane letters. On what our Elizabethan literature owes to the Classical revival hundreds of volumes have been written and hundreds more will be written; I will but remind you of what Spencer talked about with Gabriel Harvey, what Daniel disputed with Campion; that Marlowe tried to reincarnate Machiavelli, that Jonson was a sworn Latinist and the “tribe of Ben” a classical tribe; while, as for Shakespeare, go and reckon the proportion of Italian and Roman names in his dramatis personae. Of Donne’s debt to France, Italy, Rome, Greece, you may read much in Professor Grierson’s great edition, and I daresay Professor Grierson would be the first to allow that all has not yet been computed. You know how Milton prepared himself to be a poet. Have you realised that, in those somewhat strangely constructed sonnets of his, Milton was deliberately modelling upon the “Horatian Ode,” as his confrère, Andrew Marvell, was avowedly attempting the like in his famous Horation Ode on Cromwell’s Return from Ireland; so that if Cromwell had returned (like Mr. Quilp), walked in and caught his pair of Latin Secretaries scribbling verse, one at either end of the office table, both might colourably have pleaded that they were, after all, writing Latin. Waller’s task in poetry was to labour true classical polish where Cowley laboured sham-classical form. Put together Dryden’s various Prefaces and you will find them one solid monument to his classical faith. Of Pope, Gray, Collins, you will not ask me to speak. What is salt in Cowper you can taste only when you have detected that by a stroke of madness he missed, or barely missed, being our true English Horace, that almost more nearly than the rest he hit what the rest had been seeking. Then, of the “romantic revival”﻿—enemy of false classicism, not of classicism﻿—bethink you what, in his few great years, Wordsworth owed directly to France of the early Revolution; what Keats drew forth out of Lemprière: and again bethink you how Tennyson wrought upon Theocritus, Virgil, Catullus; upon what Arnold constantly shaped his verse; how Browning returned ever upon Italy to inspire his best and correct his worse.

===Chinese===
但我在此安于断言的，是诸位几乎无法否认的。乔叟起初对但丁与薄伽丘（Boccaccio）负下的巨大恩情，其少受争议，一如邓巴（Dunbar）之受惠于乔叟。至于托马斯·怀亚特爵士（Sir Thomas Wyat），我心爱的那位诗人，我在先前一讲里已详论过。他是此间诸位的荣光之一，十二岁便进了圣约翰学院（St. John's College）（即便在那个年代，这也必定算得上早慧）。安东尼·伍德（Anthony Wood）断言，他在此地完成学业之后，又进了牛津沃尔西枢机（Cardinal Wolsey）新办的学院；但一则基督堂学院（Christchurch）直到 1524 年才创立，二来怀亚特——依旧早慧——在那之前两年已经娶了妻，所以这一说法（援引库思罗普博士〔Dr. Courthope〕的话）「比起那位爱国却不大讲究的作者提出的许多别的说法来，未必更有根据。」更要紧的是：他曾出外游历，从法国、意大利、其后又从西班牙——总是从拉丁的祭坛上——把抒情诗的火焰带回了英格兰；那是彼特拉克派（Petrarchists）的火焰，得自行吟诗人，又（不妨这样说）经人文学识之盐的澄清。至于我们的伊丽莎白时代文学受惠于古典复兴者几何，成百上千卷书已经写过，成百上千卷书还将写出来；我只需提醒诸位：斯宾塞同加布里埃尔·哈维（Gabriel Harvey）谈过些什么，丹尼尔（Daniel）同坎皮恩（Campion）争执过什么；马洛（Marlowe）如何试图让马基雅维利（Machiavelli）还魂，本·琼森（Ben Jonson）如何是矢志不渝的拉丁派，而「本氏一族」（tribe of Ben）是一个古典的族群；至于莎士比亚，请去数一数他剧中人（dramatis personae）里意大利和罗马名字的比例。关于多恩（Donne）受惠于法国、意大利、罗马、希腊之处，诸位尽可在格里尔森教授（Professor Grierson）那部皇皇巨帙的版本文集里读到许多，而且我敢说，格里尔森教授会第一个承认，这笔账至今尚未算清。诸位知道弥尔顿（Milton）如何预备自己做诗人。诸位可曾意识到：在他那些结构颇为奇特的十四行诗里，弥尔顿是在刻意师法「贺拉斯体颂歌」，一如他的同僚安德鲁·马韦尔（Andrew Marvell）公然在那首著名的《克伦威尔自爱尔兰归来的贺拉斯体颂歌》（Horation Ode on Cromwell's Return from Ireland）里作同类的尝试；因此，倘若克伦威尔当真回来了（像奎尔普先生〔Mr. Quilp〕那样），踱进门来，撞见他的两位拉丁文秘书伏在办公桌两端各自挥毫写诗，两位尽可以振振有词地辩称：他们写的，归根结底是拉丁文。沃勒（Waller）在诗中的任务，是下真古典的打磨功夫，而考利（Cowley）下的，则是假古典的形式功夫。把德莱顿的各篇序言汇拢来看，诸位会发现它们是一座献给他古典信仰的坚实纪念碑。至于蒲柏、格雷（Gray）、柯林斯（Collins），诸位不会还要我置一词。柯珀（Cowper）文中的盐味，只有当诸位品出下面这一层，方才尝得出来：由于一阵疯狂的播弄，他错过了、或者说仅仅差一点错过，没能成为我们真正的英格兰贺拉斯；而他所命中的，几乎比其余所有人都更贴近众人一直寻求的东西。再者，说到「浪漫主义复兴」——它以假古典主义为敌，不以古典主义为敌——请想想，华兹华斯在他短短几年的伟大岁月里，直接受惠于大革命初期法国的是什么；济慈（Keats）从莱姆普里埃（Lemprière）的辞书里汲取出了什么：再请想想，丁尼生（Tennyson）如何师法忒奥克里托斯（Theocritus）、维吉尔（Virgil）、卡图卢斯（Catullus）；阿诺德不断以什么来范铸他的诗行；勃朗宁（Browning）如何一次次回到意大利，以激发他最好的诗，矫正他较逊的诗。

===Original===
Of Anglo-Saxon prose I know little indeed, but enough of the world to feel reasonably sure that if it contained any single masterpiece﻿—or anything that could be paraded as a masterpiece﻿—we should have heard enough about it long before now. It was invented by King Alfred for excellent political reasons; but, like other ready-made political inventions in this country, it refused to thrive. I think it can be demonstrated, that the true line of intellectual descent in prose lies through Bede (who wrote in Latin, the “universal language”), and not through the Blickling Homilies, or, Ælfric, or the Saxon Chronicle. And I am sure that Freeman is perversely wrong when he laments as a “great mistake” that the first Christian missionaries from Rome did not teach their converts to pray and give praise in the vernacular. The vernacular being what it was, these men did better to teach the religion of the civilised world﻿—*orbis terrarum*﻿—in the language of the civilised world. I am not thinking of its efficiency for spreading the faith; but neither is Freeman; and, for that, we must allow these old missionaries to have known their own business. I am thinking only of how this “great mistake” affected our literature; and if you will read Professor Saintsbury’s *History of English Prose Rhythm* (pioneer work, which yet wonderfully succeeds in illustrating what our prose-writers from time to time were trying to do); if you will study the Psalms in the Authorised Version; if you will consider what Milton, Clarendon, Sir Thomas Browne, were aiming at; what Addison, Gibbon, Johnson; what Landor, Thackeray, Newman, Arnold, Pater; I doubt not your rising from the perusal convinced that our nation, in this storehouse of Latin to refresh and replenish its most sacred thoughts, has enjoyed a continuous blessing: that the Latin of the Vulgate and the Offices has been a background giving depth and, as the painters say, “value” to nine-tenths of our serious writing.

===Chinese===
至于盎格鲁-撒克逊散文，我所知确实寥寥，但对世事也知之足够，可以相当有把握地说：倘若它含有一部哪怕单独的杰作——或任何可以冒充杰作陈列的东西——我们早就会听到多得听不完的议论了。它是阿尔弗雷德王出于极好的政治理由发明出来的；但是，一如本国的其他现成政治发明，它拒绝兴旺。我想可以证明：散文真正的智识世系，经由比德（Bede）——他以「通用语言」拉丁文写作——一脉传下，而不经由《布利克灵布道集》（Blickling Homilies），不经由埃尔弗里克（Ælfric），也不经由《撒克逊编年史》（the Saxon Chronicle）。而弗里曼把下述一点当作「大错」来悲叹——第一批来自罗马的基督教传教士没有教皈依者用本土语言祷告赞美——我确信他是执拗地错了。本土语言既是那样一种语言，这些人把文明世界——*orbis terrarum*（普天之下）——的宗教，用文明世界的语言传授出来，反倒做得更对。我想到的不是它对传布信仰的效力；弗里曼想到的也不是；就此而论，我们必须承认这些老传教士是懂自己本行的。我想到的只是这个「大错」如何影响了我们的文学；倘若诸位去读圣茨伯里教授（Professor Saintsbury）的《英语散文节奏史》（*History of English Prose Rhythm*）——一部开风气之先的著作，却又出色地说明了我们的散文作家历代想要做的事；倘若诸位研读钦定本中的《诗篇》；倘若诸位细想弥尔顿、克拉伦登（Clarendon）、托马斯·布朗爵士意在何为；艾迪生、吉本（Gibbon）、约翰逊意在何为；兰多（Landor）、萨克雷（Thackeray）、纽曼、阿诺德、佩特（Pater）意在何为——我不怀疑，诸位读罢掩卷时必已确信：我们的民族，在这座拉丁文的库藏里汲取滋养、充实自己最神圣的思想，一直享用着一份绵延不绝的福分：武加大译本（Vulgate）与日课经文的拉丁文，一直是一层赋予纵深的背景，并且——如画家们所说——赋予我们十分之九的严肃写作以「明度」（value）。

===Original===
And now, since this and the previous lecture run something counter to a great deal of that teaching in English Literature which nowadays passes most acceptably, let me avoid offence, so far as may be, by defining one or two things I am *not* trying to do.

===Chinese===
而现在，既然这一讲与前一讲多少有悖于当今最受认可的那一套英国文学讲授，请容我尽可能避免开罪，先界定一两件我*并非*要做的事。

===Original===
I am not persuading you to despise your linguistic descent. English is English﻿—our language; and all its history to be venerated by us.

===Chinese===
我不是要劝说诸位轻视自己的语言世系。英语就是英语——是我们的语言；它的全部历史，都当受我们敬重。

===Original===
I am not persuading you to despise linguistic study. *All* learning is venerable.

===Chinese===
我不是要劝说诸位轻视语言研究。*一切*学问皆可敬。

===Original===
I am not persuading you to behave like Ascham, and turn English prose into pedantic Latin; nor would I have you doubt that in the set quarrel between Campion, who wished to divert English verse into strict classical channels, and Daniel, who vindicated our free English way (derived from Latin through the Provençal), Daniel was on the whole, right, Campion on the whole, wrong: though I believe that both ways yet lie open, and we may learn, if we study them intelligently, a hundred things from the old classical metres.

===Chinese===
我不是要劝说诸位效法阿斯克姆（Ascham），把英语散文变成迂腐的拉丁文；我也不愿诸位怀疑，在那场既定的争执中——一方是坎皮恩，他想把英语诗引入严格的古典轨道；一方是丹尼尔，他捍卫了我们自由的英语路数（经由普罗旺斯自拉丁文衍变而来）——总的说来丹尼尔是对的，总的说来坎皮恩是错的：虽然我相信，两条路至今都还敞着，而且，倘若我们研习得法，还可从古老的古典格律中学到一百样东西。

===Original===
I do not ask you to forget what there is of the Northmen in your blood. If I desired this, I could not worship William Morris as I do, among the later poets.

===Chinese===
我不是要诸位忘记你们血液里的北方人成分。倘若我存此想，便不会像如今这样，在较晚的诗人之中崇敬威廉·莫里斯（William Morris）了。

===Original===
I do not ask you to doubt that the barbarian invaders from the north, with their myths and legends, brought new and most necessary blood of imagination into the literary material﻿—for the time almost exhausted﻿—of Greece and Rome.

===Chinese===
我不是要诸位怀疑，来自北方的蛮族入侵者，携着他们的神话与传说，为希腊与罗马的文学材料——当时已近枯竭——注入了崭新而必不可少的想象血液。

===Original===
Nevertheless, I do contend that when Britain (or, if you prefer it, Sleswick)

===Chinese===
然而我确实主张：当不列颠（或者，倘若诸位乐意，斯勒斯维克〔Sleswick〕）

===Original===
> When Sleswick first at Heaven’s command
 

 Arose from out the azure main,

===Chinese===
> 当斯勒斯维克最初奉天命，
