 从蔚蓝的洋面升起，

===Original===
she differed from Aphrodite, that other foam-born, in sundry important features of ear, of lip, of eye.

===Chinese===
她同那另一位泡沫所生的阿佛洛狄忒（Aphrodite）比起来，在耳、唇、眼的若干重要特征上，各有不同。

===Original===
Lastly, if vehement assertions on the one side have driven me into too vehement dissent on the other, I crave pardon; not for the dissent but for the vehemence, as sinning against the very principle I would hold up to your admiration﻿—the old Greek principle of avoiding excess.
---

===Chinese===
最后，倘若一方的激烈断言把我逼成了另一方过于激烈的异议，我请求宽恕；不为异议，只为激烈，因为它冒犯的恰是我要举荐给诸位赞赏的那个原则——古希腊避免过度的原则。
---

===Original===
But I *do* commend the patient study of Greek and Latin authors﻿—in the original or in translation﻿—to all of you who would write English; and for three reasons.

===Chinese===
但我*确实*要向诸位中所有有意写英语的人推许一件事：耐心研读希腊与拉丁作家——读原文也好，读译本也好；理由有三。

===Original===
1. In the first place they will correct your insularity of mind; or, rather, will teach you to forget it. The Anglo-Saxon, it has been noted, ever left an empty space around his houses; and that, no doubt, is good for a house. It is not so good for the mind.
2. Secondly, we have a tribal habit, confirmed by Protestant meditation upon a Hebraic religion, of confining our literary enjoyment to the written word and frowning down the drama, the song, the dance. A fairly attentive study of modern lyrical verse has persuaded me that this exclusiveness may be carried too far, and threatens to be deadening. “I will sing and give praise,” says the Scripture, “with the best member that I have”﻿—meaning the tongue. But the old Greek was an “all-round man” as we say. He sought to praise and give thanks with all his members, and to tune each to perfection. I think his way worth your considering.
3. Lastly, and chiefly, I commend these classical authors to you because they, in the European civilisation which we all inherit, conserve the norm of literature; the steady grip on the essential; the clean outline at which in verse or in prose﻿—in epic, drama, history, or philosophical treatise﻿—a writer should aim.

===Chinese===
1. 首先，它们会矫正你们心智的岛国性；或者不如说，会教你们忘掉它。有人注意到，盎格鲁-撒克逊人素来在住宅四周留出空地；对一座房子而言，这无疑是对的。对心智而言，却未必如此。
2. 其次，我们有一个部族的习惯——又经新教对一种希伯来宗教的沉潜冥思而加固——便是把文学享受局限于书面的文字，而对戏剧、对歌、对舞蹙眉相向。对现代抒情诗相当用心的一段研读使我相信，这种自我封闭可能已被推得过远，并有趋于僵死之虞。「我要歌唱，我要赞美，」圣书上说，「用我身上最好的肢体」——意指舌头。但古希腊人，如我们所说，是个「全面发展的人」。他要用全部肢体来赞美感恩，并把每一件都调校到完善。我以为他这条路子值得诸位斟酌。
3. 最后，也是最重要的，我向诸位推许这些古典作家，是因为在我们大家共同承袭的欧洲文明之中，正是他们保守着文学的规范；对本质的稳稳把握；以及无论诗还是散文——史诗、戏剧、史著或哲学论著——写作者都应瞄准的那道干净的轮廓。

===Original===
So sure am I of this, and of its importance to those who think of writing, that were this University to limit me to three texts on which to preach English Literature to you, I should choose the Bible in our Authorised Version, Shakespeare, and Homer (though it were but in a prose translation). Two of these lie outside my marked province. Only one of them finds a place in your English school. But Homer, who comes neither within my map, nor within the ambit of the Tripos, would﻿—because he most evidently holds the norm, the essence, the secret of all﻿—rank first of the three for my purpose.

===Chinese===
我对这一点，及其对有意写作者的重要性，如此确信，以至于倘若这所大学限定我只用三种文本来向诸位讲授英国文学，我会选钦定本圣经、莎士比亚与荷马（Homer）（哪怕荷马只有散文译本也罢）。这三部里有两部在我标定的辖域之外。只有一部见于诸位的英文学科。然而荷马——他既不入我的版图，也不在荣誉考试的射程之内——却会，因为他最显然地握有万物的规范、本质与奥秘，为着我这个目的，在三部的名次上位居第一。

## Lecture X English Literature in Our Universities (I) / X 英国文学与大学（上）

===Original===
Wednesday, November 19

===Chinese===
11 月 19 日，星期三

===Original===
All lectures are too long. Towards the close of my last, Gentlemen, I let fall a sentence which, heard by you in a moment of exhausted or languid interest, has since, like enough, escaped your memory even if it earned passing attention. So let me repeat it, for a fresh start.

===Chinese===
一切讲座都太长了。诸位先生，上一讲临近收尾时，我随口落下一句话；你们当时听得兴趣耗竭、意兴阑珊，那句话此后多半已从你们的记忆中溜走——即便它曾博得片刻注意，也未必例外。所以，为着一个新的开端，让我把它重述一遍。

===Original===
Having quoted to you the words of our Holy Writ, “I will sing and give praise with the best member that I have,” I added, “But the old Greek was an ‘all-round’ man; he sought to praise and give thanks with all his members, and to tune each to perfection.” Now a great many instructive lectures might be written on that text: nevertheless you may think it a strange one, and obscure, for the discourse on “English Literature in our Universities,” which, according to promise, I must now attempt.

===Chinese===
先前我引了我们《圣经》上的话：「我要用我所拥有的最好肢体歌唱颂赞」，随后补了一句：「然而古希腊人是个『全面』（all-round）的人；他要以全部肢体去颂赞、去感恩，并把每一件都调校到完善。」就这条箴言，本可以写出许许多多富有教益的讲座来：然而你们或许觉得，拿它作「英国文学与大学」这篇讲演的题旨——按先前的许诺，我如今必须一试的这篇——未免奇特而费解。

===Original===
The term “an all-round man” may easily mislead you unless you take it with the rest of the sentence and particularly with the words “praise and give thanks.” Praise whom? Give thanks to whom? To *whom* did our Greek train all his members to render adoration?

===Chinese===
「全面的人」这个说法很容易把你们引入歧途，除非你们把它同句子的其余部分、尤其同「颂赞」「感恩」这几个词连在一起看。颂赞谁？向谁感恩？我们的希腊人调校他的全部肢体，是要向谁奉献崇敬？

===Original===
Why, to the gods﻿—his gods: to Zeus, Apollo, Aphrodite; and from them down to the lesser guardian deities of the hearth, the field, the farmstead. We modern men suffer a double temptation to misunderstand, by belittling, the reverence in which Hellas and Rome held their gods. To start with, our religion has superseded theirs. We approach the Olympians with no bent towards venerating them; with minds easy, detached, to which a great deal of their theology﻿—the amativeness of Zeus for example﻿—must needs seem broadly comic, and a great deal of it not only comic but childish. We are encouraged in this, moreover, when we read such writers as Aristophanes and Lucian, and observe how they poked fun at the gods. We assume﻿—so modern he seems﻿—Aristophanes’ attitude towards his immortals to be ours; that when, for example, Prometheus walks on to the stage under an umbrella, to hide himself from the gaze of all-seeing Zeus, the Athenian audience laughed just as we laugh who have read Voltaire. Believe me, they laughed quite differently; believe me, Aristophanes and Voltaire had remarkably different minds and worked on utterly different backgrounds. Believe me, you will understand Aristophanes only less than you will understand Aeschylus himself if you confuse Aristophanes’ mockery of Olympus with modern mockery. But, if you will not take my word for it, let me quote what Professor Gilbert Murray said, the other day, speaking before the English Association on Greek poetry, how constantly connected it is with religion:

===Chinese===
当然是向诸神——他的诸神：向宙斯（Zeus）、阿波罗（Apollo）、阿佛洛狄忒（Aphrodite）；再从他们一路下来，直到炉灶、田亩、农庄的那些较小的守护神明。我们现代人受着双重的诱惑，容易误解——而且是以贬低的方式误解——希腊与罗马对他们的诸神所怀的崇敬。首先，我们的宗教已经取代了他们的宗教。我们走近奥林匹斯诸神，全无尊奉之意；心境轻松而超然，于是他们的神学里有相当一部分——例如宙斯的好色多情——在我们看来必定大体上是喜剧性的，还有相当一部分不仅可哂，简直幼稚。何况，一读阿里斯托芬（Aristophanes）、琉善（Lucian）这样的作家，看到他们怎样拿诸神打趣，我们这种态度更得到了鼓励。我们想当然——他显得何等现代——把阿里斯托芬对待他不朽诸神的态度当成了我们自己的态度；以为当普罗米修斯（Prometheus）撑着一把伞走上舞台、躲避全知的宙斯的目光时，雅典观众笑得同我们这些读过伏尔泰（Voltaire）的人一模一样。相信我，他们笑得全然两样；相信我，阿里斯托芬与伏尔泰的心智大相径庭，其活动的背景也截然不同。相信我，你若把阿里斯托芬对奥林匹斯的嘲弄同现代的嘲弄混为一谈，那么你之不理解阿里斯托芬，将与你不理解埃斯库罗斯（Aeschylus）本人相去无几。但你们若不肯信我的话，且容我引一段吉尔伯特·默里教授（Professor Gilbert Murray）前些天在英国语文协会（the English Association）讲希腊诗歌时所说的话——他说希腊诗歌与宗教的联系何等恒常：

===Original===
> “All thoughts, all passions, all desires”﻿ ﻿… In our Art it is true, no doubt, that they are “the ministers of love”; in Greek they are as a whole the ministers of religion, and this is what in a curious degree makes Greek poetry matter, makes it relevant. There is a sense in each song of a relation to the whole of things, and it was apt to be expressed with the whole body, or, one may say, the whole being.14

===Chinese===
> 「一切思想、一切激情、一切欲望」……在我们的艺术里，不错，它们无疑「是爱的仆役」；而在希腊，它们整个儿是宗教的仆役：恰恰是在一种奇妙的程度上，这一点使希腊诗歌成其为大事、成其为切题。每一首歌里都有一种与万物全体的关联之感，而它惯于用整个身体——或者说整个存在——表达出来。14

===Original===
To a Greek, in short, his gods mattered enormously; and to a Roman. To a Roman they continued to matter enormously, down to the end. Do you remember that tessellated pavement with its emblems and images of the younger gods? and how I told you that a Roman general on foreign service would carry the little cubes in panniers on mule-back, to be laid down for his feet at the next camping place? Will you suggest that he did this because they were pretty? You know that practical men﻿—conquering generals﻿—don’t behave in that way. He did it because they were sacred; because, like most practical men, he was religious, and his gods must go with him. They filled his literature: for why? He believed himself to be sprung from their loins. Where would Latin literature be, for example, if you could cut Venus out of it? Consider Lucretius’ grand invocation:

===Chinese===
简言之，对希腊人而言，他的诸神至关重要；对罗马人亦然。直到最后，诸神对罗马人始终至关重要。你们还记得那方镶嵌铺面，连同其上较年轻诸神的徽记与图像么？还记得我怎么告诉你们的么——一位外任征战的罗马将军，会把那些小方块镶嵌石装进驮篮、由骡子驮着走，好在下一处营盘铺在他的脚下？你们会说，他这样做是因为那些石片好看么？你们知道，讲求实际的人——开疆取胜的将军——是不这么行事的。他这样做，是因为它们神圣；因为同大多数讲求实际的人一样，他有宗教心，他的诸神必须随他同行。诸神充盈着他的文学：何以故？因为他相信自己是他们的骨血所出。譬如说，倘若你能把维纳斯从拉丁文学里剜掉，拉丁文学还成个什么？且看卢克莱修（Lucretius）那堂皇的祷唤：

===Original===
> Æneadum genetrix, hominum divumque voluptas, Alma Venus!

===Chinese===
> Æneadum genetrix, hominum divumque voluptas, Alma Venus!（埃涅阿斯族人的母亲，人与神的欢愉，慈爱的维纳斯啊！）

===Original===
Consider the part Virgil makes her play as moving spirit of his whole great poem. So follow her down to the days of the later Empire and open the “Pervigilium Veneris” and discover her, under the name of Dione, still the eternal Aphrodite sprung from the foam amid the churning hooves of the seahorses﻿—*inter et bipedes equos*:﻿—

===Chinese===
且看维吉尔（Virgil）派定她扮演的角色：整部伟大诗篇的推动灵魂。再追随她下到帝国后期的岁月，翻开《维纳斯守夜歌》（Pervigilium Veneris），你会发现她化名狄俄涅（Dione），依然是那位永恒的阿佛洛狄忒，在翻搅的海马蹄与浪沫之间诞生——*inter et bipedes equos*（在那些双足的马之间）：——

===Original===
> Time was that a rain-cloud begat her, impregning the heave of the deep,
 

 ’Twixt hooves of seahorses a-scatter, stampeding the dolphins as sheep.
 

 Lo! arose of that bridal Dione, rainbow’d and besprent of its dew!
 

 
 *Now learn ye to love who loved never﻿—now ye who have loved, love anew!*
 
 

 Her favour it was fill’d the sails of the Trojan for Latium bound,
 

 Her favour that won her Aeneas a bride on Laurentian ground,
 

 And anon from the cloister inveigled the Virgin, the Vestal, to Mars;
 

 As her wit by the wild Sabine rape recreated her Rome for its wars
 

 With the Ramnes, Quirites, together ancestrally proud as they drew
 

 From Romulus down to our Caesar﻿—last, best of that blood, of that thew.
 

 
 *Now learn ye to love who loved never﻿—now ye who have loved, love anew!*

===Chinese===
> 昔日一片雨云孕育了她，使深海的翻腾受了孕，
 

 散蹄乱奔的海马之间，海豚如羊群般惊窜四逃。
 

 看哪！那桩婚配里升起了狄俄涅，披着虹彩、缀着雨露！
 

 
 *不曾爱过的，且学着爱——爱过的，重新去爱！*
 
 

 是她的恩宠，鼓满了特洛伊人驶往拉丁姆的船帆，
 

 是她的恩宠，在劳伦图姆为埃涅阿斯赢来了新娘，
 

 转眼又诱得贞女、灶神侍女出了圣堂，委身玛尔斯；
 

 她又以萨宾抢婚的奇智，为连绵战事重造了罗马，
 

 连同拉姆尼斯人、奎里特斯人，怀着同源的祖先之骄，
 

 从罗慕路斯直抵我们的恺撒——那血脉与筋骨里最后、最好的一个。
 

 
 *不曾爱过的，且学着爱——爱过的，重新去爱！*

===Original===
“Last, best of that blood”﻿—her blood, *fusa Paphies de cruore*, and the blood of Teucer, *revocato a sanguine Teucri*, “of that thew”﻿—the thew of Tros and of Mars. Of these and no less than these our Roman believed himself the son and inheritor.

===Chinese===
「那血脉与筋骨里最后、最好的一个」——她的血脉，*fusa Paphies de cruore*（出自帕福斯人的血）；透克洛斯（Teucer）的血，*revocato a sanguine Teucri*（从透克洛斯之血召回）；「那筋骨」——特洛斯（Tros）与玛尔斯（Mars）的筋骨。我们的罗马人相信，自己正是这些——而且不减于这些——的儿子与继承人。

===Original===
If we grasp this, that the old literature was packed with the old religion, and not only packed with it but permeated by it, we have within our ten fingers the secret of the Dark Ages, the real reason why the Christian Fathers fought down literature and almost prevailed to the point of stamping it out. They hated it, not as literature; or at any rate, not to begin with; nor, to begin with, because it happened to be voluptuous and they austere: but they hated it because it held in its very texture, not to be separated, a religion over which they had hardly triumphed, a religion actively inimical to that of Christ, inimical to truth; so that for the sake of truth and in the name of Christ they had to fight it, accepting no compromise, yielding no quarter, foreseeing no issue save that one of the twain﻿—Jupiter or Christ, Deus Optimus Maximus or the carpenter’s son of Nazareth﻿—must go under.

===Chinese===
我们若把握住这一点——古代文学里塞满了古代宗教，不仅塞满，而且浸透——那么黑暗时代的秘密、教父们打压文学并且几乎得手到把它扑灭的真实原因，就尽在我们十指之间了。他们恨它，不是把它当文学恨；或者说无论如何起初不是；起初也不是因为它恰巧耽于声色而他们厉行苦修：他们恨它，是因为它的质地里织着一种宗教，无从剥离——一种他们尚未真正战胜的宗教，一种积极敌视基督教、敌视真理的宗教；于是，为了真理的缘故、奉基督之名，他们不得不与它作战：不容妥协，不留饶赦，预见的出路只有一条——二者之一必须倒下：朱庇特（Jupiter）或基督，至高至善之神（Deus Optimus Maximus）或拿撒勒的木匠之子。

===Original===
It all ended in compromise, to be sure; as all struggles must between adversaries so tremendous. Today, in Dr. Smith’s *Classical Dictionary*, Origen rubs shoulders with Orpheus and Orcus; Tertullian reposes cheek by jowl with Terpsichore. But we are not concerned, here, with what happened in the end. We are concerned with what these forthright Christian fighters had in their minds﻿—to trample out the old literature *because* of the false religion. Milton understood this, and was thinking of it when he wrote of the effect of Christ’s Nativity﻿—

===Chinese===
当然，这一切终以妥协收场；势均力敌到这般地步的对手之间的争斗，历来只能如此。今天，在史密斯博士的《古典辞典》（*Classical Dictionary*）里，奥利金（Origen）与俄耳甫斯（Orpheus）、俄耳库斯（Orcus）摩肩接踵；德尔图良（Tertullian）与忒耳普西科瑞（Terpsichore）抵足而眠。但我们在此关心的不是最终的结局。我们关心的是这些决绝的基督教斗士心中所想——为了那种伪宗教的缘故，把旧文学踏灭。弥尔顿懂得这一点；他写到基督降生的功效时，心里想的正是它——

===Original===
> The Oracles are dumb;
 

 No voice or hideous hum
 

 Runs through the archèd roof in words deceiving.
 

 Apollo from his shrine
 

 Can no more divine,
 

 With hollow shriek the steep of Delphos leaving.
 

 No mighty trance, or breathèd spell
 

 Inspires the pale-eyed Priest from the prophetic cell.
> The lonely mountains o’er,
 

 And the resounding shore,
 

 A voice of weeping heard, and loud lament;
 

 From haunted spring, and dale
 

 Edg’d with poplar pale,
 

 The parting Genius is with sighing sent;
 

 With flower-inwoven tresses torn
 

 The Nymphs in twilight shade of tangled thickets mourn.

===Chinese===
> 神谕喑哑了；
 

 再没有声音，或可憎的嗡鸣
 

 穿过拱顶，说着欺人的话语。
 

 阿波罗离开了他的神龛，
 

 再不能占卜，
 

 只发出空洞的尖啸，丢下德尔斐的陡崖。
 

 再没有强烈的迷狂，或嘘入的咒文
 

 去感发预言密室里那眼色苍白的祭司。
> 寂寞的群山之上，
 

 还有回声隆隆的海滨，
 

 听得见哭泣之声，和高声的哀恸；
 

 从精灵出没的泉边，和山谷
 

 镶着惨白杨树的边缘，
 

 告别的守护神被叹息着送走；
 

 撕着花织的发辫，
 

 仙女们在纠缠灌木的暮色浓荫里悲悼。

===Original===
—as Swinburne understands and expresses it in his “Hymn to Proserpine,” supposed to be chanted by a Roman of the “old profession” on the morrow of Constantine’s proclaiming the Christian faith﻿—

===Chinese===
——正如斯温伯恩所理解并表达的那样；在他的《献给普罗塞皮娜的赞歌》（“Hymn to Proserpine”）里，一位操「旧业」的罗马人，在君士坦丁（Constantine）宣布基督教信仰的次日，唱出这首歌——

===Original===
> O Gods dethroned and deceased, cast forth, wiped out in a day!
 

 From your wrath is the world released, redeem’d from your chains, men say.
 

 New Gods are crown’d in the city; their flowers have broken your rods;
 

 They are merciful, clothed with pity, the young compassionate Gods.
 

 But for me their new device is barren, the days are bare;
 

 Things long past over suffice, and men forgotten that were﻿ ﻿…
 

 Wilt thou yet take all, Galilean? but these thou shalt not take,
 

 The laurel, the palms and the paean, the breasts of the nymphs in the brake;
 

 Thou hast conquer’d, O pale Galilean; the world has grown grey from thy breath;
 

 We have drunken of things Lethean, and fed on the fullness of death.

===Chinese===
> 被黜而死的诸神啊，遭放逐、遭抹煞，就在一日之间！
 

 人们说，世界已从你们的震怒中释出，从你们的锁链中赎还。
 

 新神已在城里加冕；他们的花朵折断了你们的杖；
 

 他们仁慈，身披怜悯，是年轻而悲悯的神。
 

 但在我，他们的新徽记何其荒芜，这些日子何其光秃；
 

 早已过去的一切尽够了，被忘却的人们也就罢了……
 

 加利利人啊，你还要拿走一切么？唯有这些你拿不走，
 

 桂冠，棕榈，与凯歌，还有灌丛中仙女们的胸脯；
 

 你得胜了，苍白的加利利人；世界被你的气息熏成了灰色；
 

 我们饮了忘川之物，以死亡的丰盈果腹。

===Original===
“Thou hast conquer’d, O pale Galilean!” However the struggle might sway in this or that other part of the field, Literature had to be beaten to her knees, and still beaten flat until the breath left her body. You will not be surprised that the heavy hand of these Christian fathers fell first upon the Theatre: for the actor in Rome was by legal definition an “infamous” man, even as in England until the other day he was by legal definition a vagabond and liable to whipping. The policy of religious reformers has ever been to close the theatres, as our Puritans did in 1642; and a recent pronouncement by the Bishop of Kensington would seem to show that the instinct survives to this day. Queen Elizabeth﻿—like her brother, King Edward VI﻿—signalized the opening of a new reign by inhibiting stage-plays; and I invite you to share with me the pensive speculation, “How much of English Literature, had she not relented, would exist today for a King Edward VII Professor to talk about?” Certainly the works of Shakespeare would not; and that seems to me a thought so impressive as to deserve the attention of Bishops as well as of Kings.

===Chinese===
「你得胜了，苍白的加利利人！」无论战事在这块或那块战场上如何翻覆，文学注定要被打得跪下，还要被摁倒在地，直打到断气为止。这些基督教教父的重手最先落在戏剧上，你们不必惊讶：因为在罗马，演员按法律定义便是「不名誉」之人，正如在英格兰，直到不久之前，他在法律定义上还是个流浪汉、要受鞭笞。宗教改革家的政策从来就是关闭剧院，我们的清教徒 1642 年干的就是这一手；而肯辛顿主教（Bishop of Kensington）最近的一次宣告似乎表明，这本能至今犹存。伊丽莎白女王——同她的王弟爱德华六世（King Edward VI）一样——以禁演舞台剧来标志新朝的开端；我请诸位同我一起作一番怅然的推想：「倘若她不曾心软回转，今日还会有多少英国文学，供一位爱德华七世讲席教授谈论？」莎士比亚的著作肯定是没有了；而这在我看来，是一个分量重得足以引起主教们、同样也引起国王们注意的念头。

===Original===
Apart from this instinct the Christian Fathers, it would appear, had plenty of provocation. For the actors, who had jested with the Old Religion on a ground of accepted understanding﻿—much as a good husband (if you will permit the simile) may gently tease his wife, not loving her one whit the less, taught by affection to play without offending﻿—had mocked at the New Religion in a very different way: savagely, as enemies, holding up to ridicule the Church’s most sacred mysteries. Tertullian, in an uncompromising treatise “De Spectaculis,” denounces stage-plays root and branch; tells of a demon who entered into a woman in a theatre and on being exorcised pleaded that the mistake might well be excused, since he had found her in his own demesne. Christians should avoid these shows and await the greatest *spectaculum* of all﻿—the Last Judgment. “Then,” he promises genially, “will be the time to listen to the tragedians, whose lamentations will be more poignant, for their proper pain. Then will the comedians turn and twist in capers rendered nimbler than ever by the sting of the fire that is not quenched.” By 400 AD Augustine cries triumphantly that the theatres are falling﻿—the very walls of them tumbling﻿—throughout the Empire. “*Per omnes paene civitates cadunt theatra﻿ ﻿… cadunt et fora vel moenia in quibus demonia colebantur*”; the very walls within which these devilments were practised. But the fury is unabated and goes on stamping down the embers. In the eighth century our own Alcuin (as the school of Freeman would affectionately call him) is no less fierce. All plays are anathema to him, and he even disapproves of dancing bears﻿—though not, it would appear, of bad puns: “*nec tibi sit ursorum saltantium cura, sed clericorum psallentium.*”15

===Chinese===
撇开这种本能不谈，教父们似乎也着实受了足够的挑衅。因为演员们同旧宗教打趣，原站在一种彼此认可的默契之上——好比一位好丈夫（容我用这个比方）可以温和地打趣自己的妻子，爱意丝毫不减，是深情教会了他玩笑而不冒犯——可他们讥嘲新宗教，却是全然另一副路数：凶狠地、以敌人的身份，把教会最神圣的奥义拎出来示众取笑。德尔图良在一篇毫不妥协的论著《论观赏》（*De Spectaculis*）里，把舞台剧连根带枝地痛斥；他讲到一个魔鬼在剧院里附上一个女人，被驱魔时辩解说，这个误会大可原谅，因为他是在自家庄园里撞见她的。基督徒应避开这些演出，静候那一切 *spectaculum*（观赏）中最盛大的一场——末日审判。「到那时，」他和颜悦色地许诺道，「才是听悲剧演员们哀号的时辰：他们的哀号将更加凄切，因为痛在他们自己身上。到那时，喜剧演员们将翻腾扭跳，那永不熄灭之火的螫刺，会把他们的筋斗催得空前轻捷。」到公元 400 年，奥古斯丁（Augustine）得意地高呼：剧院正在倒塌——连剧院的墙都在坍塌——遍及整个帝国。「*Per omnes paene civitates cadunt theatra… cadunt et fora vel moenia in quibus demonia colebantur*」（几乎所有城市里剧院都在倒塌……那些供奉群魔的广场与城垣也在倒塌）——正是那些墙垣之内，行过这班妖邪勾当。然而怒火毫不稍减，继续把余烬踩灭。到了第八世纪，「我们自家的」阿尔昆（Alcuin）——弗里曼（Freeman）学派的人会这样亲热地称呼他——凶悍不减。一切戏剧在他都是咒诅之物，他甚至连狗熊跳舞都不赞成——不过看样子，他对糟糕的双关语倒并不见得反对：「*nec tibi sit ursorum saltantium cura, sed clericorum psallentium*」（愿你挂心的不是跳舞的熊，而是唱诗的教士）。15

===Original===
The banning of *all* literature you will find harder to understand; nay impossible, I believe, unless you accept the explanation I gave you. Yet there it is, an historical fact. “What hath it profited posterity﻿—*quid posteritas emolumenti tulit*,” wrote Sulpicius Severus, about 400 AD, “to read of Hector’s fighting or Socrates’ philosophising?” Pope Gregory the Great﻿—St. Gregory, who sent us the Roman missionaries﻿—made no bones about it at all. “*Quoniam non cognovi literaturam*,” he quoted approvingly from the 70th Psalm, “*introibo in potentias Domini*”: “Because I know nothing of literature I shall enter into the strength of the Lord.” “The praises of Christ cannot be uttered in the same tongue as those of Jove,” writes this same Gregory to Desiderius, Archbishop of Vienne, who had been rash enough to introduce some of his young men to the ancient authors, with no worse purpose than to teach them a little grammar. Yet no one was prouder than this Pope of the historical Rome which he had inherited. Alcuin, again, forbade the reading of Virgil in the monastery over which he presided: it would sully his disciples’ imagination. “How is this, *Virgilian*!” he cried out upon one taken in the damnable act﻿—“that without my knowledge and against my order thou hast taken to studying Virgil?” To put a stop to this unhallowed indulgence the clergy solemnly taught that Virgil was a wizard.

===Chinese===
至于查禁全部文学，你们会觉得更难理解；我相信，不接受我给过你们的那个解释，便根本无从理解。然而事实俱在，这是历史上的事实。「后人得了什么好处——*quid posteritas emolumenti tulit*——去读赫克托耳（Hector）的战斗或苏格拉底的哲学？」苏尔皮基乌斯·塞维鲁（Sulpicius Severus）约在公元 400 年这样写道。教皇大格里高利（Gregory the Great）——就是差遣罗马传教士到我们这里来的那位圣格里高利——则直截了当得很。他从《诗篇》第 70 篇里赞许地引来一句：「*Quoniam non cognovi literaturam*……*introibo in potentias Domini*」（因为我不识文学，我将进入主的力量）。同一位格里高利写信给维埃纳（Vienne）大主教德西德里乌斯（Desiderius）——此人一时孟浪，竟引他门下几个年轻人去读古代作家，目的无非教他们一点语法——说：「赞美基督的话，不能用赞美朱庇特的那种舌头说出。」然而，对于自己承袭的那座历史上的罗马，再没有谁比这位教皇更引以为傲。阿尔昆又在他主持的修道院里禁止阅读维吉尔：那会玷污他弟子们的想象。「这是怎么回事，维吉尔迷！」他对一个正干这桩该死勾当、被当场拿住的人吼道——「你怎敢瞒着我、违抗我的禁令，私自研读起维吉尔来？」为杜绝这种不圣洁的放纵，教士们郑重其事地教导说：维吉尔是个巫师。

===Original===
To us, long used as we are to the innocent gaieties of the Classical Tripos, these measures to discourage the study of Virgil may appear drastic, as the mental attitude of Gregory and Alcuin towards the Latin hexameter (so closely resembling that of Byron towards the waltz) not far removed from foolishness. But there you have in its quiddity the medieval mind: and the point I now put to you is, that *out of this soil our Universities grew.*

===Chinese===
在我们这些久已惯享古典学荣誉考试（Classical Tripos）里无害雅趣的人看来，这些阻抑维吉尔研习的措施未免严酷，而格里高利与阿尔昆对拉丁六音步长句的心理态度（同拜伦对华尔兹的态度何其神似）也离愚蠢不远。然而中世纪心智的精义，恰恰尽在于此；我现在向你们提出的论点便是：我们的大学，正是从这片土壤里生长出来的。

===Original===
We, who claim Oxford and Cambridge for our nursing mothers, have of all men least excuse to forget it. A man of Leyden, of Louvain, of Liepzig, of Berlin, may be pardoned that he passes it by. More than a hundred years ago Salamanca had the most of her stones torn down to make defences against Wellington’s cannon. Paris, greatest of all, has kept her renown; but you shall search the slums of the Latin Quarter in vain for the sixty or seventy Colleges that, before the close of the fifteenth century, had arisen to adorn her, the intellectual Queen of Europe. In Bologna, the ancient and stately, almost alone among the continental Universities, survive a few relics of the old collegiate system﻿—the College of Spain, harbouring some five or six students, and a little house founded for Flemings in 1650: and in Bologna the system never attained to real importance.

===Chinese===
我们奉牛津与剑桥为我们乳养之母，在所有人里最没有借口忘记这一点。莱顿（Leyden）、鲁汶（Louvain）、莱比锡、柏林的人，对它略过不问，尚可原谅。一百多年前，萨拉曼卡（Salamanca）的石料便被拆下大半，拿去修筑抵御威灵顿（Wellington）大炮的工事。诸城之中最伟大的巴黎，名声犹在；但你在拉丁区的贫民窟里穷搜苦索，也再寻不见那六七十所学院——十五世纪结束以前，它们纷纷拔地而起，装点着她这位欧洲的智慧女王。古老而庄严的博洛尼亚（Bologna），在欧陆各大学中几乎是硕果仅存，还保留着旧学院制的几件遗物——西班牙学院（College of Spain），收容着五六个学生；还有一座 1650 年为佛兰芒人设立的小宅：而在博洛尼亚，这套制度从未取得真正的重要性。

===Original===
But in England where, great as London is, the national mind has always harked to the country for the graces of life, so that we seem by instinct to see it as only desirable in a green setting, our Universities, planted by the same instinct on lawns watered by pastoral streams, have suffered so little and received as much from the years that now we can hardly conceive of Oxford or Cambridge as ruined save by “the unimaginable touch of Time.” Of all the secular Colleges bequeathed to Oxford, she has lost not one; while Cambridge (I believe) has parted only with Cavendish. Some have been subsumed into newer foundations; but always the process has been one of merging, of blending, of justifying the new bottle by the old wine. The vengeance of civil war﻿—always very much of a family affair in England﻿—has dealt tenderly with Oxford and Cambridge; the more calculating malignity of Royal Commissions not harshly on the whole. University reformers may accuse both Oxford and Cambridge of

===Chinese===
但在英格兰——任凭伦敦如何伟大，这个民族的心智总要回向乡村，去寻觅生活的种种雅致，以致我们仿佛出于本能，唯有安放在绿意之中的东西才觉得称心——我们的大学，也被同一种本能栽植在牧歌溪流浇灌的草坪上，受岁月的损毁如此之少、得的滋养又如此之多，以致今日我们几乎无从设想牛津或剑桥会倾颓破败，除非出于「时间那不可思议的一触」（the unimaginable touch of Time）。牛津承袭的世俗学院，她一所也不曾失去；剑桥（我相信）只送走了一所卡文迪什（Cavendish）。有几所被并入较新的院基；但这一过程从来都是合并、交融，都是拿旧酒来证明新瓶的正当。内战的报复——在英格兰，内战向来极像一桩家务事——对牛津与剑桥手下留情；皇家委员会（Royal Commissions）那更工于算计的恶意，总的说来也并不苛刻。大学改革家们尽可以指控牛津与剑桥

===Original===
> Annihilating all that’s made
 

 To a green thought in a green shade:

===Chinese===
> 把一切造化消归于
 

 绿荫之下绿意盈盈的一念：

===Original===
but with those sour men we have nothing here to do: like Isaak Walton’s milkmaid we will not “load our minds with any fears of many things that will never be.”

===Chinese===
但这些酸腐的人物，我们在此同他们打不上交道：我们要学艾萨克·沃尔顿（Isaak Walton）笔下的挤奶女工，不肯「用许多永远不会发生之事的恐惧，压满我们的心」。

===Original===
But, as they stand, Oxford and Cambridge﻿—so amazingly alike while they play at differences, and both so amazingly unlike anything else in the wide world﻿—do by a hundred daily reminders connect us with the Middle Age, or, if you prefer Arnold’s phrase, whisper its lost enchantments. The cloister, the grave grace in hall, the chapel bell, the men hurrying into their surplices or to lectures “with the wind in their gowns,” the staircase, the nest of chambers within the oak﻿—all these softly reverberate over our life here, as from belfries, the medieval mind.

===Chinese===
然而，就其现状而言，牛津与剑桥——一面扮演着彼此相异的游戏，一面又相似得惊人，并且同这广阔世界上的任何别物都不同得惊人——正以成百上千的日常提醒，把我们同中世纪连接起来；或者，倘若你们偏爱阿诺德（Arnold）的说法，向我们低语它那失落的迷魅。回廊，食堂里庄重的风仪，礼拜堂的钟声，匆匆披上白袍或赶去听课、「袍角兜着风」的人们，楼梯，厚橡木门内那一窝居室——这一切，都如钟楼传声一般，把中世纪的心智，轻柔地回响在我们的生活上空。

===Original===
And that medieval mind actively hated (of partial acquaintance or by anticipation) almost everything we now study! Between it and us, except these memorials, nothing survives today but the dreadful temptation to learn, the dreadful instinct in men, as they grow older and wiser, to trust learning after all and endow it﻿—that, and the confidence of a steady stream of youth.

===Chinese===
而那中世纪的心智，恰恰主动地仇恨着（凭着片面的相识，或凭着预感）我们今日研习的几乎一切！在它与我们之间，除了这些纪念物，今日尚存的只有那可怕的向学之惑、那可怕的本能——人随着年岁愈长、智慧愈增，终究还是会信任学问、资助学问——再有，便是一川源源不绝的青春所怀的信赖。

===Original===
The Universities, then, sprang out of medieval life, out of the medieval mind; and the medieval mind had for centuries been taught to abominate literature. I would not exaggerate or darken the Dark Ages for you by throwing too much bitumen into the picture. I know that at the beginning there had been a school of Origen which advocated the study of Greek poetry and philosophy, as well as the school of Tertullian which condemned it. There is evidence that the “humanities” were cultivated here and there and after a fashion behind Gregory’s august back. I grant that, while in Alcuin’s cloister (and Alcuin, remember, became a sort of Imperial Director of Studies in Charlemagne’s court) the wretched monk who loved Virgil had to study him with an illicit candle, to copy him with numbed fingers in a corner of the bitter-cold cloister, on the other hand many beautiful manuscripts preserved to us bear witness of cloisters where literature was tolerated if not officially honoured. I would not have you so uncritical as to blame the Church or its clergy for what happened; as I would have you remember that if the Church killed literature, she﻿—and, one may say, she alone﻿—kept it alive.

===Chinese===
于是，大学从中世纪的生活里、从中世纪的心智里生长出来；而那中世纪的心智，几百年来受的教导是憎恶文学。我不愿往画面里泼进太多沥青，替你们把黑暗时代涂得过分黑暗。我知道，起初既有德尔图良那一派谴责希腊诗歌与哲学的研究，也同样有过奥利金那一派为它张目。有迹象表明，「人文学科」也曾在这里那里、以这样那样的方式，在格里高利那威严的背影之后被人耕种。我承认：一边是，在阿尔昆的回廊里（别忘了，阿尔昆后来做了查理曼（Charlemagne）宫廷里某种帝国学业总监），那个爱维吉尔的可怜修士只能点着一支犯禁的蜡烛读他，在寒气砭骨的回廊一角用冻僵的手指抄他；一边是，传到我们手中的许多精美手稿，也见证着那些修道院——对文学纵未明令尊崇，至少加以容忍。我不愿你们如此不假思索，把发生的一切归罪于教会或教士；正如我不愿你们忘记：倘若教会杀死了文学，那么也正是她——而且可以说，唯独是她——使它活了下来。

===Original===
Yet, and after all these reservations, it remains true that Literature had gone down disastrously. Even philosophy, unless you count the pale work of Boethius﻿—*real* philosophy had so nearly perished that men possessed no more of Aristotle than a fragment of his Logic, and “*the* Philosopher” had to creep back into Western Europe through translations from the Arabic! But this is the point I wish to make clear.﻿—Philosophy came back in the great intellectual revival of the twelfth century; Literature did not. Literature’s hour had not come. Men had to catch up on a dreadful leeway of ignorance. The form did not matter as yet: they wanted science﻿—to know. I should say, rather, that as yet form *seemed* not to matter: for in fact form always matters: the personal always matters: and you cannot explain the vast crowds Abelard drew to Paris save by the fascination in the man, the fire communicated by the living voice. Moreover (as in a previous lecture I tried to prove) you cannot divorce accurate thought from accurate speech; but for accuracy, even for hairsplitting accuracy, of speech the Universities had the definitions of the Schoolmen. In literature they had yet to discover a concern. Literature was a thing of the past, inanimate. Nowhere in Europe could it be felt even to breathe. To borrow a beautiful phrase of Wordsworth’s, men numbered it among “things silently gone out of mind or things violently destroyed.”

===Chinese===
然而，尽管有这许多保留，文学惨遭覆没，仍是事实。连哲学——倘若你不算波爱修斯（Boethius）那部苍白的作品——真正的哲学也几乎灭绝，人们手里的亚里士多德（Aristotle），只剩他那《逻辑学》的一个残篇，「那位」大哲学家还得借着从阿拉伯文转译的本子，悄悄爬回西欧！但我要讲明白的正是这一点。——哲学在十二世纪那场伟大的智识复兴里回来了；文学没有。文学的时辰未到。人们得先去填补无知积欠下来的可怕亏空。形式在那时还无关紧要：他们要的是知识——求知。或者我该说，那时形式似乎还无关紧要：因为事实上形式永远要紧：个人性的东西永远要紧：而阿伯拉尔（Abelard）能把那样浩荡的人群吸到巴黎，除了他本人的魅力、除了活生生的嗓音所传递的火焰，你别无解释之道。况且（正如我在先前一次讲座里力图证明的），精确的思想离不开精确的语言；而论语言的精确，甚至锱铢必较的精确，大学手里有经院学者（the Schoolmen）的种种定义。至于文学，大学还须待来日才对它生出关切。文学是一件属于过去的东西，没有生气。在整个欧洲，谁也感觉不到它还有一丝呼吸。借华兹华斯（Wordsworth）一个美妙的说法，人们把它归入「悄然泯灭于心头之物，或遭暴力摧毁之物」。

===Original===
Nobody quite knows how these Universities began. Least of all can anybody tell how Oxford and Cambridge began. In Bede, for instance﻿—that is, in England as the eighth century opens﻿—we see scholarship already moving towards the *thing*, treading with sure instinct towards the light. Though a hundred historians have quoted it, I doubt if a feeling man who loves scholarship can read the famous letter of Cuthbert describing Bede’s end and not come nigh to tears.

===Chinese===
这些大学当初怎样发端，没有人真正知道。牛津与剑桥怎样发端，尤其无人能道其详。比如在比德（Bede）的著述里——也就是说，在八世纪拉开帷幕时的英格兰——我们已看到学问在朝那件事移动，踏着笃定的本能朝光走去。尽管一百位历史学家都引用过它，我仍怀疑：一个有情识、爱学问的人，读着卡思伯特（Cuthbert）记述比德临终情形的那封著名的信，能否不濒于落泪。

===Original===
And Bede’s story contains no less wonder than beauty, when you consider how the fame of this holy and humble man of heart, who never left his cloisters at Jarrow, spread over Europe, so that, though it sound incredible, our Northumbria narrowly missed in its day to become the polestar of Western culture. But he was a disinterested genius, and his pupil, Alcuin, a pushing dull man and a born reactionary; so that, while Alcuin scored the personal success and went off to teach in the court of Charlemagne, the great chance was lost.

===Chinese===
而当你想一想——这位心地圣洁谦卑、从未离开过贾罗（Jarrow）修道院的人，名声如何传遍整个欧洲，以至于（听来难以置信）我们诺森布里亚（Northumbria）在那个时代竟险些成为西方文化的北辰——比德的故事便惊奇不减于其美丽。但他是一位无所营求的天才，而他的学生阿尔昆，却是个钻营的庸人、天生的反动派；于是，阿尔昆挣得了个人的成功，跑去查理曼的宫廷执教，那伟大的机缘却失掉了。

===Original===
No one knows when the great Universities were founded, or precisely out of what schools they grew; and you may derive amusement from the historians when they start to explain how Oxford and Cambridge in particular came to be chosen for sites. My own conjecture, that they were chosen for the extraordinary salubrity of their climates, has met (I regret to say) with derision, and may be set down to the caprice of one who ever inclines to think the weather good where he is happy. Our own learned historian, indeed﻿—Mr. J. Bass Mullinger﻿—devotes some closely reasoned pages to proving that Cambridge was chosen as the unlikeliest spot in the world, and is driven to quote the learned Poggio’s opinion that the unhealthiness of a locality recommended it as a place of education for youth; as Plato, knowing naught of Christianity, but gifted with a soul naturally Christian, “had selected a noisome spot for his Academe, in order that the mind might be strengthened by the weakness of the body.” So difficult still it is for the modern mind to interpret the medieval!

===Chinese===
这些伟大学府创建于何时，又究竟从哪些学校里生长出来，无人知晓；待历史学家们着手解释牛津与剑桥——尤其是这两家——何以中选为校址，你们尽可以从他们那里获得消遣。我自己的猜想是：两地中选，全因气候异乎寻常地有益健康；这个猜想（我遗憾地说）遭到了奚落，而且尽可以归之于一种任性——持有它的人，向来倾向于认为：他在哪里幸福，哪里的天气就好。的确，我们自己那位饱学的历史学家——J. 巴斯·马林杰尔先生（Mr. J. Bass Mullinger）——用了好几页推理绵密的文字，证明剑桥之所以被选中，正因它是世上最不像样的一角；他还不得不援引博学的波焦（Poggio）的见解：一地之有害健康，恰恰使它宜于充当青年受教育的处所；正如柏拉图（Plato）——虽对基督教一无所知，却天生怀着一颗自然属于基督徒的灵魂——「为他的学园拣了一处恶浊之地，好让心灵借肉体的孱弱而得强健」。现代心智要诠释中世纪心智，至今仍是这等艰难！

===Original===
Most likely these Universities grew as a tree grows from a seed blown by chance of the wind. It seems easy enough to understand why Paris, that great city, should have possessed a great University; yet I surmise the processes at Oxford and Cambridge to have been only a little less fortuitous. The schools of Remigius and of William of Champeaux (we will say) have given Paris a certain prestige, when Abelard, a pupil of William’s, springs into fame and draws a horde of students from all over Europe to sit at his feet. These “nations” of young men have to be organised, brought under some sort of discipline, if only to make the citizens’ lives endurable: and lo! the thing is done. In like manner Irnerius at Bologna, Vacarius at Oxford, and at Cambridge some innominate teacher, “of importance,” as Browning would put it, “in his day,” possibly set the ball rolling; or again it is suggested that a body of scholars dissatisfied with Oxford (such dissatisfaction has been known even in historical times) migrated hither﻿—a laborious journey, even nowadays﻿—and that so

===Chinese===
这些大学多半像树从一粒被风偶然吹来的种子里长成。伟大的巴黎城拥有一所伟大学府，缘由似乎不难明白；但我揣测，牛津与剑桥的发端经过，也不过略少几分偶然罢了。（姑且这么说吧）雷米吉乌斯（Remigius）与香浦的威廉（William of Champeaux）的学校已给巴黎挣下几分声望，这时，威廉的门生阿伯拉尔一举成名，把欧洲各地的学生成群引到座前听讲。这一伙伙以「国族」（nations）相称的年轻人必须组织起来、纳入某种管束，哪怕只为叫市民的日子过得下去：看哪！事情便成了。同样，博洛尼亚的伊尔内留斯（Irnerius）、牛津的瓦卡里乌斯（Vacarius）、以及剑桥某位无名教师——照勃朗宁（Browning）的说法，是个「在他那个年代」颇有「分量」的人物——可能都曾把这球滚动起来；又或者，如另一种说法：一批对牛津不满的学者（这类不满，即便在有史可考的年代也曾有过）迁徙至此——即便在今天，这也是一趟劳顿的旅程——于是——

===Original===
> A brighter Hellas rears its mountains
 

 From waves serener far!

===Chinese===
> 一片更明亮的希腊正耸起它的群山，
 

 从远为安宁的波浪之间！

===Original===
These young or nascent bodies had a trick of breaking away after this fashion. For reasons no longer obvious they hankered specially towards Stamford or Northampton. Until quite recently, within living memory, all candidates for a Mastership of Arts at Oxford had to promise never to lecture at Stamford. A flood here in 1520, which swept away Garret Hostel Bridge, put Cambridge in like mind and started a prophecy (to which you may find allusion in the fourth book of *The Faerie Queene*) that both Universities would meet in the end, and kiss, at Stamford. Each in turn broke away for Northampton, and the worthy Fuller (a Northamptonshire man) has recorded his wonder that so eligible a spot was not finally chosen.

===Chinese===
这些年轻的、方兴的学人团体就有这么一个老脾气：动辄这样分家出走。为着一些如今已不甚了然的理由，他们特别倾心于斯塔姆福德（Stamford）或北安普顿（Northampton）。直到晚近——就在人们记忆所及的年月之内——牛津所有报考文学硕士学位的人，还须立誓永不在斯塔姆福德开课。1520 年的一场洪水冲走了加勒特旅舍桥（Garret Hostel Bridge），也叫剑桥起了同样的心思，还催生出一则预言（在《仙后》第四卷里你们可以寻到它的影踪）：两所大学最终将在斯塔姆福德相会、相吻。两校又先后为北安普顿出走；可敬的富勒（Fuller，北安普顿郡人）记下了他的惊异：这样一处可人意的地方，竟没有最终中选。

===Original===
I have mentioned a flood: but the immediate causes of the migrations or attempted migrations were not usually respectable enough to rank with any such act of God. They started as a rule with some Town and Gown row, or some bloody affray between scholars of the North and of the South. Without diminishing your sense of the real fervour for learning which drew young men from the remotest parts of Europe to these centres, but having for my immediate object to make clear to you that, whatever these young men sought, it was not literature, I wish you first to have in your minds a vivid picture of what a University town was like, and what its students were like during the greater part of the 12th and 13th centuries; that is to say, after the first enthusiasm had died down, when Oxford or Cambridge had organised itself into a *Studium Generale*, or *Universitas* (which, of course, has nothing to do with Universality, whether of teaching or of frequenting, but simply means a Society. *Universitas* = all of us).

===Chinese===
我提到了一场洪水：但那些迁徙或未遂迁徙的直接起因，通常不够体面，够不上同任何此类天灾相提并论。它们照例起于某一场「市民对学袍」的骚乱，或北方学者与南方学者之间某场血腥的殴斗。我不愿减损你们心中那股真实的求知热忱——正是它把欧洲最遥远角落里的年轻人引到这些中心来；但我眼下的目的，是要向你们讲清楚：这些年轻人所求的无论是什么，总不是文学。为此，我希望你们心里先有一幅鲜活的图景，看看 12 至 13 世纪的大部分时间里，一座大学城是什么模样，它的学生又是什么模样；也就是说，在最初的热情消歇之后，在牛津或剑桥已把自己组织成一个 *Studium Generale*（通学）、或 *Universitas* 的时候（此词当然同「普遍性」毫无关系——无论所授之普遍还是所聚之普遍——它的意思不过是一个社团。*Universitas*＝我们全体）。

===Original===
To begin with, the town was of wood, often on fire in places; with the alleviation of frequent winter floods, which in return, in the words of a modern poet, would “leave a lot of little things behind them.” It requires but a small effort of the imagination in Cambridge to picture the streets as narrow, dark, almost meeting overhead in gables out of which the house slops would be discharged after casual warning down into a central gutter. That these narrow streets were populous with students remains certain, however much discount we allow on contemporary bills of reckoning. And the crowd was noisy. Men have always been ingenious in their ways of celebrating academical success. Pythagoras, for example, sacrificed an ox on solving the theorem numbered forty-seven in the first book of Euclid; and even today a professor in his solitary lodge may be encouraged to believe now and then, from certain evidences in the sky, that the spirit of Pythagoras is not dead but translated.

===Chinese===
首先，城是木头城，常常这里那里地着火；幸有频繁的冬洪作为调剂，而冬洪礼尚往来，用一位现代诗人的话说，会「在身后留下许许多多的小东西」。在剑桥，只需少许想象力，便能想见当年街巷的模样：狭窄，幽暗，两侧的山墙几乎在头顶相接，家家的泔水经一声随口的招呼，便从山墙间泼下，落进街心的明沟。这些窄巷里挤满了学生，则仍属确凿——任凭我们对当年的估算账单打多大的折扣。而这人群是喧闹的。人类庆祝学业成功的方式，从来别出心裁。比如毕达哥拉斯（Pythagoras），解出欧几里得（Euclid）第一卷里编号第四十七的那条定理，便宰了一头牛献祭；甚至在今天，一位独居寓所的教授，也可以从天上的某些迹象获得鼓舞，不时相信毕达哥拉斯的精魂并未死灭，只是移居天界。

===Original===
But of the medieval University the lawlessness, though well attested, can scarcely be conceived. When in the streets “nation” drew the knife upon “nation,” “town” upon “gown”; when the city bell started to answer the clang of St. Mary’s; horrible deeds were done. I pass over massacres, tumults such as the famous one of St. Scholastica’s Day at Oxford, and choose one at a decent distance (yet entirely typical) exhumed from the annals of the University of Toulouse, in the year 1332. In that year

===Chinese===
但中世纪大学的目无法纪，虽有明文可考，却几乎无从想象。当街巷里「国族」对「国族」拔刀，「市民」对「学袍」亮刃；当市政的钟声开始应和圣马利亚教堂（St. Mary’s）的铿锵：种种骇人的行径便做出来了。屠杀、骚乱——比如牛津那场有名的圣斯科拉斯蒂卡日（St. Scholastica’s Day）之乱——我一概略过，单拣一件相隔久远的（却十足典型的）事，从图卢兹大学（University of Toulouse）的年鉴里发掘出来，事情发生在 1332 年。那一年——

===Original===
> Five brothers of the noble family de la Penne lived together in a Hospicium at Toulouse as students of the Civil and Canon Law. One of them was Provost of a Monastery, another Archdeacon of Albi, another an Archpriest, another Canon of Toledo. A bastard son of their father, named Peter, lived with them as squire to the Canon. On Easter Day, Peter, with another squire of the household named Aimery Béranger and other students, having dined at a tavern, were dancing with women, singing, shouting, and beating “metallic vessels and iron culinary instruments” in the street before their masters’ house. The Provost and the Archpriest were sympathetically watching the jovial scene from a window, until it was disturbed by the appearance of a Capitoul and his officers, who summoned some of the party to surrender the prohibited arms which they were wearing. “*Ben Senhor, non fassat*” was the impudent reply. The Capitoul attempted to arrest one of the offenders; whereupon the ecclesiastical party made a combined attack upon the official. Aimery Béranger struck him in the face with a poniard, cutting off his nose and part of his chin and lips, and knocking out or breaking no less than eleven teeth. The surgeons deposed that if he recovered (he eventually did recover) he would never be able to speak intelligibly. One of the watch was killed outright by Peter de la Penne. That night the murderer slept, just as if nothing had happened, in the house of his ecclesiastical masters. The whole household, masters and servants alike, were, however, surprised by the other Capitouls and a crowd of 200 citizens, and led off to prison, and the house is alleged to have been pillaged. The Archbishop’s Official demanded their surrender. In the case of the superior ecclesiastics this, after a short delay, was granted. But Aimery, who dressed like a layman in “divided and striped clothes” and wore a long beard, they refused to treat as a clerk, though it was afterwards alleged that the tonsure was plainly discernible upon his head until it was shaved by order of the Capitouls. Aimery was put to the torture, admitted his crime, and was sentenced to death. The sentence was carried out by hanging, after he had had his hand cut off on the scene of the crime, and been dragged by horses to the place of execution. The Capitouls were then excommunicated by the Official, and the ecclesiastical side of the quarrel was eventually transferred to the Roman Court. Before the Parlement of Paris the University complained of the violation of the Royal privilege exempting scholars’ servants from the ordinary tribunals. The Capitouls were imprisoned, and after long litigation sentenced to pay enormous damages to the ruffian’s family and erect a chapel for the good of his soul. The city was condemned for a time to the forfeiture of all its privileges. The body was cut down from the gibbet on which it had been hanging for three years, and accorded a solemn funeral. Four Capitouls bore the pall, and all fathers of families were required to walk in the procession. When they came to the Schools, the citizens solemnly begged pardon of the University, and the cortège was joined by 3,000 scholars. Finally, it cost the city 15,000 livres tournois or more to regain their civic privileges.16

===Chinese===
> 德拉佩纳（de la Penne）贵族世家的五兄弟同住在图卢兹的一所寓馆（Hospicium）里，攻读民法与教会法。五人中，一位是某修道院的院长，另一位是阿尔比（Albi）的副主教，另一位是司铎长，另一位是托莱多（Toledo）的咏礼司铎。他们父亲的一个私生子名叫彼得，随他们同住，充当那位托莱多咏礼司铎的侍从。复活节那天，彼得同家中另一名侍从埃梅里·贝朗热（Aimery Béranger）及另外几名学生，在一家酒馆用过饭，便带着女人跳舞，唱歌，叫喊，还在众位主人的宅前街头上敲打「金属器皿与铁制炊具」。修道院院长与司铎长正从一扇窗口欣然观赏这欢闹场面，直到一名卡皮托尔（Capitoul，市政长官）偕属吏登场，喝令席间数人交出身上私佩的违禁兵器，才搅了兴头。「Ben Senhor, non fassat」（好个长官，偏不交）——回答得厚颜无耻。卡皮托尔试图逮捕一名闹事者；于是教方一众合力扑向这位官员。埃梅里·贝朗热挥匕首刺中他的脸，削去他的鼻子和部分下巴与嘴唇，还打落或打断了不少于十一颗牙齿。外科医师们作证说，他纵然活命（他后来确实活了过来），也再不能口齿清楚地说话了。巡夜兵丁中有一人被彼得·德拉佩纳当场杀死。当夜，这杀人犯就在他教方主人的宅子里安睡，仿佛什么事也不曾发生。然而满宅的人，主人仆役一概在内，被其余几位卡皮托尔和 200 名市民围了个人赃并获，押赴监狱，宅子据说也遭了洗劫。大主教的代表官员要求移交人犯。对几位高级教士，短暂拖延之后照准了。但埃梅里穿得像俗人，「一身分色条纹衣裳」，又蓄着长须，他们拒绝把他当教士对待，尽管事后有人指称：他头顶的剃发圈本来看得分明，是被卡皮托尔们下令剃去的。埃梅里受了酷刑，招认罪行，被判死刑。判决以绞刑执行：行刑前先在作案现场剁去他的手，再用马拖到处刑场。随后卡皮托尔们遭代表官员处以绝罚，争端的教方一造最终移送罗马教廷。大学又在巴黎高等法院（Parlement of Paris）控告对方侵犯王家特权——该特权豁免学者仆役于普通法庭的管辖。卡皮托尔们锒铛入狱，经漫长讼诉，被判向那恶棍的家属支付巨额赔偿，并为他灵魂的益处建一座小堂。全城一度被判处褫夺全部特权。那具在绞架上已吊挂三年的尸身被解下，予以隆重安葬。四位卡皮托尔执绋，全体为人父者均须加入送葬行列。行至学堂之前，市民们向大学郑重乞恕，送葬行列又汇入了 3,000 名学者。最后，全城为赎回各项市政特权，花费了 15,000 图尔里弗（livres tournois）有余。16

===Original===
The late Mr. Cecil Rhodes once summarized all Fellows of Colleges as children in matters of finance. Be that as it may, you will find nothing more constant in history than the talent of the Universities for extracting money or money’s worth out of a riot. Time (I speak as a parent) has scarcely blunted that faculty; and still﻿—since where young men congregate, noise there must be﻿—our Universities like Wordsworth’s Happy Warrior

===Chinese===
已故的塞西尔·罗兹先生（Mr. Cecil Rhodes）曾把各学院的院士们一言以蔽之：在钱财事务上都是孩子。姑置此论不论，你会发现历史上再没有比这更恒常的东西了：大学从一场骚乱里榨出钱财或钱财等价物的天才。时间（我以家长的身份说话）几乎没有磨钝这种本领；而且——既然年轻人聚处必有喧闹——我们的大学便依旧像华兹华斯笔下「快乐的战士」那样

===Original===
> turn their necessity to glorious gain.

===Chinese===
> 把他们的必然化作光荣的收益。

===Original===
These were the excesses of young “bloods,” and their servants: but with them mingled scholars not less ferocious in their habits because almost desperately poor. You all know, I dare say, that very poor scholars would be granted licences to beg by the Chancellor. The sleeve of this gown in which I address you represents the purse or pocket of a Master of Arts, and may hint to you by its amplitude how many crusts he was prepared to receive from the charitable.

===Chinese===
这些是年轻「纨绔子」及其仆从的过火行径：但同他们混在一起的还有穷学者，其习性的凶悍并不稍减，只因穷得几乎绝望。想必你们都知道，赤贫的学者可以由校长发给乞食的执照。我此刻对你们演讲所穿的这件学袍，它的袖子便代表文学硕士的钱袋或衣袋；它那般宽大，或许正暗示诸位：他准备从善心人手里接过多少块面包皮。

---

===Original===
Now, choosing to ignore (because it has been challenged as overpainted) a picture of penury endured by the scholars of St. John’s College in this University, let me tell you two stories, one well attested, the other fiction if you will, but both agreeable as testifying to the spirit of youth which, ever blowing upon their sacred embers, has kept Oxford and Cambridge perennially alive.

===Chinese===
如今，本大学圣约翰学院（St. John’s College）学者们忍穷受饿的那幅图景，且不去管它（因为有人指摘它渲染过甚）；让我给你们讲两个故事：一个佐证确凿，另一个你们不妨当作虚构；但两个都令人愉快，因为它们共同见证了青春的精气——它不断吹拂着两校神圣的余烬，使牛津与剑桥常葆生机。

===Original===
My first is of three scholars so poor that they possessed but one “cappa” and gown between them. They took it in turns therefore, and when one went to lecture the other two kept to their lodgings. I invite you even to reflect on the joy of the lucky one, in a winter lecture room, dark, with unglazed windows, as he listened and shuffled his feet for warmth in the straw of the floor. (No one, by the way, can understand the incessant harping of our early poets upon May-time and the return of summer until he has pictured to himself the dark and cold discomfort of a Middle-English winter.) These three poor scholars fed habitually on bread, with soup and a little wine, tasting meat only on Sundays and feasts of the Church. Yet one of them, Richard of Chichester, who lived to become a saint, *saepe retulit quod nunquam in vita sua tam jucundam, tam delectabilem duxerat vitam*﻿—that never had he lived so jollily, so delectably.

===Chinese===
头一个故事讲三个穷学者，穷到三人合共只有一件「cappa」（斗篷）和一袭学袍。于是他们轮着穿：一人去听课，另外两人便守在寓所里。我甚至请你们细想那幸运儿在冬日讲堂里的快乐——讲堂昏暗，窗上没有玻璃——他一边听讲，一边在地板铺的干草里搓脚取暖。（顺带说一句：谁若不曾想见中古英语时代冬天那又黑又冷、瑟缩难熬的光景，谁就无从理解我们早期诗人为何喋喋不休地咏叹五月与夏日的归来。）这三位穷学者日常以面包果腹，佐以汤和一点酒，只有礼拜日和教会的节期才尝得着肉。然而他们中的一人——奇切斯特的理查（Richard of Chichester），后来活成了圣徒——*saepe retulit quod nunquam in vita sua tam jucundam, tam delectabilem duxerat vitam*（他常说，自己一生从没过得那样快活、那样惬意）。

===Original===
That is youth, youth blessed by friendship. Now for my second story, which is also of youth and friendship.﻿—

===Chinese===
这就是青春，得友谊之福的青春。接下来是第二个故事，讲的也是青春与友谊。——

===Original===
Two poor scholars, who had with pains become Masters of Arts and saved their pence to purchase the coveted garb, on the afternoon of their admission took a country walk in it, together flaunting their new finery. But, the day being gusty, on their return across the bridge, a puff of wind caught the *biretta* of one and blew it into the river. The loss was irrecoverable, since neither could swim. The poor fellow looked at his friend. His friend looked at him. “Between us two,” he said, “it is all or naught,” and cast his own cap to float and sink with the other down stream.

===Chinese===
两个穷学者，历尽辛苦挣得文学硕士学位，攒下一枚枚铜子买来渴慕已久的袍服；受衔那天下午，他们穿着这身行头到乡间散步，一同炫耀这崭新的体面。无奈那天风阵不定，回程过桥时，一阵风掀走其中一人的四角帽（biretta），把它吹进了河里。损失无从挽回，因为两人都不会水。那可怜人望着他的朋友。他的朋友望着他。「咱俩之间，」他说，「要么全有，要么全无。」说着，他把自己的帽子也抛了出去，让它同那一顶一道顺流而下、漂浮沉没。

===Original===
You will never begin to understand literature until you understand something of life. These young men, your forerunners, understood something of life while as yet completely careless of literature. After the impulse of Abelard and others had died down, the mass of students betook themselves to the Universities, no doubt, for quite ordinary, mercenary reasons. The University led to the Church, and the Church, in England at any rate, was the door to professional life.

===Chinese===
不懂得一点人生，你们永远谈不上开始理解文学。这些年轻人——你们的前驱——懂得一点人生，与此同时却对文学全然无心。阿伯拉尔等人的那股冲劲消歇之后，大批学生投身大学，无疑是为了十分寻常的、图谋衣食的缘故。大学通向教会，而教会——至少在英格兰——是进入职业生活的大门。

===Original===
Nearly all the civil servants of the Crown﻿—I am here quoting freely﻿—the diplomatists, the secretaries or advisers of great nobles, the physicians, the architects, at one time the secular lawgivers, all through the Middle Ages the then large tribe of ecclesiastical lawyers, were ecclesiastics.﻿ ﻿… Clerkship did not necessarily involve even minor orders. But as it was cheaper to a King or a Bishop or a temporal magnate to reward his physician, his legal adviser, his secretary, or his agent by a Canonry or a Rectory than by large salaries, the average student of Paris or Oxford or Cambridge looked toward the Church as the “main chance” as we say, and small blame to him! He never at any rate looked towards Literature: nor did the Universities, wise in their generation, encourage him to do anything of the sort.

===Chinese===
王室的文官几乎全体——我在此处是大意转述——外加外交官、大贵族的秘书与顾问、医师、建筑师、一度还包括世俗立法者，还有贯穿整个中世纪、当时人丁兴旺的那一族教会律师，都是教士。……而充任教士甚至未必需要领受哪怕低级神品。但是，对一位国王、主教或世俗显贵来说，用一个咏礼司铎席位或一份圣俸来酬谢他的医师、法律顾问、秘书或代理人，总比付大宗薪俸便宜；于是巴黎、牛津或剑桥的寻常学生，便把教会当作——照我们的说法——「最要紧的机会」，而这又何足深责！无论如何，他从不曾朝文学望上一眼：大学呢，深谙其时势，也从不鼓励他生此念头。

===Original===
You may realise, Gentlemen, how tardily, even in later and more enlightened times, the study of Literature has crept its way into official Cambridge, if you will take down your “University Calendar” and study the list of Professorships there set forth in order of foundation. It begins in 1502 with the Lady Margaret’s Chair of Divinity, founded by the mother of Henry VII Five Regius Professorships follow: of Divinity, Civil Law, Physic, Hebrew, Greek, all of 1540. So Greek comes in upon the flush of the Renaissance; and the Calendar bravely, yet not committing itself to a date, heads with Erasmus the noble roll which concludes (as may it long conclude) with Henry Jackson. But Greek comes in last of the five. Close on a hundred years elapse before the foundation of the next chair﻿—it is of Arabic; and more than a hundred before we arrive at Mathematics. So Sir William Hamilton was not without historical excuse when he declared the study of Mathematics to be no part of the business of this University! Then follow Moral Philosophy (1683), Music (1684), Chemistry (1702), Astronomy (1704), Anatomy (1707), Modern History and more Arabic, with Botany (1724), Geology (1727), closely followed by Mr. Hulse’s Christian Advocate, more Astronomy (1749), more Divinity (1777), Experimental Philosophy (1783): then in the nineteenth century more Law, more Medicine, Mineralogy, Archaeology, Political Economy, Pure Mathematics, Comparative Anatomy, Sanskrit and yet again more Law, before we arrive in 1869 at a Chair of Latin. Faint yet pursuing, we have yet to pass chairs of Fine Art (belated), Experimental Physics, Applied Mechanics, Anglo-Saxon, Animal Morphology, Surgery, Physiology, Pathology, Ecclesiastical History, Chinese, more Divinity, Mental Philosophy, Ancient History, Agriculture, Biology, Agricultural Botany, more Biology, Astrophysics, and German, before arriving in 1910 at a Chair of English Literature which by this time I have not breath to defend.

===Chinese===
诸位先生，只要取下你们的《大学一览》（University Calendar），按创设先后研究一遍那份讲席名录，便可明白：即便在后来更开明的时代，文学研究爬进官方的剑桥，也何等迟缓。名录以 1502 年的玛格丽特夫人神学讲席开篇，创设者是亨利七世（Henry VII）之母。继之是五个钦定讲席：神学、民法、医学、希伯来文、希腊文，皆设于 1540 年。于是，希腊文乘文艺复兴之盛而来；《一览》勇气可嘉地——却又不肯把话说死在哪一年——以伊拉斯谟（Erasmus）领衔这张高贵的名单，而以亨利·杰克逊（Henry Jackson）殿后（愿它长长久久地以他为殿后）。但在五席之中，希腊文忝居末位。下一个讲席创设之前，将近一百年过去了——那是阿拉伯文讲席；抵达数学，则又过了一百多年。所以，威廉·汉密尔顿爵士（Sir William Hamilton）宣称数学研究并非本校分内之事时，在历史上并非全无借口！随后是道德哲学（1683）、音乐（1684）、化学（1702）、天文学（1704）、解剖学（1707）、近代史与又一阿拉伯文讲席、植物学（1724）、地质学（1727），紧跟着赫尔斯先生的「基督教辩护者」席位、又一天文学（1749）、又一神学（1777）、实验哲学（1783）：进入十九世纪，又是法学、又是医学、矿物学、考古学、政治经济学、纯数学、比较解剖学、梵文，外加又是法学，我们才在 1869 年抵达一个拉丁文讲席。力竭犹追，我们还得越过美术（迟迟其来）、实验物理、应用力学、盎格鲁-撒克逊、动物形态学、外科、生理学、病理学、教会史、汉文、又一神学、心灵哲学、古代史、农学、生物学、农业植物学、又一生物学、天体物理，以及德文诸席，方才在 1910 年抵达英国文学讲席——讲到此处，我已没有气息替它辩护了。

===Original===
The enumeration has, I hope, been instructive. If it has also plunged you in gloom, to that atmosphere (as the clock warns me) for a fortnight I must leave you: with a promise, however, in another lecture to cheer you, if it may be, with some broken gleams of hope.

===Chinese===
这番罗列，我希望已使你们有所获益。倘若它还把你们抛进了阴郁，那么（钟声在提醒我了）我必须把你们留在那种气氛里两个星期；不过我允诺，在另一次讲座里，倘若能够，用几缕断续的希望之光替你们提一提神。

## Lecture XI English Literature in Our Universities (II) / XI 英国文学与大学（下）

===Original===
Wednesday, December 3

===Chinese===
12 月 3 日，星期三

===Original===
We broke off, Gentlemen, upon the somewhat painful conclusion that our Universities were not founded for the study of literature, and tardily admitted it. The dates of our three literary chairs in Cambridge﻿—I speak of our Western literature only, and omit Arabic, Sanskrit, and Chinese﻿—clenched that conclusion for us. Greek in 1540, Latin not until 1869, English but three years ago﻿—from the lesson of these intervals there is no getting away.

===Chinese===
诸位先生，我们上回是在一个多少令人痛心的结论上收住的：我们的大学并非为研究文学而设，而且迟迟才承认了这一点。剑桥（Cambridge）三座文学讲席的设立年代——我只谈我们的西方文学，阿拉伯文、梵文、中文一概略去——替我们把这个结论敲得死死的。希腊文在 1540 年，拉丁文迟至 1869 年，英文不过三年之前——这些间隔所给的教训，是躲不开的。

===Original===
Now I do not propose to dwell on the Renaissance and how Greek came in: for a number of writers in our time have been busy with the Renaissance, and have﻿—I was going to say “overwritten the subject,” but no﻿—it is better to say that they have focused the period so as to distort the general perspective at the cost of other periods which have earned less attention; the twelfth century, for example. At any rate their efforts, with the amount they claim of your reading, absolve me from doing more than remind you that the Renaissance brought in the study of Greek, and Greek necessarily brought in the study of literature: since no man can read what the Greeks wrote and not have his eyes unsealed to what I have called a norm of human expression; a guide to conduct, a standard to correct our efforts, whether in poetry, or in philosophy, or in art. For the rest, I need only quote to you Gibbon’s magnificent saying, that the Greek language gave a soul to the objects of sense and a body to the abstractions of metaphysics. (May I add, in parenthesis, that, while no believer in compulsory Greek, holding, indeed, that you can hardly reconcile learning with compulsion, and still more hardly force them to be compatibles, I subscribe with all my heart to Bagehot’s shrewd saying, “while a knowledge of Greek and Latin is not necessary to a writer of English, he should at least have a firm conviction that those two languages existed.”)

===Chinese===
眼下我不打算细谈文艺复兴（Renaissance），也不细谈希腊文是如何传进来的：因为今世已有不少作家在文艺复兴上大做文章，他们——我本想说「把这个题目写滥了」，不过还是不说为好——更妥当的说法是：他们把这段时期对到焦点之上，以致歪曲了整体的透视，牺牲了其他较少受人注意、却也当得起注意的时代；比如十二世纪。无论如何，他们的那些劳作，连同它们要求占去的你们那一份阅读，已足以解除我的义务，使我只须提醒你们：文艺复兴带来了希腊文研究，而希腊文必然带来文学研究——因为没有人读了希腊人的著作，还能不睁开眼睛，看见我所谓的人类表达的规范；它是指引行为的向导，是校正我们努力的标准，无论在诗歌里、在哲学里，还是在艺术里。至于其余，我只需向你们引吉本（Gibbon）那句宏丽的名言：希腊语赋予感官的对象以灵魂，赋予形而上学的抽象以肉身。（请容我插一句题外话：我虽不信强制希腊文那一套——我确实认为，学习与强制几乎无法调和，更难强使二者并行不悖——我却全心全意赞同白芝浩（Bagehot）那句精明的话：「懂希腊文和拉丁文，对一个英语写作者并非必要；但他至少应当坚定地相信，这两种语言存在过。」）

===Original===
But, assuming you to know something of the Renaissance, and how it brought Greek into Oxford and Cambridge, I find that in the course of the argument two things fall to be said, and both to be said with some emphasis.

===Chinese===
但是，姑且假定你们对文艺复兴、对它如何把希腊文带进牛津（Oxford）与剑桥已略有了解，我发现在论证的进程中，有两件事当说，而且两件都须说得着力。

===Original===
In the first place, without officially acknowledging their native tongue or its literature, our two Universities had no sooner acquired Greek than their members became immensely interested in English. Take, for one witness out of many, Gabriel Harvey, Fellow of Pembroke Hall. His letters to Edmund Spenser have been preserved, as you know. Now Gabriel Harvey was a man whom few will praise, and very few could have loved. Few will quarrel with Dr. Courthope’s description of him as “a person of considerable intellectual force, but intolerably arrogant and conceited, and with a taste vitiated by all the affectations of Italian humanism,” or deny that “his tone in his published correspondence with Spenser is that of an intellectual bully.”17 None will refuse him the title of fool for attempting to mislead Spenser into writing hexameters. But all you can urge against Gabriel Harvey, on this count or that or the other, but accumulates proof that this donnish man was all the while giving thought﻿—giving even ferocious thought﻿—to the business of making an English Literature.

===Chinese===
第一，我们的两所大学虽从未正式承认自己的母语或其文学，却是一得到希腊文，其成员便立刻对英文生出了莫大的兴趣。且从众多证人中举出一位：彭布罗克堂（Pembroke Hall）研究员加布里埃尔·哈维（Gabriel Harvey）。如你们所知，他致埃德蒙·斯宾塞（Edmund Spenser）的书信留存了下来。这位加布里埃尔·哈维，赞得出口的人寥寥，爱得起来的人更寥寥。库思罗普博士（Dr. Courthope）形容他是「一个智力相当可观的人，却傲慢自负得令人无法容忍，趣味又被意大利人文主义的种种矫饰败坏无余」，又说「他在与斯宾塞公开刊布的通信中，口吻是一副恃智凌人的霸王腔」17——对这两句评语，怕没有几个人会来争辩。他企图诱使斯宾塞去写六音步诗行，单凭这一桩，谁也不会拒绝授予他「傻瓜」的徽号。然而，你们无论从这一条、那一条还是另一条罪状上加诸加布里埃尔·哈维，指摘来指摘去，无非是在一层层累积证据，证明这个学究气的人物自始至终都在为一桩大事运思——为缔造一门英国文学，而且运的是凶猛的思虑。

===Original===
Let me adduce more pleasing evidence. At or about Christmas, in the year 1597, there was enacted here in Cambridge, in the hall of St. John’s College, a play called *The Pilgrimage to Parnassus*, a skittish work, having for subject the “discontent of scholars”; the misery attending those who, unsupported by a private purse, would follow after Apollo and the Nine. No one knows the author’s name: but he had a wit which has kept something of its salt to this day, and in Christmas, 1597, it took Cambridge by storm. The public demanded a sequel, and *The Return from Parnassus* made its appearance on the following Christmas (again in St. John’s College hall); to be followed by a *Second Part of the Return from Parnassus*, the author’s overflow of wit, three years later. Of the popularity of the first and second plays﻿—*The Pilgrimage* and *The Return, Part I*﻿—we have good evidence in the prologue to *The Return, Part II*, where the author makes Momus say, before an audience which knew the truth:

===Chinese===
让我举一件更叫人愉快的证据。1597 年圣诞节前后，就在剑桥此地，圣约翰学院（St. John's College）的大厅里上演了一出名为《帕纳索斯朝圣记》（The Pilgrimage to Parnassus）的戏，一部轻佻跳脱的戏文，主题是「学子的不满」：写那些没有私人钱袋作后盾、偏要追随阿波罗（Apollo）与九位缪斯的人所遭的罪。作者姓名已无人知晓：但他有一种机锋，至今还留着几分咸味，而在 1597 年圣诞节，它曾风靡整个剑桥。公众要求续篇，于是次年圣诞节《帕纳索斯归来》（The Return from Parnassus）问世（仍在圣约翰学院大厅演出）；三年之后，又有了《帕纳索斯归来第二部》（Second Part of the Return from Parnassus），那是作者才思的漫溢。前两出戏——《朝圣记》与《归来·第一部》——如何叫座，我们在《归来·第二部》的开场白里有很好的证据：作者让莫穆斯（Momus）在一班深知底细的观众面前说道：

===Original===
> *The Pilgrimage to Parnassus* and *The Returne from Parnassus* have stood the honest Stagekeepers in many a crowne’s expense for linckes and vizards: purchased many a Sophister a knocke with a clubbe: hindred the butler’s box, and emptied the Colledge barrells; and now, unlesse you have heard the former, you may returne home as wise as you came: for this last is the last part of *The Returne from Parnassus*; that is, the last time that the Author’s wit will turne upon the toe in this vaine.

===Chinese===
> 《帕纳索斯朝圣记》与《帕纳索斯归来》害得老实的舞台看守们在火把与假面上破费了不止一顶克朗：让不少学子换了一记棍棒：耽误了司酒的捐款箱，喝空了学院的大酒桶；如今，除非你们已听过前两部，否则你们尽可怎么来的怎么回去，同来时一般「聪明」：因为这是《帕纳索斯归来》的末部；也就是说，作者的才思再要踮起脚尖、循着这个路数旋转，这是最后一回了。

===Original===
In other words, these plays had set everybody in Cambridge agog, had been acted by link-light, had led to brawls﻿—either between literary factions or through offensive personal allusions to which we have lost all clue﻿—had swept into the box-office much money usually spent on Christmas gambling, and had set up an inappeasable thirst for College ale. The point for us is that (in 1597﻿–﻿1601) they abound in topical allusions to the London theatres: that Shakespeare is obviously just as much a concern to these young men of Cambridge as Mr. Shaw (say) is to our young men today, and an allusion to him is dropped in confidence that it will be aptly taken. For instance, one of the characters, Gullio, will have some love-verses recited to him “in two or three diverse veins, in Chaucer’s, Gower’s and Spenser’s and Mr. Shakespeare’s.” Having listened to Chaucer, he cries, “Tush! Chaucer is a foole”; but coming to some lines of Mr. Shakespeare’s “Venus and Adonis,” he cries, “Ey, marry, Sir! these have some life in them! Let this duncified world esteeme of Spenser and Chaucer, I’le worship sweet Mr. Shakespeare, and to honoure him I will lay his ‘Venus and Adonis’ under my pillowe.” For another allusion﻿—“Few of the University pen plaies well,” says the actor Kempe in Part II of the *Returne*; “they smell too much of that writer *Ovid* and that writer *Metamorphosis*, and talke too much of Proserpina and Jupiter. Why here’s our fellow *Shakespeare* puts them all downe, ay and *Ben Jonson*, too.” Here you have Cambridge assembling at Christmas-tide to laugh at well-understood hits upon the theatrical taste of London. Here you have, to make Cambridge laugh, three farcical quasi-Aristophanic plays all hinging on the tribulations of scholars who depart to pursue literature for a livelihood. For a piece of definite corroborative evidence you have a statute of Queens’ College (quoted by Mr. Bass Mullinger) which directs that “any student refusing to take part in the acting of a comedy or tragedy in the College and absenting himself from the performance, contrary to the injunctions of the President, shall be expelled from the Society”﻿—which seems drastic. And on top of all this, you have evidence enough and to spare of the part played in Elizabethan drama by the “University Wits.” Why, Marlowe (of Corpus Christi) may be held to have invented its form﻿—blank verse; Ben Jonson (of St. John’s) to have carried it on past its meridian and through its decline, into the masque. Both Universities claim Lyly and Chapman. Marston, Peel, Massinger, hailed from Oxford. But Greene and Nashe were of Cambridge﻿—of St. John’s both, and Day of Caius. They sought to London, and there (for tragic truth underlay that Christmas comedy of *The Pilgrimage of Parnassus*) many of them came to bitter ends: but before reaching their sordid personal ruin﻿—and let the deaths of Marlowe and Greene be remembered﻿—they built the Elizabethan drama, as some of them lived to add its last ornaments. We know what, meanwhile, Spenser had done. I think it scarcely needs further proof that Cambridge, towards the end of the sixteenth century, was fermenting with a desire to read, criticise, yes and write, English literature, albeit officially the University recognised no such thing.

===Chinese===
换句话说，这几出戏曾使剑桥上下为之轰动，曾在火把光下开演，曾引出斗殴——或是文学宗派之间的斗殴，或是因为那些无礼的人身影射而起，那些影射的所指，我们如今已无线索可寻——曾把往常流向圣诞节赌桌的大笔钱财卷进票房，还催生出一种对学院麦酒餍足不了的渴。与我们相干的要点是：（在 1597 至 1601 年间）这些戏里满是针对伦敦剧院的时新影射：莎士比亚（Shakespeare）之于这些剑桥青年，显然正如（比如说）萧伯纳先生（Mr. Shaw）之于我们今天的青年，是一样挂在心上的关切；提到他处，作者放胆落笔，笃信听者自会恰切领会。譬如，剧中一个人物古利奥（Gullio），要人给他诵读情诗，「用两三种不同的风味，用乔叟（Chaucer）的、高厄（Gower）的、斯宾塞的和莎士比亚先生的风味」。听罢乔叟，他嚷道：「呸！乔叟是个蠢货」；待听到莎士比亚先生《维纳斯与阿多尼》（Venus and Adonis）中的几行，他嚷道：「哎，对啦，先生！这才有些生气！任凭这蠢化了的世界去推崇斯宾塞与乔叟吧，我要崇拜亲爱的莎士比亚先生，为表敬意，我要把他的《维纳斯与阿多尼》搁在枕头底下。」再举一例影射——演员坎普（Kempe）在《归来》第二部里说道：「大学里能把戏写好的没有几个：他们身上那股子作家奥维德（Ovid）、那部作家《变形记》（Metamorphosis）的气味太重，普洛塞庇娜（Proserpina）与朱庇特（Jupiter）又谈得太多。嘿，咱们这位伙伴莎士比亚把他们统统压倒，对了，还有本·琼森（Ben Jonson）也是。」你们看，这是剑桥人在圣诞节聚拢来，笑赏那些人人会意的、对伦敦戏剧趣味的抨击。你们看，为博剑桥一笑，三出闹剧风的准阿里斯托芬式（quasi-Aristophanic）戏文，全系在学子们出门以文学谋生所历的磨难上。要一件确凿的旁证，还有王后学院（Queens' College）的一条院规（巴斯·马林杰先生〔Bass Mullinger〕曾予征引）：「凡学生拒绝参加学院内喜剧或悲剧之演出，并抗命缺席观剧者，应即逐出本社」——这看来未免严苛。而在这之上，你们还有绰乎有余的证据，可证「大学才子」（University Wits）在伊丽莎白时代戏剧中所扮演的角色。可不是么：马洛（Marlowe，出身基督圣体学院）尽可算是发明了它的形式——素体诗；本·琼森（出身圣约翰学院）则携它越过中天、走过衰落，直送入假面剧。两所大学都认领黎里（Lyly）与查普曼（Chapman）。马斯顿（Marston）、皮尔（Peel）、马辛格（Massinger）出身牛津。而格林（Greene）与纳什（Nashe）是剑桥人——两人同出圣约翰学院，戴（Day）则出自凯厄斯学院（Caius）。他们奔赴伦敦，在那儿（因为那出《帕纳索斯朝圣记》圣诞喜剧底下正伏着悲剧的真情）他们中不少人落得凄惨的结局：但在抵达各自不光彩的个人覆灭之前——且让马洛与格林之死被记取——他们缔造了伊丽莎白时代的戏剧，其中一些人还活到后来，为它添上最后的藻饰。与此同时斯宾塞成就了什么，我们业已知道。我想，几乎无须更多的证明：十六世纪末的剑桥，正发酵着一股要读英国文学、要批评英国文学、是的还要写英国文学的欲望——尽管在官方，大学对此从无承认。

===Original===
There remains a second question﻿—How happened it that Cambridge, after admitting Greek, took more than three hundred years to establish a Chair of Latin, and that a Chair of English is, so to speak, a mushroom (call it not toadstool!) of yesterday? Why simply enough. Latin continued to be the working language of Science. In Latin Bacon naturally composed his *Novum Organum* and indeed almost all his scientific and philosophical work, although a central figure of his age among English prose-writers. In Latin, in the eighteenth century, Newton wrote his *Principia*: and I suppose that of no two books written by Englishmen before the close of that century, or indeed before Darwin’s *Origin of Species*, can it be less extravagantly said than of the *Novum Organum* and the *Principia* that they shook the world. Now, without forgetting our Classical Tripos (founded in 1822), as without forgetting the great names of Bentley and Porson, we may observe it as generally true, that whenever and wherever large numbers of scientific men use a particular language as their working instrument, they have a disposition to look askance on its refinements; to be jealous of its literary professors; to accuse these of treating as an end in itself what is properly a means. Like the Denver editor I quoted to you in a previous lecture, these scientific workers want to “get there” in a hurry, forgetting that (to use another Americanism) the sharper the chisel the more ice it is likely to cut. You may observe this disposition﻿—this suspicion of “literature,” this thinly veiled contempt﻿—in many a scientific man today; though because his language has changed from Latin to English, it is English he now chooses to cheapen. Well, we cannot help it, perhaps. Perhaps he cannot help it. It is human nature. We must go on persuading him, not losing our tempers.

===Chinese===
还剩第二个问题——剑桥既已接纳希腊文，何以用了三百多年才设立拉丁文讲席，而英文讲席，打个比方，不过是昨天才冒头的一朵蘑菇（可别叫它毒菌！）？为什么呢，说来简单。拉丁文一直是科学的工作语言。培根（Bacon）用拉丁文写他的《新工具》（Novum Organum），而且几乎全部科学与哲学著述皆用拉丁文，尽管他在同时代的英语散文作家中间是一位中枢人物。十八世纪，牛顿（Newton）用拉丁文写成他的《原理》（Principia）：而且我想，在英国人于该世纪结束之前、乃至达尔文（Darwin）《物种起源》（Origin of Species）问世之前所写的任何两部书之中，「它们震动了世界」这句话，说得最不夸张的，莫过于用在《新工具》和《原理》上了。再者，我们既不忘我们的古典学荣誉考试（Classical Tripos，创设于 1822 年），也不忘本特利（Bentley）与波尔森（Porson）的赫赫大名，却仍可以把下面的话当作通则来观察：每当何时何地，有大群科学工作者把某一种语言用作工作工具，他们便倾向于斜眼看待这语言的精微之处，忌妒它的文学教授，指摘他们把本该是工具的东西奉为了目的本身。正如我在先前一次讲座里向你们引述过的那位丹佛主编，这些科学工作者急着要「到达那里」，忘了（再借用一句美国腔）凿子越锋利，可能切下的冰越多。这种性向——这种对「文学」的疑忌，这层薄纱半掩的轻蔑——你们在今天许多科学工作者身上都见得到；只不过，他的语言既已由拉丁文改换为英文，如今他挑出来作践的，也就成了英文。唉，这或许无可奈何。或许他也无可奈何。这原是人性。我们只有继续说服他，而不动气。

===Original===
None the less we should not shut our eyes to the fact that while a language is the working instrument of scientific men there will always be a number of them to decry any study of it for its beauty, and even any study of it for the sake of accuracy﻿—its beauty and its accuracy being indeed scarcely distinguishable.

===Chinese===
尽管如此，我们不该闭眼不看这个事实：只要一种语言还是科学工作者的工作工具，就总会有若干人站出来，贬斥一切为它的美而作的研究，甚至贬斥一切为准确而作的研究——须知它的美与它的准确，实在难分彼此。

===Original===
I fear, Gentlemen, you may go on from this to the dreadful conclusion that the date 1869, when Cambridge at length came to possess a Chair of Latin, marks definitely the hour at which Latin closed its eyes and became a dead language; that you may proceed to a yet more dreadful application of this to the Chair of English founded in 1910: and that henceforward (to misquote what Mr. Max Beerbohm once wickedly said of Walter Pater) you will be apt to regard Professor Housman and me as two widowers engaged, while the undertaker waits, in composing the features of our belovèds.

===Chinese===
诸位先生，我怕你们会由此再进一步，得出那个可怕的结论：1869 年——剑桥终于有了拉丁文讲席的那一年——恰恰标明了拉丁文阖上双眼、沦为死语的时刻；我怕你们还会把这一点更可怕地应用到 1910 年设立的英文讲席上：从此（容我窜改一下马克斯·比尔博姆先生（Max Beerbohm）当年刻薄地评沃尔特·佩特（Walter Pater）的那句话）你们动辄便要把豪斯曼教授（Professor Housman）和我看作两个鳏夫，趁殡仪人还在一旁候着，正为我们心爱的人儿整理遗容。

===Original===
But (to speak seriously) that is what I stand here to controvert: and I derive no small encouragement when﻿—as has more than once happened﻿—A, a scientific man, comes to me and complains that he for his part cannot understand B, another scientific man, “because the fellow can’t express himself.” And the need to study precision in writing has grown far more instant since men of science have abandoned the “universal language” and taken to writing in their own tongues. Let us, while not on the whole regretting the change, at least recognise some dangers, some possible disadvantages. I will confine myself to English, considered as a substitute for Latin. In Latin you have a language which may be thin in its vocabulary and inelastic for modern use; but a language which at all events compels a man to clear his thought and communicate it to other men precisely.

===Chinese===
但是（说正经的），我站在这里要驳斥的正是这一点：而我不无鼓舞地看到——这类情形已不止一回——科学家甲来找我，抱怨说他怎么也弄不懂科学家乙，「因为那家伙没法表达自己」。自从科学工作者放弃了那门「通用语言」、改用各自的语言写作以来，研习写作之精确的必要，已变得远为迫切。这一变化，我们总体上并不惋惜，但至少让我们承认其中有些危险、有些可能的弊病。我只就英文立论，把它看作拉丁文的替代者。拉丁文这门语言，词汇或许单薄，用于现代或许僵硬不灵；但它无论如何迫令人把思想理清，并把思想精确地传达给他人。

===Original===
> Thoughts hardly to be packed
 

 Into the narrow act

===Chinese===
> 思绪几乎难以打包
> 塞进狭窄的行动

===Original===
—may be all impossible of compression into the Latin speech. In English, on the other hand, you have a language which by its very copiousness and elasticity tempts you to believe that you can do without packing, without compression, arrangement, order; that, with the Denver editor, all you need is to “get there”﻿—though it be with all your intellectual belongings in a jumble, overflowing the portmanteau. Rather I preach to you that having proudly inherited English with its *copia fandi*, you should keep your estate in order by constantly applying to it that *jus et norma loquendi* of which, if you seek to the great models, you will likewise find yourselves inheritors.

===Chinese===
——这样的思绪，或许全然无法压缩进拉丁语。另一方面，英语这门语言，恰以其繁富与弹性诱使你相信：打包可以免了，压缩、安排、条理也都可以免了；信那位丹佛主编的话，你只消「到达那里」——哪怕你全部的心智行李乱作一团，正从旅行箱里漫溢出来。我倒要向你们宣讲的是：你们既骄傲地承继了英语连同它的 *copia fandi*（言辞的繁富），就当不断把 *jus et norma loquendi*（言说的法度与规范）施用于这份产业，使家业井然有序；而这份法度与规范，只要你们向伟大的典范去求索，便会发现自己同样是它的承继人。

===Original===
“But,” it is sometimes urged, “why not leave this new study of English to the younger Universities now being set up all over the country?” “Ours is an age of specialising. Let these newcomers have something﻿—what better than English?﻿—to specialise upon.”

===Chinese===
「可是，」有时有人这样进言，「为什么不把英文这门新研究留给如今正开办于全国的那些年轻大学呢？」「我们这是个讲专门化的时代。总得让这些新来者有点东西好专门研究——还有什么比英文更好的呢？」

===Original===
I might respond by asking if the fame of Cambridge would stand where it stands today had she followed a like counsel concerning other studies and, resting upon Mathematics, given over this or that branch of Natural Science to be grasped by new hands. What of Electricity, for example? Or what of Physiology? Yes, and among the unnatural sciences, what of Political Economy? But I will use a more philosophical argument.

===Chinese===
我满可以反问一句：倘若当年剑桥对其他学科也依了类似的主意，安枕于数学之上，把自然科学的这一门或那一门拱手交给新人去把握，她今日的声名还能立在如今这个位置上么？试问电学（Electricity）怎么说？生理学（Physiology）又怎么说？对了，在各门「不自然」的科学里，政治经济学（Political Economy）又怎么说？不过，我更愿用一个较为哲学的论证。

===Original===
Some years ago I happened on a collection of Bulgarian proverbs of which my memory retains but two, yet each an abiding joy. In a lecture on English Literature in our Universities you will certainly not miss to apply the first, which runs, “Many an ass has entered Jerusalem.”

===Chinese===
数年前我偶然碰到一部保加利亚谚语集，其中我的记忆只留住了两条，但每一条都是一份长久的快乐。在一场论我们大学里英国文学的讲座上，你们笃定少不了要用上第一条，它说的是：「多少头驴子都进过耶路撒冷（Jerusalem）。」

===Original===
The application of the second may elude you for a moment. It voices the impatience of an honest Bulgar who has been worried overmuch to subscribe to what, in this England of ours, we call Church Purposes; and it runs, “All these twopenny saints will be the ruin of the Church.”

===Chinese===
第二条的用处，你们或许一时捉摸不着。它道出一位老实保加利亚人的不耐烦：他被人缠得太狠，非要他为那种款项认捐不可——在我们这个英格兰，人们管它叫「教会事业」（Church Purposes）；这条谚语说：「这帮两便士的圣徒，要把教会给毁了。」

===Original===
Now far be it from me to apply the term “twopenny saint” to any existing University. To avoid the accusation I hereby solemnly declare my deep conviction that every single University at this moment in England, Scotland, Wales or Ireland has reasons﻿—strong in all, in some overwhelmingly strong﻿—for its existence. That is plainly said, I hope? Yet I do maintain that if we go on multiplying Universities we shall not increase the joy; that the reign of twopenny saints lies not far off and will soon lie within measurable distance; and that it will be a pestilent reign. As we saw in our last lecture the word “University”﻿—*Universitas*﻿—had, in its origin, nothing to do with Universality: it meant no more than a Society, organised (as it happened) to promote learning. But words, like institutions, often rise above their beginnings, and in time acquire a proud secondary connotation. For an instance let me give you the beautiful Wykehamist motto “Manners Makyeth Man,” wherein “manners” originally meant no more than “morals.” So there has grown around our two great Universities of Oxford and Cambridge a connotation (secondary, if you will, but valuable above price) of universality; of standing like great beacons of light, to attract the young wings of all who would seek learning for their sustenance. Thousands have singed, thousands have burned themselves, no doubt: but what thousands of thousands have caught the sacred fire into their souls as they passed through and passed out, to carry it, to drop it, still as from wings, upon waste places of the world! Think of country vicarages, of Australian or Himalayan outposts, where men have nourished out lives of duty upon the fire of three transient, priceless years. Think of the generations of children to whom their fathers’ lives, prosaic enough, could always be re-illumined if someone let fall the word “Oxford” or “Cambridge,” so that they themselves came to surmise an aura about the name as of a land very far off; and then say if the ineffable spell of those two words do not lie somewhere in the conflux of generous youth with its rivalries and clash of minds, ere it disperses, generation after generation, to the duller business of life. Would you have your mother University, Gentlemen, undecorated by some true study of your mother-English?

===Chinese===
我当然绝无把「两便士圣徒」这个字眼扣到任何一所现存大学头上的意思。为了避免这项指控，我在此郑重声明我的深信：当今英格兰、苏格兰、威尔士或爱尔兰的每一所大学，其存在都有理由——所所充分，其中一些更是强得压倒一切。这话我说得明白了吧，我希望？然而我确实主张：倘若我们继续成倍增设大学，我们并不会增添快乐；两便士圣徒的治世已在不远处，不久便将进入可测的距离之内；而那将是一场瘟疫般的治世。正如上次讲座所见，「大学」（University）一词——*Universitas*——在其起源上与「普遍性」（Universality）毫无干系：它不过意指一个社团，（碰巧）为促进学问而组织起来罢了。然而词语也如制度，每每超拔于自身的开端，久而久之获得一种可贵的引申义。举一个例子：威克姆派（Wykehamist）那条优美的箴言「Manners Makyeth Man」（举止造就人），其中「manners」一词原初也不过意指「德行」（morals）。就这样，在我们牛津、剑桥两所伟大学府的周围，生长出一种普遍性的涵义（你尽可称之为引申义，但其价值贵逾任何珍宝）：它们如巨大的灯塔矗立着，吸引一切以学问为滋养的幼翼前来。被燎灼的以千计，被焚毁的也以千计，无疑：但千千万万的人在穿堂而过、振翅而出之际，把那圣火迎进了自己的灵魂，携着它，撒落它——依旧如同从翼上撒落——撒向世界的荒僻之地！想想那些乡村牧师寓所，那些澳洲或喜马拉雅山麓的前哨站：在那里，人们凭着三载短暂而无价的时光所燃之火，把尽忠职守的一生滋养起来。想想一代代的孩子：他们父亲的人生本已够平淡，却只要有人漏出「牛津」或「剑桥」二字，便总能重新被照亮，以致孩子们自己也渐渐疑心那名字四周笼着光晕，仿佛一片极遥远的国土；然后请你们说说：这两个字那难以言喻的魔力，是否就藏在慷慨的青春——连同其竞胜与心智交锋——的汇流之中，在它一代又一代散向生活那较为沉闷的事务之前？诸位先生，你们可愿意自己的母校，缺少一份对你们母语英文的真正研究作装点吗？

===Original===
I think not, having been there, and known such thoughts as you will carry away, and having been against expectation called back to report them.

===Chinese===
我看是不愿意的：我自己曾在那里，也体认过你们行将带走的那些念头，而且出乎意料地被召了回来，向你们讲述它们。

===Original===
> And sometimes I remember days of old
 

 When fellowship seem’d not so far to seek,
 

 And all the world and I seem’d far less cold,
 

 And at the rainbow’s foot lay surely gold,
 

 And hope was strong, and life itself not weak.

===Chinese===
> 而我有时忆起旧日的时光，
> 那时情谊似乎并不难寻，
> 而整个世界和我似乎远没有那么冷漠，
> 而彩虹脚下必定埋着黄金，
> 而希望正强，而生命本身也不羸弱。

===Original===
My purpose here (and I cannot too often recur to it) is to wean your minds from hankering after false Germanic standards and persuade you, or at least point out to you, in what direction that true study lies if you are men enough to take up your inheritance and believe in it as a glory to be improved.

===Chinese===
我在此的目的（这一点我反复重提也不嫌多），是把你们的心思从艳羡虚假的日耳曼标准上引开，并说服你们——至少向你们指出——那真正的研究所在的方位，只要你们有足够的男子气概，去承接自己的产业，并相信它是一份可以愈加光大的荣耀。

===Original===
Neither Oxford nor Cambridge nor any University on earth can study English Literature truthfully or worthily, or even at all profitably, unless by studying it in the category for which Heaven, or Nature (call the ultimate cause what you will), intended it; or, to put the assertion more concretely, in any other category than that for which the particular author﻿—be his name Chaucer or Chesterton, Shakespeare or Shaw﻿—designed it; as neither can Oxford nor Cambridge nor any University study English Literature, to understand it, unless by bracing itself to consider a living art. Origins, roots, all the gropings towards light﻿—let these be granted as accessories; let those who search in them be granted all honour, all respect. It is only when they preach or teach these preliminaries, these accessories, to be more important than Literature itself﻿—it is only when they, owing all their excuse in life to the established daylight, din upon us that the precedent darkness claims precedence in honour, that one is driven to utter upon them this dialogue, in monosyllables:

===Chinese===
牛津也罢，剑桥也罢，世上任何一所大学，都无法真实而相称地研究英国文学，甚至无法研究得稍有裨益，除非把它放在上天、或自然（终极原因随你怎么称呼）为它意定的那个范畴里来研究；或者把这断言说得更具体些：除了个别作者为它设计的那个范畴，不能放进任何别的范畴——不管他名叫乔叟还是切斯特顿（Chesterton），莎士比亚还是萧伯纳；同样，牛津也罢，剑桥也罢，世上任何一所大学，要研究英国文学以求理解，也无法不振奋起来，把它当作一门活的艺术来观照。起源、根由、一切朝向光明的摸索——这些尽可许为附庸；在其中探求的人尽可享有一切敬意、一切尊重。只是当他们宣讲或传授这些初步、这些附庸，说它们比文学本身还重要时——只是当他们，毕生立足的借口全拜既已确立的白昼所赐，却向我们聒噪不休，说先前的黑暗在荣誉上理当居先——人才不得不对他们道出这段对话，用的全是单音节词：

===Original===
> And God said, “Let there be light”: and there was light.
> “Oh, thank you, Sir,” said the Bat and the Owl; “then we are off!”

===Chinese===
> 上帝说：「要有光」：就有了光。
> 「哦，谢谢您，先生，」蝙蝠和猫头鹰说；「那么我们就此告辞了！」

===Original===
I grant you, Gentlemen, that there must always inhere a difficulty in correlating for the purposes of a Tripos a study of Literature itself with a study of these accessories; the thing itself being *naturally* so much more difficult: being so difficult indeed that (to take literary criticism alone and leave for a moment the actual practice of writing out of the question) though some of the first intellects in the world﻿—Aristotle, Longinus, Quintilian, Boileau, Dryden, Lessing, Goethe, Coleridge, Sainte-Beuve﻿—have broken into parcels of that territory, the mass of it remains unexplored, and nobody as yet has found courage to reduce the reports of these great explorers to any system; so that a very eminent person indeed found it easy to write to me the other day, “The principles of Criticism? What are they? Who made them?” To this I could only answer that I did not know Who made them; but that Aristotle, Dryden, Lessing, had, as it was credibly reported, discovered five or, it might be, six. And this difficulty of appraising literature absolutely inheres in your study of it from the beginning. No one can have set a General Paper on Literature and examined on it, setting it and marking the written answers, alongside of papers about language, inflections and the rest, without having borne in upon him that *here* the student finds his difficulty.
