While in a paper set about inflections, etc., a pupil with a moderately retentive memory will easily obtain sixty or seventy percent of the total marks, in a paper on the book or play considered critically an examiner, even after setting his paper with a view to some certain inferiority of average, has to be lenient before he can award fifty, forty, or even thirty percent of the total.

===Chinese===
我向你们承认，诸位先生：为荣誉考试（Tripos）之用，把文学本身的研究同这些附庸之研究相互配置，永远内在着一种困难；因为这桩事情本身，按本性就难得多：确实难到这般地步，以致（姑且单取文学批评一项，暂把写作的实际操练撇开不问）尽管世上一些第一流的智士——亚里士多德（Aristotle）、朗吉努斯（Longinus）、昆体良（Quintilian）、布瓦洛（Boileau）、德莱顿（Dryden）、莱辛（Lessing）、歌德（Goethe）、柯尔律治（Coleridge）、圣伯夫（Sainte-Beuve）——曾把那片疆域剖开成若干属地，其主体至今仍未勘探，而且迄今无人鼓起勇气，把这些伟大探险家的报告归纳成任何体系；于是前些天，一位着实显赫的人物便觉得不难写信问我：「批评的原则？它们是什么？谁定的？」对此我只能回答：是谁定的，我不知道；不过据可信的传闻，亚里士多德、德莱顿、莱辛已经发现了五条，或者六条也未可知。而这种衡鉴文学的困难，从你们入门之初，便内在于你们的研究。凡出过一张文学通论试卷并主持其考试的人——拟卷、批阅答卷，与那些关于语言、屈折变化等等的试卷并列——无不深深省悟：学生正是在此处遇到他的难点。在一张关于屈折变化等等的试卷上，记性中等偏牢的学生轻易便能拿到总分的六成或七成；而在一张以批评眼光考察某部书、某出戏的试卷上，考官纵然出卷时已存心把平均水准压低一档，也还须手下留情，才给得出总分的五成、四成、乃至三成。

===Original===
You will find a somewhat illuminating passage﻿—illuminating, that is, if you choose to interpret and apply it to our subject﻿—in Lucian’s “True History,” where the veracious traveller, who tells the tale, affirms that he visited Hades among other places, and had some conversation with Homer, among its many inhabitants﻿—

===Chinese===
你们会在琉善（Lucian）的《信史》（True History）里找到一段颇有照映之效的文字——所谓照映，是指你们若肯把它诠释一番、应用到我们的题目上——文中那位讲述故事、「据实而言」的旅人声称，他游历过冥府（Hades）等诸多地方，并在冥府的众多居民中间，同荷马（Homer）有过一席交谈——

===Original===
> Before many days had passed, I accosted the poet Homer, when we were both disengaged, and asked him, among other things, where he came from; it was still a burning question with us, I explained. He said he was aware that some derived him from Chios, others from Smyrna, and others again from Colophon; but in fact he was a Babylonian, generally known not as Homer but as Tigranes; but when later in life he was given as a *homer* or hostage to the Greeks, that name clung to him. Another of my questions was about the so-called spurious books; had he written them or not? He said that they were all genuine: so I now knew what to think of the critics Zenodotus and Aristarchus and all their lucubrations. Having got a categorical answer on that point, I tried him next on his reason for starting the *Iliad* with the wrath of Achilles. He said he had no exquisite reason; it just came into his head that way.

===Chinese===
> 没过几天，趁我俩都得闲，我便上前与诗人荷马攀谈，在诸多问题之外，问他原是何处人氏；我解释说，这在我们人间至今仍是个聚讼纷纭的问题。他说，他知道有人把他归于希俄斯（Chios），有人归于士麦那（Smyrna），还有人归于科洛丰（Colophon）；但其实他是个巴比伦人，通名并不叫荷马，而叫提格兰尼斯（Tigranes）；只因年长之后，他被当作 *homer*、即人质交给了希腊人，这名字才一直粘在他身上。我的另一个问题，是关于那些所谓的托名之作：究竟是不是他写的？他说，统统出自真笔：这一来，我明白了该怎样看待批评家芝诺多托斯（Zenodotus）与阿里斯塔库斯（Aristarchus），以及他们全部的熬夜辛苦。在这点上得了斩钉截铁的回答之后，我接着又问他：拿阿喀琉斯（Achilles）的愤怒作《伊利亚特》（Iliad）的开端，可有什么精妙的缘由？他说，并没有什么精妙的缘由；不过是脑子里碰巧那样起了头。

===Original===
Even so diverse are the questions that may be asked concerning any great work of art. But to discover its full intent is always the most difficult task of all. That task, however, and nothing less difficult, will always be the one worthiest of a great University.

===Chinese===
关于任何一件伟大的艺术品，可提的问题竟是这般纷纭。但要探明它的全部意图，始终是诸多任务中最艰巨的一桩。然而，正是这一桩，而非任何一桩更容易的，才永远是一所伟大学府最当得起的那一桩。

===Original===
On that, and on that alone, Gentlemen, do I base all claims for our School of English Literature. And yet in conclusion I will ask you, reminding yourselves how fortunate is your lot in Cambridge, to think of fellow-Englishmen far less fortunate.

===Chinese===
诸位先生，我为我们的英国文学学科（School of English Literature）所提的一切主张，全部建立在这一点上，而且仅建立在这一点上。然而临了，我还要请你们——自己记着你们在剑桥的命途何等有幸——去想想那些远不及你们有幸的英国同胞。

===Original===
Years ago I took some pains to examine the examination papers set by a renowned Examining Body and I found this﻿—“I humbly solicit” (to use a phrase of Lucian’s) “my hearers’ incredulity”﻿—that in a paper set upon three acts of *Hamlet*﻿—three acts of *Hamlet*!﻿—the first question started with “G.tt. p..cha” “Al..g.tor” and invited the candidate to fill in the missing letters correctly. Now I was morally certain that the words “gutta-percha” and “alligator” did not occur in the first three acts of *Hamlet*; but having carefully reread them I invited this examining body to explain itself. The answer I got was that, to understand Shakespeare, a student must first understand the English Language! Some of you on leaving Cambridge will go out﻿—a company of Christian folk dispersed throughout the world﻿—to tell English children of English Literature. Such are the pedagogic fetters you will have to knock off their young minds before they can stand and walk.

===Chinese===
多年前，我曾花过些力气，检视一家著名考试机构所出的试卷，结果发现了这样一桩事——「我谦卑地恳请」（借用琉善的一句话）「诸位听者切莫轻信」——在一份以《哈姆雷特》（Hamlet）三幕为范围的试卷上——《哈姆雷特》的三幕！——第一道题以「G.tt. p..cha」「Al..g.tor」开头，请考生把缺落的字母正确补齐。我本来拿得极稳：「gutta-percha」（古塔波胶）与「alligator」（短吻鳄）二词，断不会出现在《哈姆雷特》的前三幕里；但细读重核之后，我还是请这家考试机构自行解释。得到的答复是：要理解莎士比亚，学生必先理解英语！你们中间有些人离开剑桥之后，将走出去——作为散布世界各地的一群基督徒——向英伦的孩童讲授英国文学。这等便是教学套上的枷锁，你们非先从他们幼小的心智上敲落不可，他们才能站得起、走得动。

===Original===
Gentlemen, on a day early in this term I sought the mound which is the old Castle of Cambridge. Access to it, as perhaps you know, lies through the precincts of the County Prison. An iron railing encloses the mound, having a small gate, for the key of which a notice-board advised me to ring the prison bell. I rang. A very courteous gaoler answered the bell and opened the gate, which stands just against his wicket. I thanked him, but could not forbear asking “Why do they keep this gate closed?” “I don’t know, sir,” he answered, “but I suppose if they didn’t the children might get in and play.”

===Chinese===
诸位先生，本学期开初的一天，我去寻访那座土丘，即剑桥的古城堡。通往那里的路径，或许你们知道，须穿过郡监狱的地界。土丘四周围着铁栏杆，开有一扇小门；一块告示牌指点我，要钥匙可按监狱门铃。我按了铃。一位彬彬有礼的狱卒应铃前来，开了门——那门正贴着他的门房小窗。我谢过他，却忍不住问：「这扇门为什么老关着？」「我不知道，先生，」他答道，「不过我琢磨，要是不关，孩子们怕会钻进去玩耍。」

===Original===
So with his answer I went up the hill and from the top saw Cambridge spread at my feet; Magdalene below me, and the bridge which﻿—poor product as it is of the municipal taste﻿—has given its name to so many bridges all over the world; the river on its long ambit to Chesterton; the tower of St. John’s, and beyond it the unpretentious but more beautiful tower of Jesus College. To my right the magnificent chine of King’s College Chapel made its own horizon above the yellowing elms. I looked down on the streets﻿—the narrow streets﻿—the very streets which, a fortnight ago, I tried to people for you with that medieval throng which has passed as we shall pass. Still in my ear the gaoler’s answer repeated itself﻿—“*I suppose, if they didn’t keep it locked, the children might get in and play*”: and a broken echo seemed to take it up, in words that for a while had no more coherence than the scattered jangle of bells in the town below. But as I turned to leave, they chimed into an articulate sentence and the voice was the voice of Francis Bacon﻿—“*Regnum Scientiae ut regnum Coeli non nisi sub persona infantis intratur.*﻿—Into the Kingdom of Knowledge, as into the Kingdom of Heaven, whoso would enter must become as a little child.”

===Chinese===
就这样，揣着他的答话，我登上了山丘，从丘顶望见剑桥铺展在脚下；麦格达伦学院（Magdalene）在我下方，还有那座桥——它虽是市政趣味的可怜产物——却把它的大名赠给了全世界的许多桥梁；河流沿长长的弯道流往切斯特顿（Chesterton）；圣约翰学院的塔楼，以及塔后那座朴质而更美的耶稣学院（Jesus College）之塔。右手边，国王学院礼拜堂（King's College Chapel）壮丽的脊线，在泛黄的榆树上方自成一脉天际。我俯瞰那些街道——那些狭窄的街道——正是两星期前，我力图为你们移入那中世纪人群的街道；那人群已如我们终将逝去一般逝去了。狱卒的答话仍在我耳际萦回——「*我琢磨，要是不关着，孩子们怕会钻进去玩耍*」：一个断续的回声似乎把它接了过去，那些字句起初凌乱无序，恰似山下城中散乱的钟鸣。但当我转身欲去，它们忽然谐响成一句清晰的话，而那声音，是弗兰西斯·培根的声音——「*Regnum Scientiae ut regnum Coeli non nisi sub persona infantis intratur.*」——「进知识的王国，正如进天国的王国，凡要进去的，都必须变成小孩子的样式。」

## Lecture XII On Style / XII 论风格

===Original===
Wednesday, January 28, 1914

===Chinese===
1914 年 1 月 28 日，星期三

===Original===
Should Providence, Gentlemen, destine any one of you to write books for his living, he will find experimentally true what I here promise him, that few pleasures sooner cloy than reading what the reviewers say. This promise I hand on with the better confidence since it was endorsed for me once in conversation by that eminently good man the late Henry Sidgwick; who added, however, “Perhaps I ought to make a single exception. There was a critic who called one of my books ‘epoch-making.’ Being anonymous, he would have been hard to find and thank, perhaps; but I ought to have made the effort.”

===Chinese===
诸位先生，倘若天意注定你们中间有哪一位要以写书为生，他便会从经验里证实我在此向他许下的话：诸般快乐之中，很少有几样比阅读评论家们的高论更快令人腻味。这句许诺我转赠给你们时格外有信心，因为已故的那位大好人亨利·西奇威克（Henry Sidgwick）曾在一席谈话中亲口替我作过保；只是他补充道：「也许我该承认一个例外。曾有一位批评家，称我的一本书『划时代』。他既是匿名，要寻着他道谢，或许本非易事；但这番努力，我原是应当做的。」

===Original===
May I follow up this experience of his with one of my own, as a preface or brief apology for this lecture? Short-lived as is the author’s joy in his critics, far-spent as may be his hope of fame, mournful his consent with Sir Thomas Browne that “there is nothing immortal but immortality,” he cannot hide from certain sanguine men of business, who in England call themselves “Press-Cutting Agencies,” in America “Press-Clipping Bureaux,” and, as each successive child of his invention comes to birth, unbecomingly presume in him an almost virginal trepidation. “Your book,” they write falsely, “is exciting much comment. May we collect and send you notices of it appearing in the World’s Press? We submit a specimen cutting with our terms; and are, dear Sir,” etc.

===Chinese===
可否容我拿我自己的经历续上他的这段经历，权作本次讲座的引子或简短的辩白？作者得自批评家的快乐纵然短促，成名的指望纵然将尽，纵然他凄然附和托马斯·布朗爵士（Sir Thomas Browne）所言「除不朽之外，无物不朽」，他却躲不开某几家血气旺盛的生意人——在英格兰自称「剪报代理行」（Press-Cutting Agencies），在美国称「剪报局」（Press-Clipping Bureaux）——每逢他心智所出的新生儿一一坠地，他们便不甚得体地认定他又怀着一份近乎处女般的惶恐。「您的大作，」他们虚言相欺道，「正激起广泛评论。可否由我们收集世界各地报刊上的评介，寄奉尊览？随函谨呈剪报样品一纸暨价目章程；敬爱的先生，敝公司」云云。

===Original===
Now, although steadily unresponsive to this wile, I am sometimes guilty of taking the enclosed specimen review and thrusting it for preservation among the scarcely less deciduous leaves of the book it was written to appraise. So it happened that having this vacation, to dust﻿—not to read﻿—a line of obsolete or obsolescent works on a shelf, I happened on a review signed by no smaller a man than Mr. Gilbert Chesterton and informing the world that the author of my obsolete book was full of good stories as a kindly uncle, but had a careless or impatient way of stopping short and leaving his readers to guess what they most wanted to know: that, reaching the last chapter, or what he chose to make the last chapter, instead of winding up and telling “how everybody lived ever after,” he (so to speak) slid you off his avuncular knee with a blessing and the remark that nine o’clock was striking and all good children should be in their beds.

===Chinese===
如今，我虽对这套伎俩始终置之不理，有时却也难免有过：把随函附来的样张评论抽出来，塞进它所评的那本书里存着——那书页凋落的频繁，其实也不比叶子逊色多少。于是便有了这一桩事：这个假期，我去拂拭（并非去读）架上一排已废或将废的著作，偶然撞见一篇书评，署名者绝非小人物，正是吉尔伯特·切斯特顿先生（Mr. Gilbert Chesterton）；他向世人宣告：我那本已废之书的作者，满肚子好故事，像一位慈祥的叔叔，只是有个漫不经心或不耐烦的毛病——话到半途便戛然而止，撇下读者去猜他们最想知道的事：那就是，写到最后一章——或者说，写到他乐意当作最后一章的地方——他非但不把故事收拢，告诉人家「后来每个人一辈子过得怎样」，反而（不妨这么说）为你祝个福，道一声九点钟正在敲、好孩子们都该上床了，便把你从他叔叔的膝头放下地来。

===Original===
That criticism has haunted me during the vacation. Looking back on a course of lectures which I deemed to be accomplished; correcting them in print; revising them with all the nervousness of a beginner; I have seemed to hear you complain﻿—“He has exhorted us to write accurately, appropriately; to eschew Jargon; to be bold and essay Verse. He has insisted that Literature is a living art, to be practised. But just what we most needed he has not told. At the final doorway to the secret he turned his back and left us. Accuracy, propriety, perspicuity﻿—these we may achieve. But where has he helped us to write with beauty, with charm, with distinction? Where has he given us rules for what is called *Style* in short?﻿—having attained which an author may count himself set up in business.”

===Chinese===
假期里，那篇批评一直萦绕着我。回顾这一门自以为已然功德圆满的讲座；将它们付印勘校；怀着初学者的全部惶恐加以修订；我仿佛听见你们抱怨——「他劝勉我们写得准确、写得得体；叫我们避开套话（Jargon）；叫我们放大胆子去试作诗（Verse）。他坚持说文学是一门活的艺术，须得实践。可是我们最需要的东西，他偏偏没有讲。到了通向秘密的最后一道门跟前，他却转过身去，把我们撇下了。准确、得体、明晰——这些我们或许做得到。可是，他又在何处帮助我们写得美、写得迷人、写得卓然出众呢？总而言之，对那所谓风格（Style），他何曾给过我们一条规则？——得了它，一个作者便可自认为开业挂牌了。」

===Original===
Thus, Gentlemen, with my mind’s ear I heard you reproaching me. I beg you to accept what follows for my apology.

===Chinese===
诸位先生，我便是这样，用心灵的耳朵听见了你们的责备。以下的话，请权当我的辩白，望你们收纳。

===Original===
To begin with, let me plead that you have been told of one or two things which Style is *not*; which have little or nothing to do with Style, though sometimes vulgarly mistaken for it. Style, for example, is not﻿—can never be﻿—extraneous Ornament. You remember, may be, the Persian lover whom I quoted to you out of Newman: how to convey his passion he sought a professional letter-writer and purchased a vocabulary charged with ornament, wherewith to attract the fair one as with a basket of jewels. Well, in this extraneous, professional, purchased ornamentation, you have something which Style is not: and if you here require a practical rule of me, I will present you with this: “Whenever you feel an impulse to perpetrate a piece of exceptionally fine writing, obey it﻿—wholeheartedly﻿—and delete it before sending your manuscript to press. *Murder your darlings.*”

===Chinese===
首先，请容我申明：有一两样东西是风格（Style）所不是的，你们已经被告知过了；它们与风格关系甚微或全无关系，只是有时被庸俗地误认作风格。譬如，风格不是——永远也不可能是——外在的藻饰（Ornament）。你们或许还记得，我曾从纽曼（Newman）著作里给你们引过那个波斯情郎：为了传达他的热情，他去寻了一位职业代书人，购置了一整套满载藻饰的词汇，好用来吸引那位美人，仿佛提着一篮珠宝前去。好了，在这种外在的、职业的、花钱买来的装点之中，你们看到的正是风格所不是的东西：倘若你们此刻要我给一条实用的规则，我便奉上这一条：「每当你感到一阵冲动，要炮制一段格外漂亮的文字，那就服从它——全心全意地服从——然后在把稿子付印之前，把它删掉。杀死你的心头爱（*Murder your darlings*）。」

===Original===
But let me plead further that you have not been left altogether without clue to the secret of what Style is. That you must master the secret for yourselves lay implicit in our bargain, and you were never promised that a writer’s training would be easy. Yet a clue was certainly put in your hands when, having insisted that Literature is a living art, I added that therefore it must be personal and of its essence personal.

===Chinese===
但请容我进一步申明：风格是什么，这个秘密的线索，你们并非全然未曾到手。你们必须亲自参透这个秘密，这一点本已暗含在我们的约定之中；而且从来没有人应许过你们，一个作家的训练会是容易的。然而线索确曾交到你们手里——当我坚持说文学是一门活的艺术之后，我又补了一句：因此它必须是个人的，而且就其本质而言，是个人的。

===Original===
This goes very deep: it conditions all our criticism of art. Yet it conceals no mystery. You may see its meaning most easily and clearly, perhaps, by contrasting Science and Art at their two extremes﻿—say Pure Mathematics with Acting. Science as a rule deals with things, Art with man’s thought and emotion about things. In Pure Mathematics things are rarefied into ideas, numbers, concepts, but still farther and farther away from the individual man. Two and two make four, and fourpence is not ninepence (or at any rate four is not nine) whether Alcibiades or Cleon keep the tally. In Acting on the other hand almost everything depends on personal interpretation﻿—on the gesture, the walk, the gaze, the tone of a Siddons, the *rusé* smile of a Coquelin, the exquisite, vibrant intonation of a Bernhardt. “English Art?” exclaimed Whistler, “there is no such thing! Art is art and mathematics is mathematics.” Whistler erred. Precisely because Art is Art, and Mathematics is Mathematics and a Science, Art being Art can be English or French; and, more than this, must be the personal expression of an Englishman or a Frenchman, as a “Constable” differs from a “Corot” and a “Whistler” from both. Surely I need not labour this. But what is true of the extremes of Art and Science is true also, though sometimes less recognisably true, of the mean: and where they meet and seem to conflict (as in History) the impact is that of the personal or individual mind upon universal truth, and the question becomes whether what happened in the Sicilian Expedition, or at the trial of Charles I, can be set forth naked as an alegebraical sum, serene in its certainty, indifferent to opinion, uncoloured in the telling as in the hearing by sympathy or dislike, by passion or by character. I doubt, while we should strive in history as in all things to be fair, if history can be written in that colourless way, to interest men in human doings. I am sure that nothing which lies further towards imaginative, creative, Art can be written in that way.

===Chinese===
这一层涵义极深：它规定着我们全部的艺术批评。然而其中并无玄秘。把科学与艺术各推到其极端加以对照——譬如拿纯粹数学对照表演——你们或许最能轻易而清楚地看出它的意思。科学照例处理事物，艺术处理人对事物的思想与情感。在纯粹数学中，事物被提纯为观念、数字、概念，却仍旧一步步远离个体的人。二加二等于四，四便士不是九便士（或者无论如何，四不是九），管账的是亚西比德（Alcibiades）还是克里昂（Cleon），全不相干。反过来，在表演中，几乎一切都系于个人的诠释——系于一位西登斯（Siddons）的身段、步态、顾盼与声口，系于科克兰（Coquelin）那 *rusé*（狡黠）的微笑，系于贝尔纳（Bernhardt）那精妙而颤动的行腔。「英国艺术？」惠斯勒（Whistler）呼道，「没有这回事！艺术就是艺术，数学就是数学。」惠斯勒错了。恰恰因为艺术是艺术，而数学是数学、是一门科学，艺术之为艺术才可以是英国的或法国的；更进一层——它必定是一个英国人或一个法国人的个人表达，正如一幅「康斯太勃」（Constable）之异于一幅「柯罗」（Corot），而一幅「惠斯勒」又与此二者皆异。这一点想必无须我多费唇舌。但艺术与科学两端成立的道理，于其中间地带同样成立，尽管有时不那么容易辨认；而在二者相遇、似相冲突之处（如在历史学中），相撞击的乃是个人的、个体的心智与普遍的真理，问题于是变成：西西里远征中发生的事、查理一世（Charles I）受审时发生的事，能否像一道代数和那样赤裸裸地铺陈出来——确然安详，无动于议，讲述也罢、听闻也罢，一概不因同情或厌恶、激情或性格而着色。我们固然应当在历史中一如在万事中力求公正，我却怀疑，历史若用那种不染颜色的写法写出，还能不能叫人对人类的行事生出兴味。至于更靠近想象的、创造的艺术一侧的东西，我确信断不能用那种写法写成。

===Original===
It follows then that Literature, being by its nature personal, must be by its nature almost infinitely various. “Two persons cannot be the authors of the sounds which strike our ear; and as they cannot be speaking one and the same speech, neither can they be writing one and the same lecture or discourse.” *Quot homines tot sententiae.* You may translate that, if you will, “Every man of us constructs his sentence differently”; and if there be indeed any quarrel between Literature and Science (as I never can see why there should be), I for one will readily grant Science all her cold superiority, her ease in Zion with universal facts, so it be mine to serve among the multifarious race who have to adjust, as best they may, Science’s cold conclusions (and much else) to the brotherly give-and-take of human life.

===Chinese===
由此推论，文学既以个人为本性，也就必以近乎无限的多样为本性。「两个人不可能同为那敲击我们耳鼓的声音的作者；正如他们不可能说着同一篇话，他们也不可能写着同一篇讲演或论说。」*Quot homines tot sententiae*（有多少人，便有多少种见解）。你们若愿意，不妨把它译作「我们每个人构句的方式各不相同」；而倘若文学与科学之间当真有什么龃龉（我始终看不出为什么会有），我个人乐于把科学那全部冷峻的优越、她在普遍事实之间安坐锡安的自在，悉数拱手奉让，只消容我在那形形色色的族类中间效力——他们不得不把科学的冰冷结论（还有其余许多东西）尽力调停，安放进人类生活兄弟般的往来互济之中。

===Original===
*Quicquid agunt homines, votum, timor, ira, voluptas﻿ ﻿…* Is it possible, Gentlemen, that you can have read one, two, three or more of the acknowledged masterpieces of literature without having it borne in on you that they are great because they are alive, and traffic not with cold celestial certainties, but with men’s hopes, aspirations, doubts, loves, hates, breakings of the heart; the glory and vanity of human endeavour, the transience of beauty, the capricious uncertain lease on which you and I hold life, the dark coast to which we inevitably steer; all that amuses or vexes, all that gladdens, saddens, maddens us men and women on this brief and mutable traject which yet must be home for a while, the anchorage of our hearts? For an instance:﻿—

===Chinese===
*Quicquid agunt homines, votum, timor, ira, voluptas…*（人们的一切所为——祈愿、恐惧、愤怒、欢乐……）诸位先生，你们可曾读过一部、两部、三部乃至更多的公认文学杰作，却始终不曾被这样一个念头穿透：它们伟大，是因为它们活着；它们打交道的不是天界冷冰冰的确定性，而是人的希望与抱负、疑惑与爱憎、心碎肠断；是人类努力的荣耀与虚妄，是美的易逝，是你我持有生命的那纸任性而无定的租约，是我们无可回避地驶向的那片黑暗海岸；是在这短暂而变幻的航程上——它仍须充当我们的家宅一时、我们心灵的锚地——使你我这些男女或欢愉或烦恼、或喜或悲、或至于癫狂的一切？且举一例：——

===Original===
> Here lies a most beautiful lady,
 

 Light of step and heart was she:
 

 I think she was the most beautiful lady
 

 That ever was in the West Country.
> But beauty vanishes, beauty passes,
 

 However rare, rare it be;
 

 And when I crumble who shall remember
 

 That lady of the West Country?18

===Chinese===
> 这里长眠着一位最美丽的夫人，
> 她的脚步轻盈，心地也轻盈：
> 我想，在西部之乡曾有过的美人里，
> 她要算是最美丽的一位。
> 可是美会消殒，美会流逝，
> 任它再稀罕，再稀罕也罢；
> 待我化为尘土，谁还会记起
> 那位西部之乡的夫人？18

===Original===
Or take a critic﻿—a literary critic﻿—such as Samuel Johnson, of whom we are used to think as of a man artificial in phrase and pedantic in judgment. He lives, and why? Because, if you test his criticism, he never saw literature but as a part of life, nor would allow in literature what was false to life, as he saw it. He could be wrongheaded, perverse; could damn Milton because he hated Milton’s politics; on any question of passion or prejudice could make injustice his daily food. But he could not, even in a friend’s epitaph, let pass a phrase (however well turned) which struck him as empty of life or false to it. All Boswell testifies to this: and this is why Samuel Johnson survives.

===Chinese===
或者举一位批评家——一位文学批评家——比如塞缪尔·约翰逊（Samuel Johnson）：我们惯于把他想成一个措辞造作、判断迂腐的人。他至今活着，为什么？因为你们一旦检验他的批评，便会发现他从来不曾把文学看作别的，只看作生命的一部分；凡是他眼中虚假失真的东西，他一概不容其存在于文学之中。他可以执拗，可以乖张；可以因为憎恶弥尔顿（Milton）的政治而贬斥弥尔顿；在任何一个牵动激情或偏见的问题上，可以把不公当作日常的食粮。但即便是在一位朋友的墓志铭里，他也无法放过一个在他看来空洞无生命或虚假失真的句子——无论那句子雕琢得多么漂亮。鲍斯韦尔（Boswell）的全部记载都可为此作证：而这，正是塞缪尔·约翰逊至今犹存的原因。

---

===Original===
Now let me carry this contention﻿—that all Literature is personal and therefore various﻿—into a field much exploited by the pedant, and fenced about with many notice-boards and public warnings. “*Neologisms not allowed here*,” “*All persons using slang, or trespassing in pursuit of originality.﻿ ﻿…*”

===Chinese===
现在，让我把这一论旨——一切文学都是个人的，因而是多样的——带进一片被学究大力垦殖过的园地；那园子四周围着许多告示牌，张贴着公家的告诫：「*Neologisms not allowed here*」（此处禁用新词），「*All persons using slang, or trespassing in pursuit of originality.…*」（凡使用俚语、或为猎取新异而擅入者……）

===Original===
Well, I answer these notice-boards by saying that, literature being personal, and men various﻿—and even the Oxford English Dictionary being no canonical book﻿—man’s use or defiance of the dictionary depends for its justification on nothing but his success: adding that, since it takes all kinds to make a world, or a literature, his success will probably depend on the occasion. A few months ago I found myself seated at a bump-supper next to a cheerful youth who, towards the close, suggested thoughtfully, as I arose to make a speech, that, the bonfire (which of course he called the “bonner”) being due at nine-thirty o’clock, there was little more than bare time left for “langers and godders.” It cost me, who think slowly, some seconds to interpret that by “langers” he meant “Auld Lang Syne” and by “godders” “God Save the King.” I thought at the time, and still think, and will maintain against any schoolmaster, that the neologisms of my young neighbour, though not to be recommended for essays or sermons, did admirably suit the time, place, and occasion.

===Chinese===
对于这些告示牌，我的回答是：文学既是个人的，人是多样的——就连《牛津英语词典》（Oxford English Dictionary）也并非什么钦定经典——一个人使用词典也好、违抗词典也好，其正当与否，全然只系于他的成败：我还要补一句，既然要各色人等才凑得起一个世界、或一门文学，他的成败多半还要看场合。几个月前，我在一次碰杯庆功晚宴（bump-supper）上，恰与一位快活的青年邻座；席近尾声，我起身正要致辞，他体贴地提醒道：篝火（他当然管篝火叫「bonner」）定于九点半点燃，留给「langers and godders」的时间，已不过刚刚够用了。我这人思维迟缓，花了好几秒才参透：他所谓「langers」，指的是《友谊地久天长》（Auld Lang Syne），所谓「godders」，指的是《天佑国王》（God Save the King）。我当时便想，如今仍这样想，并且愿在任何一位校长面前坚持：我这位年轻邻座的新造词，虽说不宜举荐给论文或布道文，用在那时、那地、那场合，却是妙不可言地合宜。

===Original===
Seeing that in human discourse, infinitely varied as it is, so much must ever depend on *who* speaks, and to *whom*, in what mood and upon what occasion; and seeing that Literature must needs take account of all manner of writers, audiences, moods, occasions; I hold it a sin against the light to put up a warning against any word that comes to us in the fair way of use and wont (as “wire,” for instance, for a telegram), even as surely as we should warn off hybrids or deliberately pedantic impostors, such as “antibody” and “picture-drome”; and that, generally, it is better to err on the side of liberty than on the side of the censor: since by the manumitting of new words we infuse new blood into a tongue of which (or we have learnt nothing from Shakespeare’s audacity) our first pride should be that it is flexible, alive, capable of responding to new demands of man’s untiring quest after knowledge and experience. Not because it was an ugly thing did I denounce Jargon to you, the other day: but because it was a dead thing, leading no-whither, meaning naught. There is *wickedness* in human speech, sometimes. You will detect it all the better for having ruled out what is *naughty*.

===Chinese===
须知在人类的言谈之中——它本就无限多样——多少事情永远取决于说话的是谁、说给谁听、带着何种心绪、在何种场合；又须知文学不得不顾虑到形形色色的作者、听众、心绪与场合；因此我认为，凡循着使用与习惯的正当途径来到我们面前的词（例如以「wire」指电报），对它竖起禁令便是得罪光明的罪；正如对杂种词、对存心学究气的冒牌货——如「antibody」（抗体）与「picture-drome」（影剧场）——我们确当将其逐出一样；而且总的说来，宁可在放纵一边犯错，也不可在审查官一边犯错：因为唯有解放新词，我们才为这门语言注入新的血液——对这门语言（否则，我们便算对莎士比亚的胆量一无所学），我们首先引以为傲的，应当正是它的柔韧、它的鲜活、它回应人类对知识与经验那不知疲倦的求索所提出的新需求的能力。前些天我向你们痛斥套话，并非因为它丑陋，而是因为它是一件死物，引不到任何去处，说不出任何意义。人类的言语之中，有时确有*邪恶*（wickedness）。你们把*淘气*（naughty）的东西先行排除之后，反能把邪恶辨认得更加清楚。

===Original===
Let us err, then, if we err, on the side of liberty. I came, the other day, upon this passage in Mr. Frank Harris’s study of *The Man Shakespeare*:﻿—

===Chinese===
那么，倘若一定要犯错，就让我们在放纵一边犯错吧。前几天，我偶然读到弗兰克·哈里斯（Frank Harris）先生那部《莎士比亚其人》（The Man Shakespeare）中的一段：——

===Original===
> In the last hundred years the language of Molière has grown fourfold; the slang of the studios and the gutter and the laboratory, of the engineering school and the dissecting table, has been ransacked for special terms to enrich and strengthen the language in order that it may deal easily with the new thoughts. French is now a superb instrument, while English is positively poorer than it was in the time of Shakespeare, thanks to the prudery of our illiterate middle class.19

===Chinese===
> 近百年来，莫里哀（Molière）的语言已扩充到原先的四倍；画室、街衢与实验室的俚语，连同工程学校与解剖台的俚语，都被搜罗殆遍，取其专门术语来丰富、来增强这门语言，好让它从容应对新的思想。法语如今是一件精良的乐器；而英语呢，拜我们那不学无术的中产阶级的假正经所赐，竟比莎士比亚的时代还要贫乏。19

===Original===
Well, let us not lose our heads over this, any more than over other prophecies of our national decadence. The Oxford English Dictionary has not yet unfolded the last of its coils, which yet are ample enough to enfold us in seven words for every three an active man can grapple with. Yet the warning has point, and a particular point, for those who aspire to write poetry: as Francis Thompson has noted in his Essay on Shelley:﻿—

===Chinese===
好了，对这段话，我们不必失了方寸，正如不必对其他种种关于我们民族颓败的预言失了方寸。《牛津英语词典》还没有把它的躯体最末一圈盘绕舒展开来，而它现存的盘绕已绰绰有余——凡一个精力充沛的人所能对付的三个词之处，它自有七个词把我们层层裹住。然而这警告自有道理，而且对有志写诗的人另有一层特别的道理，正如弗朗西斯·汤普森（Francis Thompson）在《论雪莱》（Essay on Shelley）里指出的：——

===Original===
> Theoretically, of course, one ought always to try for the best word. But practically, the habit of excessive care in word-selection frequently results in loss of spontaneity; and, still worse, the habit of always taking the best word too easily becomes the habit of always taking the most ornate word, the word most removed from ordinary speech. In consequence of this, poetic diction has become latterly a kaleidoscope, and one’s chief curiosity is as to the precise combinations into which the pieces will be shifted. There is, in fact, a certain band of words, the Praetorian cohorts of Poetry, whose prescriptive aid is invoked by every aspirant to the poetic purple.﻿ ﻿… Against these it is time some banner should be raised.﻿ ﻿… It is at any rate curious to note that the literary revolution against the despotic diction of Pope seems issuing, like political revolutions, in a despotism of his own making;

===Chinese===
> 理论上，人自然应当永远力求最好的词。但实际做起来，选词过分矜慎的习惯，往往导致天机的丧失；更糟的是，永远取最好之词的习惯，太容易变成永远取最藻饰之词、即离寻常言语最远之词的习惯。其结果是，诗歌的语汇晚近已成了一只万花筒，人们主要的好奇，只在那些碎片究竟会转出怎样的具体组合。事实上，存在着一小队词，堪称诗歌的禁卫军团，凡觊觎诗人紫袍的人，无不按成例祈请它们相助。……该有一面旗帜竖起，向它们宣战了。……无论如何，有一桩事颇可玩味：那场针对蒲柏（Pope）专断词藻的文学革命，看来正如政治革命一般，正以一场革命自己造出的专制收场；

===Original===
and he adds a note that this is the more surprising to him because so many Victorian poets were prose-writers as well.

===Chinese===
他还在一条按语里补充说，这一点在他看来尤为可怪，因为维多利亚时代的诗人，有许多同时也是散文作家。

===Original===
> Now, according to our theory, the practice of prose should maintain fresh and comprehensive a poet’s diction, should save him from falling into the hands of an exclusive coterie of poetic words. It should react upon his metrical vocabulary to its beneficial expansion, by taking him outside his aristocratic circle of language, and keeping him in touch with the great commonalty, the proletariat of speech. For it is with words as with men: constant intermarriage within the limits of a patrician clan begets effete refinement; and to reinvigorate the stock, its veins must be replenished from hardy plebeian blood.

===Chinese===
> 依照我们的理论，散文的写作本应使诗人的语汇保持新鲜与宏通，本应救他免于落进一个垄断性的诗歌词藻小圈子的掌握。它本应反作用于他的格律词汇，使之得到有益的扩张——办法是带他走出语言的上流圈子，让他与伟大的平民大众、与言语的无产者保持接触。因为词与人同理：贵族氏族范围之内的世代通婚，只会生育出颓靡的精致；要使这一族重振生机，它的血脉便必须用强健的平民血液来补充。

===Original===
In diction, then, let us acquire all the store we can, rejecting no coin for its minting but only if its metal be base. So shall we bring out of our treasuries new things and old.

===Chinese===
那么，在措辞上，凡能积攒的储藏，让我们尽数积攒；一枚钱币，不因它的铸工而拒收，只在它的金属低劣时拒收。这样，我们便能从自己的库房里，拿出新的东西和旧的东西来。

===Original===
Diction, however, is but a part of Style, and perhaps not the most important part. So I revert to the larger question, “What is Style? What its *τὀ τἱ ἧν εἷναι*, its essence, the law of its being?”

===Chinese===
然而，措辞只是风格的一部分，或许还不是最重要的部分。于是让我回到那个更大的问题：「什么是风格？它的 *τὀ τἱ ἧν εἷναι*（是其所是）——它的本质、它存在的法则——是什么？」

===Original===
Now, as I sat down to write this lecture, memory evoked a scene and with the scene a chance word of boyish slang, both of which may seem to you irrelevant until, or unless, I can make you feel how they hold for me the heart of the matter.

===Chinese===
且说，当我坐下来写这篇讲稿时，记忆唤起了一个场景，又随场景唤出了一句少年人的俚语；这两样东西，在你们看来或许文不对题——除非、直到我能让你们感觉到，它们在我这里所攥住的，正是问题的核心。

===Original===
I once happened to be standing in a corner of a ballroom when there entered the most beautiful girl these eyes have ever seen or now﻿—since they grow dull﻿—ever will see. It was, I believe, her first ball, and by some freak or in some premonition she wore black: and not pearls﻿—which, I am told, maidens are wont to wear on these occasions﻿—but one crescent of diamonds in her black hair. *Et vera incessu patuit dea.* Here, I say, was absolute beauty. It startled.

===Chinese===
有一回，我恰巧站在舞厅的一角，这时进来了这位姑娘——这双眼睛见过的最美的姑娘，而且如今——既然它们日渐昏花——今后将见到的、最美的姑娘。我相信那是她生平第一场舞会；不知是出于一时奇想还是某种预感，她穿了一身黑：没有戴珍珠——听说少女在这种场合惯于戴珍珠——只在那头黑发间，簪着一弯新月似的钻石。*Et vera incessu patuit dea.*（观其步态，女神毕现。）我说，这便是绝对的美。它令人一震。

===Original===
> I think she was the most beautiful lady
 

 That ever was in the West Country.
 

 But beauty vanishes, beauty passes.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
> 我想，在西部之乡曾有过的美人里，
> 她要算是最美丽的一位。
> 可是美会消殒，美会流逝。……

===Original===
She died a year or two later. She may have been too beautiful to live long. I have a thought that she may also have been too good.

===Chinese===
一两年后，她便去世了。她或许是太美了，美得活不长久。我还有一个念头：她或许也太善良了。

===Original===
For I saw her with the crowd about her: I saw led up and presented among others the man who was to be, for a few months, her husband: and then, as the men bowed, pencilling on their programmes, over their shoulders I saw her eyes travel to an awkward young naval cadet (Do you remember Crossjay in Meredith’s *The Egoist*? It was just such a boy) who sat abashed and glowering sulkily beside me on the far bench. Promptly with a laugh, she advanced, claimed him, and swept him off into the first waltz.

===Chinese===
因为我见过她被人群簇拥着的模样：我见人们把那位男子——将做她几个月丈夫的男子——同旁人一起领上前来，引见给她；随后，当男子们躬身在节目单上用铅笔写名时，我越过他们的肩头，看见她的目光飘向一个局促不安的年轻海军见习生（你们可记得梅瑞狄斯（Meredith）《利己主义者》（The Egoist）里的克罗斯杰（Crossjay）？正是那样一个男孩），他坐在远处那条长凳上、就在我旁边，又窘又恼，郁郁地瞪着眼。她立刻笑着迎上前去，点定了他，把他一卷而走，带进了第一支华尔兹。

===Original===
When it was over he came back, a trifle flushed, and I felicitated him; my remark (which I forget) being no doubt “just the sort of banality, you know, one does come out with”﻿—as maybe that the British Navy kept its old knack of cutting out. But he looked at me almost in tears and blurted, “It isn’t her beauty, sir. You saw? It’s﻿—it’s﻿—my God, it’s the *style*!”

===Chinese===
一曲既终，他回来了，脸颊微微发红，我向他道贺；我的话（原话我已忘了）无疑「正是那种你知道的、人人都会脱口而出的陈词滥调」——譬如说，皇家海军夺船突袭的老绝技犹在，云云。可他望着我，眼里几乎含着泪，脱口说道：「先生，这不是她的美貌。您看见了吗？这是——这是——我的天哪，这是*风格*（style）！」

===Original===
Now you may think that a somewhat cheap, or at any rate inadequate, cry of the heart in my young seaman; as you may think it inadequate in me, and moreover a trifle capricious, to assure you (as I do) that the first and last secret of a good Style consists in thinking with the heart as well as with the head.

===Chinese===
你们也许觉得，我这位年轻水兵这一声发自心坎的呼喊，未免有些廉价，至少不够周全；你们也许同样觉得，我向你们保证（如今便保证）良好风格的第一重也是最后一重秘密，在于用心思索一如用脑思索——这在我也不够周全，而且未免有点心血来潮。

===Original===
But let us philosophise a little. You have been told, I daresay often enough, that the business of writing demands *two*﻿—the author and the reader. Add to this what is equally obvious, that the obligation of courtesy rests first with the author, who invites the séance, and commonly charges for it. What follows, but that in speaking or writing we have an obligation to put ourselves into the hearer’s or reader’s place? It is *his* comfort, *his* convenience, we have to consult. To *express* ourselves is a very small part of the business: very small and almost unimportant as compared with *impressing* ourselves: the aim of the whole process being to persuade.

===Chinese===
但让我们稍稍哲学一番。你们想必已被人告知过许多次：写作这桩事业需要两造——作者与读者。再添上一条同样显见的道理：礼貌的义务首先落在作者身上，因为是他发出这场会晤（séance）的邀约，而且通常还要为此收费。由此还能推出什么别的呢，除了这一点——我们在言谈或写作中，有义务把自己放进听者或读者的位置？我们必须顾念的，是他的舒适、他的便利。表达（to express）自己，在这桩事业里只占极小的份：比起把自己印上（to impress）人心，那份量小得几乎无关紧要：而这全过程的目的，端在说服。

===Original===
All reading demands an effort. The energy, the goodwill which a reader brings to the book is, and must be, partly expended in the labour of reading, marking, learning, inwardly digesting what the author means. The more difficulties, then, we authors obtrude on him by obscure or careless writing, the more we blunt the edge of his attention: so that if only in our own interest﻿—though I had rather keep it on the ground of courtesy﻿—we should study to anticipate his comfort.

===Chinese===
一切阅读都要求一份气力。读者携至书前的精力与善意，必然、也必定要部分地耗费在那桩劳作里——阅读、标记、记诵，并在内心消化作者的本意。那么，我们这些作者以晦涩或疏懒的笔墨横加给他的困难越多，便越是锉钝了他注意力的锋刃：所以，哪怕单为我们自身的利益——虽然我宁肯把这道理安放在礼貌的地基上——我们也该用心预筹他的安适。

===Original===
But let me go a little deeper. You all know that a great part of Lessing’s argument in his *Laoköon*, on the essentials of Literature as opposed to Pictorial Art or Sculpture, depends on this﻿—that in Pictorial Art or in Sculpture the eye sees, the mind apprehends, the whole in a moment of time, with the correspondent disadvantage that this moment of time is fixed and stationary; whereas in writing, whether in prose or in verse, we can only produce our effect by a series of successive small impressions, dripping our meaning (so to speak) into the reader’s mind﻿—with the correspondent advantage, in point of vivacity, that our picture keeps moving all the while. Now obviously this throws a greater strain on his patience whom we address. Man at the best is a narrow-mouthed bottle. Through the conduit of speech he can utter﻿—as you, my hearers, can receive﻿—only one word at a time. In writing (as my old friend Professor Minto used to say) you are as a commander filing out his battalion through a narrow gate that allows only one man at a time to pass; and your reader, as he receives the troops, has to reform and reconstruct them. No matter how large or how involved the subject, it can be communicated only in that way. You see, then, what an obligation we owe to him of order and arrangement; and why, apart from felicities and curiosities of diction, the old rhetoricians laid such stress upon order and arrangement as duties we owe to those who honour us with their attention. “*La clarté*,” says a French writer, “*est la politesse.*” *Χἁρισι καἰ σαφενεἱᾳ θῦε*, recommends Lucian. Pay your sacrifice to the Graces, and to *σαφήνεια*﻿—Clarity﻿—first among the Graces.

===Chinese===
但容我再往深处走一步。你们都知道，莱辛（Lessing）在《拉奥孔》（Laoköon）里论证文学之要义以别于绘画或雕塑，其论证的一大部分正系于这一点——在绘画或雕塑中，眼睛观看，心智把握，全盘呈现于时间的一瞬，其相应的短处是：这一瞬时间是固定而静止的；而在写作中，无论散文还是诗，我们都只能凭一连串前后相续的小印象产生效果，把我们的意思（不妨这么说）一滴滴注入读者的心中——其相应的长处，就鲜活而言，是我们的画面自始至终一直在动。这样一看，显然给我们所应对的那位听者的耐心加上了更重的负担。人充其量是一只窄口的瓶子。透过言语这道导管，他一次只能吐出一个词——正如你们，我的听众，一次只能接收一个词。在写作中（正如我的老友明托教授（Professor Minto）惯常说的），你就像一位指挥官，把他的营队从一道窄门里依次带出，那门一次只容一人通过；而你的读者一边接收这些队伍，一边还须把它们重新编列、重新构筑。不论题目何等宏大、何等繁复，它只能经由这一条路传达。于是你们便明白，在条理与安排上，我们对他负着怎样一笔义务；也便明白，为什么——撇开措辞的妙笔与奇趣不谈——古代的修辞学家们把条理与安排如此着重地奉为我们对那些以其注意力惠顾我们的人所负的责任。「*La clarté*，」一位法国作家说，「*est la politesse.*」（明晰即是礼貌。）琉善（Lucian）则荐举：*Χἁρισι καἰ σαφενεἱᾳ θῦε*（向美惠与明晰献祭）。请把你的祭品献给美惠三女神，也献给 *σαφήνεια*——明晰——美惠中的第一位。

===Original===
What am I urging? “That Style in writing is much the same thing as good manners in other human intercourse?” Well, and why not? At all events we have reached a point where Buffon’s often-quoted saying that “Style is the man himself” touches and coincides with William of Wykeham’s old motto that “Manners makyth Man”: and before you condemn my doctrine as inadequate listen to this from Coventry Patmore, still bearing in mind that a writer’s main object is to *impress* his thought or vision upon his hearer.

===Chinese===
我在力主什么？「写作中的风格，与人类其他交往中的良好举止，大抵是一回事？」好吧，为什么不是呢？无论如何，我们已抵达这样一个汇合点：布封（Buffon）那句常被引用的「风格即人」（the Style is the Man），与威廉·威克姆（William of Wykeham）的古老公言「Manners makyth Man」（举止造就人）在此相触而重合；在你们判我这学说不够周全之前，且听听考文垂·帕特莫尔（Coventry Patmore）的这一句，同时仍请记着：一个作者的主要目标，是把他的思想或视象印上听者的心。

===Original===
“There is nothing comparable *for moral force* to the charm of truly noble manners.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
「就*道德力量*而言，没有什么堪与真正高贵的举止之魅力相比。……」

===Original===
I grant you, to be sure, that the claim to possess a Style must be conceded to many writers﻿—Carlyle is one﻿—who take no care to put listeners at their ease, but rely rather on native force of genius to shock and astound. Nor will I grudge them your admiration. But I do say that, as more and more you grow to value truth and the modest grace of truth, it is less and less to such writers that you will turn: and I say even more confidently that the qualities of Style we allow them are not the qualities we should seek as a norm, for they one and all offend against Art’s true maxim of avoiding excess.

===Chinese===
我自然向你们承认：拥有风格的名分，必须许给许多作家——卡莱尔（Carlyle）便是一位——他们全不着意让听者安然自在，倒宁可倚仗天才的本能力量去震骇世人。我也不会吝惜你们对他们的钦佩。但我确实要说：随着你们越来越珍视真理、珍视真理那谦逊的优雅，你们转向这类作家的时候便会越来越少；我还更有把握地说：我们容让他们的那些风格品质，并不是我们应当奉为规范去追求的品质，因为他们无一例外，都触犯了艺术「避免过度」这条真正的准则。

===Original===
And this brings me to the two great *paradoxes* of Style. For the first (1)﻿—although Style is so curiously personal and individual, and although men are so variously built that no two in the world carry away the same impressions from a show, there is always a norm somewhere; in literature and art, as in morality. Yes, even in man’s most terrific, most potent inventions﻿—when, for example, in *Hamlet* or in *Lear* Shakespeare seems to be breaking up the solid earth under our feet﻿—there is always some point and standard of sanity﻿—a Kent or an Horatio﻿—to which all enormities and passionate errors may be referred; to which the agitated mind of the spectator settles back as upon its centre of gravity, its pivot of repose.

===Chinese===
这就把我引到风格的两重伟大悖论（paradoxes）上来了。第一重（一）——尽管风格如此奇异地专属个人与个体，尽管人的构造如此多样，以致世上没有两个人能从同一场演出带走完全相同的印象——某处总归存在一个规范；在文学与艺术中，一如在道德中。是的，哪怕在人类最可惊、最威势赫赫的创造里——例如在《哈姆雷特》（Hamlet）或《李尔王》（Lear）中，莎士比亚仿佛正在把我们脚下坚实的大地掀碎——也总有某个神志清明之点、某个清明之标准——一位肯特（Kent）或一位霍拉旭（Horatio）——一切骇人的巨谬与激情之误，都可以引到它跟前核裁；观众那被激荡的心智，也便退归于它，如同退归于自己的重心、自己安息的枢轴。

===Original===
(2) The second paradox, though it is equally true, you may find a little subtler. Yet it but applies to Art the simple truth of the Gospel, that he who would save his soul must first lose it. Though personality pervades Style and cannot be escaped, the first sin against Style as against good Manners is to obtrude or exploit personality. The very greatest work in Literature﻿—the *Iliad*, the *Odyssey*, the *Purgatorio*, *The Tempest*, *Paradise Lost*, the *Republic*, *Don Quixote*﻿—is all

===Chinese===
（二）第二重悖论虽同样真确，你们或许会觉得它微妙些。然而它不过是把福音书里那条简单的真理施于艺术：凡要救自己灵魂的，必先丧掉它。个性虽弥漫于风格之中、无从逃遁，但触犯风格的第一宗罪——一如触犯良好举止——恰恰在于强行凸显个性、或贩卖个性。文学中最伟大的那些作品——《伊利亚特》（Iliad）、《奥德赛》（Odyssey）、《炼狱篇》（Purgatorio）、《暴风雨》（The Tempest）、《失乐园》（Paradise Lost）、《理想国》（Republic）、《堂吉诃德》（Don Quixote）——全都

===Original===
> Seraphically free
 

 From taint of personality.

===Chinese===
> 撒拉弗般自由，
> 不沾一点个性的痕迹。

===Original===
And Flaubert, that gladiator among artists, held that, at its highest, literary art could be carried into pure science. “I believe,” said he, “that great art is scientific and impersonal. You should by an intellectual effort transport yourself into characters, not draw *them* into *yourself*. That at least is the method.” On the other hand, says Goethe, “We should endeavour to use words that correspond as closely as possible with what we feel, see, think, imagine, experience, and reason. It is an endeavour we cannot evade and must daily renew.” I call Flaubert’s the better counsel, even though I have spent a part of this lecture in attempting to prove it impossible. It at least is noble, encouraging us to what is difficult. The shrewder Goethe encourages us to exploit ourselves to the top of our bent. I think Flaubert would have hit the mark if for “impersonal” he had substituted “disinterested.”

===Chinese===
而福楼拜（Flaubert）——艺术家中的那位角斗士——则主张，文学艺术至其极处，可以被推进纯粹科学之境。「我相信，」他说，「伟大的艺术是科学的、非个人的。你应当凭一番智识上的努力，把自己移置到人物里面去，而不是把*他们*拉进*你*里面来。这至少是方法。」另一方面，歌德（Goethe）说：「我们应当努力使用这样的词：它们尽可能贴切地对应于我们所感、所见、所思、所想象、所经历、所推证者。这一努力我们无从规避，且必须日日更新。」我称福楼拜的忠告为更好的忠告，尽管本讲的一部分光阴，我已用于试图证明它行不通。它至少是高贵的，激励我们奔赴艰难。更精明的歌德，则激励我们把自己驱遣到才性的极限。我想，福楼拜若把「非个人的」换成「无私的」（disinterested），他便正中鹄的了。

===Original===
For﻿—believe me, Gentlemen﻿—so far as Handel stands above Chopin, as Velasquez above Greuze, even so far stand the great masculine objective writers above all who appeal to you by parade of personality or private sentiment.

===Chinese===
因为——诸位先生，请相信我——正如亨德尔（Handel）之高出于肖邦（Chopin），委拉斯开兹（Velasquez）之高出于格勒兹（Greuze），那些阳刚而客观的伟大作家，也正是以同样的程度，高出于一切靠炫示个性或私人情感来打动你们的人。

===Original===
Mention of these great masculine “objective” writers brings me to my last word: which is, “Steep yourselves in *them*: habitually bring all to the test of *them*: for while you cannot escape the fate of all style, which is to be personal, the more of catholic manhood you inherit from those great loins the more you will assuredly beget.”

===Chinese===
提到这些阳刚的「客观」作家，便把我引到最后的一句话上，那就是：「浸淫于*他们*之中：习惯成自然地把一切拿到*他们*面前去检验：因为你们既逃不脱一切风格共同的命运——个人化——那么，你们从那些伟大的腰胯所承继到的普世男子气概越多，你们所确然诞育的也就越多。」

---

===Original===
This then is Style. As technically manifested in Literature it is the power to touch with ease, grace, precision, any note in the gamut of human thought or emotion.

===Chinese===
这，便是风格。就其在文学中的技艺显现而言，它是这样一种能力：能以从容、优雅、精确，触到人类思想或情感全音域中的任何一个音符。

===Original===
But essentially it resembles good manners. It comes of endeavouring to understand others, of thinking for them rather than for yourself﻿—of thinking, that is, with the heart as well as the head. It gives rather than receives; it is nobly careless of thanks or applause, not being fed by these but rather sustained and continually refreshed by an inward loyalty to the best. Yet, like “character” it has its altar within; to that retires for counsel, from that fetches its illumination, to ray outwards. Cultivate, Gentlemen, that habit of withdrawing to be advised by the best. So, says Fénelon, “you will find yourself infinitely quieter, your words will be fewer and more effectual; and while you make less ado, what you do will be more profitable.”

===Chinese===
但就其本质而言，它类似良好的举止。它生于体谅他人的努力，生于为他人着想甚于为自己着想——也就是说，生于用心思索，一如用脑思索。它施予而不受取；它对感谢或掌声抱着高贵的淡漠，不以这些为食，而是靠一种对至善的内心忠诚来支撑、来不断焕新。然而，它也如「品格」一般，在内里设着一座祭坛；退隐到坛前领受教诲，从坛上领取光明，再向外辐射出去。诸位先生，请培养这种退隐下来、受至善教诲的习惯。如此——费奈隆（Fénelon）说——「你将发现自己安详得不可计量，你的言语将更少而更有效验；你少一番张罗，你所做的便多一分益处。」

## Endnotes / 尾注

===Original===
1. The date of the above lecture was Wednesday, February 12th, 1913, the date on which our morning newspapers printed the first telegrams giving particulars of the fate of Captain Scott’s heroic conquest of the South Pole, and still more glorious, though defeated, return. The first brief message concerning Captain Oates, ran as follows:—

“From the records found in the tent where the bodies were discovered it appeared that Captain Oates’s feet and hands were badly frostbitten, and, although he struggled on heroically, his comrades knew on March 16 that his end was approaching. He had borne intense suffering for weeks without complaint, and he did not give up hope to the very end.

“ ‘He was a brave soul. He slept through the night hoping not to wake; but he awoke in the morning.

“ ‘It was blowing a blizzard. Oates said: “I am just going outside, and I may be some time.” He went out into the blizzard, and we have not seen him since.

“ ‘We knew that Oates was walking to his death, but though we tried to dissuade him, we knew it was the act of a brave man and an English gentleman.’”

↩︎

===Chinese===
1. 上述讲座的日期是 1913 年 2 月 12 日，星期三——正是在这一天，我们的各家晨报刊出了首批电讯，详述斯科特上校（Captain Scott）英勇征服南极、及其虽败犹更荣耀的归程的命运。关于奥茨上尉（Captain Oates）的第一则简短电讯如下：——

「从发现遗体的帐篷中找到的记录来看，奥茨上尉的手脚冻伤严重；尽管他一路英勇挣扎前行，战友们已在 3 月 16 日明白，他的大限将至。数周以来，他忍受着极大的痛苦而毫无怨言，直到最后关头也不曾放弃希望。

「『他是一个勇敢的灵魂。他睡了一夜，只盼着不再醒来；可是清晨他还是醒了。

「『外面正刮着暴风雪。奥茨说：「我到外面去一下，也许要过些时候。」他走进了暴风雪，从此我们再没有见到他。

「『我们知道奥茨这一去是走向死亡；但尽管我们曾试图劝阻，我们也知道，这是一个勇敢的人、一个英国绅士的作为。』」

↩︎

===Original===
7. I append the following specimen translations of the famous passage in St. Paul’s First Epistle to the Corinthians 15:51 sqq. I choose this because (1) it is an important passage; (2) it touches a high moment of philosophising; (3) the comparison seems to me to represent with great fairness to Tyndale the extent of the forty-seven’s debt to him; (4) it shows that they meant exactly what they said in their Preface; and (5) it illustrates, towards the close, their genius for improvement. From the Greek, Wyclif translates:—

Lo, I seie to you pryvyte of holi thingis | and alle we schulen rise agen | but not alle we schuln be chaungid | in a moment in the twynkelynge of an yë, in the last trumpe | for the trumpe schal sowne: and deed men schulen rise agen with out corrupcion, and we schuln be changid | for it bihoveth this corruptible thing to clothe uncorropcion and this deedly thing to putte aweye undeedlynesse. But whanne this deedli thing schal clothe undeedlynesse | thanne schal the word be don that is written | deeth is sopun up in victorie | deeth, where is thi victorie? deeth, where is thi pricke?

Tyndale:—

Beholde I showe you a mystery. We shall not all slepe: but we shall all be chaunged | and that in a moment | and in the twinclinge of an eye | at the sounde of the last trompe. For the trompe shall blowe, and the deed shall ryse incorruptible and we shalbe chaunged. For this corruptible must put on incorruptibilite: and this mortall must put on immortalite. When this corruptible hath put on incorruptibilite | and this mortall hath put on immortalite: than shalbe brought to pass the saying that is written, “Deeth is consumed in to victory.” Deeth, where is thy stynge? Hell, where is thy victory?

The Authorised Version:—

Behold, I show you a mystery; we shall not all sleepe, but wee shall all be changed, in a moment, in the twinckling of an eye, at the last trumpe, (for the trumpet shall sound, and the dead shall be raised incorruptible, and we shall be changed). For this corruptible must put on incorruption, and this mortall must put on immortalitie. So when this corruptible shall have put on incorruption, and this mortall shall have put on immortalitie, then shall be brought to passe the saying that is written, “Death is swallowed up in victory.” O Death, where is thy sting? O grave, where is thy victory?

↩︎

===Chinese===
7. 兹将圣保罗《哥林多前书》（First Epistle to the Corinthians）15:51 及以下各节这段著名经文的几种代表性译文附录于此。我选这段经文，是因为：（1）它是一段重要经文；（2）它触及哲学玄思的一个高昂时刻；（3）在我看来，这一对照对廷代尔（Tyndale）极为公允地显示了「四十七人」对他亏欠之深；（4）它表明他们在序言中所言确乎出于本心；（5）临近结尾处，它还例示了他们把好的变得更好的天才。威克里夫自希腊文译作：——

看哪，我把一件圣事之秘告诉你们：我们众人都要复起，却不是众人都要改变——在一瞬之间、一眨眼之间、末次号声之中；因为号筒必要吹响：死了的人要毫无朽坏地复起，我们都要改变。因为这必朽坏的必须穿上不朽坏，这必死的必须脱下不死。但当这必死的穿上不死之时，那写下的语句就要应验：死亡被吮进了胜利之中。死亡啊，你的胜利在哪里？死亡啊，你的刺在哪里？

廷代尔：——

看哪，我把一件奥秘告诉你们：我们不是都要睡觉，乃是都要改变——就在一瞬之间、一眨眼之间、末次号筒吹响之声中；因为号筒必要吹响，死了的人要复活成为不朽坏的，我们也要改变。这必朽坏的必须穿上不朽坏，这必死的必须穿上不死。这必朽坏的既穿上了不朽坏，这必死的既穿上了不死，那时所记的语句就要应验：「死亡被耗尽，归入了胜利。」死亡啊，你的毒刺在哪里？阴间啊，你的胜利在哪里？

钦定本：——

我如今把一件奥秘的事告诉你们：我们不是都要睡觉，乃是都要改变，就在一霎时，眨眼之间，号筒末次吹响的时候；因号筒要响，死人要复活成为不朽坏的，我们也要改变。这必朽坏的总要穿上不朽坏的，这必死的总要穿上不死的。这必朽坏的既穿上了不朽坏的，这必死的既穿上了不死的，那时经上所记的话就应验了：「死被得胜吞灭了。」死啊，你的毒钩在哪里？坟墓啊，你的得胜在哪里？

↩︎

10. Hrothgar spake, helm of the Scyldings: “Ask not after good tidings. Sorrow is renewed among the Dane-folk. Dead is Æschere, Yrmenlaf’s elder brother, who read me rune and bore me rede; comrade at shoulder when we fended our heads in war and the boar-helms rang. Even so should we each be an atheling passing good, as Æschere was.” ↩︎

===Chinese===
10. 赫罗斯加（Hrothgar）开口了，这位斯基尔丁人（Scyldings）的护主说道：「不要探问什么好消息了。悲哀又在丹麦人中重新生起。埃施雷尔（Æschere）死了——耶尔门拉夫（Yrmenlaf）的兄长；他曾为我解读卢恩符文、为我献上谋略；在战阵中我们各自护卫头颅、野猪纹头盔铿然相击之际，他是与我抵肩并肩的战友。同样，我们每个人都应当成为一名格外出众的贵胄，一如埃施雷尔。」 ↩︎

===Original===
19. “An oration,” says Quintilian, “may find room for almost any word saving a few indecent ones (*quae sunt parum verecunda*).” He adds that writers of the Old Comedy were often commended even for these: “but it is enough for us to mind our present business—*sed nobis nostrum opus intueri sat est.*” ↩︎

===Chinese===
19. 昆体良（Quintilian）说：「一篇演说辞几乎容得下任何词语，只除少数几个不雅之词（*quae sunt parum verecunda*）。」他又补充道，旧喜剧（Old Comedy）的作家们甚至常因用了这些词而受人称许：「但对我们来说，照管好自己眼下的本分就足够了——*sed nobis nostrum opus intueri sat est.*」 ↩︎
